To Dr. John Elliotson,

My Dear Doctor,

Thirteen months back, when it looked like this tale was about to end on a very low note, a good soul dragged you up to my bedside. Honestly, I probably wouldn't have gotten out of bed again if it hadn't been for your tireless watching and your fancy medical tricks. I just love remembering your huge kindness (along with the surprising amount of sympathy shown by others) during those dark days when kindness was the only thing anyone wanted.

Since you refused any payment but a heartfelt thank-you, let me write it down right here for me and my whole family. And please accept my deepest gratitude.

Yours most sincerely and gratefully,

W. M. Thackeray.

If this collection of scribbles, representing two years of my rather chaotic output, fails to dazzle you with artistic brilliance—which it almost certainly does, given the laws of physics and literature alike—it at least boasts a certain rugged honesty that a polished masterpiece might sacrifice. By forcing myself into a constant, gossipy dialogue with you, the reader, I am compelled to speak my mind exactly as it rattles around in my skull. I see every typo, every hasty phrase, and every printer's error that I would dearly love to erase, but alas, the ink is dry. It is a sort of intimate, confessional chat between us, one that is liable to be dull, repetitive, and occasionally embarrassing. In my endless chatter, I inevitably expose my own pettiness, my vanity, and my peculiar quirks. Just as you judge a man's character not by a single outburst or a particularly sunny Tuesday, but by the general tenor of his life, so too should you judge a writer who throws himself at your feet without a filter. The question remains: Is he honest? Does he strive for the truth, or is he merely a charlatan mouthing empty sentiments for applause? Am I chasing popularity with cheap tricks? I cannot ignore the good fortune that has landed me thousands of readers far beyond my wildest dreams. I have no right to demand you stay awake or praise my craft, but I do beg you to believe that the person writing this strives to tell the truth. Without that, there is absolutely nothing here worth your time.

Perhaps those who crave "excitement" wish to know that this book originally began with a meticulously plotted scheme that was promptly discarded. Ladies and gentlemen, you were initially promised a thrilling saga of high-stakes horror. Imagine a ruffian (a man of surprising virtues, mind you) lurking in the slums of St. Giles, constantly pursued by a debutante from Belgravia! Think of the electric contrast between high society and the gutter, the clash of slang and polite parlance, the daring escapes, the fistfights, and the potential for murder! Indeed, as late as nine o'clock this morning, my poor friend Colonel Altamont was scheduled for execution, and I only spared him when he appeared at the window, looking rather undignified.

However, this "thriller" plan was abandoned—with the very noble restraint of my publishers—because, upon attempting it, I discovered I lacked the requisite expertise. Having never been close enough to a convict to smell his breath, and finding the manners of gaol-birds utterly alien to me, I decided that competing with the great M. Eugène Sue was a fool's errand. To describe a real scoundrel, one must paint him so horrifically that he becomes too ugly to display; unless an artist paints a villain fairly, I maintain they have no right to show him at all.

Even the gentlemen of our age—this is an attempt to sketch one such man, neither better nor worse than the average educated soul—we cannot reveal as they truly are, with their notorious selfishness and foibles. Since the burial of the author of *Tom Jones*, no fiction writer among us has been permitted to depict a MAN to his fullest power. We must drape him in fine clothes and force him to wear a conventional, smiling mask. Society simply will not tolerate the "Natural" in our art. Many ladies have chided me, and some subscribers have fled, simply because, in the course of the narrative, I dared to describe a young man resisting temptation and feeling its pull.

My true objective was to suggest that he possessed the passions to feel deeply, yet the manliness and generosity to overcome them. You will not hear—and it is best you know this—what actually moves in the real world, what transpires in clubs, colleges, and mess rooms; what is the genuine life and talk of your sons. A little more frankness than is customary has been attempted in this story. With no malicious intent on my part, and hoping for no ill consequences for any reader, I offer it to you. If truth is not always pleasant, at any rate, it is the best thing we have. From whatever chair a grave thinker argues, or from the humble seat where a storyteller concludes his labor and bids his kind reader farewell, the message remains the same: Speak the truth.

KENSINGTON, Nov. 26th, 1850.

It was a brilliant morning in the height of the London season, the kind of day that makes the city sparkle like a jeweled box. Major Arthur Pendennis, a man whose very presence seemed to command the air, strode into his favorite club in Pall Mall. To the uninitiated, he was simply a retired officer; to the rest of London, he was the institution itself. As the club's premier connoisseur of vintage wines and a man of such vigorous, inquiring spirit that he effectively ran the place, the stewards and waiters bowed to him with the deference usually reserved for Dukes or Field Marshals.

At ten-thirty, punctual as a clockwork mechanism, Pendennis arrived. He was a study in perfection: boots polished to a mirror shine, a morning cravat crisp enough to cut glass, and a buff waistcoat bearing the royal crown on its buttons. His linen was so immaculate that Beau Brummel, had he not fled the country in disgrace, would have hired the Major's laundryman immediately. From his whiskers to his cane, every item was a flawless specimen of military fashion *en retraite*. To a stranger, he looked barely thirty; a closer look revealed the artificiality of his rich brown hair and the faint crow's-feet around his handsome, mottled face, but his Wellington-pattern nose and long, white-gloved hands remained undeniably aristocratic.

He claimed his usual corner table, a fortress no one dared breach. In the past, a few reckless youths had tried to evict him, but the sheer, quiet dignity with which the Major settled in, surveying the room with his gold-rimmed spectacles, made it clear that no one could breakfast under his gaze. His table was a shrine to social importance. Letters were laid out in anticipation of his arrival, a mountain of correspondence that baffled the younger set. If there was any doubt about etiquette, marital status, or the age of a Duke, Pendennis was the oracle. Even Marchionesses drove up to the club specifically to leave notes for him or fetch him out. He was affable, charming, and treated every man he met as a peer.

While the waiters brought his toast and the morning paper, Pendennis surveyed his mail. He carried his spectacles with such casual flair that they looked like accessories rather than tools of vision. He sorted through the pile: solemn dinner cards promising heavy conversation, intimate notes from women, and a formal invitation from the Marquis of Steyne to a French-speaking party at Richmond. There was also a request from the Bishop of Ealing and Mrs. Trail. He read them all with graceful satisfaction, enjoying the envy of Glowry, the Scotch surgeon sitting opposite. Glowry, who received no such invitations, watched with a mixture of hatred and admiration as the Major dismissed the letters, checking his pocket-book to decide which engagements to accept and which to decline.

He tossed aside the invitation from Cutler, the East India Director, in favor of Lord Steyne's French party. He accepted the Bishop, reasoning that bishops always made for slow, dignified dinners. With his schedule arranged, he ate his breakfast, scanned the gazette for his name among the guests of the season, and chatted cheerfully with his acquaintances.

Amidst the glittering correspondence of high society, however, lay one solitary letter. It sat apart, marked with a country postmark and a humble seal, unlike the grand notes from the grandees who had already driven away. It was written in a delicate female hand, stamped "Immediate" with a dash of anxiety. For reasons of his own, the Major had neglected this humble rural petitioner, allowing her to wait in the social ante-chamber while the nobility were served.

Finally, it was her turn. The Major broke the seal, which bore the engraving of "Fairoaks."

"My dear Major Pendennis," the letter began, "I beg and implore you to come to me *immediately*." Pendennis paused, expecting a summons to Steyne's dinner. "I am in the very greatest grief and perplexity. My dearest boy... is grieving me *dreadfully*. He has formed—an infatuation—for an actress who has been performing here. She is at least twelve years older than Arthur—who will not be eighteen till next February—and the wretched boy insists upon marrying her."

"Hay! What's making Pendennis swear now?" Glowry muttered to himself, witnessing the Major's jaw drop in open-mouthed shock.

"Do, my dear friend," the letter continued, "come to me instantly... and entreat, command, the wretched child to give up this most deplorable resolution." The letter concluded with the signature of his "unhappy affectionate sister, Helen Pendennis."

"Fairoaks, Tuesday," the Major grumbled, finishing the letter. "A d—d pretty business at Fairoaks, Tuesday; now let us see what the boy has to say." He picked up the second letter, written in a frantic, floundering boy's hand and sealed with a massive Pendennis signet, splattered with extra wax to show the writer's trembling agitation.

The epistle read:

*In informing you of my engagement with Miss Costigan, daughter of J. Chesterfield Costigan, Esq., of Costiganstown, but, perhaps, better known to you under her professional name of Miss Fotheringay, of the Theatres Royal Drury Lane and Crow Street, and of the Norwich and Welsh Circuit, I am aware that I make an announcement which cannot, according to the present prejudices of society at least, be welcome to my family...*

*My dearest mother... is deeply moved and grieved... by the intelligence which I have this night conveyed to her. I beseech you, my dear Sir, to come down and reason with her and console her. Although obliged by poverty to earn an honourable maintenance by the exercise of her splendid talents, Miss Costigan's family is as ancient and noble as our own. When our ancestor, Ralph Pendennis, landed with Richard II. in Ireland, my Emily's forefathers were kings of that country...*

*It is in vain I have attempted to argue with my dear mother... my poor mother has prejudices which it is impossible for my logic to overcome... Although Miss Costigan is some years older than myself, that circumstance does not operate as a barrier to my affection... A love like mine, Sir, I feel, is contracted once and for ever. As I never had dreamed of love until I saw her—I feel now that I shall die without ever knowing another passion. It is the fate of my life...*

*I press for a speedy marriage with my Emily—for why, in truth, should it be delayed? A delay implies a doubt, which I cast from me as unworthy. It is impossible that my sentiments can change towards Emily... I entreat you, my dear Uncle, to come down and reconcile my dear mother to our union, and I address you as a man of the world, *qui mores hominum multorum vidit et urbes*, who will not feel any of the weak scruples and fears which agitate a lady who has scarcely ever left her village.*

*Pray, come down to us immediately. I am quite confident that—apart from considerations of fortune—you will admire and approve of my Emily.*

When the Major finished reading, his countenance twisted into an expression of pure rage and horror. Glowry, the surgeon, reached for his lancet in his card-case, fearing his friend was having a stroke. The news was staggering: the head of the Pendennis house, a man of his standing, going to marry an actress ten years his senior—a headstrong boy plunging blindly into matrimony.

"The mother has spoiled the young rascal," the Major groaned inwardly. "With her cursed sentimentality and romantic rubbish. My nephew marry a tragedy queen! Gracious mercy, people will laugh at me so that I shall not dare show my head!" He felt a sharp pang of regret; he would have to cancel Lord Steyne's dinner at Richmond, lose his rest, and spend the night in a foul mail-coach instead of enjoying the select society of England.

He realized he couldn't just cancel one dinner; he would have to cancel them all for some time. Who knew how long this domestic crisis would drag on? Quitting his breakfast table, he retreated to the adjoining writing-room. There, with a rueful sigh, he penned refusals to the Marquis, the Earl, the Bishop, and every other host who had invited him. He then ordered his servant to book seats in the mail-coach for that very evening, naturally charging the cost to the account of the widow and the young scapegrace he was forced to guard.

A Pedigree and Other Family Matters

In the early days of the reign of George the Magnificent, a certain gentleman named Pendennis resided in the quiet town of Clavering, tucked away in the west of England. To those with long memories, his name was etched onto a signboard above a humble shop in Bath, crowned by a gilded pestle and mortar. Here, Mr. Pendennis practiced the dual arts of apothecary and surgeon. He was a man of considerable versatility: attending to the sick in their chambers, assisting ladies during their most delicate moments, and yet, with a condescension that bordered on the comical, selling brown-paper plasters to farmwives or vending toothbrushes and London perfumes to anyone who wandered across his counter.

Yet, beneath the apron and the mortar, John Pendennis was a man of high breeding and ancient lineage. His Cornish pedigree traced the Pendennises back to the Druids, intermingled with the Normans and kin to the great houses of Wales and Brittany. He possessed a university education that might have launched him into glory, had not fate intervened. In his second year at Cambridge, his father died insolvent, forcing poor Pen to abandon his scholarly dreams for the pestle and apron. He loathed the trade, viewing it as a necessary evil imposed by circumstance and the marriage of his father to a London apothecary of lowly stock.

After a brief apprenticeship with a coarse-minded relative, John set up his own modest practice in Bath. Poverty gnawed at him for some time; he struggled to maintain his shop's gilt ornaments and keep his bedridden mother comfortable. Then came the fateful encounter with Lady Ribstone. Passing through with an intoxicated Irish chairman who carelessly drove a chair-pole through a pink bottle in his window, she collapsed screaming. John, with manners uncommonly soothing for a man of his trade, revived her with cinnamon and sal-volatile. Lady Ribstone, the wife of Sir Pepin Ribstone of Codlingbury, was so charmed by his gentleness that she appointed him her personal apothecary. When her son, Master Ribstone, fell ill with a fever after overeating at Eton, John treated him with such skill and tenderness that he secured the enduring favor of the Codlingbury family.

From that day, his fortunes turned. The good company of Bath took notice; he was beloved by the ladies. His humble shop transformed into a smart establishment, then shed its unworthy merchandise of toothbrushes and perfumes. Soon, he shuttered the shop entirely, retaining only a genteel surgery. He acquired a gig, and before his mother's passing, she watched with joyous tears as her beloved John stepped into a private carriage, albeit a one-horse affair, proudly displaying the Pendennis arms. "What would Arthur say now?" she lamented, thinking of her younger son, who had never visited during his father's struggles.

"Captain Pendennis is with his regiment in India, mother," John replied gently, requesting she cease calling him "Johnny" before Mr. Parkins.

When his mother eventually passed, the house grew lonely without her querulous voice. John altered his night-bell to her former room and took up residence in the great bed there. He was over forty, a man who had lived before the war and before the current history began, yet he was a gentleman with a pedigree to match. He displayed his framed genealogy prominently in his drawing-room, alongside pictures of Codlingbury House and St. Boniface's College, treating his ancestry with the same reverence a soldier treats his sword.

Around the time of his mother's death, another patient, the virtuous Lady Pontypool, also passed away. She was the widow of the second Lord Pontypool and the great-grand-aunt of the present Earl of Bareacres. For her final years, she had been cared for by Miss Helen Thistlewood, a distant relative who endured the elder lady's tyranny with angelic patience. It was during the journey to the abbey for Lady Pontypool's funeral that John Pendennis, a collected thirty-four-year-old in black stockings and a bald head, resolved to ask Miss Thistlewood a question that made his pulse race.

Older than her by more than twenty years and not naturally ardent, John approached the situation with the gravity of a man who had perhaps strangled a love affair in his youth. He asked Miss Thistlewood where she intended to live now that the Pontypool fortune had been packed into boxes and buried under straw. When she tearfully admitted she did not know, having only a thousand pounds left, John proposed that she come and live with him. He was old compared to her, he admitted, but of good birth, good principles, and good temper. He needed a companion, and she, he reasoned, needed security.

Though she might have preferred a younger man, she recognized his worth, his prudence, and his honor. Blushing deeply, she made a low curtsey and asked to consider his kind proposal. They were married during the dull Bath season, which was the height of the season in London. John secured lodgings in Cavendish Square, took his wife to the theaters and parks, and introduced her to society. He left calling cards for the Earls and Lords, though the Earl of Bareacres ignored them, while Lord Pontypool merely sent a footman with a delayed invitation.

John's secret ambition had always been to be a gentleman. Through frugality, a lucky investment in copper-mining shares, and the sale of his Bath business for a handsome sum, he achieved his dream. He purchased a house and small estate near Clavering. He walked the acres he now owned with a pride he never confessed aloud. He dressed as an English gentleman should, in a bottle-green coat and brass buttons, sinking the black breeches of his medical past. He founded the Clavering Book Club, improved the local charities, and redirected the mail route through his town. At market, he inspected oats and weighed geese with a knowing air, demanding to be called "Squire" rather than "Doctor."

His dining room was adorned with portraits he claimed were Lelys and Vandykes, ancestors of his bloodline. While his wife, skeptical of these legends, tried to steer the conversation elsewhere, their young son, Arthur, believed them completely. To little Arthur, Roger Pendennis of Agincourt and General Pendennis of Blenheim were as real as Robinson Crusoe.

With an income of eight hundred pounds a year, John lived comfortably among the second-best sort of society. They dined in moonlight, hosted festivals, and enjoyed the company of the town, though they often blessed themselves that their home, Fairoaks, was a mile away from the prying eyes of Clavering. The house itself was a splendid freestone palace, built by a grandfather who ruined the family by constructing it, and inhabited by a successor who ruined it further by living in it. The present Sir Francis Clavering was abroad, leaving the mansion to decay, its halls moldy and its galleries dismal. Yet, at sunset, the lawn of Fairoaks and the park of Clavering glowed with a rich golden light, a scene that moved young Arthur to recite poetry about the "Parent of Good."

John Pendennis was a man of rigid order. His hat was always brushed, his meals served punctually, and his household ran like a well-oiled machine. He napped after dinner with the *Globe* on his knee, often accompanied by a yellow bandanna handkerchief sent by his brother, Major Pendennis, from India. While the sun set and his wife and son enjoyed the view, John remained aloof, preferring the *Gardener's Gazette* or a game of picquet.

The Major, John's brother, was the family's link to the great world. Having sold out from the army and settled on half-pay, he was the bearer of news from London and the fashion plates. He dined with lords, boasted of invitations to pheasant shoots, and famously rejected the hand of Miss Hunkle, a wealthy attorney's daughter, declaring that as a bachelor, he preferred the company of the highly placed over a fortune. Time proved the Major correct; Miss Hunkle married a French nobleman and became Baroness de Carambole.

Mrs. Pendennis was a woman of tranquil beauty and natural sweetness, revered by her husband and praised by her brother. She moved through the world with a perfection that enraged some country ladies, who found her too proud for her station. Yet, she remained calm, praying, loving her family, and doing her duty. Her only flaw, as perceived by her neighbors, was a pride that vested itself not in her person, but in her family. She spoke of her husband with the reverence due a Pope and of her son with an idolatrous ardor. This superstition, this worship of her family, would later become the source of much misfortune for the young gentleman who was to be the hero of this history.

At Greyfriars School, Arthur Pendennis was neither a dunce nor a scholar. He did exactly what was required and no more. He was distinguished for verse-writing, composing just enough lines to satisfy the regulations, unlike his more zealous peers. He devoured novels and plays outside of school hours, avoided flogging by sheer luck, and spent his money royally on tarts. He was averse to fighting and Greek grammar, telling the truth but never bullying others. When his masters called him a profligate destined for ruin, he simply grew accustomed to the insult, having not yet committed any act worthy of transportation.

While other boys smoked cigars and kept carriages, young Pen remained modest and timid, though by twelve he had heard enough talk to be awfully wise on certain points. At sixteen, he made his public appearance in a coat with a tail, anxiously checking his study-glass for whiskers that never seemed to grow. His voice had dropped into a deep bass, a change that amused his masters.

It was at the close of a forenoon session that his academic life ended abruptly. Called to construe a Greek play, Pen failed miserably, stumbling over the words despite the prompting of his form-fellow. The Doctor, a man of terrifying wrath, launched into a harangue, declaring Pen's idleness incorrigible and his stupidity beyond example. He spoke of the "root of all evil" and the crimes of filial ingratitude, threatening the rod for the next mistake.

Just as the Doctor reached his crescendo, a gentleman entered the room. It was Major Pendennis. He had written on his card, "I must take A. P. home; his father is very ill." The Doctor, startled, stopped his oration. The boys burst into laughter, then silence at the command. Pen, seeing his uncle, was delivered from the classroom.

As the Major drove Pen away in a postchaise, the hostler winked ominously, and the gardener shook his head. Upon arriving at Fairoaks, they found the blinds drawn and the atmosphere heavy with gloom. The old footman's face was blank. Inside, the room was darkened, surrounded by the gloomy portraits of the Pendennises. The doctor and a lawyer from Clavering arrived shortly after, confirming the news: John Pendennis had seized and died.

Mrs. Pendennis bore the loss wonderfully, comforted by the arrival of her son. The Major, as executor and guardian, took charge of the household affairs. The lawyer discussed business, and the Major assumed total superintendence. The old footman, John, followed the Major to bed with the candle and returned the next morning with the key to the hall clock, a ritual he had performed for years.

What passed between the mother and son in those sacred moments of grief is best left veiled. Mrs. Pendennis, who always revered her husband as the most upright and awful of men, found solace in her maternal love. She maintained the memory of her husband with the most precious oil, ensuring his memorial remained handsome.

As for Arthur Pendennis, the shock of his father's death was profound. Yet, amidst the tears and the embrace of his mother, a feeling of secret triumph sprang up in his breast. He was now the chief, the lord of the house. With his mother's blessing and the servants' deference, he resolved that his school days were over. He would not get up until he liked, and he would never again endure the bullying of the Doctor. Walking through the kitchen and kennels, he was greeted with hushed reverence by the servants, who bowed and curtseyed as they never had before. He patted the dogs, inspected the hens, and looked at the orchard, a young master dreaming of a future of endless holidays and absolute freedom.

John Pendennis, Esquire, was buried with one of the most handsome funerals the county had seen since the days of Sir Roger Clavering. A fair marble slab was erected in the abbey church, bearing the Pendennis coat of arms and the motto *nec tenui penna*. The Doctor preached a touching sermon, and Arthur Pendennis reigned in his stead, the new master of Fairoaks.

In Which Pendennis Appears as a Very Young Man Indeed

Arthur was about sixteen, a age at which he began to reign. In person, he possessed what his friends termed a "dumpy" figure, though his mother insisted on calling it "neat." His hair was a healthy brown that shimmered like gold in the sun; his face was round, rosy, and freckled, radiating a good-natured charm. When nature finally granted him the whiskers he so ardently desired, they were a decidedly reddish hue. Though not conventionally beautiful, he had a frank, honest countenance and laughed so merrily from his blue eyes that Mrs. Pendennis considered him the pride of the entire county. Between sixteen and eighteen, he grew from five feet six to five feet eight, pausing there. His mother, however, remained baffled. How could a man grow three inches taller than his father, Mr. Pendennis?

He never returned to school. The discipline of the establishment simply did not suit him; he preferred the comfort of home. The question of his return was debated, with his uncle advocating for it. The Doctor argued that a thorough knowledge of a Greek play was essential for Arthur's future success. Yet, Pen adroitly hinted to his mother about the dangers of Greyfriars and the wild fellows who inhabited it. The timid soul took alarm immediately and acceded to his desire to stay put.

Then, Pen's uncle offered to use his influence with His Royal Highness, the Commander-in-Chief, who was kind enough to propose a commission in the Foot Guards. Pen's heart leaped. He had recently visited St. James's Park to hear the band play on a Sunday. There, he had witnessed Tom Ricketts, a fourth-former known for wearing jackets so ludicrously tight that older boys used him as a target or 'cockshy.' Pen had seen this very Ricketts arrayed in crimson and gold, staggering under a bear-skin cap and regimental colours. Tom, a little wretch Pen had once cut with a hockey-stick, now recognized him, offering a patronizing nod. There he was, center stage, rallying around the flag of his country, surrounded by bayonets, crossbelts, and scarlet, while the band blew trumpets and banged cymbals. He was talking familiarly to immense warriors with tufts to their chins and Waterloo medals. What would not Pen have given to wear such epaulettes and enter such a service?

But Helen Pendennis, when this proposal was made, put on a face full of terror. She declared she did not quarrel with others' opinions, but in her view, a Christian had no right to make the army a profession. Mr. Pendennis would never have permitted it. Finally, she expressed that she would be very unhappy if he pursued it. Now, Pen would have as soon cut off his nose and ears as deliberately make his mother unhappy. Being of such a generous disposition that he would give anything to anyone, he instantly made a present of his visionary red coat, epaulettes, and ardour for military glory to his mother.

She thought him the noblest creature in the world. Major Pendennis, upon acknowledging and refusing the commission, wrote a curt and somewhat angry letter to the widow, thinking his nephew was merely a "spooney."

However, Pen was contented when he saw the boy's performances out hunting at Christmas, when the Major came down to Fairoaks. Pen had a very good mare and rode her with uncommon pluck and grace. He took his fences with great coolness and judgment, devoid of bravado. He wrote to his schoolmates about his top-boots and feats across country. He began to think seriously of a scarlet coat; his mother admitted it would become him remarkably well, though she passed hours of anguish during his absence, daily expecting to see him brought home on a shutter.

Amidst these amusements, it must not be assumed that Pen neglected his studies. He had a natural taste for every kind of book that did not fall into his school course. It was only when they forced his head into the waters of knowledge that he refused to drink. He devoured books at home from Inchbald's Theatre to White's Farriery, ransacking neighbouring bookcases. At Clavering, he found an old cargo of French novels which he read with all his might. He would sit for hours perched on the topmost bar of Doctor Portman's library steps with a folio on his knees, whether it were Hakluyt's Travels, Hobbes's Leviathan, Augustini Opera, or Chaucer's Poems. He and the Vicar were very good friends; from his Reverence, Pen learned that honest taste for port wine which distinguished him through life. As for Mrs. Portman, who was not in the least jealous—though her Doctor avowed himself in love with Mrs. Pendennis, whom he pronounced the finest lady in the county—all her grief was that her daughter, Minny, was too old for him. Miss Myra Portman was only two years younger than Pen's mother and weighed as much as Pen and Mrs. Pendennis combined.

Are these details insipid? Look back, good friend, at your own youth. I like to think of a well-nurtured boy, brave and gentle, warm-hearted and loving, looking the world in the face with kind, honest eyes. What bright colours it wore then, and how you enjoyed it! A man has not many years of such time. He does not know them whilst they are with him. It is only when they are passed long away that he remembers how dear and happy they were.

To keep Mr. Pen from indulging in the idleness prophesied by the Doctor of the Cistercians, Mr. Smirke, Dr. Portman's curate, was engaged at a liberal salary to walk or ride over from Clavering and spend several hours daily with the young gentleman. Smirke was a man perfectly faultless at a tea-table, wore a curl on his fair forehead, and tied his neck-cloth with a melancholy grace. He was a decent scholar and mathematician, and taught Pen as much as the lad was ever disposed to learn, which was not much. Pen had soon taken the measure of his tutor. When Smirke rode into the courtyard at Fairoaks on his pony, he turned out his toes so absurdly and left such a gap between his knees and the saddle that it was impossible for any lad with a sense of humour to respect such an equestrian. Pen nearly killed Smirke with terror by putting him on his mare, Rebecca (named after Pen's favourite heroine, the daughter of Isaac of York), and taking him a ride over a common where the county fox-hounds happened to meet. Smirke astounded the hounds as much as he disgusted the huntsman, laming one of the former by persisting in riding amongst the pack, and receiving a speech from the latter more remarkable for energy of language than any oration he had heard since leaving the bargemen on the banks of Isis.

Smirke confided his poems, both Latin and English, to his pupil, and presented Mrs. Pendennis with a volume of the latter, printed at Clapham, his native place. The two read the ancient poets together, rattling through them at a pleasant rate, very different from the steady grubbing pace with which the Cistercians went over classic ground, scenting out each word and digging up every root. Pen never liked to halt; he made his tutor construe when at fault, and thus galloped through the Iliad, the Odyssey, the tragic playwrights, and the charming, wicked Aristophanes, whom he vowed was the greatest poet of all. But he went at such a pace that, though he certainly galloped through a considerable extent of the ancient country, he clean forgot it in after-life, retaining only a vague remembrance of his early classic course as a man in the House of Commons who still keeps up two or three quotations, or a reviewer who, just for decency's sake, hints at a little Greek. Our people are the most prosaic in the world, but the most faithful; with curious reverence, we keep up and transmit, from generation to generation, the superstition of what we call the education of a gentleman.

Besides the ancient poets, Pen read the English with great gusto. Smirke sighed and shook his head sadly about Byron and Moore. But Pen was a sworn fire-worshipper and a Corsair; he had them by heart. He used to take little Laura into the window and say, "Zuleika, I am not thy brother," in tones so tragic that they caused the solemn little maid to open her great eyes still wider. She sat, until the proper hour for retirement, sewing at Mrs. Pendennis's knee, listening to Pen reading out to her of nights without comprehending one word of what he read.

He read Shakespeare to his mother (which she said she liked, but didn't), Byron, Pope, and his favourite *Lalla Rookh*, which pleased her indifferently. But as for Bishop Heber and Mrs. Hemans above all, this lady used to melt right away, absorbed into her pocket-handkerchief, when Pen read those authors to her in his kind boyish voice. *The Christian Year* was a book which appeared about that time. The son and the mother whispered it to each other with awe—faint, very faint. Seldom in after-life did Pendennis hear that solemn church-music, but he always loved the remembrance of it, and of the times when it struck on his heart, walking over the fields full of hope and void of doubt as the church-bells rang on Sunday morning.

It was at this period that Pen broke out in the Poets' Corner of the County Chronicle with some verses with which he was perfectly well satisfied. His verses, signed 'NEP.', included "To a Tear," "On the Anniversary of the Battle of Waterloo," "To Madame Caradori singing at the Assize Meetings," and "On Saint Bartholomew's Day"—a tremendous denunciation of Popery and a solemn warning to the people of England to rally against emancipating the Roman Catholics. All these masterpieces, Mrs. Pendennis no doubt keeps to this day, along with his first socks, the first cutting of his hair, his bottle, and other interesting relics of his infancy. He used to gallop Rebecca over the neighbouring Dumpling Downs or into the county town, which we shall call Chatteris, spouting his own poems, filled with quite a Byronic afflatus as he thought.

His genius at this time was of a decidedly gloomy cast. He brought his mother a tragedy in which, though he killed sixteen people before the second act, it made her laugh so that he thrust the masterpiece into the fire in a pet. He projected an epic poem in blank verse, *Cortez, or the Conqueror of Mexico, and the Inca's Daughter*. He wrote parts of *Seneca, or the Fatal Bath* and *Ariadne in Naxos*; classical pieces with choruses and strophes that sadly puzzled poor Mrs. Pendennis. He began a *History of the Jesuits*, in which he lashed that Order with tremendous severity and warned his Protestant fellow-countrymen of their machinations. His loyalty did his mother's heart good to witness. He was a staunch, unflinching Church-and-King man in those days. At the election, when Sir Giles Beanfield stood on the Blue interest against Lord Trehawk, a Whig and friend of Popery, Arthur Pendennis, with an immense bow for himself which his mother made and a blue ribbon for Rebecca, rode alongside the Reverend Doctor Portman on his grey mare Dowdy, leading the Clavering voters whom the Doctor brought up to plump for the Protestant Champion.

On that day, Pen made his first speech at the Blue Hotel, and also, it appears, for the first time in his life, took a little more wine than was good for him. Mercy! What a scene it was at Fairoaks when he rode back at ever so much o'clock at night! What moving about of lanterns in the courtyard and stables, though the moon was shining out! What a gathering of servants as Pen came home, clattering over the bridge and up the stableyard, with half a score of the Clavering voters yelling after him the Blue song of the election.

He wanted them all to come in and have some wine—some very good Madeira, some capital Madeira—"John, go and get some Madeira." There is no knowing what the farmers would have done had not Madam Pendennis made her appearance in a white wrapper with a candle, and scared those zealous Blues so by the sight of her pale, handsome face that they touched their hats and rode off.

Besides these amusements and occupations, there was one which forms the main business and pleasure of youth, if the poets tell us aright. Pen's heart was so ardent and his imagination so eager that it is not to be expected he should long escape the passion to which we allude, and which, ladies, you have rightly guessed to be that of Love. Pen sighed for it first in secret, and, like the love-sick swain in Ovid, opened his breast and said, "Aura, veni." What generous youth is there that has not courted some such windy mistress in his time?

Yes, Pen began to feel the necessity of a first love—a consuming passion—an object on which he could concentrate all those vague, floating fancies under which he sweetly suffered—a young lady to whom he could really make verses and whom he could set up and adore, in place of those unsubstantial Ianthes and Zuleikas to whom he addressed the outpourings of his gushing muse. He read his favourite poems over and over again, called upon Alma Venus, the delight of gods and men, translated Anacreon's odes, and picked out passages suitable to his complaint from Waller, Dryden, Prior, and the like. Smirke and he were never weary, in their interviews, of discoursing about love. The faithless tutor entertained him with sentimental conversations in place of lectures on algebra and Greek, for Smirke was in love too. Who could help it, being in daily intercourse with such a woman? Smirke was madly in love (as far as such a mild flame as Mr.

Mrs. Haller

Having retreated to the George, Mr. Foker and his guest sat down to a repast of such grandeur that the coffee-room itself seemed to lean in for a closer look. Mr. Rincer entered with the first dish, bowing with the solemn gravity of a Lord-Lieutenant serving royalty. Mr. Foker attacked the turtle and venison with a gusto that belied his previous feasts on ginger-beer and smuggled polonies. Pen, witnessing this, could not help but respect his connoisseurship, especially when Foker pronounced the champagne "condemned gooseberry" and winked knowingly at the port. The latter, Foker declared, was of the right sort; he knew the waiters by their Christian names, cared deeply for their families, and greeted the arriving London coaches with theatrical flair. Flinging the window open, he called out to the guards and coachmen, imitating the tooting of horns with such lively accuracy that the ostler, Jem, seemed to whip the horses' cloths off in sympathy as the carriages drove gaily away.

"A bottle of sherry, a bottle of sham, a bottle of port and a shass caffy, it ain't so bad, hay, Pen?" Foker asked, declaring it time to "toddle" after a mountain of nuts and fruit. Pen sprang up with bright, flushed eyes, and they moved toward the theatre, paying their money to a wheezy old lady slumbering in the box office. "Mrs. Dropsicum, Bingley's mother-in-law, great in Lady Macbeth," Foker noted, knowing her well.

They secured seats in the boxes, a sparse affair despite the playbill's promises of "galvanic thrills." A score of people dotted the pit, a few kicked and whistled in the galleries, and a dozen free-admission guests sat nearby. Lieutenants Rodgers and Podgers, along with young Cornet Tidmus of the Dragoons, occupied a private box, conversing with the players and applauding them by name.

The manager, Bingley, assumed all chief roles except when London stars descended upon Chatteris. Dressed in tight pantaloons, Hessian boots, a large cloak, and a drooping hearse feather that barely concealed his buckled brown wig, he played the 'Stranger.' He adorned himself with stage jewelry, selecting the largest, shiniest rings, allowing his little finger to quiver with a sham diamond that twiddled in the faces of the pit. Bingley treated this ring as a family heirloom, passed down from George Frederick Cooke via Mr. Quin, and delighted the young men of his company with its history.

Reading from a stage-book rouged and tawdry like the heroes it held, Bingley pointed passages ominously, lifting his eyes to the ceiling as if deriving intense consolation from the text's affinity with heaven. When the Stranger spotted the young men, he acted directly at them, calculating the effect of every ornament to fascinate his paying victims. His servant, Francis, remarked on his master's gloom, noting that nature held no beauty for him and that he hadn't smiled in three years.

"He is but a child in the leading-string of hope," Bingley observed, looking steadily at Foker, who remained unconcernedly sucking the top of his stick. "And her cradle—is the grave," Bingley added with a moan of a bassoon in agony, fixing his eyes so steadily on Pendennis that the poor lad felt the whole house staring at him. Throughout the scene, Bingley played at him, marking pages carefully to continue his perusal off-stage, determined to subjugate Pendennis. When the scene ended, Foker cried "Bravo, Bingley!" and the good-natured young gentleman, laughing Pendennis, and the Dragoons began clapping hands to their utmost ability.

As the play shifted to Wintersen Castle, servants bustled about. "That's Hicks and Miss Thackthwaite," Foker whispered. "Pretty girl, ain't she, Pendennis? But stop—hurray—bravo! Here's the Fotheringay."

The pit thrilled, umbrellas thumped, and a volley of applause erupted from the gallery. The Dragoon officers and Foker clapped furiously. Amidst the red face and ragged whiskers of Mr. Costigan peering from the side-scene, Pen's eyes opened wide. Mrs. Haller entered with a downcast look, then rallied at the applause, sweeping the house with a grateful glance before sinking into a magnificent curtsey. More applause, more umbrellas; Pen, flaming with wine and enthusiasm, clapped and sang "bravo" louder than all. Mrs. Haller saw him, and so did old Mr. Bows, the little first fiddler, who looked up from his desk with a crutch beside him and smiled at the lad's enthusiasm.

Those who have only seen Miss Fotheringay in later London life cannot imagine her beauty at that time. She was tall, twenty-six though she vowed she was nineteen, in the prime of her existence. Her vast forehead was crowned with black hair waving in natural ripples, confined in shining braids at the back of a neck reminiscent of the Louvre Venus. Her eyes, when lifted, shone with tenderness and mystery; Love and Genius seemed to peer out before retiring coyly. She never laughed, for her teeth were not good, but a smile of endless sweetness played around her lips and dimples. Her nose defied description, and her ears resembled little pearl shells, which the finest earrings only insulted. Dressed in long flowing black robes, she moved with wonderful grace, revealing only her large sandals occasionally, which Pen found ravishing.

But it was her hand and arm that this magnificent creature most excelled in. They surrounded her. Whether folded over her bosom in resignation, dropped in mute agony, or raised in superb command, they beckoned, repelled, entreated, and embraced the whole house. Thus she stood, complete and beautiful, as Pen stared. "I say, Pen, isn't she a stunner?" Foker asked.

"Hush!" Pen whispered, "she's speaking."

In a deep, sweet voice, Mrs. Haller began. The play of the 'Stranger' was notoriously stupid, filled with absurdly fine words that no idiot would use in real life. Yet, amidst the balderdash, ran a reality of love, children, and forgiveness that set the world sympathizing. With smothered sorrow and gushing pathos, she delivered her part. First, giving orders with the calm agony of despair, she then overflowed to the pit and house, crying out her griefs as if to confidants. When she reached the favorite passage about her William and his little sisters, little Bows buried his face in his blue cotton handkerchief after crying "Bravo."

All the house was affected. Foker wept piteously with a large yellow bandanna. Pen, however, was gone too far for tears. He followed the woman about the stage; when she left, the house and lights reeled wildly before his sight. Watching her at the side-scene, where her father removed her shawl, he saw only Fotheringay, Fotheringay, when the reconciliation arrived and she flung herself onto Mr. Bingley's shoulders. The curtain fell upon him like a pall. He did not hear Bingley announce the next play, nor did the manager comprehend that anyone else but himself had caused the success. Finally understanding, Bingley stepped back with a grin and appeared with Mrs. Haller on his arm. How beautiful she looked! Her hair had fallen down, officers threw her flowers, and she clutched them to her heart. Her eyes met Pen's. Down went the curtain again, and she was gone. Not one note of the overture could he hear.

"She is a crusher, ain't she now!" Foker asked.

Pen answered vaguely, unable to speak to any mortal. He felt an overwhelming, maddening, delicious fever of wild joy and undefined longing. When Rowkins and Miss Thackthwaite danced the double hornpipe, Foker abandoned himself to the ballet, but Pen cared little, remembering only that the woman had acted with him in the scene where she first came in. It was a mist before his eyes. At the end of the dance, he checked his watch and said it was time to go.

"Stay to see The Bravo of the Battle-Axe," Foker urged. "Bingley's splendid in it... It's great fun, do stop."

Pen glanced at the bill, hoping Miss Fotheringay's name might be hidden in the after-piece list, but there was no such name. He must go. He had a long ride home. Squeezing Foker's hand, he choked to speak but couldn't. He quitted the theatre, walked frantically about the town, then mounted at the George and rode homewards. As he came into the yard at Fairoaks, the Clavering clock sang out one. The lady of the house might have been awake, but she only heard him from the passage as he dashed into bed and pulled the clothes over his head.

Pen, unused to wakeful nights, fell instantly into a sound sleep. Even in later days, a man from practice or fatigue begins by sleeping as usual, getting a nap in advance of anxiety. But she soon comes up with him, jogs his shoulder, and says, "Come, my man, no more of this laziness, you must wake up and have a talk with me." Then they fall together in the midnight. Well, whatever might afterwards happen to him, poor little Pen was not come to this state yet; he tumbled into a sound sleep, not waking until an early hour when the rooks began to caw from the wood beyond his bedroom windows.

At that very instant, as his eyes started open, the beloved image was in his mind. "My dear boy," he heard her say, "you were in a sound sleep and I would not disturb you: but I have been close by your pillow all this while: and I don't intend that you shall leave me. I am Love! I bring with me fever and passion: wild longing, maddening desire; restless craving and seeking. Many a long day ere this I heard you calling out for me; and behold now I am come."

Was Pen frightened? Not he. It was all wild pleasure and delight. Just as three years previously, on entering the fifth form at the Cistercians, his father had given him a gold watch which the boy examined on waking, rubbing and polishing it in private to listen to its ticking, so the young man exulted over his new delight. He felt in his waistcoat pocket to ensure it was safe, wound it up at nights, and at the first moment of waking hugged and looked at it. By the way, that first watch of Pen's was a showy, ill-manufactured piece that never went well and was eventually swapped for a more useful time-keeper.

Pen felt himself many years older since yesterday. There was no mistake; he was as much in love as the best hero in the best romance he had ever read. He told John to bring his shaving water with utmost confidence. He dressed in some of his finest clothes and came splendidly down to breakfast, patronizing his mother and little Laura, who had been strumming her music lesson for hours. After he read the prayers—which he did not heed a single syllable of—she wondered at his grand appearance and asked him to tell her what the play was about.

Pen laughed and declined. It was quite as well that she should not know. Then she asked why he had put on his fine pin and beautiful new waistcoat. Pen blushed and told his mother that the old schoolfellow with whom he had dined at Chatteris was reading with a tutor at Baymouth, a very learned man. Since he was himself to go to College, and several young men pursued their studies there, he was anxious to ride over and see what the course of their reading was.

Laura made a long face. Helen Pendennis looked hard at her son, troubled more than ever with the vague doubt and terror haunting her since the last night when Farmer Gurnett brought news that Pen would not return home to dinner. Arthur's eyes defied her. She tried to console herself, driving off her fears. The boy had never told her an untruth. Pen conducted himself during breakfast in a very haughty and supercilious manner. Taking leave of the elder and younger lady, he was presently heard riding out of the stablecourt. He went gently at first, but galloped like a madman as soon as he thought he was out of hearing.

Smirke, thinking of his own affairs and softly riding with his toes out to give Pen his three hours' reading at Fairoaks, met his pupil, who shot by him like the wind. Smirke's pony shied as the other thundered past; the gentle curate went over his head among the stinging-nettles in the hedge. Pen laughed as they met, pointed toward the Baymouth road, and was gone half a mile before poor Smirke had picked himself up.

Pen had resolved to see Foker that morning; he must hear about her, know about her, be with somebody who knew her. Honest Smirke, sitting up among the stinging-nettles as his pony cropped quietly in the hedge, thought dismally to himself whether he ought to go to Fairoaks now that his pupil was evidently gone away for the day. Yes, he thought he might go, too. He might ask Mrs. Pendennis when Arthur would be back and hear Miss Laura recite Watts's Catechism. He got up on the little pony—both were used to his slipping off—and advanced upon the house from which his scholar had just rushed away in a whirlwind.

Thus love makes fools of all of us, big and little; and the curate had tumbled over head and heels in pursuit of it, and Pen had started in the first heat of the mad race.

Rebecca, the mare, did not break her stride. She thundered down the road to Baymouth, carrying Pendennis straight to the inn stables, and from there, he bolted for Mr. Foker's lodgings. These quarters sat precariously above a chemist's shop, a place where the stock of cigars and sodawater vanished faster than the rent money, thanks to the voracious appetites of Foker's young tenants.

Upon arriving, Pen found the place empty of its master. Instead, he encountered Mr. Spavin, Foker's chum and co-owner of the tandem that had recently driven into Chatteris. Spavin was smoking a pipe and teaching a small dog to perform tricks with a biscuit. The contrast was stark: Pen's face was flushed with the healthy red of a gallop, while Spavin's features were waxy, pale, and distinctly debauched.

"Who's that man?" Spavin muttered, eyeing Pen. "He looks fresh as a bean. His hand doesn't shake in the morning. I'd bet five to one he's got the constitution of a saint."

Foker was nowhere to be found. A disappointment washed over Pen. Spavin shrugged, admitting Foker was a creature of erratic habits, sometimes staying a day, sometimes a week. When Pen revealed his own identity via a calling card—something he was currently quite proud of—Spavin offered a fair tap of ale and dismissed him.

Left alone, Pen walked down to the beach, biting his nails as he stared at the vast, roaring sea. The blue waters foamed and crashed against the shore, indifferent to his turmoil. A tidal wave was surging within his own mind, and he possessed no power to check it. He threw stones into the surf, but the tide kept coming. Rage consumed him at Foker's absence. He *had* to see Foker.

"Suppose I go on the Chatteris road," Pen thought, mounting Rebecca. "Just to catch him."

He rode hard, passing the turn for the Clavering road without noticing the black tandem and red wheels that should have been there. At the turnpike, logic dictated he continue. He rode to the George Inn, where the hostler confirmed Foker was indeed present, having caused a "tremendous row" the night before involving drinking, singing, and a failed attempt to fight a postboy.

Pen was ushered into an apartment dominated by an immense feather bed. Mr. Harry Foker lay amidst the pillows, his sallow face obscured by red silk nightcaps and bolsters.

"Hullo!" Pen called out.

"Who goes there? Brother, speak quickly!" Foker's voice rang from the depths of the bedding. "Pendennis again? Is your mother aware of your absence? Did you sup with us last night? No stop—who supped with us last night, Stoopid?"

"There were three officers, sir, Mr. Bingley, and Mr. Costigan, sir," the servant, Stoopid, replied with grave seriousness.

"Ah, yes! The cup and merry jest went round. I wanted to fight a postboy. Did I thrash him, Stoopid?"

"No, sir. The fight didn't happen," Stoopid answered, arranging Foker's dressing case—a trunk gifted by a doting mother and filled with silverware and razors.

"Do it another day," Foker yawned, throwing his lean arms over his head. "No fight, but chanting. Bingley chanted, I chanted, the General chanted—Costigan, I mean. Did you ever hear him sing 'The Little Pig under the Bed,' Pen?"

"The man we met yesterday," Pen stammered, trembling. "The father of—"

"Of the Fotheringay! The very man. Ain't she a Venus, Pen?"

"Please sir, Mr. Costigan is in the sitting-room," Stoopid interrupted. "He says you asked him to breakfast. He's been here since eleven."

"How much is it now?" Foker asked.

"One, sir."

"What would the best of mothers say if she saw me in bed at this hour?" the young sluggard cried, bursting into a boyish laugh. "She sent me down with a grinder. She wants me to cultivate my neglected genus. He, be! I say, Pen, this isn't quite like seven o'clock school, is it, old boy?"

Pen fled to the sitting-room in great perturbation while Foker began his toilette.

The narrative of Mr. Foker is one of transformation. Born to a brewer and an earl, he had endured the harshest bullying at Cistercian School due to his trade, uncomely face, and gluttony. Released from that tyranny, he blossomed into the humorous, sarcastic, and brilliant Foker known to the world. Though a dunce by academic standards, he had become a dandy, arriving in his sitting-room scented and splendid.

In the room sat Captain Costigan, a man of questionable stability. His eyes were dim, his hat cocked askew, and his boots shabby, signaling that poverty had already claimed his extremities. He was spelling the newspaper with his lips, a rare feat for anyone.

As Pen entered, Costigan descended from the window seat, saluting with a broken glove and a jaunty twist of his chin. He spoke of Pen's introduction in the Cathedral Yard and his daughter's performance, claiming it reminded him of his glory days in the Hundred and Third. He produced an old snuffbox, presenting it with stately air.

Arthur Pendennis was flustered. This shabby-looking man was the father of the actress he had admired. Costigan, however, was perfumed with cigar smoke and spun tales of his past, boasting of his voice and his ability to charm generals. He confessed that his daughter, Miss Fotheringay, earned her living despite his pride, noting that her manager, Bingley, paid her merely two guineas a week.

Costigan's history was a blur of fact and fiction, a muzzy tale of bravery, drunkenness, and survival. Yet, he managed to extract two sovereigns from Pen for tickets to his daughter's benefit performance. Pen, possessing only two pounds, handed them over, terrified of offending the Captain's delicacy. Costigan scribbled an order for a box, slipped the gold into his waistcoat, and slapped his hand over the spot, warming his old sides.

"Faith, sir," Costigan grinned, "the bullion's scarcer with me than it used to be. I won six hundred of 'em in a single night in Gibraltar."

He regaled Pen with stories of claret, races, and the Duke of Kent until Foker arrived for breakfast. The Captain's spirits soared over deviled turkey and mutton chops, prating endlessly of his past splendor and a challenge sent to a Captain Shanty Clancy. He announced that sitting with such noble young fellows was the pride of an old soldier's existence. After a second glass of Curacoa, he began to cry, and the trio walked out, the Captain linking arms with both young men, winking at tradesmen's shops as if to say, "See the company I'm in—sure I'll pay you."

On the street, Costigan inquired about Foker's fortune. Pen, eager to please, revealed that Foker's father was a celebrated brewer and his mother Lady Agnes Milton. Costigan erupted in exaggerated praise of Foker's "native aristocracie," though he believed not a word of it.

Pen, amused and puzzled, listened to the Captain's prattle. The Captain, finding such an artless and honest listener, invited Pen to his humble abode to introduce him to his daughter. Pen was struck dumb with delight and shock. He was going to see her! She was the center of his universe.

They walked to Prior's Lane, a quiet street near the cathedral. Costigan lived in a low gabled house formerly occupied by a tailor. Two choir-boys, sweet-voiced and mischievous, greeted him at the door, jumping at his coat tails.

Inside, Pen followed the Captain up the creaking stairs, his knees trembling. He entered a room where a figure stood, waving as if making a curtsey.

"It is very fine weather," Miss Fotheringay said, her voice a deep, rich Irish melancholy.

"Very," Pen replied.

She looked even more striking off-stage. Her movements were naturally grand; she resembled a Muse contemplating or an Empress seated. Her hair was blue-black, her complexion dazzling, her eyes grey with long lashes, and her mouth a staring red that rivaled the finest geranium.

"And very warm," she continued.

The conversation flowed. She asked about the evening at the George, and he recounted the punch. She mentioned studying Ophelia for the twenty-fourth. Pen noted her natural Irish inflection, unaware that it was her native tongue. Costigan tapped his pocket, revealing Pen's sovereigns, and winked.

"My name is Pendennis," Pen blushed, declaring himself a lover of poetry with a feeling heart.

"Pendennis," she answered slowly, looking him full in the eyes. Her glance was clear, bright, and killing, shooting him through with pleasure.

"I never knew the name was so pretty before," Pen said.

"'Tis a very pretty name," Ophelia agreed. "Pentweazle's not a pretty name. Remember, papa, when we were on the Norwich Circuit, Young Pentweazle... His name was Harrington; that is, his real name was Potts..."

She spoke of her comrade Pentweazle, a man named Potts who played second old men and married a Mrs. Rancy. Costigan interjected that Pentweazle should have been Julia, or his name wasn't Jack Costigan. Emily modestly admitted she wasn't fit for the role until Bows taught her.

Costigan then revealed he had been a fencing-master in Dublin, where his friend Mr. Bows had discovered Emily's capabilities. "What have ye done since Bows went, Emily?" he asked.

"Sure, I've made a pie," Emily said simply, pronouncing it "Poy."

"If ye'll try it at four o'clock, sir, say the word," Costigan gallantly offered.

Pen, who had promised to be home by six, accepted the invitation with delight. He watched Ophelia arrange the glasses and smooth the cloth with a quiet grace that enchanted him. The "poy" arrived from the baker, and at four o'clock, Pen found himself at dinner with the greatest tragic actress in the world and her father. He ate a crust of her making, poured her a glass of beer, and watched her drink a glass of punch from the tumbler she mixed for her papa. She offered to mix one for him; it was prodigiously strong, intoxicating him more than any spirit.

During dinner, Pen tried to discuss poetry and her profession. He asked about Ophelia's madness and whether she loved Hamlet. "In love with such a little wretch as that stunted manager of a Bingley?" she bristled indignantly. Pen clarified he meant the character, not the manager. She admitted she didn't value Bingley. When Pen mentioned Kotzebue, the author of the play she performed, she asked who he was. "The author of the play in which she had been performing so admirably," Pen explained.

"She did not know that—the man's name at the beginning of the book was Thompson," she said. Pen laughed at her adorable simplicity, telling her of the author's melancholy fate and how Sand had killed him. For the first time, Miss Costigan heard of Kotzebue, and her sympathy sufficed for honest Pen.

The hour and a quarter passed too quickly. Pen left, riding Rebecca homeward.

"What was that he was talking about, the madness of Hamlet?" Emily asked her father later.

"'Deed then I don't know, Milly dear," Costigan answered. "We'll ask Bows when he comes."

"He's a nice, fair-spoken pretty young man," Emily said. "How many tickets did he take?"

"Faith, then, he took six, and gev me two guineas, Milly," Costigan replied. "I suppose them young chaps is not too flush of coin."

"He's full of book-learning," Emily continued, mentioning Kotzebue again. "Did ye ever hear such a thing? I'll ask Bows about it, papa, dear."

"A queer death, sure enough," Costigan ejaculated, changing the subject to the elegance of Pen's mare and the grand breakfast Foker had given them.

"He's good for two private boxes, and at leest twenty tickets, I should say," Emily cried, keeping her fine eyes on the main chance.

"I'll go bail of that," answered the papa.

At half-past six, Emily appeared at the theatre, accompanied by her father, who stood in the side-scene watching her.

"How beautiful she is," Pen thought, cantering home. "How simple and how tender! How charming it is to see a woman of her commanding genius busying herself with the delightful, though humble, offices of domestic life... How rude it was of me to begin to talk about professional matters, and how well she turned the conversation!"

He rode down the avenue, laughing as he saw Reverend Mr. Smirke coming demurely away from Fairoaks on his pony. Smirke had dawdled over Laura's lessons and Mrs. Pendennis's gardens, leaving just in time to miss the dinner invitation he fondly expected.

Pen was full of kindness and triumph. "What, picked up and sound?" he cried out laughing. "Come along back, old fellow, and eat my dinner—I have had mine: but we will have a bottle of the old wine and drink her health, Smirke."

Smirke turned the pony's head round, jogging along with Arthur. His mother was charmed by his spirits. Pen gave a ludicrous account of the previous night's play, mimicking Bingley in his rickety Hessians and the enormous Mrs. Bingley

Contains Both Love and War

For a while, Cicero and Euripides ceased to occupy Mr. Pen's mind, leaving honest Mr. Smirke with an unusually leisurely schedule. However, the true victim of this mental shift was Rebecca. While Pen occasionally announced his intention to travel to Chatteris for fencing lessons—a trip undertaken openly with his mother whenever he had three clear hours—he frequently vanished into the city, making a dash for Prior's Lane. When Rebecca fell lame, his distress was as frantic as Richard III's at Bosworth when his horse was killed beneath him. He delved deeply into the books of the stable-keeper at Chatteris, seeking remedies for his own imaginary ailments and those of others.

Once a week, perhaps, under the guise of reading a Greek play with Smirke, this young reprobate timed his departure perfectly to catch the *Competitor* coach. He lingered in Chatteris for a couple of hours before returning on the *Rival*, which left for London at ten o'clock. On one occasion, his secret nearly slipped due to Mr. Smirke's simplicity. Mrs. Pendennis asked if they had read much the previous night. Smirke was on the verge of confessing he hadn't seen Mr. Pen at all when Pen's boot-heel ground down on the curate's toe under the table, a silent warning that saved him from betrayal.

Conversations on the tender subject naturally ensued. It is good sport, if you are not personally involved, to overhear two men in love. When informed under solemn vows of secrecy of Pen's condition, the curate trembled, asking if the object was "unworthy" or an "unlawful attachment." If so, he felt duty-bound to inform Mrs. Pendennis, fearing a quarrel that would cost him his chance of seeing the world's greatest beauty.

"Unlawful, unworthy!" Pen bounced out. "She is as pure as she is beautiful; I would give my heart to no other woman. I keep it secret because reasons of weighty nature forbid disclosure. But any man who breathes a word against her purity insults both her honor and mine, and dammy, I won't stand it."

Smirke, with a faint laugh, merely said, "Well, well, don't call me out, Arthur, for you know I can't fight," thereby placing the wretched curate entirely in his pupil's power, causing Greek and mathematics to suffer accordingly.

Had Mr. Smirke possessed discernment and checked the 'Poet's Corner' of the *County Chronicle*, he might have seen 'Mrs. Haller,' 'Passion and Genius,' and 'Lines to Miss Fotheringay' appearing weekly. But since these verses were no longer signed NEP but subscribed EROS, neither the tutor nor Helen knew that Nep was the same flaming Eros singing the praises of the new actress.

"Who is the lady," Mrs. Pendennis finally asked, "whom your rival is always singing in the Chronicle? He writes something like you, dear Pen, but yours is much the best. Have you seen Miss Fotheringay?"

Pen admitted he had; that night he saw "The Stranger," who played Mrs. Haller. She was preparing for a benefit in Ophelia. "Suppose we go?" he suggested. "Shakespeare, you know, Mother—we can get horses from the Clavering Arms." Little Laura sprang up with delight, longing for a play.

Pen introduced "Shakespeare" because the deceased Mr. Pendennis, a man of his character, professed an uncommon respect for the Bard of Avon, claiming his works contained more poetry than all 'Johnson's Poets' combined. Though Mr. Pendennis didn't read them much, he enjoined Pen to peruse them, often speaking of the pleasure he would take in taking his son and wife to see the immortal poet's plays.

Tears welled in the kind mother's eyes remembering these speeches. She kissed her son fondly and agreed to go. Laura jumped for joy. Was Pen happy? Ashamed? Holding his mother, he longed to tell her all but kept his counsel. He would test her reaction; the play should be the thing, and he would try his mother like Hamlet's.

Helen invited Mr. Smirke to join the party. The ecclesiastic, bred by a fond parent at Clapham who objected to drama, had never seen a play. But Shakespeare! To go with Mrs. Pendennis in her carriage and sit by her side all night! He couldn't resist the idea of such pleasure. He made a feeble speech about temptation and gratitude, accepting her offer. As he spoke, he gave her a look that made her exceedingly uncomfortable. She had seen that look more than once lately, pursuing her. He became positively odious in the widow's eyes daily.

We shall not dwell extensively on Pen's courtship of Miss Fotheringay, for the reader has already sampled her conversation. Pen sat with her hour after hour, pouring forth his honest boyish soul. Everything he knew, hoped, felt, or read, he told her. He never tired of talking and longing. Her role was not to talk, but to appear understanding (a difficult feat, as the young fellow blurted out nonsense) and to look exceedingly handsome and sympathetic. While Pen ranted for twenty minutes, clothed in words, the lovely Emily, who couldn't comprehend a tenth of his talk, thought about her own affairs: arranging the cold mutton, turning the black satin, or making a bonnet from her scarf. Pen spouted Byron and Moore; her business was to throw up her eyes or fix them on his face and cry, "Oh, 'tis beautiful! Ah, how exquisite! Repeat those lines again." Off the boy went, and she returned to her simple thoughts about the turned gown or hashed mutton.

Soon, Pen's passion was no secret to Emily or her father. On his second visit, his admiration was evident. As he departed, the old gentleman winked at his daughter over his glass of grog. "Faith, Milly darling, I think ye've hooked that chap."

"Pooh, 'tis only a boy, papa dear," Milly remarked. "Sure he's but a child." Pen would have been pleased by this phrase; he was galloping home wild with pleasure, shouting her name.

"Ye've hooked 'um any how," said the Captain. "Let me tell ye he's not a bad fish. I asked Tom at the George and Flint the grocer where his mother dwells—fine fortune, drives in a chariot, splendid park and grounds—Fairoaks Park—only son, property all his own at twenty-one. Ye might go further and not fare so well, Miss Fotheringay."

"Them boys are mostly talk," said Milly seriously. "Ye know at Dublin how ye went on about young Poldoody... but he went abroad, and his mother married him to an Englishwoman."

"Lord Poldoody was a young nobleman; and in them it's natural... But ye mustn't encourage this young chap too much, for, bedad, Jack Costigan won't have any thrifling with his daughter."

"No more will his daughter, papa, you may be sure of that," Milly said. "A little sip more of the punch,—sure, 'tis beautiful. Ye needn't be afraid about the young chap—I think I'm old enough to take care of myself, Captain Costigan."

So Pen came day after day, rushing in and galloping away, growing wilder with each visit. Sometimes the Captain was present, but having perfect confidence in his daughter, he often left the young couple to themselves, cocking his hat over his eye and strutting off on some errand. How delightful those interviews were! The Captain's drawing-room was a low wainscoted room with a large window looking into the Dean's garden. There Pen sat and talked—and talked—while Emily, looking beautiful as she worked, appeared calm and serene. Sunshine streamed in, lighting up her superb face and form. Mid-conversation, the great bell would boom, and he would pause smiling until the music died away, or the rooks in the cathedral elms made noise at sunset, or the organ and choristers hushed Pen's talking.

Miss Fotheringay, in a plain shawl and close bonnet, went to church every Sunday accompanied by her indefatigable father, who gave responses in a rich brogue, joined psalms, and behaved exemplarily.

Little Bows, the house-friend, was wroth at the notion of Miss Fotheringay marrying a stripling seven or eight years her junior. A cripple and deformed even more than Bingley the manager, Bows was a singular wild man of talent and humor. Attracted by her beauty, he began teaching her to act. He shrieked parts in his cracked voice; she learned them by rote, repeating them in full rich tones. He indicated attitudes, setting and moving those beautiful arms. Those who remember the grand actress on stage recall how she always used the same gestures, looks, and tones, standing on the same plank, rolling her eyes to the same degree, and weeping with heart-rending pathos over the same syllable. After coming out trembling and tearful, she would gather her hair behind the curtain, go home to a mutton-chop and brown stout, and sleep as resolutely as a porter.

Bows was indignant that his pupil should throw her chances away by bestowing her hand on a little country squire. London managers had seen her three years prior and failed from her utter stupidity. Since then, Bows had taken her in hand, working and screaming with indomitable patience while she followed him with dull obedience. She knew he made her; she let herself be made. She was not ungrateful or ill-humored; she was simply stupid. And Pen was madly in love with her.

The post-horses from the Clavering Arms arrived, carrying the party to the theatre. Pen was gratified to see a large audience. The young gentlemen from Baymouth occupied a front box with Mr. Foker and Mr. Spavin, splendidly attired. They saluted Pen cordially, examining his party with approval. Little Laura was a pretty red-cheeked girl with shining brown ringlets, and Mrs. Pendennis, dressed in black velvet with her diamond cross, looked majestic. Behind them sat Mr. Arthur and the gentle Smirke, who blushed to be there but was happy nonetheless. They brought books of 'Hamlet' to follow the tragedy, as honest countryfolk do. Samuel, the coachman, groom, and gardener took their place in the pit, alongside Mr. Foker's man. The pit was dotted with Dragoon non-commissioned officers, whose band played in the orchestra by permission of Colonel Swallowtail. The corpulent warrior himself, with his Waterloo medal and young men, made a handsome show in the boxes.

"Who is that odd-looking person bowing to you, Arthur?" Mrs. Pendennis asked.

Pen blushed. "His name is Captain Costigan, ma'am," he said—"a Peninsular officer." It was the Captain in new clothes, waving a large pair of white kid gloves, one sprawled over his heart and buttons. Pen said no more. How was Mrs. Pendennis to know Mr. Costigan was Miss Fotheringay's father?

Mr. Hornbull played Hamlet, Mr. Bingley modestly contenting himself with Horatio, reserving his strength for William in 'Black-Eyed Susan.' We need not discuss the play, except to say Ophelia looked lovely, performing with wild pathos, laughing, weeping, waving her white arms, and flinging flowers with charming madness. Her black hair tossed splendidly. She made the most charming corpse ever seen. While Hamlet and Laertes battled in her grave, she looked out from the back scenes with curiosity toward Pen's box.

There was but one voice in her praise. Mrs. Pendennis was in ecstasies with her beauty. Little Laura was bewildered but cried out great praises. Pen was charmed by the effect she produced on his mother, and the clergyman was enthusiastically enthusiastic.

When the curtain fell on the slaughtered personages, there was immense shouting and applause. The intrepid Smirke clapped his hands, crying "Bravo, Bravo" louder than the Dragoons. They were led cheering into action by the portly Swallowtail, who waved his cap. There was a roar of bravos; Pen bellowed the loudest, "Fotheringay! Fotheringay!" while Messrs. Spavin and Foker gave the view-halloo from their box. Even Mrs. Pendennis waved her pocket-handkerchief, and little Laura danced and looked up at Pen with wonder.

Hornbull led the beneficiaire forward. She looked handsome and radiant, hair still over her shoulders, and Pen hardly could contain himself for rapture. He leaned over his mother's chair, shouting and hurray-ing, waving his hat. It was all he could do to keep his secret from Helen and not say, "Look! That's the woman! Isn't she peerless? I tell you I love her." He disguised these feelings under enormous bellowing.

Miss Fotheringay surveyed the house with glances of gratitude, trembling over her favorite trap-door. She seized the flowers Foker discharged and even the shy rose Smirke offered, pressing them to her swelling heart. Twinkling in her breast, poor old Pen saw a locket he had bought with his last shilling and a sovereign borrowed from Smirke.

'Black-Eyed Susan' followed, charming our gentle-hearted friends. Susan, in a russet gown and pink ribbon, looked as lovely as Ophelia. Bingley was great as William; Goll, as the Admiral, looked like a figurehead; and Garbetts, as Captain Boldweather, a miscreant planning to carry off Susan, waved an immense cocked hat saying, "Come what may, he will be the ruin of her." All performed with accustomed talent, and it was with sincere regret that the curtain dropped.

If Pen had been alone with his mother in the carriage, he would have told her all that night. Instead, he sat on the box in the

In Which the Major Makes His Appearance

Major Arthur Pendennis arrived at Fairoaks in due time, though the journey there had been a trial of endurance that would make a modern commuter weep. He had spent a dreary night in a mail-coach where the atmosphere was thick with the sins of others: a burly fellow-passenger, swollen by layers of great-coats, had crowded him into a corner and kept him awake with snoring that bordered on indecency; opposite sat a widow who had sealed the windows shut, turning the vehicle into a sauna of Jamaica rum and water, which she sucked perpetually from a reticule bottle. Every moment of slumber was shattered by the twang of turnpike horns, the scuffling of his neighbor wedging him tighter, or the widow's feet brushing his tender toes. It was a life of horrors, a jolly old time of travel that has since vanished into fond memory, leaving behind only the question: how did anyone bear eight miles an hour for twenty-five hours on a hard seat with a gouty tendency and grumbling coachmen? Yet they did, and they were merry.

Among the chaos were two schoolboys heading home for midsummer holidays, sitting next to the Major's servant on the roof. At the inn at Bagshot, they had unloaded a cargo of ham, eggs, pie, pickles, tea, coffee, and boiled beef, a spectacle that left the Major, sipping feeble tea with a yellow face and bristly beard, in a state of tender dejection. He imagined Lord Steyne's dinner happening simultaneously, while the little lord beside him slept soundly through the noise, emerging at Fairoaks looking fresh as paint.
