The Major, meanwhile, descended with rheumatic griefs shooting through his limbs, a wig out of curl, and a yellow complexion, to be greeted reverentially by the porteress, the gardener's wife, and Mr. Morgan, his man.

Helen was waiting in the window, watching for her brother-in-law. She knew the Major did not like surprises; he required preparation. Pen, as a boy, had once incurred sad disgrace by stealing the Major's morocco box containing his back teeth, which he habitually removed during jolting coach rides. Without them, the Major refused to appear. Morgan made a mystery of the wigs, curling them in private places before introducing them to the master. The Major groaned, moaned, wheezed, and cursed Morgan through his toilet, behaving like an old buck who had been up all night with rheumatism and a long duty ahead. Finally belted, curled, and set straight, he descended into the drawing-room with a grave, majestic air, fitting for a man of business and fashion.

Pen was not there. Only Helen and little Laura were present, sewing at her knees. The Major offered her only a forefinger after saluting his sister-in-law. Laura took the finger trembling, dropped it, and fled the room. The Major did not wish to keep her; he had private reasons for disapproving of her existence in the house, reasons we shall reserve for a future occasion. Laura wandered out seeking Pen, finding him in the orchard pacing a walk in earnest conversation with Mr. Smirke. So absorbed was he that he did not hear Laura's clear voice until Smirke pulled him by the coat and pointed her out.

She ran up, seizing his hand. "Come in, Pen," she said. "There's somebody come; Uncle Arthur's come."

"He is, is he?" Pen replied, feeling her small hand. He looked at Smirke with uncommon fierceness, signaling *I am ready for him or any man*. Smirke cast up his eyes and sighed gently.

"Lead on, Laura," Pen said, with a half-fierce, half-comic air. "Lead on, and say I wait upon my uncle." He was laughing to hide a great anxiety, screwing his courage inwardly to face the ordeal he knew awaited him.

Pen had taken Smirke into his confidence over the last two days, especially after the outbreak surrounding the discovery of Doctor Portman. For forty-eight hours, he had talked nothing but Miss Fotheringay—Miss Emily Fotheringay—to his tutor. Smirke listened with ease, for he was in love himself, eager to propitiate Pen and enraptured by the goddess Emily, whose like he had never seen until now. Pen's fire, volubility, hot eloquence, and rich poetic tropes had half-convinced Smirke that the arrangement was feasible: Emily settled at Fairoaks, Captain Costigan in the yellow room for life, and Pen married at eighteen.

Indeed, the boy had almost talked his mother down too, parrying her objections with indignant good sense. He had convinced her that if the marriage was doomed in heaven, it was doomed; if the young woman was good, that was all she could ask. He dreaded the arrival of the guardian uncle, who would view the matter differently from the widow's simple, romantic, and utterly absurd perspective.

For as in the allegory of the gold and silver shield, each knight is right from his own point of view: so with marriage. Whether it is foolish or good depends on the vantage point. To the town folk, it means a snug house in Belgravia, dinner parties, a brougham for the Park, and a decent provision for the little Belgravians to come. To talk of love in lodgings is a babyish folly that cannot pay coach-hire. But to the country-bred, the book of life tells a different story. Helen, like many soft and sentimental women, had long speculated about her son's love life. It pleased her with a dismal pleasure to imagine the day she would give up everything—keys, the best bedroom, her place at the table—to see him happy. An empress was too good for him, but even humble Esther would do. However, an actress—a mature woman who had long ceased blushing except with rouge, standing under the eager glances of thousands, likely illiterate and ill-bred—was hard to accept. It was hard that such a one should be chosen, deposing the matron to give place to a Sultana.

These doubts Helen laid before Pen during the two days before the uncle's arrival. He met them with happy frankness, routing her objections with infinite satisfaction. Miss Costigan was a paragon of virtue, as sensitive as a timid maiden, as pure as unsullied snow, possessing the finest manners, graceful wit, and charming refinement. Her father was a good old gentleman of high family and fallen fortunes, accustomed to the best society in Europe. He was in no hurry and could wait till Pen was twenty-one. But Pen felt, with awful solemnity, that he was engaged in the one only passion of his life, and that DEATH alone could close it.

Helen told him, with a sad smile, that people survived these passions, citing the fatal example of Laura's poor father. Pen, however, was resolved that death must be his doom in case of disappointment. Rather than baulk him, the widow would have submitted to any sacrifice, gone on her knees, and kissed the feet of a Hottentot daughter-in-law.

Arthur knew his power over the widow. In those two days, he brought her almost into submission, patronizing her kindly. He spent one evening with the lovely pie-maker at Chatteris, bragging of his influence over his mother, and the other night composing a flaming, conceited copy of verses to his divinity, vowing like Montrose to make her famous with his sword and glorious by his pen, loving her as no mortal woman had been adored since the creation of womankind.

It was late that night, long after midnight, that wakeful Helen passed her son's door. She saw a light streaming through the chink into the dark passage and heard Pen tossing, tumbling, and mumbling verses. She waited anxiously, then turned the lock softly and entered. Pen did not see her at first; his face was turned away. Papers were scattered on his desk and bed. He was biting a pencil, thinking of rhymes and follies and passions. He was Hamlet jumping into Ophelia's grave; he was the Stranger taking Mrs. Haller to his arms, beautiful Mrs. Haller with raven ringlets. Despair, Byron, Thomas Moore, the Loves of the Angels, Waller, Herrick, Beranger—all the love songs he had ever read—were seething in his mind. He was at the height of imaginative frenzy when his mother found him.

"Arthur," said the mother's soft silver voice. He started up, turning round, clutching papers and pushing them under the pillow.

"Why don't you go to sleep, my dear?" she asked with a sweet tender smile, sitting on the bed and taking one of his hot hands.

Pen looked at her wildly. "I couldn't sleep," he said. "I—I was—I was writing." Upon this, he flung his arms around her neck and cried, "O mother! I love her, I love her!" How could such a kind soul help soothing and pitying him? The gentle creature did her best, wondering with tenderness that it was only yesterday he was a child in that bed, and how she used to say her prayers over it before he woke on holiday mornings.

The verses were grand, though Miss Fotheringay did not understand them. Old Cos, with a wink and a knowing finger on his nose, said, "Put them up with th' other letthers, Milly darling. Poldoody's pomes was nothing to this." So Milly locked up the manuscripts.

When the Major, dressed and presentable, presented himself to Mrs. Pendennis, he found within ten minutes that the poor widow was not merely distressed by the contemplated marriage, but actually more distressed by the thought that the boy himself was unhappy, fearing a violent altercation between uncle and nephew. She besought Major Pendennis to be very gentle with Arthur, hinting that he had a high spirit and would not brook unkind words. She recalled Dr. Portman's rough speech the previous night and how Pen's indignant answer had frightened her. "Recollect he is a man now," she said, laying a fair long hand on the Major's sleeve. "Be very—very cautious."

He took the hand, kissed it gallantly, and looked in her alarmed face with wonder and a scorn he was too polite to show. *Bon Dieu!* thought the old negotiator. *The boy has actually talked the woman round, and she'd get him a wife as she would a toy if Master cried for it. Why are there no such things as lettres-de-cachet—and a Bastille for young fellows of family?* The Major lived in such good company that he might be excused for feeling like an Earl. He kissed the widow's timid hand, pressed it in both his, and laid it down on the table with one of his own over it as he smiled.

"Confess," said he, "now, that you are thinking how you possibly can make it up to your conscience to let the boy have his own way."

She blushed and was moved in the usual manner of females. "I am thinking that he is very unhappy—and I am too——"

"To contradict him or to let him have his own wish?" asked the other, adding with great comfort to his inward self, "I'm d——d if he shall."

"To think that he should have formed so foolish and cruel and fatal an attachment," the widow said, "which can but end in pain whatever be the issue."

"The issue shan't be marriage, my dear sister," the Major said resolutely. "We're not going to have a Pendennis, the head of the house, marry a strolling mountebank from a booth. No, no, we won't marry into Greenwich Fair, ma'am."

"If the match is broken suddenly off," the widow interposed, "I don't know what may be the consequence. I know Arthur's ardent temper, the intensity of his affections, the agony of his pleasures and disappointments, and I tremble at this one if it must be. Indeed, indeed, it must not come on him too suddenly."

"My dear madam," the Major said with an air of deepest commiseration, "I've no doubt Arthur will have to suffer confoundedly before he gets over the little disappointment. But is he, think you, the only person who has been so rendered miserable?"

"No, indeed," said Helen, holding down her eyes. She was thinking of her own case, feeling seventeen again and most miserable.

"I, myself," whispered her brother-in-law, "have undergone a disappointment in early life. A young woman with fifteen thousand pounds, niece to an Earl—most accomplished creature—a third of her money would have run up my promotion in no time, and I should have been a lieutenant-colonel at thirty: but it might not be. I was but a penniless lieutenant: her parents interfered: and I embarked for India, where I had the honour of being secretary to Lord Buckley, when commander-in-chief—without her. What happened? We returned our letters, sent back our locks of hair (the Major here passed his fingers through his wig), we suffered—but we recovered. She is now a baronet's wife with thirteen grown-up children; altered, it is true, in person; but her daughters remind me of what she was, and the third is to be presented early next week."

Helen did not answer. She was still thinking of old times. One supposes that if one lives to be a hundred, there are certain passages of early life whereof the recollection will always carry one back to youth again, and that Helen was thinking of one of these.

"Look at my own brother, my dear creature," the Major continued gallantly. "He himself, you know, had a little disappointment when he started in the—the medical profession—an eligible opportunity presented itself. Miss Balls, I remember the name, was daughter of an apoth—a practitioner in very large practice; my brother had very nearly succeeded in his suit.—But difficulties arose: disappointments supervened, and—and I am sure he had no reason to regret the disappointment, which gave him this hand," said the Major, once more politely pressing Helen's fingers.

In Which Pen Is Kept Waiting at the Door, While the Reader Is Informed Who Little Laura Was.

Once upon a time, there existed a young gentleman of Cambridge University who descended upon a village to spend the long vacation. His destination was the home of young Helen Thistlewood, living with her mother, the widow of a lieutenant slain at Copenhagen. This youth, the Reverend Francis Bell, was the nephew of Mrs. Thistlewood and, consequently, the cousin of Miss Helen. It being proper etiquette, he took lodgings in his aunt's modest house, bringing with him three or four pupils to accompany him. Mr. Bell was a fellow of a college, renowned for his learning and skill as a tutor.

His two kinswomen quickly deduced that the reverend gentleman was engaged to be married, merely awaiting a college living to formalize the union. His intended bride was the daughter of another parson, who had served as Mr. Bell's private tutor in his youth. Indeed, it was under Mr. Coacher's roof, when Bell was but a boy of seventeen or eighteen, that the impetuous young man had thrown himself at the feet of Miss Martha Coacher while assisting her in picking peas in the garden. On his knees, amidst the peas and her presence, he pledged himself to an endless affection.

Miss Coacher was many years his senior, and her heart had been lacerated by numerous previous matrimonial disappointments. No fewer than three of her father's pupils had trifled with her affections; the village apothecary had despicably jilted her, and a dragoon officer, with whom she had danced many times during a happy season spent at Bath with her gouty grandmother, had once gaily shaken his bridle-rein and galloped away forever. Wounded by repeated ingratitude, it is no wonder that Martha Coacher panted for rest. She listened to the proposals of the gawky, gallant, honest boy with great kindness and good humor. At the end of his speech, she remarked, "Law, Bell, I'm sure you are too young to think of such things," but intimated that she would revolve the matter in her own virgin bosom. She could not refer Mr. Bell to her mother, for Mr. Coacher was a widower, immersed in his books, and thus unable to direct such a frail and wondrous article as a lady's heart, which Miss Martha had to manage alone.

A lock of her hair, tied up in a piece of blue ribbon, conveyed the result of the Vestal's conference to the happy Bell. Thrice before had she snipped off one of her auburn ringlets and given them away; the possessors were faithless, but the hair had grown again. Martha had indeed occasion to say that men were deceivers when she handed over this token of love to the simple boy.

Number six, however, was an exception to former passions—Francis Bell was the most faithful of lovers. When his time arrived to go to college, and it became necessary to acquaint Mr. Coacher of the arrangements, the latter cried, "God bless my soul, I hadn't the least idea what was going on," as was indeed very likely, for he had been taken in three times before in precisely a similar manner. Francis went to the University resolved to conquer honors so as to lay them at the feet of his beloved Martha.

This prize in view made him labor prodigiously. News came, term after term, of the honors he won. He sent the prize-books for his college essays to old Coacher and his silver declamation cup to Miss Martha. In due season, he was high among the Wranglers and a fellow of his college. During all these transactions, a constant tender correspondence was kept up with Miss Coacher, to whose influence, and perhaps with justice, he attributed the successes he had won.

By the time the Rev. Francis Bell, M.A., and Fellow and Tutor of his College, was twenty-six years of age, it happened that Miss Coacher was thirty-four, nor had her charms, manners, or temper improved since that sunny day in the springtime of life when he found her picking peas in the garden. Having achieved his honors, he relaxed in the ardor of his studies, and his judgment and tastes perhaps became cooler. The sunshine of the pea-garden faded away from Miss Martha, and poor Bell found himself engaged—and his hand pledged to that bond in a thousand letters—to a coarse, ill-tempered, ill-favored, ill-mannered, middle-aged woman.

It was in consequence of one of many altercations (in which Martha's eloquence shone, and in which she frequently indulged) that Francis refused to take his pupils to Bearleader's Green, where Mr. Coacher's living was, and where Bell was in the habit of spending the summer. He bethought himself that he would pass the vacation at his aunt's village, which he had not seen for many years—not since little Helen was a girl and used to sit on his knee. Down then he came and lived with them. Helen was grown a beautiful young woman now. The cousins were nearly four months together, from June to October. They walked in the summer evenings; they met in the early morn. They read out of the same book when the old lady dozed at night over the candles. What little Helen knew, Frank taught her. She sang to him; she gave her artless heart to him. She was aware of all his story. Had he made any secret? Had he not shown the picture of the woman to whom he was engaged, and with a blush, her letters, hard, eager, and cruel? The days went on and on, happier and closer, with more kindness, more confidence, and more pity. At last, one morning in October came, when Francis went back to college, and the poor girl felt that her tender heart was gone with him.

Frank too wakened up from the delightful midsummer dream to the horrible reality of his own pain. He gnashed and tore at the chain which bound him. He was frantic to break it and be free. Should he confess? Give his savings to the woman to whom he was bound and beg his release? There was time yet; he temporized. No living might fall in for years to come. The cousins went on corresponding sadly and fondly; the betrothed woman, hard, jealous, and dissatisfied, complained bitterly, and with reason, of her Francis's altered tone.

At last, things came to a crisis, and the new attachment was discovered. Francis owned it, cared not to disguise it, rebuked Martha with her violent temper and angry imperiousness, and, worst of all, with her inferiority and her age. Her reply was that if he did not keep his promise, she would carry his letters into every court in the kingdom—letters in which his love was pledged to her ten thousand times; and, after exposing him to the world as the perjurer and traitor he was, she would kill herself.

Frank had one more interview with Helen, whose mother was dead then, and who was living companion with old Lady Pontypool—one more interview, where it was resolved that he was to do his duty; that is, to redeem his vow; that is, to pay a debt cozened from him by a sharper; that is, to make two honest people miserable. So the two judged their duty to be, and they parted.

The living fell in only too soon; but yet Frank Bell was quite a grey and worn-out man when he was inducted into it. Helen wrote him a letter on his marriage, beginning "My dear Cousin" and ending "always truly yours." She sent him back the other letters and the lock of his hair—all but a small piece. She had it in her desk when she was talking to the Major.

Bell lived for three or four years in his living, at the end of which time, the Chaplainship of Coventry Island falling vacant, Frank applied for it privately, and having procured it, announced the appointment to his wife. She objected, as she did to everything. He told her bitterly that he did not want her to come; so she went. Bell went out in Governor Crawley's time and was very intimate with that gentleman in his later years. And it was in Coventry Island, years after his own marriage and five years after he had heard of the birth of Helen's boy, that his own daughter was born.

She was not the daughter of the first Mrs. Bell, who died of island fever very soon after Helen Pendennis and her husband, to whom Helen had told everything, wrote to inform Bell of the birth of their child. "I was old, was I?" said Mrs. Bell the first; "I was old, and her inferior, was I? but I married you, Mr. Bell, and kept you from marrying her?" and hereupon she died. Bell married a colonial lady, whom he loved fondly. But he was not doomed to prosper in love; and, this lady dying in childbirth, Bell gave up too, sending his little girl home to Helen Pendennis and her husband, with a parting prayer that they would befriend her.

The little thing came to Fairoaks from Bristol, which is not very far off, dressed in black, and in company of a soldier's wife, her nurse, at parting from whom she wept bitterly. But she soon dried up her grief under Helen's motherly care. Round her neck she had a locket with hair, which Helen had given, ah, how many years ago! to poor Francis, dead and buried. This child was all that was left of him, and she cherished, as so tender a creature would, the legacy which he had bequeathed to her. The girl's name, as his dying letter stated, was Helen Laura. But John Pendennis, though he accepted the trust, was always rather jealous of the orphan; and gloomily ordered that she should be called by her own mother's name, and not by that first one which her father had given her. She was afraid of Mr. Pendennis to the last moment of his life. And it was only when her husband was gone that Helen dared openly to indulge in the tenderness which she felt for the little girl.

Thus it was that Laura Bell became Mrs. Pendennis's daughter. Neither her husband nor that gentleman's brother, the Major, viewed her with very favorable eyes. She reminded the first of circumstances in his wife's life which he was forced to accept but would have forgotten much more willingly; and as for the second, how could he regard her? She was neither related to his own family of Pendennis nor to any nobleman in this empire, and she had but a couple of thousand pounds for her fortune.

And now let Mr. Pen come in, who has been waiting all this while.

Having strung up his nerves and prepared himself, without at the door, for the meeting, he came to it, determined to face the awful uncle. He had settled in his mind that the encounter was to be a fierce one and was resolved on bearing it through with all the courage and dignity of the famous family which he represented. And he flung open the door and entered with the most severe and warlike expression, armed cap-a-pie as it were, with lance couched and plumes displayed, and glancing at his adversary as if to say, "Come on, I'm ready."

The old man of the world, as he surveyed the boy's demeanor, could hardly help a grin at his admirable pompous simplicity. Major Pendennis too had examined his ground; and finding that the widow was already half won over to the enemy, and having a shrewd notion that threats and tragic exhortations would have no effect upon the boy, who was inclined to be perfectly stubborn and awfully serious, the Major laid aside the authoritative manner at once. With the most good-humored natural smile in the world, he held out his hands to Pen, shook the lad's passive fingers gaily, and said, "Well, Pen, my boy, tell us all about it."

Helen was delighted with the generosity of the Major's good humor. On the contrary, it quite took aback and disappointed poor Pen, whose nerves were strung up for a tragedy, and who felt that his grand entree was altogether baulked and ludicrous. He blushed and winced with mortified vanity and bewilderment. He felt immensely inclined to begin to cry—"I—I—I didn't know that you were come till just now," he said; "is—is—town very full, I suppose?"

If Pen could hardly gulp his tears down, it was all the Major could do to keep from laughter. He turned round and shot a comical glance at Mrs. Pendennis, who too felt that the scene was at once ridiculous and sentimental. And so, having nothing to say, she went up and kissed Mr. Pen; as he thought of her tenderness and soft obedience to his wishes, it is very possible too the boy was melted.

"What a couple of fools they are," thought the old guardian. "If I hadn't come down, she would have driven over in state to pay a visit and give her blessing to the young lady's family."

"Come, come," said he, still grinning at the couple, "let us have as little sentiment as possible, and, Pen, my good fellow, tell us the whole story."

Pen got back at once to his tragic and heroical air. "The story is, sir," said he, "as I have written it to you before. I have made the acquaintance of a most beautiful and most virtuous lady; of a high family, although in reduced circumstances: I have found the woman in whom I know that the happiness of my life is centred; I feel that I never, never can think about any woman but her. I am aware of the difference of our ages and other difficulties in my way. But my affection was so great that I felt I could surmount all these; that we both could: and she has consented to unite her lot with mine, and to accept my heart and my fortune."

"How much is that, my boy?" said the Major. "Has anybody left you some money? I don't know that you are worth a shilling in the world."

"You know what I have is his," cried out Mrs. Pendennis.

"Good heavens, madam, hold your tongue!" was what the guardian was disposed to say; but he kept his

In Which the Major Opens the Campaign

Let those who possess a genuine, hearty appreciation for the glittering machinery of London society—the exclusive privilege of slipping into its most select circles—admit that Major Pendennis was a man of extraordinary generosity and deep affection in the sacrifice he now undertook. In May, he surrendered the city. He gave up his newspapers and his mornings, his afternoons spent hopping from club to club, his intimate visits to "my Ladies," his gallops along Rotten Row, his dinners, and his coveted stall at the Opera. He abandoned his rapid escapades to Fulham or Richmond on weekends, the bows exchanged with my Lord Duke or my Lord Marquis at great entertainments, and the inevitable mention of his name in the *Morning Post* the following day. He traded these quieter, more select, and delightful festivals for a solitary retreat to a small country house, sharing it with a simple widow, a greenhorn of a son, a mawkish curate, and a little girl of ten.

He made the sacrifice, and it was the greater because few understood its extent. His letters arrived franked from town, and he presented the invitations to Helen with a visible sigh. It was a scene of beautiful, tragic self-denial to watch him refuse party after party—at least to those who could comprehend the melancholy grandeur of his exile, though Helen merely smiled at the awful pathos with which the Major spoke of the *Court Guide*. Young Pen, however, looked upon the great names on the superscriptions of his uncle's letters with profound respect. He listened with constant interest and sympathy to the Major's stories of the fashionable world.

The elder Pendennis's rich memory was a vault storing thousands of such delightful tales, which he poured into Pen's willing ear with unfailing eloquence. He knew the name and pedigree of everyone in the Peerage and their intricate relations. "My dear boy," he would say, with mournful earnestness and veracity, "you cannot begin your genealogical studies too early. I wish to Heaven you would read in *Debrett* every day. Not so much the historical part—for the pedigrees, between ourselves, are many of them fabulous, and few families can show such a clear descent as our own—as the account of family alliances and who is related to whom. I have known a man's career blasted by ignorance on this all-important subject. Why, only last month, at dinner at my Lord Hobanob's, a young man recently received among us, young Mr. Suckling (author of a work, I believe), began to speak lightly of Admiral Bowser's conduct for 'ratting' to Ministers in the most audacious manner. But who do you think sat next to this Mr. Suckling? Next to him was Lady Grampound Bowser's daughter, and opposite him was Lord Grampound Bowser's son-in-law. The infatuated young man went on cutting jokes at the Admiral's expense, fancying the whole world laughed with him. I leave you to imagine Lady Hobanob's feelings—Hobanob's!—as the wretched intruder exposed himself. He will never dine again in South Street. I promise you that."

With such discourses, the Major entertained his nephew as he paced the terrace for his two-hour constitutional or as they sat over wine after dinner. He grieved that Sir Francis Clavering had not moved to the park to create a local society, and mourned that Lord Eyrie was absent, preventing the presentation of Pen to his lordship. "He has daughters," the Major noted. "Who knows? You might have married Lady Emily or Lady Barbara Trehawk; but all those dreams are over. My poor fellow, you must lie on the bed you have made for yourself."

These words inclined young Pendennis seriously. While printed anecdotes lack the oral magic of delivery, the Major's tales of Great George, the Royal Dukes, and the statesmen and beauties of the day filled young Pen's soul with longing. He found the conversations with his guardian far from tedious, whereas they sadly bored and perplexed poor Mrs. Pendennis.

It cannot be said that Mr. Pen's new guide, philosopher, and friend discoursed on the most elevated subjects, nor treated them with the highest manner. Yet his morality, such as it was, was consistent. It might not advance a man's prospects in the next world, but it was calculated to secure his interests in this one. The Major never doubted that his views were the only practicable ones and that his conduct was perfectly virtuous. He was a man of honor, in a word, with his eyes wide open. He took pity on this young greenhorn nephew and sought to open his eyes as well.

No man attended church more regularly in the country than the old bachelor. "It doesn't matter so much in town, Pen," he explained, "for there the women go and the men are not missed. But when a gentleman is *sur ses terres*, he must set an example to the country people. If I could turn a tune, I'd even sing. The Duke of Saint David's, whom I have the honor of knowing, always sings in the country, and let me tell you, it has a doosed fine effect from the family pew. And you are somebody down here. As long as the Claverings are away, you are the first man in the parish: and as good as any. You might represent the town if you played your cards well. Your poor dear father would have done so had he lived; so might you.—Not if you marry a lady, however amiable, whom the country people won't meet.—Well, well: it's a painful subject. Let us change it, my boy."

But if Major Pendennis changed the subject once, he returned to it a score of times that day. The moral of his discourse was always that Pen was throwing himself away. Now, it requires little coaxing to make a simple boy believe he is a very fine fellow.

Pen took his uncle's counsels to heart. He was glad enough to listen, for the conversation of Captain Costigan had become anything but pleasant. The image of that tipsy old father-in-law haunted him with terror. He could not bring that unshaven, punch-reeking man to associate with his mother. Even regarding Emily, he faltered when the pitiless guardian questioned him. "Was she accomplished?" He was obliged to admit, no. "Was she clever?" Well, she possessed a very good average intellect, but he could not absolutely say she was clever. "Come, let us see some of her letters." So Pen confessed he possessed only three, which were trivial invitations or answers.

"She is cautious enough," the Major said dryly. "She is older than you, my poor boy;" and then he apologized with utmost frankness and humility, flinging himself upon Pen's good feelings. He begged the lad to excuse a fond old uncle who had only his family's honor in view—for Arthur was ready to flame up in indignation whenever Miss Costigan's honesty was doubted, swearing he would never have her name mentioned lightly and would never part from her.

He repeated this to his uncle and friends at home, and also, it must be confessed, to Miss Fotheringay and the amiable family at Chatteris, with whom he still spent some time. Miss Emily was alarmed upon hearing of the guardian's arrival, rightly conceiving that the Major came with hostile intentions. "I suppose ye intend to leave me, now your grand relation has come down from town. He'll carry ye off, and you'll forget your poor Emily, Mr. Arthur!"

Forget her! In her presence, or that of Miss Rouncy, the Columbine and Milly's confidential friend, or even the Captain himself, Pen swore he could think of no other woman but his beloved Miss Fotheringay. The Captain, looking up at his foils hung as trophies on the wall where Pen and he used to fence, grimly declared he would advise no man to meddle rashly with the affections of his darling child. He vowed he would never believe his gallant young Arthur, whom he treated as a son, would ever be guilty of conduct so revolting to every idea of honor and humanity.

He went up and embraced Pen after speaking. He cried, wiping his eye with one large, dirty hand as he clasped Pen with the other. Arthur shuddered in that grasp, thinking of his uncle at home. His father-in-law looked unusually dirty and shabby; the odor of whisky-and-water was even more decided than usual. How was he to bring that man and his mother together? He trembled when he recalled writing to Costigan (enclosing a sovereign, a loan the worthy gentleman needed) and saying that one day he hoped to sign himself his affectionate son, Arthur Pendennis. He was glad to escape Chatteris that day—from Miss Rouncy the confidante, from the old toping father-in-law, from the divine Emily herself. "O, Emily, Emily," he cried inwardly as he rattled homewards on Rebecca, "you little know what sacrifices I am making for you!—for you who are always so cold, so cautious, so mistrustful;" and he thought of a character in Pope to whom he had often involuntarily compared her.

Pen never rode over to Chatteris on a certain errand, but the Major discovered on what errand the boy had been. Faithful to his plan, Major Pendennis gave his nephew no direct hindrance; yet the constant feeling that the senior's eye was upon him, accompanied by an uneasy shame attendant upon the inevitable confession evening conversation would elicit, made Pen visit his charmer less frequently than before. There was no use trying to deceive him; no pretext of dining with Smirke or reading Greek plays with Foker sufficed. Pen felt, upon returning from his flying visits, that everybody knew whence he came, appearing quite guilty before his mother and guardian over their books or game of picquet.

Once, having walked half a mile to the Fairoaks Inn beyond the Lodge gates to catch the *Competitor* coach, a man on the roof touched his hat to the young gentleman. It was his uncle's man, Mr. Morgan, going on a message and taken up at the Lodge. Mr. Morgan returned by the *Rival*, so Pen enjoyed the domestic's company both ways. Nothing was said at home. The lad seemed to have every decent liberty; yet he felt dimly watched and guarded, sensing eyes upon him even in the presence of his Dulcinea.

In fact, Pen's suspicions were not unfounded. His guardian had sent forth to gather all possible information regarding the lad and his interesting young friend. The discreet and ingenious Mr. Morgan, a London confidential valet whose fidelity could be trusted, had visited Chatteris more than once. He delicately cross-examined the waiters, ostlers, and inmates of the bar at the George, extracting what little they knew of the worthy Captain. He was not held in high regard there; the waiters never saw the color of his money and were warned not to furnish him with liquor for which others were not responsible. He swaggered sadly about the coffee-room, consumed a toothpick, looked over the paper, and if any friend asked him to dinner, he stayed. Morgan heard of Pen's acquaintance with Mr. Foker and went to Baymouth to enter relations with that gentleman's man, but the young student was gone to a Coast Regatta, and his servant traveled in charge of the dressing-case.

From the servants of the officers at the barracks, Mr. Morgan learned that the Captain had so frequently and outrageously inebriated himself there that Colonel Swallowtail had forbidden him the messroom. The indefatigable Morgan then communicated with inferior actors at the theatre, pumping them over cigars and punch. All agreed that Costigan was poor, shabby, and given to debt and drink. But there was not a breath against the reputation of Miss Fotheringay. Her father's courage had displayed itself on more than one occasion toward persons disposed to treat his daughter with freedom. She never came to the theatre without her father; in his most inebriated moments, that gentleman kept a watch over her. Finally, Mr. Morgan, from his own experience, added that he had seen her act and was uncommonly delighted with the performance, besides thinking her a most splendid woman.

Mrs. Creed, the pew-opener, confirmed these statements to Doctor Portman, who examined her personally and threatened her with the terrors of the Church one afternoon after service. Mrs. Creed had nothing unfavorable to divulge about her lodger. She saw nobody but one or two ladies of the theatre. The Captain did intoxicate himself sometimes and did not always pay his rent regularly, but he did when he had money, or rather, Miss Fotheringay did. Since the young gentleman from Clavering had arrived to take fencing lessons, one or two more had come from the barracks; Sir Derby Oaks and his young friend, Mr. Foker, often together, driving over from Baymouth in the tandem. But on the occasions of the lessons, Miss F. was very seldom present, generally coming downstairs to Mrs. Creed's own room.

The Doctor and the Major, consulting together as they often did, groaned in spirit over that information. Major Pendennis openly expressed his disappointment; and I believe the Divine himself was ill-pleased at not being able to jack a hole in poor Miss Fotheringay's reputation.

Even regarding Pen himself, Mrs. Creed's reports were desperately favorable. "Whenever he come," Mrs. Creed said, "she always have me or one of the children with her. And Mrs. Creed, marm, says she, if you please, marm, you'll on no account leave the room when that young gentleman's here. And many's the time I've seen him a lookin' as if he wished I was away, poor young man: and he took to coming in service-time, when I wasn't at home, of course: but she always had one of the boys up if her Pa wasn't at home, or old Mr. Bowser with her teaching her her lesson, or one of the young ladies of the theayter."

It was all true: whatever encouragements might have been given him before he avowed his passion, the prudence of Miss Emily was prodigious after Pen had declared himself. The poor fellow chafed against her hopeless reserve, which maintained his ardour as it excited his anger.

The Major surveyed

Major Pendennis sauntered home with the weary gait of a man who has seen the world but not necessarily enjoyed its finer points. Upon reaching the George Inn, he found Mr. Morgan, his valet, standing sentinel at the door like a loyal hound waiting for his master's return. The Major, intending to retreat to his room with a candle, was intercepted by the valet's knowing deference.

"I think, sir, if you'd grace the coffee-room, there's a young gentleman there you'd like to see," Morgan announced, as if revealing a state secret.

"Is Mr. Arthur here?" the Major demanded, his voice rising with the heat of a sudden storm.

"No, sir. But his great friend, Mr. Foker, sir. Lady Agnes Foker's son. He's been asleep in the coffee-room since dinner, just roused by the bell for coffee. I thought, p'raps, you might like to engage him in conversation," Morgan suggested, ushering the Major inside.

The Major entered to find Mr. Foker occupying the entire space. The young man was rubbing his eyes, seated before a table littered with empty decanters and the skeletal remains of a heavy dessert. He had intended to attend the play, but a copious meal had summoned a more potent sedative than drama; his legs were propped on the bench, and he had traded the stage for a nap. The Major pondered how to approach this sleeping giant, but Foker woke with a start and a smile.

"Like to look at the evening paper, sir?" Foker offered, holding up *The Globe* with the affability of a man who knows he is charming.

"I am very much obliged," the Major replied, bowing with a mixture of gratitude and suspicion. "If I don't mistake the family likeness, I have the pleasure of addressing Mr. Henry Foker, Lady Agnes Foker's son. I have the happiness to name her ladyship among my acquaintances—and you bear, sir, a Rosherville face."

"Hullo! I beg your pardon," Foker corrected himself, realizing his slip. "I took you for a commercial gent." He paused, then added, "To whom have I the pleasure of speaking?"

"To a relative of a friend and schoolfellow of yours—Arthur Pendennis, my nephew, who speaks of you in terms of great regard. I am Major Pendennis, of whom you may have heard. May I take my soda-water at your table? I have sat at your grandfather's."

"Sir, you do me proud," Foker said, pouring on the courtesy. "And so you are Arthur Pendennis's uncle?"

"And guardian," the Major added.

"He's as good a fellow as ever stepped, sir," Foker declared.

"I am glad you think so."

"And clever, too—I was always a stupid chap, I was—but you see, sir, I know 'em when they are clever, and like 'em of that sort."

"You show your taste and your modesty, too," the Major observed. "I have heard Arthur repeatedly speak of you, and he said your talents were very good."

"I'm not good at the books," Foker admitted, wagging his head. "Never could manage that—Pendennis could; he used to do half the chaps' verses. And yet"—the young gentleman broke out candidly—"you are his guardian; and I hope you will pardon me for saying that I think he's what we call flat."

The Major found himself instantly plunged into a most interesting and confidential conversation. "And how is Arthur a flat?" he asked, smiling.

"You know," Foker answered, winking with a lack of scruple that would have impressed the Duke of Wellington, "You know Arthur's a flat,—about women I mean."

"He is not the first of us, my dear Mr. Harry," the Major replied. "I have heard something of this—but pray tell me more."

"Why, sir, you see—it's partly my fault. He went to the play one night—for you see I'm down here reading for my little go during the Long, only I come over from Baymouth pretty often in my drag—well, sir, we went to the play, and Pen was struck all of a heap with Miss Fotheringay—Costigan her real name is—an uncommon fine gal she is too; and the next morning I introduced him to the General, as we call her father—a regular old scamp and such a boy for the whisky-and-water!—and he's gone on being intimate there. And he's fallen in love with her—and I'm blessed if he hasn't proposed to her," Foker said, slapping his hand on the table until the dessert began to jingle.

"What! you know it too?" asked the Major.

"Know it! don't I? and many more too. We were talking about it at mess yesterday, chaffing Derby Oaks—until he was as mad as a hatter. Know Sir Derby Oaks? We dined together, and he went to the play: we were standing at the door smoking, I remember, when you passed in to dinner."

"I remember Sir Thomas Oaks, his father, before he was a Baronet or a Knight; he lived in Cavendish-square, and was physician to Queen Charlotte."

"The young one is making the money spin, I can tell you," Mr. Foker said.

"And is Sir Derby Oaks," the Major said, with great delight and anxiety, "another soupirant?"

"Another what?" inquired Mr. Foker.

"Another admirer of Miss Fotheringay?"

"Lord bless you! we call him Mondays, Wednesdays, and Fridays, and Pen Tuesdays, Thursdays, and Saturdays. But mind you, nothing wrong! No, no! Miss F. is a deal too wide-awake for that, Major Pendennis. She plays one off against the other. What you call two strings to her bow."

"I think you seem tolerably wide-awake, too, Mr. Foker," the Major laughed.

"Pretty well, thank you, sir—how are you?" Foker replied, imperturbably. "I'm not clever, p'raps: but I am rather downy; and partial friends say I know what's o'clock tolerably well. Can I tell you the time of day in any way?"

"Upon my word," the Major answered, quite delighted, "I think you may be of very great service to me. You are a young man of the world, and with such one likes to deal. And as such I need not inform you that our family is by no means delighted at this absurd intrigue in which Arthur is engaged."

"I should rather think not," said Mr. Foker. "Connexion not eligible. Too much beer drunk on the premises. No Irish need apply. That I take to be your meaning."

The Major confirmed it was exactly so, though he did not fully grasp the valet's cryptic logic. He proceeded to examine his new acquaintance regarding the amiable family into which his nephew proposed to enter, and soon extracted a number of particulars regarding the House of Costigan.

One must do Mr. Foker the justice to say that he spoke most favorably of Mr. and Miss Costigan's moral character. "You see," said he, "I think the General is fond of the jovial bowl, and if I wanted to be very certain of my money, it isn't in his pocket I'd invest it—but he has always kept a watchful eye on his daughter, and neither he nor she will stand anything but what's honourable. Pen's attentions to her are talked about in the whole Company, and I hear all about them from a young lady who used to be very intimate with her, and with whose family I sometimes take tea in a friendly way. Miss Rouncy says, Sir Derby Oaks has been hanging about Miss Fotheringay ever since his regiment has been down here; but Pen has come in and cut him out lately, which has made the Baronet so mad, that he has been very near on the point of proposing too. Wish he would; and you'd see which of the two Miss Fotheringay would jump at."

"I thought as much," the Major said. "You give me a great deal of pleasure, Mr. Foker. I wish I could have seen you before."

"Didn't like to put in my oar," replied the other. "Don't speak till I'm asked, when, if there's no objections, I speak pretty freely. Heard your man had been hankering about my servant—didn't know myself what was going on until Miss Fotheringay and Miss Rouncy had the row about the ostrich feathers, when Miss R. told me everything."

"Miss Rouncy, I gather, was the confidante of the other."

"Confidant? I believe you. Why, she's twice as clever a girl as Fotheringay, and literary and that, while Miss Foth can't do much more than read."

"She can write," said the Major, remembering Pen's breast-pocket.

Foker broke out into a sardonic "He, he! Rouncy writes her letters," he said; "every one of 'em; and since they've quarrelled, she don't know how the deuce to get on. Miss Rouncy is an uncommon pretty hand, whereas the old one makes dreadful work of the writing and spelling when Bows ain't by. Rouncy's been settin' her copies lately—she writes a beautiful hand, Rouncy does."

"I suppose you know it pretty well," said the Major archly, upon which Mr. Foker winked at him again.

"I would give a great deal to have a specimen of her hand-writing," continued Major Pendennis, "I dare say you could give me one."

"No, no, that would be too bad," Foker replied. "Perhaps I oughtn't to have said as much as I have. Miss F.'s writin' ain't so very bad, I dare say; only she got Miss R. to write the first letter, and has gone on ever since. But you mark my word, that till they are friends again the letters will stop."

"I hope they will never be reconciled," the Major said with great sincerity; "and I can't tell you how delighted I am to have had the good fortune of making your acquaintance. You must feel, my dear sir, as a man of the world, how fatal to my nephew's prospects in life is this step which he contemplates, and how eager we all must be to free him from this absurd engagement."

"He has come out uncommon strong," said Mr. Foker; "I have seen his verses; Rouncy copied 'em. And I said to myself when I saw 'em, 'Catch me writin' verses to a woman,—that's all.'"

"He has made a fool of himself, as many a good fellow has before him. How can we make him see his folly, and cure it? I am sure you will give us what aid you can in extricating a generous young man from such a pair of schemers as this father and daughter seem to be. Love on the lady's side is out of the question."

"Love, indeed!" Foker said. "If Pen hadn't two thousand a year when he came of age——"

"If Pen hadn't what?" cried out the Major in astonishment.

"Two thousand a year: hasn't he got two thousand a year?—the General says he has."

"My dear friend," shrieked out the Major, with an eagerness which this gentleman rarely showed, "thank you!—thank you!—I begin to see now.—Two thousand a year! Why, his mother has but five hundred a year in the world.—She is likely to live to eighty, and Arthur has not a shilling but what she can allow him."

"What! he ain't rich then?" Foker asked.

"Upon my honour he has no more than what I say."

"And you ain't going to leave him anything?"

The Major had sunk every shilling he could scrape together on an annuity, and of course was going to leave Pen nothing; but he did not tell Foker this. "How much do you think a Major on half-pay can save?" he asked. "If these people have been looking at him as a fortune, they are utterly mistaken—and—and you have made me the happiest man in the world."

"Sir to you," said Mr. Foker, politely. When they parted for the night, they shook hands with the greatest cordiality; the younger gentleman promising the elder not to leave Chatteris without a further conversation in the morning. And as the Major went up to his room, and Mr. Foker smoked his cigar against the door pillars of the George, Pen, very likely, ten miles off, was lying in bed kissing the letter from his Emily.

The next morning, before Mr. Foker drove off in his drag, the insinuating Major had actually secured a letter of Miss Rouncy's in his own pocket-book. Let it be a lesson to women how they write. And in very high spirits Major Pendennis went to call upon Doctor Portman at the Deanery, telling him what happy discoveries he had made on the previous night. As they sat in confidential conversation in the Dean's oak breakfast-parlour, they could look across the lawn and see Captain Costigan's window, at which poor Pen had been only too visible some three weeks prior. The Doctor was most indignant against Mrs. Creed, the landlady, for her duplicity in concealing Sir Derby Oaks's constant visits to her lodgers, and threatened to excommunicate her out of the Cathedral. But the wary Major thought that all things were for the best; and, having taken counsel with himself overnight, felt himself quite strong enough to go and face Captain Costigan.

"I'm going to fight the dragon," he said, with a laugh, to Doctor Portman.

"And I shrive you, sir, and bid good fortune go with you," answered the Doctor. Perhaps he and Mrs. Portman and Miss Myra, as they sat with their friend, the Dean's lady, in her drawing-room, looked up more than once at the enemy's window to see if they could perceive any signs of the

The Major and Captain Costigan were veterans of the old school, men who had stared down the enemy without flinching, so their composure remained unshaken. But the rest of the assembly in Cos's sitting-room found themselves thoroughly rattled by Pendennis's sudden appearance. Miss Fotheringay's heart, usually as steady as a clockwork, began to hammer like a trapped bird; her cheeks erupted in a flush so vibrant and healthy it seemed to glow from within, especially as Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks fixed her with a glare that could curdle milk. Meanwhile, the little crooked old man perched in the window-seat, who had been trying to transcribe theatre music while watching the two gentlemen fence (a spectacle of such vigorous stamping and leaping that he had already abandoned his pen), looked up with wide-eyed anticipation as the Major, sporting boots blacker than midnight, swept into the room distributing bows so graceful they nearly knocked over the furniture.

"Me daughter—me friend, Mr. Bows—me gallant young pupil and friend, I may call 'um, Sir Derby Oaks," announced Costigan, waving his hand with the flourish of a man conducting an orchestra. "In one moment, Meejor, I'm your humble servant." With that, he dashed into his tiny sleeping chamber to perform a rapid transformation: twisting his lank hair with an ancient brush, tearing off a threadbare stock to reveal a pristine new one crafted by Emily, donning a crisp collar, and slipping into a fresh coat ordered specifically for her benefit. For the active Captain, this metamorphosis took less than a minute.

Sir Derby followed suit, emerging moments later encased in his tight-fitting shell-jacket, which hugged his large frame with the precision of a second skin—a garment that elicited admiration from Miss Fotheringay, poor Pen, and presumably everyone else present.

While the Major conversed with the actress, exchanging pleasantries about the weather, Costigan reappeared in his new "Shoot."

"I needn't apologize to ye, Meejor," he said, adopting his richest, most courteous tone, "for receiving ye in me shirt-sleeves."

"An old soldier can't be better employed than in teaching a young one the use of his sword," the Major replied gallantly. "I recall hearing in old times that you could wield yours with considerable skill, Captain Costigan."

"What, ye've heard of Jack Costigan, Major?" the other exclaimed, feigning great surprise.

The Major had indeed; he had pumped his nephew about this new Irish acquaintance. Whether he possessed any other knowledge of the Captain beyond this gossip, or if he genuinely remembered him, remains unclear. However, Major Pendennis was a man of honor and undoubted veracity. He stated with perfect conviction that he recalled meeting Mr. Costigan and hearing him sing at Sir Richard Strachan's table during the Walcheren campaign.

At this revelation, delivered with such bland cordiality, Bows looked up, utterly bewildered. "But we will discuss these matters another time," the Major continued, perhaps wishing to avoid committing himself further. "It is to Miss Fotheringay that I came to pay my respects today." He executed another bow so courtly and gracious that, if she had been a duchess, he could not have made it more handsome.

"I had heard of your performances from my nephew, madam," the Major said, "who raves about you, as I believe you know pretty well. But Arthur is but a boy, a wild and enthusiastic young fellow whose opinions one must not take *au pied de la lettre*. I confess I was anxious to judge for myself. Permit me to say your performance delighted and astonished me. I have seen our finest actresses, and on my word, I think you surpass them all. You are as majestic as Mrs. Siddons."

"Faith, I always said so," Costigan winked at his daughter. "Major, take a chair." Milly rose, retrieved an unripped satin garment from the only vacant seat, and brought the chair to Major Pendennis with one of her finest curtseys.

"You are as pathetic as Miss O'Neill," he continued, bowing and seating himself. "Your snatches of song reminded me of Mrs. Jordan in her prime, Captain Costigan; and your manner reminded me of Mars. Did you ever see the Mars, Miss Fotheringay?"

"There were two Mahers in Crow Street," remarked Miss Emily. "Fanny was well enough, but Biddy was no great shakes."

"Sure, the Major means the god of war, Milly, my dear," interjected the parent.

"It is not that Mars I meant, though Venus, I suppose, may be pardoned for thinking about him," the Major replied with a smile directed squarely at Sir Derby Oaks, who had now re-entered in his shell-jacket. But the lady failed to grasp the words, nor did the compliment pacify Sir Derby, who, likely misunderstanding it entirely, received it with great sulkiness and stiffness. He scowled uneasily at Miss Fotheringay with an expression that seemed to ask, *What the deuce is this man doing here?*

Major Pendennis was not in the least annoyed by the gentleman's ill-humor; on the contrary, it delighted him. "So," he thought, "a rival is in the field." He offered up silent vows that Sir Derby might be not only a rival but a winner in this love-match in which he and Pen were engaged.

"I fear I interrupted your fencing lesson," the Major said aloud. "But my stay in Chatteris is very short, and I was anxious to make myself known to my old fellow-campaigner, Captain Costigan, and to see a lady nearer who had charmed me so much from the stage. I was not the only man *épris* last night, Miss Fotheringay (if I must call you so, though your own family name is a very ancient and noble one). There was a reverend friend of mine who went home in raptures with Ophelia; and I saw Sir Derby Oaks fling a bouquet which no actress ever merited better. I should have brought one myself, had I known what I was going to see. Are not those the very flowers in a glass of water on the mantelpiece yonder?"

"I am very fond of flowers," said Miss Fotheringay, casting a languishing ogle at Sir Derby Oaks—but the Baronet still scowled sulkily.

"Sweets to the sweet—isn't that the expression of the play?" Mr. Pendennis asked, determined to remain good-humored.

"'Pon my life, I don't know. Very likely it is. I ain't much of a literary man," answered Sir Derby.

"Is it possible?" the Major continued, feigning surprise. "You don't inherit your father's love of letters, then, Sir Derby? He was a remarkably fine scholar, and I had the honor of knowing him very well."

"Indeed," said the other, giving a sulky wag of his head.

"He saved my life," continued Pendennis.

"Did he now?" cried Miss Fotheringay, rolling her eyes first upon the Major with surprise, then toward Sir Derby with gratitude—but the latter was proof against those glances. Far from appearing pleased that the Apothecary, his father, had saved Major Pendennis's life, the young man actually looked as if he wished the event had turned the other way.

"My father, I believe, was a very good doctor," the young gentleman said by way of reply. "I'm not in that line myself. I wish you good morning, sir. I've got an appointment—Cos, bye-bye—Miss Fotheringay, good morning." And, despite the young lady's imploring looks and appealing smiles, the Dragoon bowed stiffly out of the room. The clatter of his sabre echoed as he strode down the creaking stairs, followed by angry tones as he cursed little Tom Creed, who was playing in the passage, and whose peg-top Sir Derby kicked away with an oath into the street.

The Major did not smile, though he had every reason to be amused. "Monstrous handsome young man that—as fine a looking soldier as ever I saw," he said to Costigan.

"A credit to the army and to human nature in general," answered Costigan. "A young man of refined manners, polite affability, and princely fortune. His table is sumptuous: he's adored in the regiment: and he rides sixteen stone."

"A perfect champion," said the Major, laughing. "I have no doubt all the ladies admire him."

"He's very well, in spite of his weight, now he's young," said Milly; "but he's no conversation."

"He's best on horseback," Mr. Bows said; on which Milly replied that the Baronet had ridden third in the steeple-chase on his horse Tareaways, leading the Major to comprehend that the young lady herself lacked particular genius, and to wonder how she could be so dull yet act so well.

Costigan, with typical Irish hospitality, pressed refreshment upon his guest. The Major, who was no more hungry than one would be after a Lord Mayor's dinner, declared that he should like a biscuit and a glass of wine above all things, claiming to feel faint from long fasting—but he knew that receiving small kindnesses flattered the donors immensely, and that people must grow well-disposed toward you as they offer you hospitality.

"Some of the old Madeira, Milly, love," Costigan said, winking at his child. That lady, turning to her father with a glance of intelligence, went out of the room and down the stairs, where she softly summoned her little emissary, Master Tommy Creed. Giving him a piece of money, she ordered him to buy a pint of Madeira wine at the Grapes and sixpence worth of sorted biscuits at the baker's, instructing him to return in a hurry, noting he might have two biscuits for himself.

While Tommy Creed was gone on this errand, Miss Costigan sat below with Mrs. Creed, telling her landlady how Mr. Arthur Pendennis's uncle, the Major, was upstairs—a nice, soft-spoken old gentleman whose butter wouldn't melt in his mouth—and how Sir Derby had stormed out of the room in a rage of jealousy, pondering what must be done to pacify both of them.

"She keeps the keys of the cellar, Major," said Mr. Costigan as the girl left the room.

"Upon my word you have a very beautiful butler," answered Pendennis, gallantly. "And I don't wonder at the young fellows raving about her. When we were of their age, Captain Costigan, I think plainer women would have done our business."

"Faith, and ye may say that, sir—and lucky is the man who gets her. Ask me friend Bob Bows here whether Miss Fotheringay's mind is not even superior to her person, and whether she does not possess a cultivated intellect, a refined understanding, and an amiable disposition?"

"Oh of course," said Mr. Bows, rather drily. "Here comes Hebe blushing from the cellar. Don't you think it is time to go to rehearsal, Miss Hebe? You will be fined if you are later"—and he gave the young lady a look intimating that they had much better leave the room and the two elders together.

At this order, Miss Hebe took up her bonnet and shawl, looking uncommonly pretty, good-humored, and smiling. Bows gathered his roll of papers, hobbled across the room for his hat and cane.

"Must you go?" said the Major. "Can't you give us a few minutes more, Miss Fotheringay? Before you leave us, permit an old fellow to shake you by the hand, and believe that I am proud to have had the honor of making your acquaintance, and am most sincerely anxious to be your friend."

Miss Fotheringay made a low curtsey at the conclusion of this gallant speech, and the Major followed her retreating steps to the door, where he squeezed her hand with the kindest and most paternal pressure. Bows was puzzled by this exhibition of cordiality: "The lad's relatives can't be really wanting to marry him to her," he thought—and so they departed.

"Now for it," thought Major Pendennis. As for Mr. Costigan, he profited instantly by his daughter's absence to drink up the rest of the wine, tossing off bumper after bumper of the Madeira from the Grapes with an eager shaking hand. The Major came up to the table, took up his glass, and drained it with a jovial smack. If it had been Lord Steyne's particular vintage rather than public-house Cape, he could not have appeared to relish it more.

"Capital Madeira, Captain Costigan," he said. "Where do you get it? I drink the health of that charming creature in a bumper. Faith, Captain, I don't wonder that the men are wild about her. I never saw such eyes in my life, or such a grand manner. I am sure she is as intellectual as she is beautiful; and I have no doubt she's as good as she is clever."

"A good girl, sir,—a good girl, sir," said the delighted father. "And I pledge a toast to her with all my heart. Shall I send to the cellar for another pint? It's handy by. No? Well, indeed sir, ye may say she is a good girl, and the pride and glory of her father—honest old Jack Costigan. The man who gets her will have a jewel to a wife, sir; and I drink his health, sir, and ye know who I mean, Major."

"I am not surprised at young or old falling in love with her," said the Major. "And frankly must tell you, that though I was very angry with my poor nephew Arthur when I heard of the boy's passion—now I have seen the lady I can pardon him any extent of it. By George, I should like to enter for the race myself, if I weren't an old fellow and a poor one."

"And no better man, Major, I'm sure," cried Jack, enraptured.

"Your friendship, sir, delights me. Your admiration for my girl brings tears to

The history of Mr. Garbetts, the Principal Tragedian, is a tale of promise, athleticism, and irregular habits. He was a jovial giant, a man of loud voice and fierce aspect, possessing the finest legs in the company and the strength to snap a poker across his arm merely for sport. He shared a considerable intimacy with Mr. Costigan, the two serving as the chief ornaments of the convivial club at the Magpie Hotel. They were friends of sorts, though their alliance was built on mutual financial necessity rather than deep affection; they frequently lent each other their valuable signatures to cover various bills. While Mr. Garbetts rarely visited Costigan's home—largely because Miss Fotheringay disliked him, a fact that fueled Mrs. Garbetts' jealousy—it was worth noting that Garbetts had once courted Miss Fotheringay and been rejected before ever offering his hand to Mrs. G. That romantic subplot, however, was irrelevant to the current crisis.

When Captain Costigan's daughter and Mr. Bows departed the house, the Captain summoned Mr. Garbetts immediately. He needed a friend capable of handling the delicate, albeit explosive, situation at hand.

"Run, Tommy," the Captain instructed his little messenger. "Fetch Mr. Garbetts from his lodgings over the tripe shop. Tell him to send two glasses of hot whisky-and-water from the Grapes."

Tommy vanished, and shortly thereafter, the formidable Mr. Garbetts arrived, accompanied by the requested spirits. The Captain, his mind sharpened by the alcohol, did not reveal the full extent of the previous events to his emissary. Instead, he dictated a letter of a threatening nature addressed to Major Pendennis. In this missive, he demanded that the Major offer no hindrance to the projected marriage between his daughter and Mr. Arthur Pendennis, setting an early date for the nuptials. He warned that failure to comply would result in a satisfaction customary among gentlemen of honor—a duel. Should the Major be disinclined to this, the Captain hinted grimly that he would force compliance using a horsewhip. The letter was sealed elaborately with the great silver seal of the Costigans, the only piece of family plate the Captain possessed.

Garbetts was dispatched with the letter and a handshake from the General, who squeezed his ambassador's hand while wishing Heaven blessings upon him. Once the messenger was gone, the Captain retrieved his venerable and murderous duelling pistols, flint-locked weapons that had claimed the lives of many a pretty fellow in Dublin. After inspecting them to ensure they were in satisfactory condition, he reached into a drawer to retrieve Pen's letters and poems. These were the treasures he always read before permitting his Emily to enjoy them.

Within twenty minutes, Garbetts returned, his countenance anxious and crestfallen.

"Have you seen him?" the Captain asked.

"Yes," Garbetts replied.

"And when is it for?" Costigan inquired, testing the lock of an ancient pistol and bringing it to his eye, which he referred to as his "oi."

"When is what for?" Garbetts asked, confused.

"The meeting, my dear fellow?"

"You don't mean to say you mean mortal combat, Captain," Garbetts exclaimed, aghast.

"What the devil else do I mean, Garbetts? I want to shoot that man who has trampled on my honor, or throw myself a victim on the sod!"

"I'll have nothing to do with carrying challenges," Garbetts replied doggedly. "I'm a family man, Captain, and I will have nothing to do with pistols. Take back your letter." To the surprise and indignation of Captain Costigan, the tragedian flung the letter down, its sprawling superscription and blotched seal landing with a thud.

"You don't mean to say you saw him and didn't give him the letter?" the Captain roared in a fury.

"I saw him, but I could not speak with him, Captain," Garbetts explained.

"And why the devil not?"

"There was one there I cared not to meet, nor would you," the tragedian answered in a sepulchral voice. "The minion Tatham was there, Captain."

"The cowardly scoundrel!" Costigan screamed. "He's frightened and already going to swear the peace against me."

"I'll have nothing to do with the fighting, mark that," Garbetts insisted. "And I wish I hadn't seen Tatham, nor that bit of—"

"Hold your tongue, Bob Acres. It's my belief you're no better than a coward," Costigan retorted, quoting Sir Lucius O'Trigger, a character he had performed with credit both on and off the stage. After some further parley, the two separated in poor humor.

The reader will note that the Captain's letter to Major Pendennis was never opened by that gentleman. Meanwhile, when Miss Costigan returned from rehearsal accompanied by the faithful Mr. Bows, she found her father pacing their apartment in a state of great agitation, surrounded by the powerful odor of spirits-and-water. The Pendennis papers lay on the table amidst empty goblets and a useless teaspoon. As Emily entered, he seized her in his arms, crying out, "Prepare yourself, me child, me blessed child," in a voice of agony, his eyes brimful of tears.

"You're tipsy again, Papa," Miss Fotheringay said, pushing him back. "You promised me you wouldn't take spirits before dinner."

"It's to forget my sorrows, me poor girl, that I've taken just a drop," cried the bereaved father. "It's to drown my care that I drain the bowl."

"Your care takes a deal of drowning, Captain dear," Mr. Bows mimicked, adopting his friend's accent. "What has happened? Has that soft-spoken gentleman in the wig been vexing you?"

"The oily miscreant! I'll have his blood!" roared Cos. Miss Milly had fled to her room to remove her bonnet and shawl, escaping the embrace.

"I thought he meant mischief. He was so uncommon civil," she noted.

"Oh, Bows! He has overwhelmed me," the Captain declared. "There's a hellish conspiracy on foot against my poor girl. Both those Pendennises, nephew and uncle, are infernal traitors and scoundrels who should be consumed from off the face of the earth."

"What is it? What has happened?" Mr. Bows asked, growing excited.

Costigan recounted the Major's statement that young Pendennis had neither two thousand nor two hundred pounds a year. He expressed his fury that he had allowed such an impostor to coax and wheedle his innocent girl, nourishing a viper in his own bosom. "I have shaken the reptile from me, however," Costigan said. "As for his uncle, I'll have such a revenge on that old man as shall make him rue the day he ever insulted a Costigan."

"What do you mean, General?" Bows asked.

"I mean to have his life, Bows—his villanous, skulking life, my boy," the Captain rapped ominously on the battered old pistol-case. Bows had often heard him appeal to that box of death, but the Captain did not reveal that he had actually written and sent a challenge to Major Pendennis. Consequently, Bows rather disregarded the pistols in the present instance.

Just then, Miss Fotheringay returned to the common sitting-room, looking perfectly healthy, happy, and unconcerned—a striking contrast to her father's delirious tremor of grief and anger. She brought in a pair of ex-white satin shoes, which she intended to rub clean with breadcrumbs before wearing them on Tuesday evening for her role as Ophelia.

She glanced at the papers on the table, paused as if to ask a question, but thought better of it. Instead, she went to the cupboard, selected a piece of bread, and returned to the table to sit comfortably with the shoes. In her honest Irish brogue, she asked her father, "What have you got those letters, poems, and stuff of Master Arthur's out for, Pa? Sure you don't want to be reading over that nonsense."

"Oh, Emilee!" cried the Captain. "That boy whom I loved as the boy of my bosom is only a scoundrel and a deceiver, me poor girl." He looked tragically at Mr. Bows, who gazed back with some anxiety.

"He! Pooh! Sure the poor lad's as simple as a schoolboy," she dismissed. "All those children write verses and nonsense."

"He's been acting the part of a viper to this fireside and a traitor in this family," the Captain cried. "I tell you he's no better than an impostor."

"What has the poor fellow done, Papa?" Emily asked.

"Done? He has deceived us in the most atrocious manner," her father declared. "He has trifled with your affections and outraged my own fine feelings. He represented himself as a man of property, and it turns out he is no better than a beggar. Haven't I often told you he had two thousand a year? He's a pauper, I tell you, Miss Costigan; dependent on the bounty of his mother, a good woman who may marry again and likely live forever, but who has but five hundred a year. How dare he ask you to marry into a family without the means to provide for you? You've been grossly deceived, Milly, and I believe his old ruffian of an uncle in a wig is in the plot against us."

"That soft old gentleman? What has he been doing, Papa?" Emily continued, still imperturbable.

Costigan informed Milly that when she was gone, Major Pendennis had told him in his double-faced Pall Mall polite manner that young Arthur had no fortune at all. The Major had asked Costigan to visit the lawyers—wherein he knew the scoundrels had a bill of his, and he couldn't meet them—to see the lad's father's will. Finally, he claimed an infernal swindle had been practiced upon him by the pair, and he was resolved either on a marriage or on the blood of both of them.

Milly looked grave and thoughtful, rubbing the white satin shoes. "Sure, if he's no money, there's no use marrying him, Papa," she said sententiously.

"Why did the villain say he was a man of property?" Costigan asked.

"The poor fellow always said he was poor," the girl answered. "'Twas you who wanted him to be rich, Papa—and made me agree to take him."

"He should have been explicit and told us his income, Milly," the father replied. "A young fellow who rides a blood mare and makes presents of shawls and bracelets is an impostor if he has no money. As for his uncle, bedad, I'll pull off his wig whenever I see him. Bows, here, shall take a message to him and tell him so. Either it's a marriage, or he meets me in the field like a man, or I tweak him on the nose in front of his hotel or in the gravel walks of Fairoaks Park before all the county, bedad."

"Bedad, you may send somebody else with the message," Bows laughed. "I'm a fiddler, not a fighting man, Captain."

"Pooh, you've no spirit, sir," roared the General. "I'll be my own second if no one will stand by and see me injured. And I'll take my case of pistols and shoot them in the Coffee-room of the George."

"And so poor Arthur has no money?" Miss Costigan sighed plaintively. "Poor lad, he was a good lad too: wild and talking nonsense with his verses and poems and that, but a brave, generous boy, and indeed I liked him—and he liked me too," she added softly, rubbing away at the shoe.

"Why don't you marry him if you like him so?" Mr. Bows said savagely. "He is not more than ten years younger than you are. His mother may relent, and you might go and live and have enough at Fairoaks Park. Why not go and be a lady? I could go on with the fiddle, and the General live on his half-pay. Why don't you marry him? You know he likes you."

"There's others that like me as well, Bows, that have no money and that's old enough," Miss Milly said sententiously.

"Yes, damn it," Bows cursed bitterly. "That are old enough and poor enough and fools enough for anything."

"There's old fools, and young fools too. You've often said so, you silly man," the imperious beauty replied, casting a conscious glance at the old gentleman. "If Pendennis has not enough money to live upon, it's folly to talk about marrying him: and that's the long and short of it."

"And the boy?" Bows asked. "By Jove! you throw a man away like an old glove, Miss Costigan."

"I don't know what you mean, Bows," Miss Fotheringay said placidly, rubbing the second shoe. "If he had had half of the two thousand a year that Papa gave him, or even half of that, I would marry him. But what is the good of taking on with a beggar? We're poor enough already. There's no use in my going to live with an old lady that's testy and cross, maybe, and would grudge me every morsel of meat." She noted that it was nearly dinner time and Suky had not yet laid the cloth. "And then," she added simply, "suppose there was a family?—why, Papa, we shouldn't be as well off as we are now."

"Deed, then, you would not, Milly dear," answered the father.

"And there's an end to all the fine talk about Mrs. Arthur Pendennis of Fairoaks Park—the Member of Parliament's lady," Milly laughed. "Pretty carriages and horses we should have to ride!—that you were always talking about, Papa! But it's always the same. If a man looked at me, you fancied he was going to marry me; and if he

At Fairoaks, the silence surrounding the Major's absence was becoming a subject of genuine wonder. Dr. Portman and his wife, returning to Clavering, paused at Helen's lodge-gate to deliver a brief note from Major Pendennis. The Major explained he intended to stay at Chatteris another day, eager to consult with Messrs. Tatham regarding some legal business scheduled for the afternoon. Notably, the note omitted any mention of the morning's dramatic transaction. Indeed, it was penned during a lull in the Major's defeat, a document written when he had decisively lost the battle.

Pen, meanwhile, found himself in a peculiar predicament. He had no desire to venture into the town while his uncle was present. The thought of his guardian potentially spying on him from the Dean's grass-plat while he attempted to woo Miss Costigan was unbearable. The sheer joy of a solitary walk—a rare indulgence for him—would be ruined by the sight of the man with the polished boots. Pen's affection was a secret, confined to his eyes, which he used to gaze violently at his object, yet remained utterly silent in the presence of others. It was a prudent strategy; the language of love-making, to the uninitiated, is often the vocabulary without the key, a lamp without the flame. Consider the old love-letters one might find: blank, meaningless, and devoid of the glamour that once made them beautiful. Yet, there were times when one kissed those silly lines with rapture, living on six absurd words for a week, only to suffer a reactionary period of misery until fresh folly arrived.

For this reason, we respectfully decline to publish the letters and verses Mr. Pen composed during this tumultuous phase of his life. They are too spooney and wild for the young ladies of today to read in cold blood. Bide your time, fair readers; perhaps you will compose similar missives soon. For now, let us keep Mr. Pen's first outpourings sealed behind Miss Fotheringay's string and Captain Costigan's great silver seal.

The Major emerged from his interview with Captain Costigan in a state of concentrated fury that made him terrifying to approach. "The impudent bog-trotting scamp," he fumed internally, "dare to threaten me! Dare to suggest his damned Costigans marry the Pendennises! Send me a challenge! If the fellow can find a gentleman to carry it, I have the greatest mind in life not to baulk him.—Psha! What would the world say if I went out with a tipsy mountebank over a row with an actress in a barn!"
