Consequently, when Dr. Portman approached anxiously to inquire about the outcome of his battle with the dragon, the Major chose not to reveal the General's insolence. Instead, he labeled the affair ugly and disagreeable, insisting it was far from over.

He strictly enjoined Doctor and Mrs. Portman to say nothing about the business at Fairoaks, sending only the aforementioned note. Then, he retreated to his hotel, where he vented his wrath upon Mr. Morgan, his valet, cursing upstairs and downstairs, a spectacle Mr. Morgan later recounted to Mr. Foker's man in the servants' room of the George.

The servant relayed the news to his master. Mr. Foker, having finished his breakfast around two in the afternoon, remembered his eagerness to learn the result of the Major's interview. Dressed in his brocade dressing-gown, he knocked at the Major's sitting-room.

Inside, the Major was engaged in business regarding a lease for the widow's estate, consulting with old Mr. Tatham, the lawyer who had managed his brother's affairs. Seeing the Major deep in red tape with an elderly gentleman, the modest youth initially hesitated to intrude. However, the Major beckoned him in with a smile. Mr. Foker removed his embroidered tarboosh, bowed graciously, and seated himself, spreading out his crimson skirts. "You seem to like my dressing-gown, sir," he remarked to Mr. Tatham. "A pretty thing, isn't it? Neat, but not gaudy."

There was something in Foker's manner that would have put an Inquisitor in a good mood, smoothing the wrinkles under Pendennis's head of hair.

"I have had an interview with that Irishman," the Major began, addressing Mr. Tatham openly since Foker was present. "It was not satisfactory. He refuses to believe my nephew is poor; he calls us both liars. He even hinted I was a coward as I took my leave. I thought you might be the gentleman bringing a challenge from Mr. Costigan—that is how the world treats me, Mr. Foker."

"You don't mean the Irishman, the actress's father?" cried Mr. Tatham, a dissenter who held no patronage for the drama.

"That Irishman, the actress's father—the very man. Have you heard what a fool my nephew has made of himself about the girl?" Mr. Tatham, having never entered a theater, knew nothing of the affair, so the Major recounted the story of his nephew's loves, with Mr. Foker adding appropriate comments in his familiar tongue.

Mr. Tatham was lost in wonder at the narrative, lamenting that Mrs. Pendennis had not married a serious man and kept this unfortunate boy from perdition. Regarding Miss Costigan, he offered no defense; her profession was sufficient characterization. Mr. Foker interjected that he had known uncommon good people in the booths, the Temple of the Muses. While Mr. Tatham hoped so, he knew the father personally: a wine-bibber, an idler in taverns, and a notorious insolvent. "I understand why the fellow wouldn't come to my office," Tatham explained. "We have a writ out against him and another disreputable actor for a bill given to Mr. Skinner, a respectable grocer and Friend. This Costigan came crying to Mr. Skinner in the shop, and we haven't proceeded against them as neither were worth powder and shot."

Just as Mr. Tatham was finishing this tale, a third knock interrupted them. An athletic gentleman in a shabby braided frock entered, bearing a letter with a large, blotched red seal.

"Can I have the honor of speaking with Major Pendennis in private?" he began, claiming to be a bearer of a mission from Captain Costigan. But the man with the bass voice faltered, turned pale, and caught sight of the red-faced Mr. Tatham.

"Hullo, Garbetts, speak up!" cried Mr. Foker, delighted.

"Why, bless my soul, it is the other party to the bill!" exclaimed Mr. Tatham. "Stop, sir!" But Garbetts, his face as blank as Macbeth's upon seeing Banquo's ghost, gasped inarticulate words and fled.

The Major's gravity dissolved instantly into laughter, followed by Mr. Foker and the attorney, despite his profession's seriousness.

"I don't think there'll be any fight, Major," young Foker said, mimicking the tragedian. "If there is, the old gentleman—Mr. Tatham—may send the bailiffs to separate the men." Mr. Tatham promised to do so. The Major was not sorry for the ludicrous conclusion to the quarrel. "It seems to me, sir," he told Foker, "that you always arrive to put me into good humor."

This was not the only occasion Mr. Foker was destined to serve the Pendennis family that day. Knowing Captain Costigan's haunts, he decided to visit the General to hear the morning's conversation firsthand. Costigan was absent, having been encouraged by his daughter to visit the convivial club at the Magpie Hotel, where he was likely bragging of his desire to murder a certain ruffian.

Captain Costigan was gone, but Miss Fotheringay was at home, washing tea-cups while Mr. Bows sat opposite.

"Just done breakfast, I see—how do?" said Mr. Foker, popping his funny little head in.

"Get out, you funny little man," cried Miss Fotheringay.

"You mean come in," answered the other. "Here we are!" Entering the room, he folded his arms and began twirling his head with immense rapidity, like Harlequin emerging from his cocoon. Miss Fotheringay laughed with all her heart; a wink from Foker would set her off, whereas the bitterest jokes from Bows or the finest speeches from poor Pen would only puzzle her. At the end of his harlequinade, he sank to one knee and kissed her hand. "You're the drollest little man," she said, giving him a great good-natured slap. Pen used to tremble as he kissed her hand; Pen would have died of a slap.

Once these preliminaries were over, the three began to talk. Mr. Foker recounted the scene of Garbetts' discomfiture, revealing how far the General had carried his wrath. He spoke strongly in favor of the Major's veracity and honor, describing him as a tip-top swell who would never submit to deceit, especially not to deceive a charming young woman like Miss Foth.

He touched delicately on the marriage question, though he couldn't help showing he held Pen rather cheap. In fact, he possessed a perhaps just contempt for Mr. Pen's high-flown sentimentality, his own weakness lying elsewhere. "I knew it wouldn't do, Miss Foth," he nodded. "Couldn't do. Didn't like to put my hand into the bag, but knew it couldn't do. He's too young for you: too green: a deal too green: and he turns out to be poor as Job. Can't have him at no price, can she, Mr. Bo?"

"Indeed he's a nice poor boy," said the Fotheringay rather sadly.

"Poor little beggar," said Bows, stealing a queer look at Miss Fotheringay. Perhaps he wondered at the way women played with men, coaxed them, won them, and dropped them.

Mr. Bows had no objection to acknowledging that Miss Fotheringay was perfectly right in giving up Mr. Arthur Pendennis; in his view, the match was always absurd. Miss Costigan admitted she thought so herself, only she couldn't send away two thousand a year. "It all comes of believing Papa's silly stories," she said. "Faith, I'll choose for myself another time"—and very likely, the large image of Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks entered her mind at that instant.

After praising Major Pendennis, whom Miss Costigan declared to be a proper gentleman smelling of lavender and as neat as a pin, Mr. Foker suddenly invited the pair to meet the Major that evening for dinner at his apartment at the George. "He agreed to dine with me, and I think, after the little shindy this morning, in which I must say the General was wrong, it would look kind, you know. I know the Major fell in love with you, Miss Foth: he said so."

"So she may be Mrs. Pendennis still," Bows said with a sneer. "No, thank you, Mr. F.—I've dined."

"Sure, that was at three o'clock," said Miss Costigan, who had an honest appetite, "and I can't go without you."

"We'll have lobster-salad and champagne," said the little monster, who couldn't construe a line of Latin or do a sum beyond the Rule of Three. Now, for lobster-salad and champagne in an honorable manner, Miss Costigan would have gone anywhere. Thus, Major Pendennis found himself at seven o'clock seated at a dinner table with Mr. Bows, a professional fiddler, and Miss Costigan, whose father had wanted to blow his brains out a few hours prior.

To complete the happy meeting, Mr. Foker, knowing Costigan's haunts, dispatched Stoopid to the Magpie club where the General was singing a pathetic song, bringing him off to supper. Finding his daughter and Bows at the board was a surprise indeed. Major Pendennis laughed and cordially held out his hand, which the General Officer grasped *avec effusion*. In fact, he was considerably inebriated and had already been crying over his own song before joining the little party. He burst into tears more than once during the entertainment, calling the Major his dearest friend. Stoopid and Mr. Foker walked him home; the Major gallantly gave his arm to Miss Costigan.

He was received with great friendliness the next day, and many civilities passed between the gentlemen. On taking leave, he expressed his anxious desire to serve Miss Costigan on any occasion and shook hands with Mr. Foker most cordially, saying that gentleman had done him the very greatest service.

"All right," said Mr. Foker. They parted with mutual esteem.

On his return to Fairoaks the next day, the Major did not disclose what had happened the previous night or allude to the company he had kept. However, he engaged Mr. Smirke to stop for dinner. Any observer might have noted a constraint in his hilarity and talkativeness, and an unusual graciousness in his communications with his nephew. He gave Pen an emphatic "God-bless-you" when the lad went to bed. As they prepared to part for the night, he seemed about to say something to Mrs. Pendennis but bethought himself that speaking might spoil her rest, allowing her to sleep in peace.

The next morning, he was down in the breakfast-room earlier than usual, saluting everyone with great cordiality. When John, the old servant, discharged the mail, the Major looked hard at Pen as the lad retrieved his letters. Arthur blushed and put his letter down. He knew the hand; it was that of old Costigan, and he did not care to read it in public. The Major knew the letter too; he had put it in the post himself in Chatteris the day before.

He sent little Laura away, the child having a thorough dislike for him. As the door closed, he took Mrs. Pendennis's hand, gave her a look full of meaning, and pointed to the letter under the newspaper Pen was pretending to read. "Will you come into the drawing-room?" he said. "I want to speak to you." She followed

The theater in Chatteris was so empty it felt less like a performance space and more like a mausoleum for forgotten dreams. Mr. Manager Bingley, straining to project his famous role of 'Rolla' in *Pizarro*, found himself shouting into a void. The audience was a skeleton crew; Arthur Pendennis sat alone in the royal box, his eyes red-rimmed and his spirit hollow, staring blankly at the stage. To him, the Spanish armies, the Peruvian processions, and the virgins of the sun were mere background noise. He saw only one thing: Cora Fotheringay. He later confessed that in his feverish state of love and despair, he considered grabbing a pistol and ending it all right there in front of the Chatteris gaol, had it not been for his mother's silent, steady presence at home.

On stage, Miss Fotheringay was a vision in white raiment and leopard skin, her golden eyes gleaming with a predatory lust that had once nearly destroyed Pendennis's soul. Yet, tonight, those eyes weren't fixed on him. They were locked onto two gentlemen in the box beside him. Pendennis, blinded by his own misery, missed the subtle shift in the atmosphere. The play itself was dying; the actors were yawning through their lines, and even the great Bingley was listless. Then, suddenly, the mood snapped. Mrs. Bingley began to bellow like a bull, Bingley darted about like a possessed man, and the entire cast scrambled to impress the newcomers.

The intruders were Mr. Dolphin, the mighty London impresario, and his secretary, Mr. William Minns. Dolphin was a towering figure of wealth and power, dressed in velvet and gold, smelling faintly of expensive musk and bergamot. The moment he entered, the Chatteris troupe transformed. Every actor, including the usually dull-hearted Miss Fotheringay, poured their energy into winning his favor. She stood in picturesque poses, looking stunning, though she knew her part required little more than beauty. It wasn't until the final scene, when Rolla stumbled in with a monstrous child and cried out about blood, that Dolphin finally clapped his hands and shouted, "Bravo!"

"By Jove, Billy, she'll do!" Dolphin declared, slapping his secretary on the back.

Old Billy Minns, a sardonic soul, chuckled. "Who taught her that dodge?" he asked, recalling how the girl couldn't say "Bo" to a goose at the Olympic. It was little Mr. Bows in the orchestra who had coached her. As the curtain fell, the entire company swarmed around Miss Fotheringay, congratulating her with a mixture of genuine joy and professional envy.

Why had the great Dolphin descended to this tiny provincial theater? Because his empire was crumbling. Despite his flashy advertisements and past triumphs, the Museum Theatre was bleeding money. Legitimate drama, animal shows, and grand operas had all failed to draw crowds. He was dragging out his season on half-salaries, waiting for the day his name would appear in the bankruptcy gazette.

His salvation, however, lay in the patronage of the Marquis of Steyne. The Marquis, a man of high station and questionable taste, attended the theater mostly for the ballet, hiding behind a curtain while his aides-de-camp, Wenham and Wagg, managed the proceedings. One evening, amidst raucous laughter and scandalized whispers from the pit, Wenham read a letter from Major Pendennis.

"The secret is out," Wenham announced. "There's a woman in the case."

"Damn it, Wenham, he's your age," the Marquis grumbled from behind the velvet drape.

"For my part, I hope to be a victim till I die," Wenham replied gallantly, though the Marquis knew he was merely boasting about his own vitality.

"Tell us about old Pendennis," Wagg urged, eager to hear the gossip.

Wenham read the letter aloud. Pendennis, writing with the desperation of a man in love, begged the Marquis to intervene. He described Miss Fotheringay as a splendid actress, superior to anyone on the London scene, and pleaded for a London engagement for her. He promised anything in return, asking the Marquis to use his influence to get Dolphin to sign her.

"It's a clear case," Wenham concluded. "Old Pendennis is in love."

"And wants to get the woman up to London," Wagg added.

"I'd like to see Pendennis on his knees with the rheumatism," Wenham mused.

"Or accommodating the beloved object with a lock of his hair," Wagg joked.

The Marquis dismissed their cynicism with a wave of his hand. "He has relations in the country, hasn't he? A nephew who could return a member? It's the nephew's affair. The young one is in a scrape. I was mad about a market-gardener's daughter myself at Eton... But I say, she must be a fine woman from Pendennis's account. Have in Dolphin, and let us hear if he knows anything of her."

Wenham sprang from the box, navigated the backstage chaos, and found Dolphin, who was currently cursing a ballerina for missing a step. The manager's curses died instantly upon seeing the Marquis's emissary. He greeted Wenham with a smile, pretending he had never been angry in his life, and eagerly followed him to pay his respects to the great patron.

The visit to Chatteris yielded immediate results. Dolphin wrote to the Marquis, confirming he had seen the lady and was struck by both her talents and her appearance. He had already made an engagement with Miss Fotheringay, promising she would soon appear before a London audience and the Marquis himself.

Back in Chatteris, the news spread like wildfire. The local newspaper printed a glowing review of her upcoming departure, praising her beauty and prophesying her success in the metropolis. Bingley immediately began advertising "The Last Night of Miss Fotheringay's Engagement." Only Pendennis and Sir Derby Oaks remained constant attendees; Sir Derby threw bouquets from the stage box, while Pendnis sat in the shadows, haggard and lonely.

One night, Mr. Bows, the little man from the orchestra, left his seat and approached Pendennis's box. He invited the brokenhearted major to walk. They strolled down the street and sat on Chatteris Bridge under the moonlight, talking about the woman who had captured them both.

"We may sit on the same bridge," Bows said, his voice gravelly. "We've been in the same boat for a long time. You aren't the only fool who's made a mess of himself over that woman. I have less excuse than you; I'm older and know her better. She has no more heart than the stone you're leaning against. If either of us fell into the water, she wouldn't care. Yes, she would care for me, because she needs me to teach her. She can't get on without me, so she'll be forced to send for me from London. But she wouldn't if she didn't want me. She has no heart, no head, no sense, no feelings, no griefs or cares. I was going to say no pleasures, but the truth is, she likes her dinner, and she's pleased when people admire her."

"And you do?" Pendennis asked, fascinated by the crabbed honesty of the old man.

"It's a habit, like taking snuff or drinking drams," Bows replied. "I've been taking her for five years and can't do without her. If she doesn't send for me, I'll follow her. But I know she will. She wants me. Some day she'll marry and fling me over, just as I'll drop the end of this cigar."

He flicked the burning tip into the dark water below, watching it vanish. As Pendennis rode home that night, for the first time in months, his mind wandered to someone other than himself.

Major Pendennis had drawn a line in the sand regarding Fairoaks. Until the enemy withdrew completely from the vicinity, the Major was resolved to keep his garrison there. He didn't bother watching Pen's every move or imposing strict rules on his nephew's conduct; instead, he employed a subtle, invisible net. Young Arthur's comings and goings were meticulously tracked by the Major's agents, ensuring the lad remained perpetually under surveillance, even if he wasn't directly in the Major's sight.

Let us pause here to address a universal truth of the human condition: almost every man who reads a novel—or indeed, lives in the world—has been thwarted in love by fate, circumstance, deceitful women, or his own foolishness. Let the worthy reader recall his own heartbreak and apply it to Mr. Pen's current agony. Ah, the weary nights! The sickening fevers! The mad desires crashing against rocks of indifference and bouncing back from unimpressionable granite! Imagine a list compiled tonight in London of the groans, thoughts, and curses of tossing lovers. What a catalogue! I wonder what percentage of the male population of the metropolis will be lying awake at two or three o'clock tomorrow, counting the hours, rolling restlessly from left to right, yearning and heart-sick. What a pang it is! I have never known a man die of love certainly, but I have seen a twelve-stone man shrink to nine-stone five under a disappointed passion; nearly a quarter of him has perished. He will likely bounce back to his old size, perhaps bigger than ever, as a new affection closes round his heart and makes him comfortable. Young Pen will console himself like the rest of us. We say this so the ladies won't prematurely deplore him or worry unduly about his complaint. His mother worried, of course, but what will not a maternal fondness fear or invent? "Depend on it, my dear creature," Major Pendennis would say gallantly to her, "the boy will recover. As soon as we get her out of the country, we'll take him somewhere and show him a little life. Meantime, make yourself easy. Half a fellow's pangs at losing a woman result from vanity more than affection. To be left by a woman is the deuce and all, to be sure; but look how easily we leave 'em."

Mrs. Pendennis, however, knew nothing of this specific knowledge. It was beyond the simple lady's scope. Indeed, she disliked the subject; her own heart had suffered a private misadventure, which she had borne and cured, leaving her with little patience for other folk's passions—except, naturally, Arthur's. She felt his sufferings as her own, experiencing many of his illnesses and pains far more acutely than Pen himself endured. She watched him through this present grief with a jealous, silent sympathy, though he never spoke to her of his unfortunate condition.

One must grant the Major considerable merit and forbearance, exhibiting a highly creditable degree of family affection. Life at Fairoaks was uncommonly dull for a man who had the entrée to half the houses in London and was accustomed to making his bows in three or four drawing-rooms nightly. A dinner with Doctor Portman or a neighboring Squire now and then; a dreary rubber at backgammon with the Widow, who did her utmost to amuse him; these were his chief pleasures. He longed for the arrival of the bag with the letters and devoured every word of the evening paper. He doctored himself assiduously, believing a course of quiet living suited him well after London banquets. He dressed laboriously every morning and afternoon, taking regular exercise up and down the terrace walk. Thus, armed with his cane, his toilet, his medicine-chest, his backgammon-box, and his newspaper, this worthy worldly philosopher fenced himself against ennui. If he didn't improve each shining hour like bees by the widow's garden wall, Major Pendennis made one hour after another pass as he could, rendering his captivity just tolerable. After this period, it was remarked that he was fond of steering the conversation toward the American War, the massacre of Wyoming, and the brilliant actions of Saint Lucie, the fact being that he possessed two volumes of the *Annual Register* in his bedroom which he studiously consulted. It is thus a well-regulated man accommodates himself to circumstances and shows himself calmly superior to fortune.

Pen sometimes took the box at backgammon at night or listened to his mother's simple music on summer evenings, but he remained restless and wretched in spite of everything. He was known to be up before early daylight, descending to a carp-pond in Clavering Park—a dreary pool with innumerable whispering rushes and green alders, where a milkmaid had drowned herself in the Baronet's grandfather's time, and her ghost was said to walk still. But Pen did not drown himself, as perhaps his mother fancied might be his intention. He liked to go and fish there, thinking and thinking at leisure as the float quivered in the little eddies of the pond and the fish flapped about him. If he got a bite, he was excited enough; in this way, he occasionally brought home carps, tenches, and eels, which the Major cooked in the Continental fashion.

By this pond, under a tree which was his favorite resort, Pen composed a number of poems suitable to his circumstances, verses over which he blushed in after days, wondering how he could ever have invented such rubbish. As for the tree, it is in a hollow of this very tree, where he used to put his tin-box of ground-bait and other fishing commodities, that he afterwards... well, we are advancing matters. Suffice it to say, he wrote poems and relieved himself very much. When a man's grief or passion is at this point, it may be loud, but it is not very severe. When a gentleman is cudgelling his brain to find any rhyme for sorrow besides "borrow" and "tomorrow," his woes are nearer at an end than he thinks for. So were Pen's. He had his hot and cold fits, his days of sullenness and peevishness, of blank resignation and despondency, and occasional mad paroxysms of rage and longing, in which fits Rebecca would be saddled and galloped fiercely about the country or into Chatteris, her rider gesticulating wildly on her back, astonishing carters and turnpikemen as he passed, crying out the name of the false one.

Mr. Foker became a very frequent and welcome visitor at Fairoaks during this period, where his good spirits and oddities always amused the Major and Pendennis, while they astonished the Widow and little Laura not a little. His tandem made a great sensation in Clavering marketplace, where he upset a market stall, cut Mrs. Pybus's poodle over the shaven quarters, and drank a glass of raspberry bitters at the Clavering Arms. All the society in the little place heard who he was and looked up his name in their Peerages. He was so young, and their books so old, that his name did not appear in many of their volumes; his mother, now quite an antiquated lady, figured amongst the progeny of the Earl of Rosherville as Lady Agnes Milton still. But his name, wealth, and honorable lineage were speedily known about Clavering, where you may be sure that poor Pen's little transaction with the Chatteris actress was also pretty freely discussed.

Looking at the little old town of Clavering St. Mary from the London road as it runs by the lodge at Fairoaks, and seeing the rapid and shining Brawl winding down from the town and skirting the woods of Clavering Park, and the ancient church tower and peaked roofs of the houses rising up amongst trees and old walls, behind which swells a fair background of sunshiny hills that stretch from Clavering westwards towards the sea—the place looks so cheery and comfortable that many a traveler's heart must have yearned towards it from the coach-top, and he must have thought that it was in such a calm friendly nook he would like to shelter at the end of life's struggle. Tom Smith, who used to drive the Alacrity coach, would often point to a tree near the river, from which a fine view of the church and town was commanded, and inform his companion on the box that "Artises come and take hoff the Church from that there tree—It was a Habby once, sir:"—and indeed a pretty view it is, which I recommend to Mr. Stanfield or Mr. Roberts for their next tour.

Like Constantinople seen from the Bosphorus; like Mrs. Rougemont viewed in her box from the opposite side of the house; like many an object which we pursue in life and admire before we have attained it; Clavering is rather prettier at a distance than it is on closer acquaintance. The town so cheerful of aspect a few furlongs off looks very blank and dreary. Except on market days there is nobody in the streets. The clack of a pair of pattens echoes through half the place, and you may hear the creaking of the rusty old ensign at the Clavering Arms without being disturbed by any other noise. There has not been a ball in the Assembly Rooms since the Clavering volunteers gave one to their Colonel, the old Sir Francis Clavering; and the stables which once held a great part of that brilliant, but defunct regiment, are now cheerless and empty, except on Thursdays, when the farmers put up there, and their tilted carts and gigs make a feeble show of liveliness in the place, or on Petty Sessions, when the magistrates attend in what used to be the old card-room.

On the south side of the market rises up the church, with its great grey towers, of which the sun illuminates the delicate carving, deepening the shadows of the huge buttresses and gilding the glittering windows and flaming vanes. The image of the Patroness of the Church was wrenched out of the porch centuries ago; such of the statues of saints as were within reach of stones and hammer at that period of pious demolition are maimed and headless, and of those who were out of fire, only Doctor Portman knows the names and history, for his curate, Smirke, is not much of an antiquarian, and Mr. Simcoe (husband of the Honourable Mrs. Simcoe), incumbent and architect of the Chapel of Ease in the lower town, thinks them the abomination of desolation.

The Rectory is a stout, broad-shouldered brick house of the reign of Anne. It communicates with the church and market by different gates and stands at the opening of Yew-tree Lane, where the Grammar School (Rev. —— Wapshot) is; Yew-tree Cottage (Miss Flather); the butchers' slaughtering-house, an old barn or brew-house of the Abbey times; and the Misses Finucane's establishment for young ladies. The two schools had their pews in the loft on each side of the organ until the Abbey Church, getting rather empty through the falling-off of the congregation who were inveigled to the Heresy-shop in the lower town, induced the Misses Finucane to bring their pretty little flock downstairs; and the young ladies' bonnets make a tolerable show in the rather vacant aisles. Nobody is in the great pew of the Clavering family except the statues of defunct baronets and their ladies: there is Sir Poyntz Clavering, Knight and Baronet, kneeling in a square beard opposite his wife in a ruff; a very fat lady, the Dame Rebecca Clavering, in alto-relievo, is borne up to Heaven by two little blue-veined angels who seem to have a severe task—and so forth. How well in after life Pen remembered those effigies, and how often in youth he scanned them as the Doctor was grumbling the sermon from the pulpit and Smirke's mild head and forehead curl peered over the great prayer-book in the desk!

The Fairoaks folks were constant at the old church; their servants had a pew, so had the Doctor's, so had Wapshot's, and those of the Misses Finucane's establishment, three maids and a very nice-looking young man in a livery. The Wapshot Family were numerous and faithful. Glanders and his children regularly came to church; so did one of the apothecaries. Mrs. Pybus went, turn and turn about, to the Low Town church and to the Abbey; the Charity School and their families of course came; Wapshot's boys made a good cheerful noise, scuffling with their feet as they marched into church and up the organ-loft stair, and blowing their noses a good deal during the service. To be brief, the congregation looked as decent as might be in these bad times. The Abbey Church was furnished with a magnificent screen and many hatchments and heraldic tombstones. The Doctor spent a great part of his income in beautifying his darling place; he had endowed it with a superb painted window bought in the Netherlands and an organ grand enough for a cathedral.

But in spite of organ and window, in consequence of the latter very likely, which had come out of a Papistical place of worship and was blazoned all over with idolatry, Clavering New Church prospered scandalously in the teeth of Orthodoxy, and many of the Doctor's congregation deserted to Mr. Simcoe and the honorable woman his wife. Their efforts had thinned the very Ebenezer hard by them, which building before Simcoe's advent used to be so full that you could see the backs of the congregation squeezing out of the arched windows thereof. Mr. Simcoe's tracts fluttered into the doors of all the Doctor's cottages and were taken as greedily as honest Mrs. Portman's soup, with the quality of which the graceless people found fault. With the folks at the Ribbon Factory situated by the weir on the Brawl side, and round which the Low Town had grown, Orthodoxy could make no way at all. Quiet Miss Myra was put out of court by impetuous Mrs. Simcoe and her female aides-de-camp. Ah, it was a hard burden for the Doctor's lady to bear, to behold her husband's congregation dwindling away; to give the precedence on the few occasions when they met to

News of Pen's latest escapade spread through Chatteris with the speed of wildfire, sparking a firestorm of reactions that ranged from genuine fury to delighted amusement. To the good Doctor Portman, Pen's behavior was a personal affront, a breach of decorum that demanded immediate correction. But to Major Pendennis, the whole affair was nothing more than a source of mirth, a charming display of youthful exuberance that confirmed his nephew's robust constitution. Meanwhile, Mrs. Pendennis stood on the precipice of maternal hysteria. Hearing tales of the squabble and her son's "unchristian" conduct, she felt as though the transaction had unleashed a torrent of wretchedness, crime, and sheer annoyance upon her household. Her mind raced with visions of disaster, and she longed with desperate intensity to see Pen whisked away from Chatteris, anywhere but near the woman who, in her frantic imagination, had somehow tangled him in this mess.

When the fond mother remonstrated and the stern Doctor rebuked him for his violence, Pen treated the situation with the grave, self-important air of a young man discovering the weight of honor. He declared himself deeply sorry, insisting the insult had come entirely unprovoked. With a pomposity that belied his age, he vowed that no man could insult him without him vindicating his honor with the utmost dignity, even appealing to his uncle for validation. He asked, with feigned solemnity, whether a gentleman could have acted differently than to offer satisfaction to the chastised party.

"Vous allez trop vite, my good sir," Uncle Pendennis replied, looking slightly puzzled. He had been trying to instill in his nephew some of his own old-world notions of honor—ideas that smelled strongly of camp life and pistol duels rather than the sober sensibilities of the modern age. "Between men of the world, perhaps, I don't say; but between two schoolboys, this sort of thing is ridiculous, my dear boy—perfectly ridiculous."

"It is extremely wicked, and unlike my son," sobbed Mrs. Pendennis, her eyes wet with tears and her mind bewildered by her boy's stubborn obstinacy.

Pen kissed her cheek and declared with theatrical flair, "Women, dear mother, don't understand these matters. I put myself into Foker's hands; I had no other course to pursue."

Major Pendennis grinned and shrugged, observing that the young ones were certainly making great progress in their education. Mrs. Pendennis, however, declared Foker to be a wicked, horrid little wretch, convinced he would lead her dear boy straight into mischief if they attended the same college. "I have a great mind not to let him go at all," she confessed. Only the memory that her husband had always destined him for that specific university prevented her from issuing a total veto.

The decision was made: Pen would go to college in the next October term. Foker had promised to introduce him to the "right set," and the Major laid immense store upon this social engineering. "Mr. Foker knows the very best young men now at the University," the Major boasted. "Pen will form acquaintances there who will be of the greatest advantage through life. The young Marquis of Plinlimmon is there, eldest son of the Duke of Saint David's—Lord Magnus Charters, Lord Runnymede's son, and a first cousin of Mr. Foker... Lady Agnes will certainly invite him to Logwood." He spoke of the intimacy with Foker's son, Lord Runnymede, with such enthusiasm that he dismissed any alarm, viewing it as one of the luckiest breaks that could have happened to Pen.

Helen sighed, assuming the Major knew best. She acknowledged that Mr. Foker had been kind in the wretched business with Miss Costigan, and she was grateful, yet she couldn't shake a dim presentiment of evil. All these quarrels, riots, and displays of worldliness scared her for her boy's future.

Doctor Portman, conversely, was decidedly of the opinion that Pen must go to college. He hoped the lad would read and enjoy a moderate indulgence of the best society, confident that Pen would distinguish himself. Smirke had spoken highly of his proficiency, and the Doctor himself had heard him construe texts, finding him remarkably well-acquitted. Getting him out of Chatteris was a great point in itself. Pen, distracted from his private grief by the various rows swirling around him, gloomily agreed to obey.

August and September brought assizes, races, and a flux of company to Chatteris. Miss Fotheringay continued to act, taking her farewells from the audiences at the Chatteris Theatre. Yet, nobody seemed particularly affected by her presence or her announced departure, save for the few we have named. The polite county folk, who likely admired the Fotheringay prodigiously in London when fashion dictated it, found nothing remarkable in the actress performing on the little Chatteris boards. Many a genius and many a quack had met a similar fate before and since Miss Costigan's time. Meanwhile, this honest woman bore up against public neglect and life's crosses with her usual equanimity, eating, drinking, acting, and sleeping with the regularity and comfort belonging to her temperament. What a deal of grief, care, and harmful excitement a healthy dullness and cheerful insensibility avoids! Nor do I mean to say that Virtue is not Virtue because it is never tempted to go astray; only that dullness is a much finer gift than we give it credit for, and some people are very lucky whom Nature has endowed with that great anodyne.

Pen used to go drearily in and out from the play during this season, proceeding much according to his fancy. His antics tortured his mother, and her anxiety would have led her to interfere often had not the Major constantly checked her, while simultaneously encouraging her. The wily man of the world fancied he saw a favorable turn in Pen's malady. It was the violent efflux of versification, among other symptoms, that gave Pen's guardian and physician satisfaction. He might be heard spouting verses in the shrubbery walks or muttering them between his teeth while sitting with the home party in the evenings. One day, prowling about the house in Pen's absence, the Major found a great book full of verses in the lad's study. They were in English and Latin, with quotations from classic authors given in the scholastic manner in the footnotes. *He can't be very bad,* wisely thought the Pall-Mall Philosopher. He made Pen's mother remark, perhaps without a secret feeling of disappointment for she loved romance like other soft women, that the young gentleman had come home quite hungry to dinner at night and showed a very decent appetite at breakfast. "Gad, I wish I could," said the Major, thinking ruefully of his dinner pills. "The boy begins to sleep well, depend upon that." It was cruel, but it was true.

Having no other soul to confide in—he could not speak to his mother of his loves and disappointments—his uncle treated them in a scornful and worldly tone that, though carefully guarded and polite, jarred greatly on Mr. Pen's feelings. Foker was much too coarse to appreciate those refined sentimental secrets. Consequently, the lad's friendship for the Curate redoubled; he was never tired of having Smirke as a listener on that one subject. What is a lovee without a confidant? Pen employed Mr. Smirke, as Corydon does the elm-tree, to cut out his mistress's name upon. He made him echo with the name of the beautiful Amaryllis. When men have left off playing the tune, they do not care much for the pipe; but Pen thought he had a great friendship for Smirke because he could sigh out his loves and griefs into his tutor's ears. Smirke, for his own reasons, was always ready at the lad's call.

Pen's affection gushed out in a multitude of sonnets to the friend of his heart, which the other received with great sympathy. He plied Smirke with Latin Sapphics and Alcaics. The love-songs multiplied under his fluent pen, and Smirke declared and believed they were beautiful. On the other hand, Pen expressed a boundless gratitude that Heaven should have sent him such a friend at such a moment. He presented his tutor with his best-bound books, his gold guard-chain, and wanted him to take his double-barrelled gun. He went into Chatteris and got a gold pencil-case on credit (for he had no money and was still in debt to Smirke for some of the Fotheringay presents), which he presented to Smirke with an inscription indicative of his unalterable and eternal regard. The Curate, of course, was pleased with every mark of the boy's attachment.

The poor Curate was naturally very much dismayed at the contemplated departure of his pupil. When Arthur should go, Smirke's occupation and delight would go too. What pretext could he find for a daily visit to Fairoaks and that kind word or glance from the lady there, which was as necessary to the Curate as the frugal dinner which Madame Fribsby served him? Arthur gone, he would only be allowed to make visits like any other acquaintance. Little Laura could not accommodate him by learning the Catechism more than once a week. He had curled himself like ivy round Fairoaks; he pined at the thought that he must lose his hold of the place. Should he speak his mind and go down on his knees to the widow? He thought over any indications in her behavior which flattered his hopes. She had praised his sermons three weeks before; she had thanked him exceedingly for his present of a melon, for a small dinner-party which Mrs. Pendennis gave; she said she should always be grateful to him for his kindness to Arthur. When he declared that there were no bounds to his love and affection for that dear boy, she had certainly replied in a romantic manner, indicating her own strong gratitude and regard to all her son's friends. Should he speak out? Or should he delay? If he spoke and she refused him, it was awful to think that the gate of Fairoaks might be shut upon him forever—and within that door lay all the world for Mr. Smirke.

Thus, oh friendly readers, we see how every man in the world has his own private griefs and business, by which he is more cast down or occupied than by the affairs or sorrows of any other person. While Mrs. Pendennis is disquieting herself about losing her son, and that anxious hold she has had of him, as long as he has remained in the mother's nest, whence he is about to take flight into the great world beyond—while the Major's great soul chafes and frets, inwardly vexed as he thinks what great parties are going on in London, and that he might be sunning himself in the glances of Dukes and Duchesses, but for those cursed affairs which keep him in a wretched little country hole—while Pen is tossing between his passion and a more agreeable sensation, unacknowledged yet, but swaying him considerably, namely, his longing to see the world—Mr. Smirke has a private care watching at his bedside, and sitting behind him on his pony; and is no more satisfied than the rest of us. How lonely we are in the world; how selfish and secret, everybody! You and your wife have pressed the same pillow for forty years and fancy yourselves united. Psha, does she cry out when you have the gout, or do you lie awake when she has the toothache? Your artless daughter, seemingly all innocence and devoted to her mamma and her piano-lesson, is thinking of neither, but of the young Lieutenant with whom she danced at the last ball—the honest frank boy just returned from school is secretly speculating upon the money you will give him, and the debts he owes the tart-man. The old grandmother crooning in the corner and bound to another world within a few months, has some business or cares which are quite private and her own—very likely she is thinking of fifty years back, and that night when she made such an impression, and danced a cotillon with the Captain before your father proposed for her: or, what a silly little overrated creature your wife is, and how absurdly you are infatuated about her—and, as for your wife—O philosophic reader, answer and say,—Do you tell her all? Ah, sir—a distinct universe walks about under your hat and under mine—all things in nature are different to each—the woman we look at has not the same features, the dish we eat from has not the same taste to the one and the other—you and I are but a pair of infinite isolations, with some fellow-islands a little more or less near to us. Let us return, however, to the solitary Smirke.

Smirke had one confidante for his passion—that most injudicious woman, Madame Fribsby. How she became Madame Fribsby, nobody knows: she had left Clavering to go to a milliner's in London as Miss Fribsby—she pretended that she had got the rank in Paris during her residence in that city. But how could the French king, were he ever so much disposed, give her any such title? We shall not inquire into this mystery, however. Suffice to say, she went away from home a bouncing young lass; she returned a rather elderly character, with a Madonna front and a melancholy countenance—bought the late Mrs. Harbottle's business for a song—took her elderly mother to live with her; was very good to the poor, was constant at church, and had the best of characters. But there was no one in all Clavering, not Mrs. Portman herself, who read so many novels as Madame Fribsby. She had plenty of time for this amusement, for, in truth, very few people besides the folks at the Rectory and Fairoaks employed her; and by a perpetual perusal of such works (which were by no means so moral or edifying in the days of which we write, as they are at present) she had got to be so absurdly sentimental, that in her eyes life was nothing but an immense love-match; and she never could see two people together, but she fancied they were dying for one another.

On the day after Mrs

The afternoon sun poured through the windows of the Rectory, illuminating dust motes that danced like tiny, chaotic spirits. Inside Pen's study, the Curate, Mr. Smirke, sat pretending to read with his pupil, while outside, the air was thick with the scent of blooming flowers and the promise of a delightful walk. Mrs. Portman, having finished her business with Mrs. Pybus, decided the weather was simply too fine to ignore. She strolled toward Fairoaks to visit her dear friend, carrying with her a secret that would soon unravel the delicate social fabric of the neighborhood.

In the course of their conversation, Mrs. Portman revealed a scandalous truth about Mr. Smirke: he harbored an ancient, hidden attachment. "And on whom is it that Mr. Smirke has bestowed his heart?" Mrs. Pendennis asked, her face a mask of superior composure that barely hid her internal alarm.

"Why, my dear," Mrs. Portman replied, leaning in conspiratorially, "when he first arrived, everyone whispered he was destined for Myra. We were forced to withdraw our inquiries. Then rumors swirled of another interest, but I always contradicted them, insisting it was you—"

"That I!" Mrs. Pendennis cried, feigning outrage. "People are dreadfully impertinent. Mr. Smirke came here solely as Arthur's tutor."

"Pon my soul, it is a little too much," the Major interjected, lowering his newspaper and double eyeglass. "I've no patience with Mrs. Pybus," Helen added indignantly.

Mrs. Portman confirmed the gossip: Mr. Smirke had been secretly engaged to Miss Thompson of Clapham Common for years. She expressed delight, noting that an unmarried curate was often an awkward presence in a household, and promised strict confidentiality.

Mrs. Pendennis, with perfect sincerity, professed her gladness for the news, hoping the kind man would find a deserving wife. Once the visitor departed, Helen and her brother discussed the matter with satisfaction. Helen chastised herself for her recent haughty behavior toward the Curate, regretting that she hadn't appreciated his constant kindness to Arthur.

"Gratitude to this sort of person is well enough," the Major grumbled, "but familiarity breeds contempt. This gentleman teaches and takes his money like any other master. There must be distinctions in ranks."

Helen, however, felt differently. Now that Arthur was leaving, she recalled how polite Mr. Smirke had been—bringing books, copying music, teaching Laura, and offering thoughtful gifts. Her heart ached with guilt. When the Curate emerged from the study, looking eager to leave, she summoned him to her drawing-room, a place he rarely visited. With a slightly flushed face, she invited him to dinner, a summons he accepted with boundless joy.

During the meal, Helen was exceptionally gracious, perhaps overcompensating because the Major treated his nephew's tutor with icy disdain, addressing him as if he were a sovereign speaking to a lowly retainer. Pen laughed at his uncle's condescension, though he was equally prone to his own youthful arrogance. Smirke, oblivious to the Major's impertinence, charmed Helen with a blend of clerical wisdom and worldly wit, discussing bazaars, missionary meetings, and the latest novels. He employed every art to please the object of his affection, proving himself a man of sprightly talent and susceptible heart.

Soon after, the Major yawned out of the dining room. "What an unsufferable bore that man is," he complained. "He talks endlessly."

"He has been very good to Arthur," Mrs. Pendennis noted, wondering who Miss Thompson was.

"I always thought he was looking elsewhere," the Major muttered.

"Where?" Mrs. Pendennis asked innocently. "Toward Myra Portman?"

"Toward Helen Pendennis, if you must know," her brother-in-law replied bluntly.

"Himself! Impossible!" Helen exclaimed, knowing full well the truth. "His marriage will be a happy thing. I hope Arthur won't drink too much."

Arthur, flushed with the pride of managing the cellar, had stocked the table with generous supplies of claret. As the elders retired with little Laura, Pen and Smirke began to drink freely. One bottle vanished quickly; another lost half its contents before an hour had passed. Pen, with a hollow laugh, toasted the falsehood of women, declaring that wine was a mistress who never deceived. Smirke countered that some women were all truth and tenderness, sighing dramatically as he drained his glass.

Pen then recited verses he had composed that morning, lamenting a woman who had slighted his passion and vowing to become a man worthy of his race. His voice trembled with emotion, his honest grey eyes shining with genuine sensitivity. Smirke, moved by his young friend's sentimentality, gripped Pen's hand across the dessert dishes. "You are a poet, Arthur," he declared. "Go on and prosper. When you are famous, will you remember your old tutor?"

"I shall never forget," Pen swore, raising his glass. "You are my best friend."

"Am I, really, Arthur?" Smirke asked, his heart pounding audibly against his ribs.

"My best friend, my friend for ever," Pen replied, finishing the second bottle. "We'll have another! By Jove, we will. My uncle says Sheridan drank five bottles at Brookes's. This is the finest wine in England." Pen began to sing a drinking song from *Der Freischuetz*, his voice ringing through the open windows. Outside, Helen paced the lawn, her heart swelling with pride as she heard her son's sweet notes.

"You are taking too much wine, Arthur," Smirke warned softly.

"No," Pen insisted, "women give headaches, but this doesn't. Tell us about the lady, Smirke. I've seen you sighing for her."

Smirke's cambric shirtfront heaved with emotion. "Here's her health," Pen shouted, forcing a toast. "May she soon be Mrs. Smirke!"

"Do you say so?" Smirke trembled. "Do you really say so, Arthur?"

"Of course! Here's Mrs. Smirke's good health!" Pen cheered, waving his glass overhead until his mother and Laura wondered what was happening.

"I accept the omen," Smirke gasped, blushing furiously. "Oh, my dear Arthur, you—you know her?"

"What—Myra Portman? I wish you joy; she has a devilish large waist, but I wish you joy, old fellow."

"Oh, Arthur!" Smirke groaned, nodding speechlessly.

"Sorry I offended you—but she has a large waist," Pen continued, the third bottle clearly affecting him.

"It's not Miss Portman," Smirke cried in agony.

"Is it someone at Chatteris? Madame Fribsby?" Pen guessed, laughing hysterically.

The Curate could contain no more. "O Pen, how can you suppose any of those beings could influence this heart when I contemplate perfection? For two years, my heart has known no other idol. Haven't I loved you as a son, Arthur?"

"Yes, old boy, you've been very good," Pen said, though his liking was not truly filial.

"My means are limited," Smirke rushed on, "but whatever I inherit—five hundred a year at least—will be settled upon her and you at my death."

"What the deuce do you mean?" Pen asked, puzzled.

"Arthur, you say I am your dearest friend—Let me be more. Oh, can't you see that the angelic being I love is no other than your dear, dear angel of a mother?"

"My mother!" Pen jumped up, sobering instantly. "Pooh! Damn it, Smirke, you must be mad—she's seven or eight years older than you."

"Did you find that any objection?" Smirke wailed.

"The cases are not similar," Pen said, blushing red. "Our positions are very different."

"How do you mean?" Smirke asked sadly.

"I mean what I say," Arthur declared. "My tutor has no right to ask a lady of my mother's rank to marry him. It's a breach of confidence. It's a liberty you take, Smirke!"

"O Arthur!" Smirke cried, clasping his hands, as Arthur stamped his foot and rang the bell for coffee. "Don't let's have any more of this."

John, the butler, entered to serve coffee, glancing in wonder at the three empty claret bottles. Smirke declined to go into the drawing-room. Arthur, with majestic air, dismissed him and ordered his horse. Smirke, knowing the way, fetched his own pony from the stable.

Pen followed him out, holding his hand. "Forgive me if I have said anything harsh," he said in an agitated voice. "But it can't be, old fellow. Be a man. God bless you."

Smirke nodded silently and rode out of the lodge gate. Pen watched until the pony's hooves faded into the distance. Helen lingered on the lawn, brushing hair from her son's forehead and kissing it. "Smirke is unwell," Pen said with a laugh, his usual cheerfulness returning. He put his arm around her waist, walking her up and down the path as Laura laughed from the window.

Later that night, Pen recounted the absurd scene to his mother, describing the dismal yet ludicrous encounter. Helen blushed deeply, while Arthur enjoyed the roguish perplexity of the moment. "Confound the fellow's impudence," the Major muttered as he took his candle. That night, Pen and his mother talked long and deeply, filled with love and laughter, and the boy slept soundly for the first time in months.

Meanwhile, Captain Costigan secured a splendid engagement for his daughter, Miss Fotheringay, and settled his debts with Mr. Dolphin. The Buccaneer club of Chatteris, usually a riotous gathering of local gentlemen, threw a magnificent farewell banquet for the Captain. After speeches of exaggerated patriotism and heartfelt gratitude, the Captain sang his favorite songs, and the evening ended in triumph. The next day, Miss Costigan left a necklace and a white satin gown for her friend Miss Rouncy, and she and her father departed in the Competitor coach, never to be seen by Pendennis again.

Pen learned of their departure only through the county papers. Rushing to Chatteris, he found a "Lodgings to let" card in the familiar window where he and Emily had once gazed at the Dean's garden. He wandered into her empty bedroom, burying his face in the vacant pillow, overwhelmed by a sense of finality and loss. He distributed coins to the servants who had cared for the Costigans, feeling the weight of years passing in mere months.

The entire household felt the shift. Smirke wanted to flee the sight of the "siren widow"; Foker dreamed of supper-parties in Saint Boniface; the Major longed for pheasant shooting; and Helen and Laura nervously packed Pen's trunks. They wrote cards with his name, their tearful eyes reflecting their wistful goodbye.

Days later, the mail coach arrived at Fairoaks, its lamps blazing against the night. Helen and Laura stood by the shrubbery as the carriage wheels crunched over the gravel. In an instant, the lights disappeared, and Helen's prayers followed the departing boy. The house seemed lonelier without him, the empty study echoing with his absence. Laura slept in her mother's room, while Helen knelt by Pen's vacant bed, praying for her son as mothers alone know how to plead. She knew his pure blessings were following him, miles away, as he tried his restless wings in the vast world beyond.

There is a peculiar magic in the memory of university days, a golden haze that clings to every man, no matter how brief or unglorious his academic tenure. It is the era when the boy's leading strings are severed, and he rushes into the novel, dizzying freedom of adulthood. He knows nothing yet of cares, ill health, roguery, poverty, or the crushing weight of tomorrow's disappointments. The play is fresh, unacted, and vibrant. While the after-drink of life eventually turns stale and bitter, that first sparkling draught of pleasure remains pure and brilliant in the mind. Even the most seasoned epicure, restricted now to a poached egg and a glass of water, finds joy in watching others feast; and surely, no amount of age or cynicism should make a man so glum that he cannot smile at the sight of happy youth.

I recall returning recently from a visit to the ancient universities of Oxbridge, where my friend Mr. Arthur Pendennis once spent a significant portion of his life. On the railway, I sat beside a young student of Saint Boniface who had somehow secured an exeat for a day's lark in London. This honest lad never ceased rattling on from the moment we departed until the train screeched to a halt—a performance so relentless it nearly drove me to distraction, though I confess I was never tired of his jokes and cheery laughter. Upon arriving at the terminus, he demanded a hansom cab immediately, eager to plunge into the pleasures awaiting him. Away he went, whirling through the streets with joy lighting up his face. As for me, burdened only by a small carpet-bag, I climbed onto the outside of the omnibus and sat contentedly sandwiched between a Jewish pedlar smoking foul cigars and a gentleman's servant guarding a poodle-dog. We drifted leisurely toward the city, neither of us in a rush to join the smoking Babylon of town, nor thinking of club dinners or casino dances. Yet, a few years later, my young friend of the railway would likely feel no less eager than I do now.

Railroads were nonexistent when Arthur Pendennis attended the famous University of Oxbridge. Instead, he traveled in a well-appointed coach, crammed with dons, gownsmen, prospective freshmen, and their guardians. Inside sat a fat old gentleman from the City in grey stockings, accompanied by his pale-faced son, who was terrified to learn the coach was being driven by young Mr. Foker of Saint Boniface. Foker was the friend of all men, including coachmen, and could drive with the skill of Tom Hicks himself. Perched on the roof, Pen examined the coach, the passengers, and the countryside with boundless delight. His heart leaped as the university came into view, the magnificent prospect of venerable towers, pinnacles, tall elms, and the shining river spreading before him.

Before setting out, Pen had spent a few days with his uncle at the Major's lodgings in Bury Street. The Major felt the lad's wardrobe needed renewal, and Arthur was only too happy to accept any plan involving new coats and waistcoats. The self-denying uncle spared no sacrifice for the youth. London felt awfully lonely; the Pall Mall pavement was deserted, the red jackets had fled the city, and the club windows were empty. The Major escorted his nephew into a couple of these ghostly mansions and wrote Arthur's name on the candidate list of one. Pen's pleasure at this gesture of his guardian was excessive. He read his name and titles in the parchment volume—"Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, of Fairoaks Lodge, —shire and Saint Boniface College, Oxbridge; proposed by Major Pendennis, and seconded by Viscount Colchicum"—with a thrill of intense gratification. "You will come in for ballot in about three years, by which time you will have taken your degree," the guardian noted. Pen longed for those three years to vanish, surveying the stucco halls, vast libraries, and drawing-rooms as if they were already his private property. The Major laughed slyly at the pompous airs of the simple young fellow as he strutted out.

Later, the Major and Foker drove down to the Grey Friars in the latter's cab to renew acquaintance with old comrades. Boys crowded around the cab, admiring the chestnut horse and the gravity of Stoopid, the tiger. As the bell for afternoon school rang, the awful Doctor passed into the school with his grammar book. Foker slunk away uneasily at his presence, but Pen went up blushing, shaking the dignitary's hand, laughing as he recalled how that well-remembered Latin Grammar had boxed his ears many a time. Generous, good-natured, and perfectly conceited, Pen was thoroughly satisfied with himself.

Their next stop was the parental brew-house. Foker's Entire was an enormous pile of buildings near the Grey Friars, its name gilded on innumerable signs. The venerable junior partner honored the young lord of the vats and his friend, serving them silver flagons of brown-stout so potent that one might think the very horse Mr. Harry Foker drove was affected by the potency. Stoopid rushed home at a pace that endangered pie-stalls and women on crossings, causing the cab steps to collide with posts and the horse to swing fearfully on his board.

The Major was delighted to see Pen with his young acquaintance, listening intently to Mr. Foker's artless stories. He treated the two boys to a fine dinner at a Covent Garden Coffee-house before proceeding to the play. But his greatest happiness came when Mr. and Lady Agnes Foker, visiting London, invited the Major and Arthur Pendennis to dinner in Grosvenor Street. "Having obtained the entree into Lady Agnes Foker's house," the Major told Pen with affectionate solemnity, "it behoves you to keep it. Never neglect to call in Grosvenor Street. Read up carefully in Debrett on the alliances of the Earls of Rosherville, and make some trifling, historical, complimentary allusions. You, with your poetic fancy, can do this well." He added that Mr. Foker always insisted on serving family porter after dinner, which one must never refuse, even though the Major himself confessed that all beer disagreed with him. True to his word, the heroic martyr sacrificed himself on the day of the dinner, drinking the porter while old Mr. Foker made his time-honored joke about Foker's Entire. We should all have loved to see the Major's grin at the worthy old gentleman's jest.

Lady Agnes, wrapped up in her son Harry, was the fondest of mothers and one of the most good-natured, if not the wisest, of women. She received her son's friend with great cordiality, astonishing Pen with accounts of the severe studies her darling boy pursued, fearing they might injure his health. Foker the elder burst into a horse-laugh at these speeches, and the heir winked knowingly at his friend. Lady Agnes then recounted her son's history from the earliest time—his miraculous escapes from drowning, his suffering from measles and hooping-cough, and the tyrannies of the horrid school where Mr. Foker had sent him. She never forgave that disagreeable Doctor. After an hour of incessant prattling about her son, she voted the two Messieurs Pendennis most agreeable men. When pheasants arrived with the second course—which the Major knew perfectly well came from Logwood—she hoped they would both visit there at Christmas or during Harry's vacations.

"God bless you, my dear boy," Pendennis said to Arthur as they lit their candles in Bury Street to go to bed. "You made that little allusion to Agincourt, where one of the Roshervilles distinguished himself, very neatly, although Lady Agnes did not quite understand it. It was exceedingly well for a beginner—though you oughtn't to blush so, by the way. Remember through life that with a good entree, it is just as easy to have good society as bad. It costs a man, when properly introduced, no more trouble to keep a footing in the best houses in London than to dine with a lawyer in Bedford Square. Be particular in the acquaintances you make. The premier pas in life is the most important of all. Did you write to your mother today? No? Well, do so before you go, and ask Mr. Foker for a frank. They like it. Good night. God bless you."

Pen wrote a droll account of his doings in London, the play, the visit to the old Friars, the brewery, and the party at Mr. Foker's, to his dearest mother, who was saying her prayers at home in the lonely house at Fairoaks, her heart full of unutterable love and tenderness for the boy. She and Laura read those letters many times, brooding over them as women do. It was the first step in life Pen was making—Ah! what a dangerous journey it is, and how the bravest may stumble and the strongest fail. Brother wayfarer! May you have a kind arm to support yours on the path, and a friendly hand to succour those who fall beside you. May truth guide, mercy forgive at the end, and love accompany always. Without that lamp, how blind the traveler would be, and how black and cheerless the journey!

So the coach drove up to the ancient and comfortable inn, the Trencher, in Main Street, Oxbridge. For the first time, Pen remarked with delight and eagerness the gownsmen going about, the chapel bells clinking from morning-tide to even-song, and the towers and pinnacles rising calm and stately over the gables of the homely busy city. Previous communications had taken place between Dr. Portman on Pen's part and Mr. Buck, Tutor of Boniface, on whose side Pen was entered. Once the Major had arranged his personal appearance to make a satisfactory impression on the tutor, they walked down Main Street, passed the great gate and belfry-tower of Saint George's College, and arrived at Saint Boniface. Again, Pen's heart began to beat as they entered the wicket of the venerable ivy-mantled gate. It was surmounted by an ancient dome almost covered with creepers and adorned with the effigy of the Saint from whom the House takes its name, along with many coats-of-arms of its royal and noble benefactors.

The porter pointed out a queer old tower at the corner of the quadrangle where Mr. Buck's rooms were approached. Walking across the square, the main features were instantly and forever stamped in Pen's mind: the pretty fountain playing in the center of the fair grass plats; the tall chapel windows and buttresses rising to the right; the hall with its tapering lantern and oriel window; the lodge from which the Master issued with rustling silks; and the lines of surrounding rooms broken by carved chimneys, grey turrets, and quaint gables. Pen drank in these sights with an eagerness belonging to first impressions, while the Major surveyed them with the calmness of a gentleman who does not care for the picturesque and whose eyes have been somewhat dimmed by the constant glare of the Pall Mall pavement.

Saint George's is the great College of the University, with its four vast quadrangles, beautiful hall, gardens, and the Georgians, who wear gowns of a peculiar cut and give themselves airs of superiority over all other young men. Little Saint Boniface is but a petty hermitage compared to the huge consecrated pile alongside it. Yet, considering its size, it has always kept an excellent name. Its tone is very good; the best families of certain counties have sent up their young men there for generations. The college livings are remarkably good, the fellowships easy, and the Boniface men have had more than their fair share of university honors. Their boat was third upon the river, their chapel-choir not inferior to Saint George's itself, and the Boniface ale the best in Oxbridge. In the comfortable old wainscoted College-Hall, round about Roubilliac's statue of Saint Boniface (who stands in an attitude of seraphic benediction over the uncommonly good cheer of the fellows' table), hang portraits of many eminent Bonifacians. There is the learned Doctor Griddle, who suffered in Henry VIII.'s time, and Archbishop Bush who roasted him; Lord Chief Justice Hicks; the Duke of St. David's, K.G., Chancellor of the University and Member of this College; Sprott the Poet, of whose fame the college is justly proud; Doctor Blogg, the late master and friend of Doctor Johnson, who visited him at Saint Boniface; and other lawyers, scholars, and divines whose portraitures look from the walls or whose coats-of-arms shine in emerald, ruby, gold, and azure in the tall windows of the refectory. The venerable cook of the college is one of the best artists in Oxbridge (his son took the highest honors in the other University of Camford), and the wine in the fellows' room has long been famed for its excellence and abundance.

Into this certainly not the least snugly sheltered arbour amongst the groves of Academe, Pen now found his way, leaning on his uncle's arm. They speedily reached Mr. Buck's rooms and were conducted into the apartment of that courteous gentleman. He had received previous information from Dr. Portman regarding Pen, whose family, fortune, and personal merits the honest Doctor had described with no small enthusiasm. Indeed, Portman had described Arthur to the tutor as "a young gentleman of some fortune and landed estate, of one of the most ancient families in the kingdom, and possessing such a character and genius as were sure, under the proper guidance, to make him a credit to the college and the university." Under such recommendations, the tutor was most cordial to the young freshman and his guardian, inviting the latter to dine in hall to see his nephew wear his gown and eat his dinner for the first time. He also requested the pair to take wine at his rooms after hall and, in consequence of the highly favorable report he had received of Mr. Arthur Pendennis, said he should be happy to give him the best set of rooms to be had in college—a gentleman-pensioner's set, indeed, which were just luckily vacant. So they parted until dinner-time, which was very near at hand, and the Major pronounced Mr. Buck to be uncommonly

Our friend Pen breathed a sigh of relief when his Mentor finally departed on the second day of their arrival in Oxford, and we can safely assume the Major felt a profound sense of liberation at having discharged his duty. More than three months of his life had been sacrificed to his nephew—a martyrdom rarely matched by any man, let alone a Major. While a man will readily risk his life for honor, asking him to surrender his ease or his heart's desire is a trial few can bear. Yet, the Major had done it. He deserved his holiday. Foker and Pen saw him off in the carriage, with Foker giving specific instructions to the driver to ensure the Major's comfort. The elder Pendennis was pleased to entrust his nephew to a young man who would introduce him to the university's elite. The Major, meanwhile, rushed to London and then to Cheltenham, descending upon neighboring estates where the families hadn't fled abroad, seeking good shooting and fine company.

A quarter of the serial space has been dedicated to this single passage of Pen's career, a testament to the fact that his entire academic journey cannot be detailed with such granularity unless a future chronicler picks up the pen after his death. We shall not recount his university life with such minute detail, for the lives of such boys are not meant to be told in full. As long as one's honor is clear, the mind remains at ease. Women are often portrayed as pure and unselfish, but men? Men are complicated. I would not dare claim Arthur Pendennis was worse than his peers, only that his peers were, for the most part, flawed. Can you name ten spotless men in your acquaintance? My list is large, yet I cannot find ten saints among them.

During his first term, Pen attended classical and mathematical lectures with tolerable diligence. However, quickly discovering he lacked the genius for exact sciences, and annoyed by being beaten in the lecture hall by vulgar youths who wore shoes and stockings so thick they required straps to hold them up, he abandoned mathematics. He announced to his indulgent parent that he would now devote himself exclusively to Greek and Roman literature. Mrs. Pendennis was delighted, provided he didn't ruin his health. She had heard tragic tales of students dying of brain fever from overwork, and she insisted his delicate constitution took precedence over vain honors. Pen, though unaware of any lurking disease, promised not to stay up late reading. He kept this promise with surprising tenacity, unlike his behavior in other areas where he was often remiss.

Soon, Pen realized he learned little in the classical lectures. His fellow students were dull, and the tutor, Mr. Buck, was no better scholar than a fifth-form boy at Grey Friars. Mr. Buck might have some humdrum notions about Aeschylus or Aristophanes, but possessed no true poetic sense, much like Mrs. Binge, his bed-maker. Pen grew weary of watching dull minds blunder through lines he could read in a tenth of the time they spent. He concluded that private reading was the only truly profitable study and announced he would read far more privately and far less in public. His mother, knowing nothing of Homer or Algebra herself, was content with his arrangements, confident he would secure the place he merited.

Pen did not return home until after Christmas, disappointing his mother and Laura, who had been longing for him to build a fine snow fortification as he had three winters prior. Instead, he was invited to Logwood, Lady Agnes Foker's estate, for private theatricals and a gay Christmas party of fine folk whom the Major would never want his nephew to neglect. However, he stayed home for the final three weeks of the vacation. Laura noted the quantity of fine new clothes he brought, and his mother admired his improved appearance and manly tone.

He did not return at Easter, but when he arrived for the long vacation, he brought even smarter attire. He appeared in morning wearing wonderful shooting jackets with remarkable buttons, and in the evening in gorgeous velvet waistcoats, richly embroidered cravats, and curious linen. Peering into his room, his mother discovered a beautiful dressing-case with silver mountings, lovely rings, and jewelry. He had a new French watch and gold chain, replacing the big old chronometer with its bunch of jingling seals that had once hung from John Pendennis's fob—the very watch the late doctor had used to feel patients' pulses. Just months prior, Pen had longed for this watch, considering it the most splendid timepiece in the world. Before leaving for college, Helen had taken it from her trinket-box, where it had sat unwound since her husband's death, and given it to Pen with a solemn speech about his father's virtues and the proper use of time. Now, Pen pronounced the portly chronometer "out of date," comparing it disrespectfully to a warming-pan. Laura thought this rude, but he left the watch in a drawer alongside soiled primrose gloves and cravats that had gone out of fashion, joining another school watch previously mentioned in this history. His old horse, Rebecca, was deemed too light for his weight; he swapped her for a more powerful steed, paying a heavy price for it. Mr. Pendennis provided the funds, and Laura wept when Rebecca was fetched away.

Pen also brought a large box of cigars branded with exotic names like Colorados, Afrancesados, and Telescopios. He began consuming them not only around the stables and greenhouses—where they were beneficial for Helen's plants—but in his own study. His mother initially disapproved, but he claimed he was working on a prize poem and could not compose without a cigar, quoting the late Lord Byron in favor of the habit. As he smoked with such apparent purpose, his mother eventually relented. One day, finding him on the sofa consulting a recent novel to cultivate light literature, Helen simply went to his bedroom, retrieved a light-box and his cigar-case, placed the cigar in his mouth, and lit the match. Pen laughed and kissed her hand as it rested fondly over the back of the sofa. "Dear old mother," he said, "if I were to tell you to burn the house down, I think you would do it." It is likely he was right; the foolish woman would have done almost anything for him.

Besides English light literature, Pen imported boxes of French light literature. When Helen dipped into these pages, she read things that opened her eyes in wonder. Pen assured her he did not make the books, but it was his duty to keep up his French by reading celebrated contemporary writers, including the eminent Paul de Kock, just as much as Swift or Molière. Mrs. Pendennis sighed with perplexity but yielded. Miss Laura, however, was strictly warned off the books by both her anxious mother and the rigid moralist Arthur Pendennis himself, who believed such reading was unsuitable for a young lady whose business in life differed from his.

During this long vacation, Pen drank through the bin of claret his father had stored, a vintage the son had remarked contained not a single headache in a hogshead. Once exhausted, he wrote to his wine merchants, Messrs. Binney and Latham of Mark Lane, London, for a further supply. Old Doctor Portman had recommended them, advising against Oxbridge tradesmen whose liquor was deleterious and exorbitantly priced. "You will have to entertain your young friends at Boniface with wine-parties," the honest rector had noted. The obedient young gentleman followed this advice. When he hinted that the university account for wine should arrive with the Fairoaks bill, the poor widow was frightened by the amount. Pen laughed at her old-fashioned views, claiming the bill was moderate and that everyone now drank claret and champagne. Finally, the widow paid, feeling dimly that her household expenses were swelling and her narrow income struggling to meet them. Yet, these were only occasional costs. Pen merely visited for a few weeks at a time; Laura and his mother could pinch when he was gone. In the brief time he was with them, ought they not to make him happy?

Arthur's allowances were liberal, far exceeding those of sons of far wealthier men. Years ago, the thrifty and affectionate John Pendennis, determined to give his son a university education denied to him by his own father's extravagance, had begun laying aside a store of money called Arthur's Education Fund. Year after year, his executors found entries of sums vested as A.E.F., and during the widow's lifetime before Pen's entry, she added sundry sums. Thus, when Arthur went up to Oxford, the fund reached a considerable amount. "Let him be liberally allowanced," was the Major's maxim. "Let him make his first entrée into the world as a gentleman, take his place with men of good rank and station; after giving it to him, it will be his own duty to hold it." There is no worse policy than stinting a boy or putting him on a lower allowance than his fellows. Arthur must face the world and fight for himself presently; meanwhile, they would procure him good friends, gentlemanly habits, and back him well against the time the real struggle came. These liberal opinions the Major probably advanced both because they were just and because he was not dealing with his own money.

Thus, young Pen, the only son of an estated country gentleman, with a good allowance, a gentlemanlike bearing, and a handsome person, looked to be a lad of much more consequence than he truly was. He was held by Oxbridge authorities, tradesmen, and undergraduates as quite a young buck and a member of the aristocracy.
