His manner was frank, brave, and perhaps a little impertinent, fitting a high-spirited youth. He was perfectly generous and free-handed with his seemingly plentiful money. He loved joviality and had a good voice for a song. Boat-racing had not yet reached the frenzy it would attain later; riding and tandem-driving were the fashions of the ingenuous youth. Pen rode well to hounds, appeared in pink as became a young buck, and, not particularly extravagant in equestrian pursuits, managed to run up a fine bill at Nile's, the livery-stable keeper, and in other quarters. In fact, this lucky young gentleman had almost every taste to a considerable degree. He was very fond of books of all sorts; Doctor Portman had taught him to like rare editions, and his own taste led him to beautiful bindings. It was marvelous what tall copies, gilding, marbling, and blind-tooling the booksellers and binders put upon Pen's shelves. He had a fair taste in art and a keen relish for prints of a high school—Stranges, Rembrandt etchings, and Wilkies before the letter—with which his apartments were furnished in perfect good taste, earning him no small reputation in the university. He exhibited a partiality for rings, jewelry, and fine raiment. During his time at the university, he was rather a dressy man, loving to array himself in splendour. He and his polite friends would dress with as much care to dine at each other's rooms as others would to enslave a mistress. They said he wore rings over his kid gloves, which he always denied, but what follies will not youth perpetrate with its own admirable gravity? That he took perfumed baths was a truth; he claimed to take them after meeting men of a very low set in hall.

In Pen's second year, when Miss Fotheringay made her chief hit in London and scores of prints were published of her, Pen had one hung in his bedroom. He confided to his set how awfully, wildly, and passionately he had loved that woman. He showed them the verses he had written, his brow darkening, eyes rolling, and chest heaving with emotion as he recalled that fatal period, describing the woes and agonies he had suffered. The verses were copied, handed about, sneered at, admired, and passed from coterie to coterie. Few things elevate a lad in the estimation of his brothers more than a character for great and romantic passion. Perhaps there is something noble in it among very young men, considered heroic. Pen was pronounced a tremendous fellow. They said he had almost committed suicide; that he had fought a duel with a baronet about her. Freshmen pointed him out to each other. As he swaggered out of college at promenade time, surrounded by cronies, he was famous to behold. Elaborately attired, he would ogle the ladies who came to lionize the university, passing before him on the arms of happy gownsmen, and give his opinion on their charms or toilettes with the gravity of a critic. Men said they had been walking with Pendennis and were as pleased to be seen in his company as some would be walking with a duke down Pall Mall. He and the Proctor capped each other as they met, as if they were rival powers, and the men hardly knew which was the greater.

In fact, during his second year, Arthur Pendennis became one of the men of fashion in the university. It is curious to watch that facile admiration and simple fidelity of youth. They hang round a leader, wonder at him, love him, and imitate him. No generous boy ever lived without some wonderment for another; Monsieur Pen at Oxbridge had his school, his faithful band of friends, and his rivals. When the young men heard at haberdashers' shops that Mr. Pendennis of Boniface had just ordered a crimson satin cravat, you would see a couple of dozen crimson satin cravats in Main Street within the week. Simon the Jeweller sold no less than two gross of Pendennis pins from a pattern the young gentleman had selected.

Now, if any person with an arithmetical turn of mind calculates the cost of indulging freely in all the propensities we have attributed to Pen, it will be seen that a young fellow with such tastes must spend or owe a very handsome sum in two or three years. We said Pen had not a calculating turn. No single propensity was outrageously extravagant, yet Paddington's tailor's account, Guttlebury's cook's bill for dinners, Dillon Tandy's bill with Finn the print seller for Raphael-Morgheus and Landseer proofs, Wormall's dealings with Parkton the great bookseller for Aldine editions and illuminated Missals of the XVI. Century, and Snaffle's or Foker's score with Nile the horsedealer, were each incomparably greater than any little bills Mr. Pen might run up with the above tradesmen. But Pendennis of Boniface had the

Just before Mr. Foker departed for Oxford, a certain gentleman arrived at Boniface College. He hailed from Cambridge, having been expelled for a row with the tutors, and his name was Horace Bloundell. The young wags dubbed his ancestral home "Bloundell-Bloundell Hall, Bloundell-Bloundellshire," a title that sounded grander than reality. Yet, Bloundell's lineage was genuine; he was of the ancient Suffolk Bloundells. It was likely this pedigree, combined with the fact that their Master, Dr. Donne, was also a Suffolk man, that secured Bloundell a spot at Boniface after St. George's and a couple of others had rejected him.

A family living was at stake, and Bloundell needed it desperately. While serving in a dragoon regiment, his third brother—the original beneficiary of the living—had fallen ill and died. Determined to swap his crimson pantaloons and black shako for the black coat and white cravat of the clergy, Bloundell set out to secure his future. Though his initial plans at Cambridge had stumbled, the ex-dragoon was undeterred. He simply shifted his battlefield.

By Pen's second year, Major Pendennis visited his nephew and was introduced to the university's elite: the gentle Lord Plinlimmon, the gallant Magnus Charters, the sly Harland, the intrepid Ringwood (known as "Rupert" for his brave blunders), the republican Broadbent (a Bristol dissenting firebrand), and finally, Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, who had already claimed his place among the select.

The Major, though baffled by Harland's Greek quotes and Broadbent's dirty shoes, loved the company. He was particularly delighted with the stories he told, which were fresh to Oxford ears despite being old news in London. The students listened with honest laughter and respect—a rarity in the city. However, Mr. Bloundell wore a sneer. He once dared to correct the Major on a detail of an anecdote, boasting of a version he'd heard at a club. The Major, with a melancholy grace, accepted the correction, thanking Bloundell for his "accuracy." Later, Bloundell openly mocked the Major as a "regular old trencherman" and a "fogy."

Sympathies are usually mutual. As the Major noted, dislike is like a bad smell; if someone abuses you, you naturally dislike them. Consequently, the Major declared Bloundell "ineligible."

"Bless my soul, sir!" Pen laughed. "He's the most popular man in the university! We elected him Barmecides the first week. He's from an excellent family, descended from Richard's Blondel, bearing a harp in chief and the motto *O Mong Roy*."

"A man may have a fine coat of arms and still be a tiger," the Major retorted, chipping his egg. "That man is a tiger. I'll wager he left his regiment in bad odor. Look at his rings and jewelry—that's 'Scamp' written on his face. Avoid him."

Pen ignored the warning. He was eager to impress, viewing a knowledge of wines and cooking as the mark of a sophisticated roue. He wanted to be an "Admirable Crichton" of dining. Soon, his rooms were besieged by waiters and dish-covers, a stark contrast to the quiet student life he had once known.

Guardian advice fell on deaf ears. Young men crave the appearance of worldly wisdom, and Bloundell, a self-styled *Lovelace*, was the perfect guide. The college tutors noticed the moral tone dropping; Bloundell was called "Captain Macheath" by Broadbent, who predicted he'd hang. Foker, wisely cautious, merely hinted that Bloundell was a dangerous partner at whist but a terrible risk to bet against.

Pen, however, possessed an insatiable appetite for pleasure. He called it "seeing life," quoting Terence and Shakespeare to justify his hedonism. One night, after a supper party, Bloundell produced a pair of dice from his waistcoat pocket and placed them in a green wine glass. He tossed them, sang the chorus of *Robert the Devil*, and the group, including Pen, abandoned their caps for the game. By midnight, they were rolling dice in the glass until Pen shattered it after throwing six mains.

From that night, Pen plunged into the delights of hazard. Bloundell would visit in the morning, rattling the bones in a felt-lined box to avoid the tutors. Once, they were nearly caught by Mr. Buck, who heard "Two to one on the caster" through an open window. Inside, however, Pen was pretending to coach Ringwood on Homer, asking gravely about the navigability of the River Scamander.

Bloundell also shared his "legends." He spoke of winning twelve thousand francs in Paris and sleeping with Napoleon coins in his boot. Pen, glistening with excitement, respected the man who could pull off such coups. When Pen mentioned his love for Miss Fotheringay, Bloundell laughed it off, comparing her to a notorious actress and listing the men who had wasted fortunes on her. Pen, trusting implicitly, felt his heart broken. How could he love a "worthless impostor"?

During an Easter break, Pen and Bloundell visited London. They stayed at a hotel in Covent Garden and frequented a military club, where Pen drank pale ale with mustachioed officers. One evening, Pen sat in the boxes of the Museum Theatre, separated from the Major by a mere plank. Miss Fotheringay was starring.

It wasn't the first time Pen had seen her; he had visited her in Chatteris the previous year. Now, watching her perform, the illusion faded. The light was gone from her eyes. Her voice, once thrilling, now sounded coarse and mechanical. The loud sobs and sighs he had once adored seemed like a cheap imitation. He realized he had loved a dream, not a woman. He felt humiliated and lonely, running to the stage door only to find she didn't recognize him.

That night, they supped uproariously, and Pen woke with a splitting headache, having spent all his ready money.

The narrator notes that Pen's own confessions reveal the truth of his downfall. He was an easy mark for rogues. Finch the money-lender charged him a hundred percent, and even the college cook, his devoted admirer, sent bills he couldn't pay. Yet, Pen retained a generous streak. He would leave a party to visit a sick friend and, in his third year, faced a levee of creditors. When a poor tradesman's daughter, Mary Frodsham, came to plead for help due to her father's illness, Pen pawned his watch and all his jewelry except two old buttons belonging to his father. He rushed to her shop, weeping and begging forgiveness.

This act was less virtue than weakness; he still stood by the prints he had sold to pay her, leaving his mother to suffer the consequences of his follies.

Tutor Mr. Buck had long assumed Pen was wealthy, seeing him with knights of the Garter at St. James's. He was thunderstruck when Pen confessed his massive debts. Pen, terrified of hurting his mother, begged for time, lamenting that his uncle's insistence on high society had ruined him. He slunk away from parties, becoming a gloomy figure in a battered cap, the talk of freshmen's wine parties.

Finally, the Degree Examinations arrived. While many of the men Pen had mocked for their hobnailed shoes passed with honors, the lists were published. Where was Pen the superb, the wit, the dandy, the poet? Where was the widow's darling? The rumor swept through the university: Pendennis of Boniface had been plucked.

To anyone with even a smattering of heraldry and genealogy, the crest of the Pendennis family is well known: a nest teeming with tiny pelicans pecking at the bleeding breast of a large, maternal bird, which generously feeds them. It is said that female pelicans enjoy this self-flagellation; certainly, women do. There must be a peculiar, masculine-incomprehensible pleasure in the agony of being scarified. Some women would rather endure the wound than avoid it. They derive a strange joy from sacrificing themselves for the object their instincts dictate they love—whether a reckless husband, a dissipated son, or a darling rogue of a brother. Their hearts are ready to pour out their finest treasures for the sake of the cherished one. And what a feast of forgiveness we men are prepared to serve these soft creatures! Scarcely a reader here has not, at some point, administered this specific pleasure to his woman, treating her to the luxury of absolving him. They don't mind how they live themselves; but when the prodigal returns, they rejoice, kill the fatted calf, and prepare a festival where Mercy and Forgiveness smile out to welcome him. I hope this remains true forever; if we must rely solely on Justice, Heaven help us!

During the latter part of Pen's tenure at Oxford, his uncle's affection had swelled to a fever pitch. The Major was proud of Arthur—high-spirited, frank, a good soul, and possessing a bearing that screamed "gentleman." It delighted the old bachelor to see Pen striding alongside the young patricians of the university. Though stinginess was a byword among the Club wags who envied his endless engagements yet refused to acknowledge his poverty, the Major was charmed to treat his nephew and the young lords to snug dinners, fine claret, and his very best *bons mots*. Some of these jokes were so delicate they would be ruined by repetition; others were so terrible that repeating them would harm no one. He courted their parents through the boys and courted himself by their company. He visited Oxford repeatedly, where the young fellows amused the old gentleman with parties, breakfasts, and fetes, partly to joke him and partly to honor him. He regaled them with stories, becoming juvenile and hilarious in their midst. He attended a grand debate at the Union, crowing and cheering, rapping his stick in time with the students, astounded by the boy's eloquence and fire. He thought he had a young Pitt for a nephew. He possessed an almost paternal fondness for Pen, writing letters filled with playful advice and town gossip. He bragged at his clubs, introducing Arthur with pleasure, claiming that the new generation—Young Lord Plinlimmon, Young Lord Magnus Charters—would eclipse their fathers. He begged permission to bring Arthur to a grand fête at Gaunt House, watching him dance with the sisters of the nobility with ineffable satisfaction. He procured invitations with the diligence of a mother seeking a match for her daughter, boasting of Arthur's talents and the brilliant degree he was about to take. Lord Runnymede would take him on embassy; the Duke would seat him in Parliament. He wrote this over and over to Helen, who was too eager to believe anything favorable about her son.

And all this pride and affection had been trampled by Pen's wicked extravagance and idleness! I do not envy Pen's feelings as he contemplated his actions. He had slept, and the tortoise had won the race. He had marred at the outset what might have been a brilliant career. He had dipped ungenerously into a generous mother's purse, basely and recklessly spilling her little cruse. Oh, it was a coward's hand that could strike and rob a creature so tender. If Pen felt the wrong he had done others, surely a vain young gentleman felt the shame brought upon himself even more keenly. There is no cruelty equal to remorse, no groan more piteous than that of wounded self-love. Like Joel Miller's friend, the Senior Wrangler, who bowed to the audience from his box merely because the King entered the theater at the same moment—a fatuity not entirely agreeable to himself—poor Arthur Pendennis was convinced that all England would remark the absence of his name from the examination lists and discuss his misfortune. His wounded tutor, his creditors, the skip and bed-maker who waited on him, the undergraduates he had patronized or scorned—how could he bear to look any of them in the face? He rushed to his rooms, locked himself inside, and penned a letter to his tutor full of thanks, regards, remorse, and despair, requesting his name be removed from the college books and intimating that death would soon end the woes of the disgraced Arthur Pendennis.

Then he slunk out, scarcely knowing where he went, mechanically taking the unfrequented lanes behind the colleges until he cleared the university precincts and reached the banks of the Cam, now deserted, once alive with boat races and cheering gownsmen. He wandered on until he found himself miles from Oxford, or rather, was found by acquaintances leaving the city.

As Pen climbed a hill, a drizzling January rain beating in his face and his ragged gown flying behind him—he had not divested himself of his academic garb since morning—a postchaise rattled up the road. A servant sat on the box, while within, half out of the window, sat a young gentleman smoking a cigar and loudly encouraging the postboy. It was our acquaintance from Baymouth, Mr. Spavin, who had earned his degree and was driving homeward in triumph in his yellow postchaise. He spotted the figure madly gesticulating as it worked up the hill and poor Pen's pale, ghastly face as the chaise whirled past.

"Whoa!" roared Mr. Spavin to the postboy. The horses stopped in their mad career, and the carriage pulled up some fifty yards before Pen. Presently, he heard his own name shouted and beheld the upper half of Mr. Spavin's body thrust out of the side window, beckoning Pen vehemently.

Pen stopped, hesitated, nodded his head fiercely, and pointed onwards, as if desiring the postillion to proceed. He said nothing, but his countenance looked so desperate that young Spavin, staring with blank alarm, jumped out, ran toward Pen, grasped his hand, and said, "I say—hullo, old boy, where are you going, and what's the row now?"

"I'm going where I deserve to go," said Pen, with an imprecation.

"This ain't the way," said Mr. Spavin, smiling. "This is the Fenbury road. I say, Pen, don't take on because you are plucked. It's nothing when you are used to it. I've been plucked three times, old boy—and after the first time I didn't care. Glad it's over, though. You'll have better luck next time."

Pen looked at his early acquaintance—who had been plucked, rusticated, and who, despite repeated failures, had only recently learned to read and write correctly, yet attained the honor of a degree. "This man has passed," he thought, "and I have failed!" It was almost too much to bear.

"Good-bye, Spavin," said he. "I'm very glad you are through. Don't let me keep you; I'm in a hurry—I'm going to town tonight."

"Gammon," said Mr. Spavin. "This ain't the way to town; this is the Fenbury road, I tell you."

"I was just going to turn back," Pen said.

"All the coaches are full with the men going down," Spavin said. Pen winced. "You'd not get a place for a ten-pound note. Get into my yellow; I'll drop you at Mudford, where you have a chance of the Fenbury mail. I'll lend you a hat and a coat; I've got lots. Come along; jump in, old boy—go it, leathers!" Thus, Pen found himself in Mr. Spavin's postchaise, riding with that gentleman as far as the Ram Inn at Mudford, fifteen miles from Oxford, where the mail changed horses and Pen secured a place to London.

The next day, immense excitement reigned in Boniface College, Oxford. For some time, a rumor had terrified Pen's tutor and tradesmen that Pendennis, maddened by losing his degree, had made away with himself. A battered cap, in which his name was almost discernible, together with a seal bearing his crest of an eagle looking at a now-extinct sun, had been found three miles on the Fenbury road near a mill-stream. For twenty-four hours, it was believed that poor Pen had thrown himself into the stream, until letters arrived from him bearing the London postmark.

The mail reached London at the dreary hour of five. He hastened to the inn at Covent Garden where he usually stayed. The ever-wakeful porter admitted him and showed him to a bed. Pen looked hard at the man, wondering whether Boots knew he was plucked. When in bed, he could not sleep. He tossed until the dismal London daylight appeared, then sprang up desperately and walked off to his uncle's lodgings in Bury Street. The maid, scouring the steps, looked up suspiciously at him as he came with an unshaven face and yesterday's linen. She seemed to know of his mishap, too.

"Good 'evens! Mr. Harthur, what as 'appened, sir?" asked Mr. Morgan, the valet, who had just arranged the well-brushed clothes and shiny boots at the door of his master's bedroom and was carrying in his wig for the Major.

"I want to see my uncle," he cried in a ghastly voice, flinging himself down on a chair.

Morgan backed away from the pale, desperate-looking young man with terrified, wondering glances and disappeared into the master's apartment.

The Major put his head out of the bedroom door as soon as he had his wig on. "What? examination over? Senior Wrangler, double First Class, hay?" said the old gentleman. "I'll come directly;" and the head disappeared.

"They don't know what has happened," groaned Pen. "What will they say when they know all?"

Pen stood with his back to the window, bathed in the dubious light of a foggy January morning in Bury Street, so that his uncle could not see the expression of the young man's countenance or the looks of gloom and despair that even Mr. Morgan had remarked.

But when the Major came out of his dressing-room neat and radiant, preceded by faint odors from Delcroix's shop (from which emporium the Major's wig and pocket-handkerchief derived their perfume), he held out one of his hands to Pen and was about addressing him in his cheery, high-toned voice when he caught sight of the boy's face. Dropping his hand, he said, "Good God! Pen, what's the matter?"

"You'll see it in the papers at breakfast, sir," Pen said.

"See what?"

"My name isn't there, sir."

"Hang it, why should it be?" asked the Major, more perplexed.

"I have lost everything, sir," Pen groaned out. "My honour's gone; I'm ruined irretrievably; I can't go back to Oxbridge."

"Lost your honour?" screamed the Major. "Heaven alive! you don't mean to say you have shown the white feather?"

Pen laughed bitterly at the word "feather" and repeated it. "No, it isn't that, sir. I'm not afraid of being shot; I wish to God anybody would. I have not got my degree. I—I'm plucked, sir."

The Major had heard of plucking but in a very vague, cursory way, concluding it was some ceremony performed corporally upon rebellious university youth. "I wonder you can look me in the face after such a disgrace, sir," he said. "I wonder you submitted to it as a gentleman."

"I couldn't help it, sir. I did my classical papers well enough: it was those infernal mathematics, which I have always neglected."

"Was it—was it done in public, sir?" asked the Major.

"What?"

"The—the plucking?" asked the guardian, looking Pen anxiously in the face.

Pen perceived the error under which his guardian labored, and in the midst of his misery, the blunder caused the poor wretch a faint smile, serving to bring the conversation down from the tragedy key in which Pen had intended to carry it. He explained that he had gone in to pass his examination and failed. On this, the Major said that though he had expected far better things of his nephew, there was no great misfortune in this, no dishonor as far as he saw, and that Pen must try again.

"Me again at Oxbridge," Pen thought, "after such a humiliation as that!" He felt that, unless he went down to burn the place, he could not enter it.

But it was when he came to tell his uncle of his debts that the other felt surprise and anger most keenly, breaking out in severe speeches which the lad bore, as best he might, without flinching. He had determined to make a clean breast of it, forming a full, true, and complete list of all his bills and liabilities at the university and in London. They consisted of various items:

*London Tailor, Haberdasher (for shirts and gloves), College Cook, Binding, Bootmaker, Hotel bill in London, Wine Merchant in London, Sundries.*

All which items the reader may fill in at his pleasure—such accounts have been inspected by the parents of many university youths—and it appeared that Mr. Pen's bills in all amounted to about seven hundred pounds; furthermore, it was calculated that he had had more than twice that sum of ready money during his stay at Oxford. This sum he had spent, and for it had to show—what?

"You need not press a man who is down, sir," Pen said to his uncle, gloomily. "I know very well,

The letter from the Major arrived with the speed of a thunderclap, sending Doctor Portman sprinting toward Fairoaks with the frantic energy of a dentist rushing to extract a molar before it rots the jaw. He treated the news as a necessary surgery: the tooth must be pulled, and quickly. While he might hesitate if it were his own tooth, he knew his duty was to apply the forceps with vigor. After reading the epistle aloud to Myra and Mrs. Portman, showering the young scapegrace with damning commentary on his descent into perdition, the Doctor departed. The ladies, armed with their usual efficiency, spread the tidings through Clavering society before striding over to deliver the blow to the widow.

To their surprise, Helen had already received the news. Having read Pen's letter herself, a heavy cloud of dread that had hung over her for months had suddenly lifted. She knew the worst, yet her darling boy had returned, repentant and tender-hearted. Was there more to fear? The Rector's sensible, ancient warnings failed to ignite indignation in her; she felt only sorrow that her son should be so unhappy. Why did they insist on sending him to a place teeming with temptation and devoid of virtue? Why not keep him at home? As for the debts, she reasoned they were merely his father's money, and surely he had a right to spend it. Thus, the widow met the virtuous Doctor's arrows with a gentle bosom that refused to bruise.

Meanwhile, the dynamic between Pen and his sister Laura had shifted dramatically. The childish ritual of greeting each other with a kiss, once a frequent delight, had ceased. Upon returning from college, Pen found not the simple girl he remembered, but a tall, slim, handsome young woman of sixteen. She received him with a gracious curtsey and a blush that climbed to her cheeks—the very spot he had once saluted.

Pen, a man who claimed to value virtue over fleeting charms, offered no detailed description of Miss Laura Bell. Yet, at five feet four inches, she was tall enough to be called a "Maypole" by those who preferred smaller women, though she wore beautiful roses around her head that attracted many suitors. She was pale with a faint rose tint, flushing vividly when emotions struck, leaving a lingering bloom. Her eyes, large since childhood, shone with an intensity that defied control, framed by lashes so long and dark they seemed designed to soften their brilliance. Her complexion rivaled Lady Mantrap's without the powder, her smile revealed pearly teeth, and her voice was a low, sweet music. While some mocked her long dresses and implied her feet were large, she was, in essence, a sweet young lady of goodness and modesty.

However, Laura's admiration had curdled into disappointment. For two years, her mind and person had matured alongside her resentment of Pen's conduct. His letters to their mother had grown sparse and brief. "It is better he loses a prize than forget his mother," Laura argued, urging Helen to stop accepting Pen's excuses about college engagements and fine houses. She insisted he should stay home, where he would be loved more than anywhere else. Helen, clinging to maternal infatuation, tried to believe Pen's excuses about cultivating useful acquaintances and the complexities of male life, though she suspected Laura shared her skepticism.

Laura was further shocked by Pen's newfound freedom and audacity. He spoke lightly of women, neglected the little offices of courtesy, and smoked his horrid pipes indoors. He refused to accompany his mother to church or walks, often found yawning over novels in his dressing-gown while she returned from her duties. The hero of her infancy, the brave boy she had praised to Helen for years, had transformed into a bold, sarcastic young man who scorned the simple devotions of the women around him.

The Fotheringay affair sealed her outrage. Hearing of a Pendennis throwing himself at an actress, drinking with her father, and bringing such a woman into their home, Laura was furious. "I would have run away, mamma, if I had to walk barefoot through the snow!" she declared. Helen's response—that Laura would have left her too—prompted an immediate embrace, dissolving the anger into a warm, feminine solidarity. Perhaps Laura's indignation stemmed from jealousy, or perhaps she was misled by the bitter gossip of the Clavering set and the Doctor, who had been insulted by Pen's cigar-smoking in church. Regardless, her wrath against Pen did not extend to his mother. Instead, she devoted herself to Helen with a passion bordering on folly, showering her with caresses and endearments.

Yet, the moment Laura learned of Pen's true misery, her anger evaporated, replaced by unreasonable compassion. He was the old Pen again: frank, generous, and tender-hearted. She sided immediately with Helen against the Doctor's outrage. Debts? A trifle! He had been forced into expensive society by his uncle. Disgraced for failing his degree? He was ill, distracted by debt, perhaps even victimized by jealous tutors. With flushed cheeks and eyes bright with righteous anger, Laura reasoned that others were cruel and unfair. She seized Helen's hand, kissed her in the Doctor's presence, and dared him to speak against her darling mother's son.

Discomfited, the Doctor departed. Laura then redoubled her embraces and arguments with Helen, convincing her that Pen must have offended examiners who took mean revenge. The announcement of the misfortune vexed the ladies very little; indeed, calamity is often welcome to women if it promises the return of truant affection. If a mistress is reduced to a crust, she will not repine, provided her lover eats the remainder in her company.

Directly the Doctor left, Laura ordered fires lit in Pen's rooms and his bedding aired. By the time Helen finished a tender letter to Pen, the preparations were complete. Laura added a postscript calling him her "dearest Pen," urging him to come home instantly to happiness with his mother and affectionate sister.

That night, after reading their Bibles and glancing into Pen's room, the sisters lay awake. Laura, who often occupied the pillow Pen had once pressed, suddenly whispered, "Mamma, are you awake?" Helen stirred, though she had been staring at the night-lamp, thinking of Pen for hours. Laura, equally awake and plotting, proposed a notable plan. She had lived with them for ten years without spending a penny, effectively owing Helen fifty times ten pounds. "Tomorrow we will go to Chatteris," she suggested, "ask Mr. Rowdy for the five hundred pounds, and he will likely send two more. We will save and repay them, send the money to Pen to clear his debts, and live happily ever after."

Helen responded with incoherent ejaculations and embraces. They slept well. When the sun rose over the purple hills and birds sang among the leafless trees, Helen woke to find Laura sleeping beside her. The sight of the girl's sweet face, the gentle rise and fall of her bosom, filled Helen with gratitude beyond words.

In London, Pen's remorse was so profound that he refused an inside seat on the coach, sitting behind with the Guard, who lent him great-coats against the January chill. His knees trembled as he descended at the lodge gate, whether from the cold or the agitation of approaching the woman he had wronged. Old John, now a garner and stable man in a fustian jacket rather than livery, grinned with welcome. But as Pen turned the corner of the shrubbery, Helen appeared, her face beaming with love and forgiveness—a quality some women cherish above all else.

Helen had already written to Pen detailing Laura's noble, magnanimous offer, filling the letter with benedictions. This knowledge caused Pen to blush deeply when he finally saw Laura in the hall. For this one occasion, she broke their usual arrangement of non-physical greetings, though the narrator notes that too much has been said about kissing in this chapter.

Thus, the prodigal returned, the fatted calf was killed, and he was made as happy as two simple women could make him. No mention was made of the Oxbridge mishap or his future plans for some time. But Pen debated anxiously in his own mind. A few days later, he rode to Chatteris, returned on the coach, and handed his mother a cheque for the sale of his horse—an act of virtue he and Laura deemed strict justice. Though he rarely mentioned the loan, accepting it with hesitation, he was wild to repay the orphan.

He quit wine for whisky-and-water, gave up cigars for pipes, and fell into a moody melancholy. He watched coaches, read papers, dined with anyone who would ask him, and played cribbage with Captain Glanders. He avoided the Doctor, who glared severely from under his shovel-hat. He attended church regularly and read prayers at home, though the household had shrunk to a couple of maids, the silver dish-covers gathering dust, and the kitchen fire burning low. John donned his livery only for Sunday's formality. The gloom deepened.

For a while, Pen refused to return to Oxford, but Laura, blushing, urged him to get his degree as reparation for his idleness. Reluctantly, he went back. A "plucked" man is a dismal creature in a university, belonging to no set and owned by no one. Pen felt stripped of his fine feathers, rarely leaving his rooms except for morning chapel. The duns were gone, the cards stopped coming, and his yearmates had graduated. He passed his second examination with ease and felt slightly more comfortable in his bachelor's gown.

On his way back, he visited his uncle in London, only to be met with cold looks and a refusal of a handshake. Returning to Fairoaks, he threw himself into books, idleness, loneliness, and despair. He wrote gloomy tragedies and verses, formed plans to enlist or change professions, and only broke them. He chafed against his captivity, cursing the idleness that caused it. Helen saw him breaking his heart, predicting that soon they would afford to send him abroad or to London to escape their dull society. The widow's habitual melancholy deepened into a sadder gloom, and Laura watched with alarm as her dear friend grew languid, weary, and wan.

While the inmates of Fairoaks drifted through their daily routine in a state of pleasant lethargy, the great house atop the hill across the River Brawl was undergoing a dramatic transformation. It was shaking off a century-and-a-half of slumber, waking up with a vigor that seemed entirely new to its bones.

Just as Arthur Pendennis was consumed by the grief of his recent mishap, oblivious to the world around him, a rumor swept through the county like wildfire. It started in the provincial journals and quickly spread to every market, fair, church gate, and village green within miles of Clavering Park. The word was simple yet explosive: Clavering Park was to be inhabited again.

Five years prior, the county papers had announced the marriage at Florence of Francis Clavering, the only son of Sir Francis Clavering, to Jemima Augusta, daughter of Samuel Snell of Calcutta. The local legend was that the ruined baronet had finally secured a widow from India with a substantial fortune. Indeed, the newlyweds were spotted in Italy, occupying the Poggi Palace and throwing lavish parties, though they never returned to England. They were later seen in Germany and France, rumors of them drifting back to the ancestral home like gusts of wind. Their final known residence was Paris, where, following the death of her father, Lady Clavering lived in great style.

The backstory of Sir Francis Clavering was, to put it mildly, scandalous. The son of an outlaw living in a chateau near Bruges, he had attempted a military career, failed miserably, gambled away his remaining funds, spent time in the Fleet prison, and eventually joined his gout-ridden father in exile. For years, he had been a shadow in the billiard rooms and watering holes of Europe, a decayed prodigal riding steeple-chases on others' horses.

However, his luck turned at a boarding house in Lausanne. There, he executed what he called a "lucky coup" by marrying the recently widowed Miss Amory. Her father, Mr. Snell, delighted in the title of "Lady Clavering," doubling her allowance before dying and leaving her a fortune rumored to be magnificent. Yet, whispers of her questionable origins and dubious past followed her. She was the daughter of an indigo smuggler and the widow of a ship's mate, neither of whom had ever been respectable figures in Calcutta society.

Despite this, once she moved to Paris and established herself at the Hotel Bouilli with an income of ten thousand a year, her reputation underwent a magical alchemy. The Faubourg St. Germain embraced her. Even the most austere English ladies of the capital, renowned for their moral purity, found themselves drawn to her salons. Her munificence was boundless; she funded schools, missions to exotic islands, and even contradictory causes like the persecuted Jesuits and the conversion of the Pope, all on the same day. It was during this Parisian heyday that she seemingly settled her husband's English debts, allowing him to return home without fear of arrest.

One day, the housekeeper at Clavering House watched in astonishment as a carriage drove up the moss-grown avenue. Inside sat three gentlemen: Mr. Tatham of Chatteris, a small lawyer, and a portly, mustachioed man in fur collars and braiding, accompanied by a pale, languid figure.

They marched up the steps to the hall, where Mrs. Blenkinsop, her aide-de-camp Polly, and the keeper Smart appeared to greet them. Smart, kicking out his heel and bowing deeply, introduced the grand gentleman in fur as the rightful lord. But the man burst into laughter.

"You've saddled the wrong horse, old lady!" he declared, pointing to the pale man beside him. "I'm not Sir Francis Clavering. Friends and vassals! Behold your rightful lord!"

"Don't be an ass, Ned," the pale man corrected gently.

"Yes, Mrs. Blenkinsop, I am Sir Francis Clavering," the Baronet said, taking the trembling housekeeper's hand with a vacantly kind nod. "Forgot me, I suppose?"

Mrs. Blenkinsop insisted she would have recognized him anywhere, noting his resemblance to his ancestors. Sir Francis, however, dismissed the gloom of the hall with a shrug. "Dismal old place, ain't it, Ned? Never saw it but once."

"Dismal? Beautiful! The Castle of Otranto!" Ned Strong exclaimed, twirling his mustaches. "What a fireplace! You might roast an elephant in it!"

As the housekeeper detailed the history of the portraits lining the walls—from the Sheriff of the county to the wife of the second baronet depicted as Venus—Sir Francis cut her off. "Thank you, needn't go on. We'll walk about the place ourselves."

He dismissed the housekeeper and the keeper, leaving them to their duties, and wandered the vast, echoing rooms with his friend, Captain Strong. While Sir Francis remained silent and resigned, treating the mansion with indifference, Captain Strong roamed with the enthusiasm of a master renovator. He envisioned theaters in the library, gallops in the drawing rooms, and furnished suites in vibrant colors.

"I see capabilities in it!" Strong cried. "Leave it to me, and I'll make it the pride of the country."

Sir Francis merely yawned. "Put my rooms in the north wing, and out of the reach of Miss Amory's confounded piano. I can't bear it. She's screeching from morning till night."

Strong laughed, settling the arrangements with cheerful ease. Later, in the steward's room, Strong revealed the true nature of their partnership. He had organized the marriage between his friend Frank and the widow Amory; she needed rank, he needed money. It was a pragmatic union, devoid of romance, yet perfectly suited to make both parties happy.

News of the Claverings' return sent shockwaves through the town of Chatteris. Furniture vans arrived from London, and Captain Strong, a man of immense charm and theatrical flair, became the de facto master of the house. With his broad chest, black whiskers, and endless stories of battles in Hungary, Greece, and Spain, he won the hearts of everyone from the landlady to the local farmers. He was a man of action, a soldier who had escaped from Missolonghi and survived fireships, now bringing life to the stagnant county.

The anticipation reached its peak when the Clavering family finally arrived. An open carriage and a family ark rolled through the gates, greeted by a retinue of footmen in powdered wigs and a foreign chef, Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant, who composed menus while playing the piano.

Inside the church, the new family took possession of the ancient pew. Lady Clavering, dressed in a riot of jewels and shawls, looked uncommonly fine, while her daughter, Miss Amory, appeared meek and dove-colored. Beside her walked Captain Strong, and holding her arm was a young woman named Blanche.

The scene was punctuated by the antics of Master Francis, a toddler who roared in the pew until a footman had to carry him out. Later, during lunch, the boy upset a glass of wine over Arthur Pendennis's pristine shirtfront, causing the older gentleman great annoyance.

"It is very wrong," Mrs. Pendennis chided.

"Mamma says she spoils my brother," Lady Clavering replied cheerfully. "Look at him, isn't he like a little angel?"

In the privacy of Miss Amory's room, however, a different kind of magic was unfolding. Here, Blanche Amory revealed her true self. She was a creature of exquisite beauty, with green reflections in her fair hair and dark eyebrows framing brown eyes. She spoke of suffering, of a life touched by sorrow, and shared her talents as a painter, musician, and poet. She showed Laura Pendennis her locked book, *Mes Larmes* (My Tears), filled with verses of profound sadness.

Laura, the honest country girl, was captivated. She forgot her own jealousy in the face of such accomplished fragility. Blanche, in turn, fell instantly in love with Laura, declaring it an electrical attraction.

Over the following weeks, the two families grew closer. Arthur Pendennis, usually a yawn-inducing recluse, found his spirits returning. He laughed and rattled in Lady Clavering's drawing room, joining in musical concerts with Blanche and Strong. He even took to fishing the River Brawl, where he would coincidentally meet Miss Amory walking among her flowers.

Blanche, a kind-hearted soul who understood suffering, became a constant companion to both Laura and Arthur. She read French and German with them, and Arthur, in turn, translated Schiller and Goethe for her. But beneath the surface of this idyllic summer, a darker current flowed. Arthur, reading Blanche's poems, discovered a depth of despair that seemed unnatural for a girl with such a snug home. He found her verses violent and passionate, filled with longing for death.

And then, there was the matter of the poetry itself. As Arthur delved deeper into Blanche's collection, he realized with a jolt of recognition that some of the verses attributed to her were not hers at all. They were adaptations of poems he had written years ago for a certain Emily Fotheringay. He had altered the names, changing "Emily" to "Blanche," and repurposed his own passionate, hot-sweet verses for a new muse.

Was the pretty little fish that played around his fly actually Blanche? Or was she merely a vessel for his own hidden feelings, a new face for an old ghost? As the summer deepened, the line between reality and illusion in Clavering Park began to blur, leaving Arthur to wonder if he was truly falling in love with the girl before him, or simply projecting his own past sorrows onto her tear-stained pages.

There is a saying that every house harbors a skeleton in its closet, and for the unfortunate, it is a solace to believe that even the wealthiest neighbors carry their own burdens. In Clavering House, that skeleton was a tiny, radiant fairy named Blanche Amory. To the outside world, she was a singing, sweet-talking muse who seemed to sprinkle sunshine wherever she tread. Inside, however, she was the Nemesis of the household, a walking disaster waiting to happen. Just as a single pebble in a shoe can ruin an entire journey, Blanche possessed the unique ability to turn a minor inconvenience into a lifetime of misery. Who could have imagined that such a smiling sprite could be the architect of domestic discord?

One afternoon, after dinner, the Baronet and Captain Strong sat by the billiard table, cigars clutched like weapons of mass destruction.

"I say, Strong," the Baronet boomed, "I wish to the doose your wife was dead."

"So do I," Strong replied, puffing smoke with perfect equanimity. "That's a cannon, by Jove. But she won't; she'll live forever—you see if she don't. Why do you wish her off the hooks, Frank?"

"Because then you might marry Missy," the Baronet drawled. "She ain't bad-looking. She'll have ten thousand pounds, and that's a good bit of money for a poor old devil like you."

"And gad, Strong, I hate her worse and worse every day. I can't stand her, by gad, I can't."

"I wouldn't take her at twice the price," Strong laughed. "I've never seen such a little devil in my life."

"I should like to poison her," the Baronet declared sententiously. "By Jove, I should."

"Why, what has she been at now?" his friend asked.

"Nothing particular," the Baronet answered with a grimace. "Only her old tricks. That girl has such a knack of making everyone miserable that it's quite surprising. Last night she sent the governess crying away from the dinner table. Afterwards, I heard the poor little beggar howling in the dark; his sister had been frightening his soul out of his body by telling him stories about the ghost in the house. At lunch, she gave my lady a turn. Though my wife is a fool, she's a good soul—I'm hanged if she ain't."

"What did Missy do to her?" Strong inquired.

"Why, hang me, if she didn't begin talking about the late Amory, my predecessor," the Baronet grinned. "She got a picture out of the *Keepsake* and said she was sure it looked like her dear father. She wanted to know where her father's grave was. Hang her father! Whenever Miss Amory talks about him, Lady Clavering bursts into tears. And the little devil talks about him just to spite her mother. Today, when she started, I flew into a rage and claimed to be her father. Then, sir, she took a shy at me."

"And what did she say about you, Frank?" Strong asked, still chuckling.

"Gad, she said I wasn't her father; that I wasn't fit to comprehend her; that her father must have been a man of genius and fine feelings, whereas I had married her mother for money."

"Well, didn't you?" Strong pressed.

"It don't make it any the pleasanter to hear because it's true, don't you know," Sir Francis Clavering answered. "I ain't a literary man, but I ain't such a fool as she makes me out. She always manages to put me in the hole. She turns the whole house around her with her quiet ways and confounded sentimental airs. I wish she was dead, Ned."

"It was my wife you wanted dead just now," Strong pointed out, maintaining his good humor. Upon this, the Baron, with his customary candor, admitted, "Well, when people bore my life out, I do wish they were dead, and I wish Missy were down a well, with all my heart."

From this candid exchange, it becomes clear that our accomplished little friend possessed peculiarities that rendered her unpopular. She was a young lady of genius, exquisite sympathies, and considerable literary attainments, living with relatives who could not comprehend her. Neither her mother nor her stepfather were literary souls; the Baronet's reading stopped at *Bell's Life* and the *Racing Calendar*, while Lady Clavering still wrote like a thirteen-year-old schoolgirl with a total disregard for grammar. Feeling keenly that she was not appreciated and lived among intellectual inferiors, Blanche lost no opportunity to announce their inferiority to her family circle. She was a martyr, but she ensured everyone knew it. If she suffered, as she claimed, severely, should we wonder that a creature of such delicate sensibilities should shriek and cry? Without sympathy, life is nothing; would it not be a lack of candor to pretend cheerfulness when one feels nothing but despair? If a poetess may not bemoan her lot, what use is her lyre? Blanche struck hers only to the saddest tunes, singing elegies over dead hopes and dirges over frost-nipped buds of affection, fitting for such a melancholy fate.

Her actual distresses had not been great, but her griefs lay in her own soul. Being sad and habitually dissatisfied, what wonder that she wept? Tears dribbled from her eyes at command; she could furnish an unlimited supply, and her faculty for shedding them increased with practice. Sentiment, like the dropsy mentioned by Horace, increases by self-indulgence. The more one cries, the more one desires to do so.

Missy had begun to gush at a very early age. Lamartine was her favorite bard from the moment she could feel, and she subsequently improved her mind by devouring the novels of Balzac and George Sand by the time she was sixteen. However little she sympathized with her relatives, she claimed friends in the spirit-world: the tender Indiana, the passionate Lelia, the amiable Trenmor, the high-souled convict, the fiery Stenio, and countless other heroes of French romance. She had been in love with Prince Rodolph and Prince Djalma while still at school, settling the divorce question and the rights of woman with Indiana before she had even taken off her pinafores. The impetuous little lady played at love with these imaginary worthies as she once played at maternity with her doll. It is curious to watch them with those playthings; today the blue-eyed one is the favorite, tomorrow the black-eyed one is pushed behind the drawers. One day a blue-eyed doll is neglected and replaced by an odious wretch with a burnt nose and torn hair, who takes the first place in Miss's affection.

Novelists, supposedly knowing the secrets of female hearts, reveal that at eleven, Mademoiselle Betsi (as Miss Amory was then called) felt tender emotions for a young Savoyard organ-grinder she believed to be a prince. At twelve, an old, hideous drawing-master agitated her young heart. At thirteen, while at Madame de Caramel's boarding school in the Champs Élysées, a correspondence took place between the seductive Miss Betsi and two young gentlemen of the College of Charlemagne.

In the above paragraph, our young friend is called by a Christian name different from that under which we were introduced. The fact is, Miss Amory, called Missy at home, was christened Betsy but assumed the name of Blanche of her own will and fantasy. The weapon the Baronet held over her was the threat to call her publicly "Betsy," a menace he sometimes used to keep the young rebel in order.

We have spoken of children's dolls and their inconstancy. Very likely, this history shows that Miss Blanche assumed and put away her live dolls with similar girlish fickleness. She had hosted hosts of dear friends, gathering a little museum of locks of hair in her treasure-chest. Some dear friends had married; some had gone to other schools; one beloved sister she had lost from the pension, only to find again, O horror! her darling Leocadie keeping the books in her father's shop, a grocer in the Rue du Bac. In fact, she had met with numerous disappointments, estrangements, and disillusionments, which she called "disillusions" in her pretty French jargon, and had seen and suffered greatly for so young a woman. But it is the lot of sensibility to suffer, and of confiding tenderness to be deceived. She felt she was only undergoing the penalties of genius in these pangs.

Meanwhile, she managed to make her honest mother as uncomfortable as circumstances permitted and caused her worthy stepfather to wish she were dead. With the exception of Captain Strong, whose invincible good-humor was proof against her sarcasms, the little lady ruled the whole house with her tongue. If Lady Clavering talked about Sparrowgrass instead of Asparagus, or called an object a "hobject," Missy calmly corrected her, frightening the good soul into errors that became more frequent as she grew more nervous under her daughter's eye.

It is not to be supposed that Madame Fribsby alone, of all the folks in Clavering, should have remained unmoved by the arrival of the Park family. At the first appearance of the Park family in church, Madame noted every article of toilette the ladies wore, from bonnets to brodequins, and surveyed the attire of the ladies' maids in the pew. We fear that Doctor Portman's sermon, though one of his oldest compositions, had little effect upon Madame Fribsby that day.

In a few days, she secured an interview with Lady Clavering's confidential attendant in the housekeeper's room. Her cards, in French and English, stating that she received the newest fashions from Paris and made court dresses for the nobility, were favorably received by Lady Clavering and Miss Amory.

Mrs. Bonner, Lady Clavering's lady, soon became a great frequenter of Madame Fribsby's drawing-room, partaking of meals of green tea, scandal, hot Sally-Lunn cakes, and novel reading. She found much more time for these pleasures than her junior officer, Miss Amory's maid, who was worked as hard as a factory-girl by that inexorable little Muse, her mistress.

The Muse loved to be dressed becomingly. Having a lively fancy and a poetic desire for change, she altered her attire every day. Her maid, Pincott, who had been an apprentice to dressmaking in Paris, was kept from morning till night altering and remodelling Miss Amory's habiliments. Pincott rose very early and went to bed very late, obeying the untiring caprices of her little taskmistress. The girl was of respectable English parents, colonists of Paris who had seen better days but could not get on without charity. Her father was a cripple incapable of work, and her return home would only increase the burden on her family. Poor Pincott was fain to stay where she could maintain herself and spare a little relief to her parents.

Our Muse, with the candour which distinguished her, never failed to remind her attendant of the real state of matters. "I should send you away, Pincott, for you are a great deal too weak, and your eyes are failing you, and you are always crying and snivelling and wanting the doctor; but I wish that your parents at home should be supported, and I go on enduring you for their sake, mind," the dear Blanche would say to her timid little attendant. Or, "Pincott, your wretched appearance and slavish manner, and red eyes, positively give me the migraine; and I think I shall make you wear rouge, so that you may look a little cheerful;" or, "Pincott, I can't bear, even for the sake of your starving parents, that you should tear my hair out of my head in that manner; and I will thank you to write to them and say that I dispense with your services." After such speeches, and after keeping her trembling over her hair for an hour while the young lady combed it while perusing a favorite French novel, she would go to bed at one o'clock and say, "Pincott, you may kiss me. Good night. I should like you to have the pink dress ready for the morning." And so, with blessing upon her attendant, she would turn round and go to sleep.

The Muse might lie in bed as long as she chose in the morning, but Pincott had to rise very early indeed to get her mistress's task done. She had to appear next day with the same red eyes and wan face, which displeased Miss Amory by their want of gaiety and caused the mistress to be angry because the servant persisted in being and looking unwell. Not that Blanche ever thought she was a hard mistress. Indeed, she made quite a friend of Pincott at times, writing very pretty verses about the lonely little tiring-maid whose heart was far away. Our beloved Blanche was a superior being, expecting to be waited upon as such. I do not know whether there are any other ladies in this world who treat their servants so, but it may be that there are such, and that the tyranny they exercise over their subordinates, and the pangs they inflict with a soft voice and a well-bred simper, are as cruel as those a slave-driver administers with an oath and a whip.

But Blanche was a Muse—a delicate little creature, quite tremulous with excitability, whose eyes filled with tears at the smallest emotion. Who knows, but that it was the very fineness of her feelings which caused them to be froissed so easily? You crush a butterfly by merely touching it. Vulgar people have no idea of the sensibility of a Muse.

So little Pincott, occupied all day and night in stitching, hemming, ripping, combing, ironing, and crimping for her mistress, reading to her when in bed—for the girl was mistress of both languages and had a sweet voice and manner—could take no share in Madame Fribsby's soirées, nor indeed was she much missed or considered of sufficient consequence to appear at their entertainments.

But there was another person connected with the Clavering establishment

If you've ever wondered what happens when a woman marries a man who can't keep his temper, loses his fortune, and then dies leaving her with a toddler and a reputation for "eccentricity," look no further than Lady Clavering. Sir Francis Clavering, a man of sharp opinions, once noted that his wife wasn't exactly the brightest star in the sky. He was right. Lady Clavering's education consisted of two years in a London suburb she stubbornly insisted was called "Ackney" until her dying day, followed by a perilous voyage on the *Ramchunder* to Calcutta. On that ship, she met her first husband, Mr. Amory, a third mate who was promptly thrown in irons by the captain for being a "rebellious rascal."

Mr. Amory's life was a spectacular train wreck. He escaped to Calcutta, married a rich attorney's daughter, tried to be an indigo planter (failed), an agent (failed), and an editor of the *Sunderbund Pilot* (failed). He quarreled with everyone, including his own father-in-law, and eventually crashed so hard he fled to New South Wales. Somewhere amidst this chaos, he likely met Sir Jasper Rogers, the judge, and used his father-in-law's name as a fountain pen to sign checks he couldn't afford. When the truth finally caught up with him, Mr. Amory was dead, and his widow, now Mrs. Amory, was left with a four-year-old daughter named Blanche and a nurse named Mrs. Bonner.

They returned to Europe, escaping the gloomy surveillance of Captain Bragg, who had taken a house near them in Pocklington Street just to torment them. It was a bitter summer of rain, and Bragg was so pompous and disagreeable that Mrs. Amory decided London was too small for her. She packed her bags, calling Hackney "Ackney" and speaking with a charming foreign twang, and set off on a grand tour of the continent. She was a whirlwind of champagne, curry, and generosity, paying boarders in advance and feeding the poor. She was popular, jovial, and utterly fearless.

News of her husband's death reached her in Rome, where she met Captain Clavering, who was currently unable to pay his hotel bill. Being a woman of immense practicality and zero grief for her late husband's misfortunes, she married him. Thus, the mistress of Clavering Park was born, a woman who, despite her lack of refinement, possessed a purse heavy enough to buy the entire continent if she wished.

Her daughter, Blanche, followed in her mother's footsteps, learning life's lessons in Brussels, Paris, and Milan. When the family finally settled in England, Blanche was a young lady of considerable means but questionable social standing. She realized too late that she was no longer the heiress; she was now the stepdaughter of a dull baronet and the sister of a spoiled, quarrelsome boy named Francis. Her mother dropped her H's, her stepfather smoked odious cigars, and her brother constantly tripped her and spilled his dinner on her dresses. Feeling misunderstood and isolated, Blanche's heart began to wander, seeking affection where it couldn't find it at home.

Enter Arthur Pendennis, a young man of talent, charm, and significant self-doubt. Blanche was fascinated by him, and he was fascinated by her, though his mother, Helen Pendennis, saw right through the glitter. Helen, a woman of deep piety and maternal anxiety, viewed Blanche as light-minded, frivolous, and lacking reverence. She feared her son was being led astray by a "Muse" who was actually just a very pretty, very selfish girl.

The tension between the two families simmered until it boiled over. Blanche, feeling suffocated by her family's inability to comprehend her, began to act out. She boxed little Frank's ears for spilling water, commanded servants with imperial arrogance, and treated her mother with insolence. Laura Pendennis, Blanche's closest friend, watched in horror. Laura, a creature of genuine kindness and humility, could not reconcile the beautiful, poetic verses Blanche recited about eternal sisterly love with the reality of her sister slapping her brother and insulting her governess.

The breaking point came on a particularly wicked day. Blanche, in a fit of petulant rage, drove her attendant, Pincott, from the room in tears, mocking her as she left. Laura, seeing this cruelty firsthand, exploded. She lectured Blanche on her wickedness, declared that she owed an apology to heaven, and then, in a flurry of indignation, ran home across the park, leaving Blanche behind.

Later, alone with her mother, Laura confessed her disillusionment. "You were right, Mamma," she said, clutching her mother's hand. "Blanche is selfish and cruel. She has no heart. I give her up." Helen felt a surge of secret relief; her son was safe from a flighty match.

However, Laura kept one secret from her mother. Before the final blow, Blanche had cornered Laura in her room, feigning innocence about her past with the actress. "He was false to her," Blanche claimed, wagging her head. Laura, defending her friend's memory, insisted Pen had been generous. Blanche, sensing Laura's hesitation, produced a small, crumpled packet of love verses from a mother-of-pearl desk—poems about Undines and Naiads that Pen had written for her. She showed them to Laura, who stared at the stained paper, confused by the sudden intimacy. Blanche then sang a few Rossini songs, her voice flexible and perfect, while Laura sat silently, her heart heavy with the realization that the charming fairy she admired was merely a mortal girl playing at romance.

As they walked arm-in-arm to lunch, Laura paused at the door, looked at Blanche with a mixture of pity and resolve, and gave her a warm, sisterly kiss. It was a goodbye in disguise. Something trivial—perhaps Frank's eating habits or Mrs. Clavering's dropped H—would soon trigger another tantrum, sealing the fate of their friendship. But for now, the battle was won by the one who knew the difference between poetry and the messy, painful reality of human nature.

The war between the sisters was short-lived. Laura, ever the peacemaker, forgave too easily, while Miss Blanche, whose grudges were as fleeting as summer frost, had never truly been offended by the scene. Accusations of wickedness rarely sting the vanity of a woman like Blanche; indeed, she found Laura's indignation amusing, a performance staged for a cause both knew but neither dared name. Thus, Laura sighed, admitting that the romantic chapter of their friendship had closed, leaving her with only a mundane regard for her cousin.

Blanche, however, was quick to compose verses lamenting her "desertion" and "disenchantment." It was a recycled tragedy of cold love and neglected fidelity. Soon, new neighbors arrived from London, and Blanche found a fresh confidante in one of their daughters, pouring out her sorrows to this new sister. The tall footmen ceased bringing notes to Laura, and the pony-carriage rarely visited Fairoaks. Blanche adopted a look of sweet martyrdom whenever Laura dropped by, while the others laughed at her sentimentality with a good humor that lacked respect.

Yet, Blanche's new female friends were merely the tip of the iceberg; she discovered male acquaintances who offered similar consolation. Any young man she encountered in a garden walk or drawing-room window became the recipient of her "beautiful eyes" and a tone of tender, simple appeal. She would perform this little drama of heartbreak for one, then move on to the next.

When the Claverings first arrived at the Park, Blanche had few audiences for her glances, meaning Pen enjoyed the monopoly of her attention. In the town of Clavering itself, there were virtually no young men, only a curate or two and rustic squires with large feet and ill-fitting clothes. The Baronet made no advances toward the dragoons at Chatteris; unfortunately, it was his own regiment, and his departure had been marked by a messy dispute over a horse bargain, a disputed play account, and a white feather incident. Who needs to ask? It is not our business to dig too deep into the past unless it serves the present story.

However, the autumn season, the end of Parliament, and the London social circuit brought county families down to their houses, filling the watering-place of Baymouth and opening Mr. Bingley's Theatre Royal at Chatteris. For the first time, the old county families—the Fogys of Drummington, the Squares of Tozely Park, the Welbores of The Barrow—ceased their gossip about the "new neighbors." Stories circulated endlessly about Sir Francis, his Lady, Miss Amory, and Captain Strong. The family of Clavering Park had been in the county for three months before the great folk finally took notice.

The turning point came when the Earl of Trehawk, Lord Lieutenant of the County, visited Eyrie Castle, and the Countess Dowager of Rockminster occupied a mansion on the Marine Parade at Baymouth. These magnates came publicly and in state to call upon the Claverings, and the carriages of the county families swiftly followed in their wake.

It was then that Mirobolant found his art useful, distracting him from the pangs of love. The footmen were too busy to ferry messages between the Park and Fairoaks. Blanche found new friends and new walks, leaving Pen to fish alone by the river. He came day after day, whipping the stream, but the fish wouldn't bite, nor did the elusive "Peri" appear. Here, in strict confidence, we must mention a delicate matter hinted at before: a certain hollow tree where Pen once stationed himself for his passion for Miss Fotheringay. He later repurposed this cavity into a post-office. Under a piece of moss and a stone, he placed little poems or letters addressed to an "Undine" or "Naiad" who frequented the stream. Occasionally, these were replaced by a receipt in the shape of a flower or a modest word or two in French or English on pink scented paper. Miss Amory, we know, walked by this stream and used pink paper. But after the great folks invaded Clavering Park, evening after evening, nobody came to fetch Pen's letters. The white paper remained undisturbed under its stone and moss while the Brawl rolled by. The letters contained little—merely half-jocular, half-sympathetic words suitable for any young lady. Oh, silly Pendennis, if you wanted this one, why didn't you speak? Perhaps neither party was in earnest; you were merely playing at being in love, and the sportive Undine was humming along.

When a man is baulked in this game, he often loses his temper. When nobody came for Pen's poems, he took them seriously again, feeling tragic and romantic once more. He demanded an explanation. One day he found the Hall full of visitors; another, Miss Amory was asleep after a ball; a third, she was walking in state with the Bishop and Bishopess of Chatteris, who scowled at him and drew up in great dignity when his name was presented. The Right Reverend Prelate knew of the little transaction in the Dean's garden.

"The Bishop says you're a sad young man," good-natured Lady Clavering whispered. "What have you been doing? Nothing to vex such a dear Mar as yours? How is your dear Mar? Why doesn't she come and see us? We aren't seeing any neighbors now. Give my love to her and Laura, and come to dinner tomorrow."

Mrs. Pendennis was too unwell to come, but Laura and Pen attended. At the great party, Pen managed only a hurried word with Miss Amory. "You never come to the river now," he said.

"I can't," Blanche replied. "The house is full of people."

"Undine has left the stream," Pen continued, choosing to be poetic.

"She never ought to have gone there," Miss Amory answered. "She won't go again. It was very foolish, very wrong. It was only play. Besides, you have other consolations at home," she added, looking him full in the face for an instant before dropping her eyes.

If he wanted her, why didn't he speak then? She might have said "Yes." But as she mentioned other consolations at home, he thought of Laura—so affectionate, so pure—and his mother, who had bent her fond heart on uniting him with her adopted daughter.

"Blanche!" he began in a vexed tone. "Miss Amory!"

"Laura is looking at us, Mr. Pendennis," the young lady said. "I must go back to the company," and she ran off, leaving Mr. Pendennis to bite his nails in perplexity and gaze out into the moonlit garden.

Laura was indeed looking at Pen. She was conversing with Mr. Pynsent, Lord Rockminster's son and grandson of the Dowager Lady, who sat in state receiving Lady Clavering's bad grammar and patronizing the vacuous Sir Francis, whose interest in the county she sought to secure. Pynsent and Pen had met at Oxford, where the latter, in his heyday of fashion, had been the superior of the young patrician. They exchanged that exceedingly impertinent and amusing demi-nod of recognition practiced only by University men—a gesture saying, "Confound you, what do you do here?"

"I knew that man at Oxford," Mr. Pynsent said to Miss Bell. "A Mr. Pendennis, I think."

"Yes," said Miss Bell.

"He seems rather sweet upon Miss Amory," the gentleman continued. Laura looked at them, perhaps thinking so too, but said nothing.

"A man of large property in the county, isn't he? He used to talk about representing it. He used to speak at the Union. Whereabouts do his estates lie?"

Laura smiled. "His estates lie on the other side of the river, near the lodge-gate. He is my cousin, and I live there."

"Where?" asked Mr. Pynsent, laughing.

"Why, on the other side of the river, at Fairoaks," answered Miss Bell.

"Many pheasants there? Cover looks rather good," said the simple gentleman.

Laura smiled again. "We have nine hens and a cock, a pig, and an old pointer."

"Pendennis doesn't preserve, then?" continued Mr. Pynsent.

"You should come and see him," the girl said, laughing, greatly amused by the notion that her Pen was a great county gentleman.

"Indeed, I quite long to renew our acquaintance," Mr. Pynsent said gallantly, with a look that fairly said, "It is you that I would like to come and see." To which Miss Laura vouchsafed a smile and a little bow.

Here Blanche stepped up with her most fascinating smile and ogle, begging Laura to sing a duet. Laura, ready to please, went to the piano. Mr. Pynsent listened until Miss Amory began for herself, then strode away.

"What a nice, frank, amiable, well-bred girl that is, Wagg," Mr. Pynsent said to a gentleman from Baymouth—"the tall one, I mean, with the ringlets and red lips—monstrous red, ain't they?"

"What do you think of the girl of the house?" asked Wagg.

"I think she's a lean, scraggy humbug," said Mr. Pynsent with great candor. "She drags her shoulders out of her dress, she never lets her eyes alone, and she goes simpering and ogling about like a French waiting-maid."

"Pynsent, be civil," cried the other, "somebody can hear."

"Oh, it's Pendennis of Boniface," Mr. Pynsent said. "Fine evening, Mr. Pendennis; we were just talking of your charming cousin."

"Any relation to my old friend, Major Pendennis?" asked Mr. Wagg.

"His nephew. Had the pleasure of meeting you at Gaunt House," Mr. Pen said with his very best air. The acquaintance between the gentlemen was made instantly.

The next afternoon, the two gentlemen stayed at Clavering Park were found by Pen on his return from a fruitless fishing excursion, seated in his mother's drawing-room in comfortable conversation with the widow and her ward. Mr. Pynsent, tall and gaunt with large red whiskers, was striding over a chair in the intimate neighborhood of Miss Laura. She was amused by his talk, which was simple, straightforward, humorous, and interspersed with homely expressions of a style sometimes called slang. It was the first specimen of a young London dandy Laura had seen.

Mr. Wagg, entering the Fairoaks premises with his companion, eyed everything. "Old gardener," he said, seeing Mr. John at the lodge—"old red livery waistcoat—clothes hanging out to dry on the gooseberry-bushes—blue aprons, white ducks—gad, they must be young Pendennis's white ducks—nobody else wears 'em in the family. Rather a shy place for a sucking county member, ay, Pynsent?"

"Snug little crib," said Mr. Pynsent, "pretty cosy little lawn."

"Mr. Pendennis at home, old gentleman?" Mr. Wagg asked the domestic. John answered, "No, Master Pendennis was agone out."

"Are the ladies at home?" asked the younger visitor. Mr. John answered, "Yes, they be." As the pair walked over the trim gravel and neat shrubberies to the hall-door, Mr. Wagg noted everything: the barometer, the letter-bag, the umbrellas, the ladies' clogs, Pen's hats and tartan wrapper. Such minutiae attracted Wagg instinctively.

"Old fellow does all the work," he whispered to Pynsent. "Caleb Balderstone. Shouldn't wonder if he's the housemaid." The next minute they were in the presence of the Fairoaks ladies, whom Pynsent recognized as perfectly well-bred. Mr. Wagg made his obeisance with florid bows and an occasional knowing leer. Mr. Pynsent ignored these signals except by extreme haughtiness toward Wagg and particular deference to the ladies. If there was one thing laughable in Wagg's eyes, it was poverty. He had the soul of a butler brought from the pantry to make fun in the drawing-room. His jokes were plentiful, his good nature genuine, but he didn't understand that a gentleman could wear an old coat or that a lady could be respectable without a carriage or a French milliner.

"Charming place, ma'am," said he, bowing to the widow. "Noble prospect—delightful to us Cocknies, who seldom see anything but Pall Mall." The widow simply replied she had never been in London but once, before her son was born.

"Fine village, ma'am, fine village," said Mr. Wagg. "Increasing every day. It'll be quite a large town soon. Not a bad place to live in for those who can't get the country, and will repay a visit when you honour it."

"My brother, Major Pendennis, has often mentioned your name to us," the widow said. "And we have been very much amused by some of your droll books, sir," Helen continued, who detested their tone thoroughly.

"He is my very good friend," Mr. Wagg said with a low bow. "One of the best known men about town, and where known, ma'am, appreciated—I assure you appreciated. He is with our friend Steyne at Aix-la-Chapelle. Steyne has a touch of the gout, and so, between ourselves, has your brother. I am going to Stillbrook for the pheasant-shooting, and afterwards to Bareacres, where Pendennis and I shall probably meet." He poured out a flood of fashionable talk, introducing scores of peers, rattling on with breathless spirits while the simple widow listened in silent wonder. *What a man,* she thought. *Are all the men of fashion in London like this? I am sure Pen will never like him.*

Meanwhile, Mr. Pynsent was engaged with Miss Laura. He named houses in the neighborhood, hoping to see

Arthur Pendennis, hiding behind a curtain of cheap calico drapes, adopted the most forbidding expression he could muster. His mission was simple: to observe Miss Bell's debut as a quadrille partner to Mr. Pynsent. Mr. Pynsent, a man of such rigid English solemnity that he treated social dancing with the same gravity as attending morning prayer, moved through the floor with a stiffness that suggested he feared the music might distract him from his duty. Yet, Miss Laura Bell was a different story entirely. Bathed in the glow of chandeliers and the roar of the orchestra, her face radiated a joy that seemed to defy the room's decorum. With her white gown flaring and her brown curls tumbling over her shoulders, she was the center of attention. Even Lady Fogey, a woman who imported London fashion to the countryside with considerable flair, stopped to admire her, comparing her favorably to a reigning beauty of the capital and declaring her "quite doable."

Lady Rockminster, however, viewed the scene with the critical eye of a connoisseur. Through her spectacles, she surveyed Laura with majestic indifference, noting her artless charm but critiquing the slight redness of her arms as a youthful imperfection. She found Laura's tone superior to that of Miss Blanche Amory, who was currently dancing opposite her. Miss Blanche, a creature of vanity, was indeed Laura's *vis-à-vis*. She smiled with calculated lethargy, nodding and chatting whenever paths crossed, all while ensuring her own pale shoulders and tiny feet were the focal point of the room. Her demeanor screamed, *"Look at me, not at that healthy country bumpkin who needs teaching. This is the Parisian way."*

While Laura danced with an eye fixed on Pen lurking in the shadows, the young man himself was a study in awkward pomp. He remained hidden until the second quadrille, when Lady Clavering summoned him to the dais. There, he performed a haughty bow to Lady Rockminster, who barely acknowledged him, before turning his attention to the Widow Amory, resplendent in diamonds and velvet.

The waltz brought a new partner: Young Mr. Fogey, a boy of fifteen dreaming of a beard and a dragoon commission. He was utterly smitten by Laura, declaring her far superior to the "French girl" he had previously danced with. Laura laughed at his earnestness, her amusement catching Pen's eye through the crowd. Pen, meanwhile, looked absurdly sulky. When the waltz began, Fogey confessed his inability to manage the steps, vowing to hire a master. Then came Mr. Pynsent again, and Pen watched in a silent fury as Laura twirled around him. This sight triggered a memory of happier evenings at Clavering Park, where Laura had animated even the stoic Mr. Pynsent, while Miss Blanche struggled with the lumbering Captain Broadfoot, whose lack of musical sense made him a hazard to the dance floor.

Miss Blanche, seeing Laura's success, feigned fatigue to escape the dragoon and retreat to her mother's side.
