She demanded to know why Pen hadn't asked her to waltz, complaining about the "odious man in spurs." Pen, attempting a gallant defense of the dragoon's uniform, was dismissed by Blanche as "sulky and naughty." When Pen finally asked her to dance, she politely declined, citing her current engagement, though she promised the next waltz. Pen, delighted by the prospect of mischief, turned his attention to Blanche. He became a font of satire, mocking the heavy dragoons, the country dandies, and the ladies' elaborate hairstyles—one resembling a bird's nest, another a dessert of grapes and almonds. He kept Blanche in stitches, all while Laura watched from across the room, torn between amusement and confusion at her cousin's behavior.

The waltz music swelled, and Pen, paired with the lively Miss Blanche, executed the steps with brilliant flair. He glanced hopefully toward Laura, but she was fully occupied with the energetic Chevalier Strong. Their triumph was short-lived. As Pen and Blanche whirled with the speed of opera dancers, Captain Broadfoot and his partner, Miss Roundle—a large young woman in a strawberry-colored dress—moved with agonizing slowness. The collision was inevitable. The four dancers collided with such force that gravity abandoned them all. Captain Broadfoot and Miss Roundle were toppled, but the real disaster befell Pen, who crashed flat onto the floor, sprawling amidst the ruin.

The Captain, good-natured, laughed at his own misfortune, but the atmosphere shifted instantly. Miss Amory was savage about her fall, while Miss Roundle sat up, presenting a pitiful spectacle that drew giggles from the crowd. Pen, a young man who feared ridicule above all else, boiled with rage. He blamed the "woman in pink," apologized clumsily to Miss Amory, and stormed away in a fury. Miss Amory, feigning injury, refused further dancing and accepted a glass of water from a stranger—a man wearing a blue ribbon and a three-pointed star. She allowed him to escort her to her mother, her shoulder turned haughtily toward Pen.

The stranger was Mirobolant, the family cook, dressed in a black wig and military orders. Blanche, unaware of his identity, mistook him for a foreign nobleman. As she departed, Pen, realizing the absurdity, shouted, "By Jove, it's the cook!" The words hung in the air, heavy with unintended insult. Mirobolant, hearing the exclamation, cast a furious, murderous glance over his shoulder. Pen, oblivious to the depth of his faux pas, felt a surge of mischievous triumph.

Just as tension mounted, Chevalier Strong intervened. Speaking rapidly in Spanish, he convinced the humiliated cook that he would lose his position if he continued to dance with Blanche. The cook, crushed, bowed profoundly and retreated. Strong explained to a bewildered Blanche that the man was indeed the chef, a Gascon with a troubled mind who was madly infatuated with her "beautiful eyes."

The scene shifted to the refreshment room, a select space reserved for the elite. Here, Colonel Altamont, a servant of the Nawab of Lucknow, sat drinking champagne with alarming enthusiasm. He was a man of eccentric habits, jingling sovereigns and staring oddly at everyone who entered. Sir Francis Clavering and Mr. Pynsent encountered him, with the Colonel offering Pynsent a glass of "Sham," which the future politician accepted with grave dignity. The Colonel was baffled by such decorum from a man he assumed was a merchant captain.

When Strong and Blanche entered, the Colonel, now glowing with red-eyed intensity, approached Blanche. "And so you're Betsy Amory," he boomed. Blanche, distracted by the noise returning to the ballroom, was left wondering who had spoken to her. The room buzzed with the aftermath of the dance, a chaotic mix of fallen partners, misunderstood nobility, and the lingering sting of Pen's accidental insult to the cook.

The ballroom at Baymouth was less a place of celebration and more a pressure cooker of social friction. A civil war, waged entirely in whispers and shoves, raged around a corner window. Below, the street crowd hurled sarcastic projectiles like "Pitch into him!" and "Where's the police?" up through the open pane. Inside, the factions were clear: on one side, a ring of admirers including the formidable Madame Fribsby, besieging Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant; on the other, a cluster of gentlemen and ladies protecting Arthur Pendennis.

The Chevalier Strong, a man of considerable physical presence, shouldered his way through the melee, cutting a path that sent Madame Fribsby into a panic. "Save him, save him!" she cried, her voice fraying into something pathetic, though she was clearly charmed by the Chevalier's rugged appearance.

The spark of this domestic insurrection was a small, fiery chef from Sir Francis Clavering's kitchen. Earlier, while Mr. Pen had attempted to cool his boiling blood by gazing at the sparkling sea—trying desperately to compose himself after the humiliation of the waltz and Miss Amory's icy behavior—a hand had landed squarely on his shoulder. It belonged to Monsieur Mirobolant. To be touched by a French cook was an indignity that made the blood of the Pendennis line surge; Pen was astounded, bordering on enraged.

"You speak French?" Mirobolant asked, his pale face glowing with intensity.

"What is that to you?" Pen retorted in English.

"Then you understand?" Mirobolant bowed, rolling his R's with the force of a Gascon bull. "Vous me comprendrez alors, Monsieur Pendennis, quand je vous dis que vous êtes un lâche. Un lâche, entendez-vous?"

"What?" Pen spun around.

"Do you understand the weight of that word among men of honor?" the artist demanded, hand on hip.

"The consequence," Pen bellowed, stepping forward, "is that I will fling you out of this window, you impudent scoundrel!"

He was a match for the artist, and the window was right there. The threat hung in the air, ready to become reality, until Captain Broadfoot and a heavy officer threw themselves between them. The ladies screamed, the fiddles stopped dead, and the crowd surged forward. Just then, Laura appeared, her face a mask of alarm, asking what was wrong. Simultaneously, Strong emerged from the refreshment room, finding Alcides grinding his teeth and Pen looking wickedly calm.

"What has happened?" Strong asked the chef in Spanish.

"I am Chevalier de Juillet," the little man hissed, slapping his chest. "He insulted me."

"He called me... Cuisinier," the chef whispered.

Strong couldn't help laughing. "Come away with me, poor Chevalier. We must not quarrel before ladies. I'll carry your message to Mr. Pendennis. The poor fellow isn't right in his head," he whispered to the surrounding group.

Gathered around Pen, the ladies and guests demanded an explanation. Pen, bewildered, confessed that the man had offered his arm to a young lady, prompting Pen to call him a cook, which led to the chef calling him a coward and challenging him to a duel. "If you gentlemen hadn't stopped me, I would have thrown him out," Pen admitted.

"D—— him," the gentlemen grumbled. "Serve the impudent foreign scoundrel right."

"But I'm sorry if I hurt his feelings," Pen added softly.

Laura, radiant with forgiveness, stepped forward. "Foreigners have different manners, Pen. If you hurt a poor man's feelings, you'd be the first to apologize, wouldn't you, dear Pen?" Her gaze was angelic. Pen took her hands, promised he would, and felt a swell of affection. *How fond she is of me,* he thought. *Shall I speak to her now? No, not yet. I must resolve this absurdity with the Frenchman.*

Laura then asked if he would waltz with her. It was an affectionate but fatal suggestion. In his mind's eye, Pen saw himself tumbling over Miss Roundle and a dragoon, flinging Blanche against the wall, all while the room erupted in laughter.

"I shall never dance again," he declared, his face darkening. "Never. I'm surprised you should ask."

"Is it because you can't get Blanche?" Laura asked, her tone shifting to something captious.

"Because I don't wish to make a fool of myself," Pen snapped. "For you to laugh at me, Laura. I saw you and Pynsent. By Jove! No man shall laugh at me."

"Pen, don't be so wicked!" Laura cried, stung by his morbid vanity. "Who thinks the worse of you for stumbling?"

Just then, Mr. Pynsent arrived, bearing a command from Lady Rockminster to escort Laura to supper. "I was going with my cousin," Laura said, her eyes dropping guiltily.

"Oh, pray, no!" Pen interjected, seizing the moment. "You are in excellent hands. I'm going home."

"Good-night, Mr. Pendennis," Pynsent said dryly—a polite wrapper for a desire to box the insolent jackanapes' ears. Pen merely bowed and departed, leaving Laura imploringly behind.

Pynsent and Laura retreated to the supper room, where Laura, distracted and pained, wondered how her mother could expect her to give her heart to such a capricious, jealous, and violent man. Relief came only when a waiter delivered a pencil note from Pen: *I met Cooky below and asked his pardon. I'm glad I did. I wanted to speak to you tonight, but will wait until you return. God bless you. Dance away all night with Pynsent and be happy.—PEN.*

Pen descended to the hall, his heart reproaching him for his behavior toward Laura, longing to apologize but unable to do so before Pynsent. He found the Chevalier Mirobolant pacing below, ready for a fight.

"I've got him in the coffee-room with pistols and a candle," Strong announced, joining them. "Or swords on the beach? Mirobolant is a dead hand with foils."

"Confound it," Pen fumed. "I can't fight a cook!"

"He is a Chevalier of July," Strong countered.

"And do you ask me to duel a servant?" Pen roared.

Strong laughed. "I was joking. I came to settle quarrels, not fight them. I've told Mirobolant you didn't mean 'cook' offensively; it was a breach of household custom for a hired officer to offer his arm to the mistress's daughter." Strong then revealed the true secret: Mirobolant was laboring under a violent, romantic passion.

Upon hearing this, Pen burst into a hearty laugh. His rage evaporated. He realized he had been absurdly jealous himself all evening, longing to insult Pynsent. Remembering his own youthful jealousy of Oaks, he felt ready to pardon anything born of such passion. He entered the coffee-room, found Mirobolant, and delivered a fluent, heartfelt apology in French, declaring himself a *galant homme*. The Chevalier withdrew, regretting his outburst, and the matter was settled.

As they drove to the Brawl, Strong praised Pen's French, comparing him to Chateaubriand. Pen, accustomed to the language since youth, smiled as Strong finally stopped laughing.

By daybreak, the ball at Baymouth had ended. Madame Fribsby and Mirobolant departed in the Clavering fly; Laura was asleep in Lady Rockminster's bed with an easy heart; and the Claverings rested at the inn. Meanwhile, Blanche emerged from the refreshment room pale as lemon ice, whispering to her maid of a romantic adventure with a man who knew her murdered father, composing a sonnet to his memory before sleep.

Pen returned to Fairoaks without delivering his intended message to Laura, deciding to wait until her return the next day. He was not seriously jealous of Pynsent's advances, confident that as a man of twenty-three, he could simply ask and receive Laura, just as his mother could refuse nothing.

When Helen inquired about the Baymouth calamity, Pen laughed it off in a gay tone, dismissing the idea of declarations under such mishap. "There will be plenty of time for sentiment, dear mother, when Laura comes back," he said, admiring himself in the glass with a killing air. Helen, thinking no woman could resist him, was exceedingly happy.

While Helen fretted, Pen packed books, arranged papers, and cleaned his gun, preparing for a departure to London. At twenty-three, he decided it was time for serious business. He planned to take chambers at an Inn of Court, supported by two hundred pounds and his nearly finished plays. Once established at the bar, he would return to marry Laura, the sweet-tempered, good-looking girl who would steady him. "Yes, she'll steady me, won't she?" he asked his dog, Ponto, who licked his hand with devoted flattery.

Two days later, Laura returned home, driven by Pynsent. Her arrival brought a dark cloud to Pen's brow. Laura saw it and was pained, but Helen, eager to move the delicate question forward, retired early, leaving Pen in suspense. His dignity offended at being kept waiting, he slept on his disappointment until morning.

Helen woke him, declaring it the most beautiful morning in the world. "Laura has been out for an hour. Everybody ought to be in the garden."

Pen laughed, seeing the matchmaking schemer at work. "Oh, you profound dissembler! Can nobody escape your wicked tricks?" Helen laughed, flushed, and fluttered, her heart full of hope.

Pen dressed, his reflections dismal. *I am going to tie myself for life to please my mother,* he thought. *Laura is the best of women, and she has given me her money. I wish I hadn't received it. But as both women set their hearts on this match, I suppose I must satisfy them.* He felt grave, not elated, viewing the union as a great sacrifice.

In the garden, Laura wore her usual uniform: a straw hat with a ribbon streamer, a blouse fastened at the waist, and gauntlets protecting her hands from rose thorns, giving her a military air. She greeted Pen with the same smile she had worn the night before, offering him her glove to shake. Pen condescended, his face remaining tragic.

"Excuse my glove," Laura laughed, pressing his hand. "We are not angry again, are we, Pen?"

"Why do you laugh at me?" Pen asked. "You made a fool of me at Baymouth."

"My dear Arthur, I meant no wrong," she answered gently. "You and Miss Roundle looked so droll... it wasn't a serious fall."

"Confound Miss Roundle," Pen bellowed.

"She looked so pitiful," Laura said archly, mimicking Miss Roundle's distress before checking herself. "But I am sure we ought to laugh at you, Pen, if you're angry about such a trifle."

"You should not laugh at me, Laura," Pen said bitterly. "Not you, of all people."

"And why not? Are you such a great man?"

"Ah no, Laura, I'm such a poor one," Pen admitted. "Haven't you baited me enough?"

"My dear Pen, and how?" Laura cried, holding her hand out again. "Indeed, I didn't think to vex you. I thought such a clever man as you could bear a harmless joke from his sister."

"It is your kindness that humiliates me more than your laughter," Pen said. "You are always my superior."

"Superior to the great Arthur Pendennis?" Laura teased. "You can't mean that any woman is your equal?"

"Those who confer benefits should not sneer," Pen replied. "I don't like my benefactor to laugh at me. You scorn me because I have taken your money, and I am worthy to be scorned."

"Money! Obligation! For shame, Pen; this is ungenerous," Laura flushed. "May not our mother claim everything that belongs to us? Don't I owe her all my happiness? What matters a few paltry guineas if we can set her tender heart at rest? I would dig in the fields, I would be a servant, I would die for her. You know I would." She looked toward Helen's window, blessing the woman within. "My life is hers."

Unseen, Helen watched her children with deep emotion, longing for the fulfillment of her prayer.

"Is your life my mother's?" Pen asked, trembling. "You know what her great object is?"

"What, Arthur?" Laura dropped his hand, looking at him, then at the window, then down. She trembled too, sensing the crisis.

"Our mother has one wish above all others," Pen said, taking her hand again. "I own she has spoken to me of it. If you fulfill it, dear sister, I am ready. I am young, but I have had so many pains and disappointments that I am old and weary. I think I have hardly a heart to offer. Before I began the race, I am a tired man. My career has been a failure; I have been protected by those I should have protected. Your nobleness shames me, yet renders me grateful. When I heard from our mother what you did for me—that you armed me and bade me struggle once more—I longed to throw myself at your feet and say, 'Laura, will you come and share the contest with me?' Your sympathy will cheer me. Will you take me, dear Laura, and make our mother happy?"

"Do you think mamma would be happy if you were otherwise, Arthur?" Laura asked in a low, sad voice.

"And why should I not be," Pen eagerly asked, "with so dear a creature as you by my side? I have not my first love to give you. I am a broken man. But indeed I would love you

The reader is invited to leave the quiet woods and the whispering sea-shores of the west, to trade the gossip of Clavering and the sleepy rhythm of Fairoaks for the roar of London. Arthur Pendennis rides the 'Alacrity' coach into the night, a vessel hurtling him toward the city where he intends to face the world and carve out his fortune. As the wheels spin through the darkness, the young man's mind races with schemes of prudence, success, and fame. He knows he has outgrown the boy who walked this road a year ago; his first failure has stripped away his naivety, leaving behind a sharper courage and a higher opinion of his own potential. A hundred eager fantasies keep him awake, fueled by the belief that his mishaps have aged him into wisdom.

His thoughts drift with tender reverence to his mother, who blesses him as he departs. He prays to the stars above her that she remains awake, sending purer prayers for his safety than he can muster himself. To him, her love is a talisman, a shield against the perils of the city. He also thinks of Laura, whose affection he has been denied because he feels unworthy. With a mix of shame and relief, he acknowledges her superior nature, comparing himself to a prodigal who flees a church he once loved, not out of hate, but because he feels he has no right to enter such a holy space.

These reflections keep him awake until the nipping dawn of an October morning. When the coach stops at the familiar breakfasting house at B——, Pen wakes refreshed. The sun breaks through, the horn blows, and the milestones fly by. The road grows busier, the coach rattles into London, and a thrill runs through the young man. "Here is my place," he thinks, "here begins my battle." From the roof of the coach, he gazes down at the vast city with the longing desire of a soldier on the eve of a campaign.

On the journey, he has struck up a conversation with a cheery fellow-passenger in a shabby cloak, a reporter named Mr. Doolan who talks endlessly of literary giants like Tom Campbell and Sydney Smith. Pointing out Mr. Hurtle, the famous reviewer, Doolan confirms Pen's admiration. Pen calculates wildly that if he can match the earnings of the novelist Wagg, he might make five thousand a year.

Upon arriving at the Gloster Coffee-house, his first sight is that of his old friend Harry Foker, prancing down Arlington Street in white kid gloves and sporting a tuft on his chin. Foker, accompanied by a knock-kneed cab-boy, waves a whip in greeting. Excited to be in London and flush with a hundred pounds, Pen invites Doolan to dine at the Covent Garden Coffee-house, where he stays. He dresses in his best, orders a modest pint of sherry to show economy, and heads to the theatre.

The lights, music, and gaiety charm the country boy. He laughs at jokes and applauds songs, delighting the bored regulars in the boxes. After the first act, he struts through the lobbies like a man of high fashion. He is joined by Foker, who introduces him to Grandy Tiptoff and Lord Colchicum, a venerable peer who has chased pleasure since the French Revolution. They rush into a private box where sits Miss Blenkinsop, the eminent actress, and her father, the "veteran" Blenkinsop, snoozing in a wig.

While Pen admires Miss Blenkinsop's beauty, believing her to sing like a nightingale, the men in the box discuss Mrs. Leary, an actress in a hussar jacket, and declare her singing out of tune. When the actor Hoppus appears, the gentlemen grow bored, but Miss Blenkinsop insists that Lord Colchicum gave her a bouquet. Pen blushes as the peer mentions introducing him to the Megatherium Club.

Suddenly, the conversation turns to "Mirabel." Harry Foker laughs, revealing that Mirabel is Miss Fotheringay, Pen's first love, who married Sir Charles Mirabel the previous year. Pen looks out and sees her again, wondering how he could have ever loved her.

Thus, on his very first night in London, Arthur Pendennis finds himself entangled with a club, a heavy-acting father, and a dashing society of jovial blades. Lord Colchicum, despite his age, is indefatigable in his pursuit of enjoyment, hosting sumptuous banquets for dramatic professors from all nations. The next day, Pen pays his entrance fee to the Club, spending a third of his hundred pounds with immense satisfaction. He writes a letter to Fairoaks, imagining the comfort of the club after his work, and visits his uncle's lodgings in Bury Street. Finding the Major absent, he leaves his card.

When the Major returns in November, he discovers a note from Arthur stating that he has joined the Upper Temple to study for the bar. Intrigued and slightly concerned, the Major sends his valet, Morgan, to investigate. Morgan reports that Arthur lives in a "shy" place on the third floor of Lamb Court, sharing rooms with Mr. Warrington. The Major, initially dismissive of the grim surroundings, decides to visit.

He navigates the dismal alleys and archways of the Temple until he reaches Lamb Court. The atmosphere is bleak, lit by feeble oil lamps, filled with the sounds of clerks and the sight of wigmakers' shops. Shuddering at the thought of his nephew living in such filth, the Major climbs the damp, black stairs to the third floor. He finds the rooms open and smoky.

An Irish charwoman lets him in. Inside, a great voice demands beer. Seated on a table is Mr. Warrington, unshorn and smoking a pipe, while Pen sits nearby with a cigar. The Major, choking on the smoke, introduces himself. Warrington, a younger son of Sir Miles Warrington of Suffolk, is a rough diamond: dressed in a ragged shooting jacket, drinking beer like a coal-heaver, yet possessing a frank, gentlemanly demeanor. He cooks mutton chops on a gridiron in the room, teaching Pen the "first principles of law, cooking, and morality" simultaneously.

During their meal, the Major learns that Pen has been to Lady Whiston's party and met an "old flame," Lady Mirabel. The Major, in his simple philosophy, advises his nephew to abandon youthful follies. He argues that a woman with money is a much easier profession than the law, urging Pen to aim for Parliament, a good carriage, and a ladylike wife rather than vegetating at Fairoaks. Pen listens, wondering what his mother and Laura would think of this pragmatic advice.

After the Major leaves, Warrington emerges from his bedroom, now dressed neatly, and shows off his ragged but cozy quarters. He contrasts his own Spartan existence—sleeping on straw, reading Plato, and cooking his own food—with Pen's "dandy" room, complete with curtains, shiny boots, and prints of opera dancers. The Major watches in amusement as Warrington prepares another meal, marveling at the eccentricity of his nephew's new life in the heart of London's legal underworld.

The Knights of the Temple

If you've ever wondered why the world seems to hold onto its dustiest traditions while the rest of us scrub the floors, look no further than the Inns of Court. While a modern workhouse or prison boasts plumbing and sanitation that would make a Victorian ghost weep with envy, the "respectable" colleges and legal halls remain stubbornly medieval. Men in these places pay a premium for the privilege of sleeping in closets that smell of centuries, while the poorest tailor in Spitalfields has running water at his elbow. The gentlemen of the Temple fetch their water in jugs carried by laundresses, living in stone boxes built before the concept of decency was invented. It's a funny thing, really; the ancestors of these current knights were likely the "Great Unwashed," yet somehow, they managed to survive.

Take Old Grump, for instance. A veteran of the Norfolk Circuit, he'd lived in the chambers beneath Warrington and Pendennis for thirty years, waking up daily to the roar of shower baths that occasionally leaked through the roof into his room. To Grump, these modern luxuries were nothing but absurd, dandified follies. He cursed the laundress who slopped the stairs and declared that he, and his fathers before him, had done just fine without them. One wonders, amidst all those painted escutcheons in the Upper Temple Hall, if there wasn't a single philanthropist good enough to invent a set of Hummums for the benefit of the lawyers. The Temple historian stays silent on such schemes, though one can't help but imagine how many a counsel of old might have benefited from a good pump.

Yet, there is a certain charm to these venerable Inns, with their Lamb and Flag and Winged Horse banners. There's a rough comfort and a freedom here that men always remember with a smile. Perhaps the law student indulges in poetical reminiscences as he walks past historical chambers, imagining Eldon, Coke, Chitty, and Barnewall in their prime. The man of letters loves this place even more, populated by the ghosts of Sir Roger de Coverley and Samuel Johnson rolling through the fog. It's a stage where Harry Fielding dashes off midnight articles with ink-stained ruffles, while the printer's boy sleeps in the passage.

Imagine the history of a single day in one of those dingy four-story houses where Pen and Warrington lived. On the ground floor, a parliamentary counsel drives off to Belgravia for lunch, leaving his clerk—who was once a boot-black—to take his place. The clerk, now a gentleman, entertains friends, unaware that six months ago he was starving in a garret, waiting for clients who never came. On the first floor, a venerable recluse named Doomsday spends fifty years amassing learning and a fortune, only to die alone, leaving his heirs to burn half his library. Over him lives Grump, playing whist with three seniors after a heavy dinner of Port. On the third landing, where Pen and Warrington dwell late into the night, sits Mr. Paley.

Paley is a different breed entirely. He takes the highest honors, reads cases until dawn, and works until an hour before dinner. He has brought a great intellect down to the level of a mean subject, fiercely excluding all higher thoughts, poetry, love, and beauty from his mind so he can master the enormous legend of the law. Once a competitor with Warrington, Paley is now praised for his industry while everyone whispers that Warrington is wasting his time. But who is using their time best? Paley has no time for kindness, friendship, or the sight of a beautiful sunset; he sees only law-books. When he turns off his lonely lamp, he feels thankless and remorseless, shuddering only when he meets Warrington on the stairs, avoiding him as if he were doomed.

It was likely this cadaverous ambition and self-complacent meanness in Paley's yellow face that deterred Pen from pursuing the Bench with the same ardor. Pen, however, enjoyed the Temple life with relish. His relatives thought he was studying hard, and his uncle wrote home saying the lad had sown his wild oats and was becoming steady. The truth was, the rough pleasures of a London bachelor were novel and agreeable to him. He walked in the Park instead of riding Rotten Row, content with the view. He was too young and lazy to succeed without a better name and fortune, so he let the Major believe he was reformed and buried in books.

In reality, Pen's day consisted of a morning's reading, a walk, a pull on the river, a stretch to Hampstead, and a modest tavern dinner. His nights were spent in joviality, not vice, for Pen admired women so heartily that he couldn't bear the company of anyone he didn't fancy as pure. Evenings were quiet, shared with a friend and a pipe, accompanied by a humble potation of British spirits that Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress, invariably tasted for quality. This was not an unpleasant life.

During term-time, Pen showed praiseworthy regularity in eating his dinners in the Hall of the Upper Temple. It is a sight not uninteresting, a meal that feels like a throwback to the seventeenth century, save for some anachronisms. The bar has their messes, the students their tables, and the benchers sit at the high table surrounded by portraits of judges and royals. Pen, on his first introduction, found the scene amusing. The hall was a melting pot: grey-headed attorneys, dandies in rowing shirts, swarthy colonials, and Irish gentlemen making a sojourn before returning home. There were squads of law-talkers, rowing enthusiasts, and political orators. Pen gradually made acquaintances with most of them, finding points of sympathy.

The Inn provides an excellent, wholesome dinner for a moderate price: soup, meat, tarts, and port or sherry. Arranged in messes of four, each quartet shares a joint, apple-pie, and a bottle of wine. But the honest habitues, especially the lower-ranking students with a taste for good living, have many harmless arts to improve their banquet.

"Wait a bit," said Mr. Lowton, one of these Temple gourmands, tugging at Pen's gown. "The side-tables are very full, and there's only three benchers to eat ten dishes. If we wait, perhaps we shall get something from their table."

Pen looked with amusement toward the high table, where three old gentlemen stood before silver dish-covers while the clerk quavered out a grace. Lowton was a captain of the mess, aiming to be the first to the table and secure the thirteenth glass of port. His pleasures were harmless; an eager epicure whose desires did not go beyond eighteen pence.

Pen was older than many of his fellow-students, with a style that stamped him as a man of ton, unlike the pale law-talkers or ferocious dandies in astonishing waistcoats. Lowton, humble and good-natured, had been attracted by Pen's superior looks and presence.

"This is boiled-beef day, I believe, sir," Lowton said.

"Upon my word, sir, I'm not aware," Pen replied, barely containing his laughter. "I'm a stranger; this is my first term."

Lowton began pointing out the notabilities. "That's Boosey the bencher, the bald one under the picture. Next is Balls, the King's Counsel, and Swettenham. That's old Grump, the senior of the bar; they say he's dined here forty years. Do you see those four fellows opposite? Those are regular swells—Mr. Trail, the Bishop of Ealing's son; Honourable Fred Ringwood, Lord Cinqbar's brother; and Bob Suckling. They'll have good places, I bet."

"Why do you like to mess with them if they don't eat?" Pen asked, puzzled.

"How green you are," Lowton chuckled. "They don't drink any wine, don't you see? A fellow gets the bottle to himself if he messes with those three chaps. That's why Corkoran got in with 'em."

Pen delighted in Lowton's slyness. Lowton explained that these "swells" only pretend to dine, going to grand clubs or parties instead. Their names appear in the *Morning Post*, but they won't be in the Hall for two hours. "They don't drink wine," Lowton insisted. "That's why you get the bottle."

"There's a lot of Irish here," Lowton continued, cataloging the room. "Corkoran, Molloy Maloney of Ballymaloney, nephew to Major-General Sir Hector O'Dowd... He's always bragging about his uncle. And that one with the long black hair is a tremendous rebel. By Jove, sir, to hear him at the Forum it makes your blood freeze."

"It's your turn to fill your glass," Lowton said, offering Pen a toast. "What? You won't have any port? Don't like port with your dinner? Here's your health."

Pen found himself attached to Lowton precisely because he disliked port.

It was during one of these dinners that a gentleman in a barrister's gown joined them, unable to find a seat among his peers. Dressed in old, faded clothes and a ragged shirt, he strode over the table and took the bench where Pen sat. He smiled when he saw Pen's name on the slip.

"We ought to know each other," he said. "We're both Boniface men; my name's Warrington."

"Are you St—— Warrington?" Pen exclaimed, delighted.

"Warrington—yes," he laughed. "I recollect you in your freshman's term. But you appear to have quite cut me out."

"The college talks about you still," Pen said, admiring the talent. "The bargeman you thrashed, Bill Simes, wants you up again at Oxbridge. The Miss Notleys..."

"Hush!" Warrington interrupted. "Glad to make your acquaintance, Pendennis. Heard a good deal about you."

The young men became friends immediately, deep in college talk. Seeing Warrington take his share of the port with gusto, Pen didn't scruple to help himself, much to Lowton's disappointment. After dinner, Warrington asked where Pen was going.

"I thought of going home to dress and hear Grisi in *Norma*," Pen said.

"Are you going to meet anybody?"

"No—only to hear the music," Pen admitted.

"You had much better come home and smoke a pipe with me," Warrington suggested. "A very short one. I live close by in Lamb Court, and we'll talk over Boniface and old times."

They left; Lowton sighed after them, knowing Warrington was a baronet's son and looking up to the aristocracy with simple reverence. From that night, Pen and Warrington were sworn friends. Warrington's cheerfulness, good sense, rough welcome, and never-failing pipe charmed Pen, who found it more pleasant to dive into shilling taverns with him than to dine in solitary state among the silent frequenters of the Polyanthus.

Soon, Pen gave up his lodgings in St. James's, finding it more economical to move into Warrington's vacant room in Lamb Court. No man was more easily led than Pen to do a thing when it was a novelty or to his liking. Pidgeon, the youth, and Flanagan, the laundress, now divided their allegiance between the two friends.

Old and New Acquaintances

Pen, elated by the prospect of seeing life, plunged into a hundred queer London haunts. He delighted in the notion of consorting with every sort of man, and so he beheld coal-heavers in their tap-rooms, boxers in their inn-parlours, and honest citizens disporting in the suburbs or on the river. He would have loved to hob-nob with celebrated pickpockets or drink a pot of ale with a company of burglars and cracksmen, had chance afforded him the opportunity. It was good to observe the gravity with which Warrington listened to the Tutbury Pet or the Brighton Stunner at the Champion's Arms, and the interest he took in the coal-heaving company assembled at the Fox-under-the-Hill. His acquaintance with the public-houses of the metropolis and its neighbourhood was prodigious. He was the personal friend of the landlord and landlady, welcome to the bar as to the clubroom. He claimed to like their society better than that of his own class, whose manners annoyed him and whose conversation bored him. "In society," he used to say, "everybody is the same, wears the same dress, eats and drinks, and says the same things; one young dandy at the club talks and looks just like another, one Miss at a ball exactly resembles another, whereas there's character here. I like to talk with the strongest man in England, or the man who can drink the most beer, or with that tremendous republican of a hatter, who thinks Thistlewood was the greatest character in history. I like better gin-and-water than claret. I like a sanded floor in Carnaby Market better than a chalked one in Mayfair. I prefer Snobs, I own it." Indeed, this gentleman was a social republican; and it never entered his head while conversing with Jack and Tom that he was in any respect their superior, although, perhaps, the deference they paid him might secretly please him.

Pen followed him to these various resorts with great glee and assiduity. But he was considerably younger, and therefore much more pompous and stately than Warrington, in fact a young prince in disguise, visiting the poor of his father's kingdom. They respected him as a high chap, a fine fellow, a regular young swell. He had somehow about him an air of imperious good-humour and a royal frankness and majesty, although he was only heir-apparent to twopence-halfpenny and but one in descent from a gallypot. If these positions are made for us, we acquiesce in them very easily; and are always pretty ready to assume a superiority over those who are as good as ourselves. Pen's condescension at this time of his life was a fine thing to witness. Amongst men of ability this assumption and impertinence passes off with extreme youth: but it is curious to watch the conceit of a generous and clever lad—there is something almost touching in that early exhibition of simplicity and folly.

So, after reading pretty hard of a morning, and, I fear, not law merely, but politics and general history and literature, which were as necessary for the advancement and instruction of a young man as mere dry law, after applying with tolerable assiduity to letters, to reviews, to elemental books of law, and, above all, to the newspaper, until the hour of dinner was drawing nigh, these young gentlemen would sally out upon the town with great spirits and appetite, bent upon enjoying a merry night as they had passed a pleasant forenoon. It was a jovial time, that of four-and-twenty, when every muscle of mind and body was in healthy action, when the world was new as yet, and one moved over it spurred onwards by good spirits and the delightful capability to enjoy. If ever we feel young afterwards, it is with the comrades of that time: the tunes we hum in our old age are those we learned then. Sometimes, perhaps, the festivity of that period revives in our memory; but how dingy the pleasure-garden has grown, how tattered the garlands look, how scant and old the company, and what a number of the lights have gone out since that day! Grey hairs have come on like daylight streaming in—daylight and a headache with it. Pleasure has gone to bed with the rouge on her cheeks. Well, friend, let us walk through the day, sober and sad, but friendly.

I wonder what Laura and Helen would have said, could they have seen, as they might not unfrequently have done had they been up and in London, in the very early morning when the bridges began to blush in the sunrise, and the tranquil streets of the city to shine in the dawn, Mr. Pen and Mr. Warrington rattling over the echoing flags towards the Temple, after one of their wild nights of carouse—nights wild, but not so wicked as such nights sometimes are, for Warrington was a woman-hater; and Pen, as we have said, too lofty to stoop to a vulgar intrigue. Our young Prince of Fairoaks never could speak to one of the sex but with respectful courtesy, and shrank from a coarse word or gesture with instinctive delicacy—for though we have seen him fall in love with a fool, as his betters and inferiors have done, and as it is probable that he did more than once in his life, yet for the time of the delusion it was always as a Goddess that he considered her, and chose to wait upon her. Men serve women kneeling—when they get on their feet, they go away.

That was what an acquaintance of Pen's said to him in his hard homely way—an old friend with whom he had fallen in again in London—no other than honest Mr. Bows of the Chatteris Theatre, who was now employed as pianoforte player, to accompany the eminent lyrical talent which nightly delighted the public at the Fielding's Head in Covent Garden: and where was held the little club called the Back Kitchen.

Numbers of Pen's friends frequented this very merry meeting. The Fielding's Head had been a house of entertainment, almost since the time when the famous author of 'Tom Jones' presided as magistrate in the neighbouring Bow Street; his place was pointed out, and the chair said to have been his, still occupied by the president of the night's entertainment. The worthy Cutts, the landlord of the Fielding's Head, generally occupied this post when not disabled by gout or other illness. His jolly appearance and fine voice may be remembered by some of my male readers: he used to sing profusely in the course of the harmonic meeting, and his songs were of what may be called the British Brandy-and-Water School of Song—such as 'The Good Old English Gentleman,' 'Dear Tom, this Brown Jug,' and so forth—songs in which pathos and hospitality are blended, and the praises of good liquor and the social affections are chanted in a baritone voice. The charms of our women, the heroic deeds of our naval and military commanders, are often sung in the ballads of this school; and many a time in my youth have I admired how Cutts the singer, after he had worked us all up to patriotic enthusiasm, by describing the way in which the brave Abercrombie received his death-wound, or made us join him in tears, which he shed liberally himself, as in faltering accents he told how autumn's falling leaf "proclaimed the old man he must die"—how Cutts the singer became at once Cutts the landlord, and, before the applause which we were making with our fists on his table, in compliment to his heart-stirring melody, had died away,—was calling, "Now, gentlemen, give your orders, the waiter's in the room—John, a champagne cup for Mr. Green. I think, sir, you said sausages and mashed potatoes? John, attend on the gentleman."

"And I'll thank ye give me a glass of punch too, John, and take care the wather boils," a voice would cry not unfrequently, a well-known voice to Pen, which made the lad blush and start when he heard it first—that of the venerable Captain Costigan; who was now established in London, and one of the great pillars of the harmonic meetings at the Fielding's Head.

The Captain's manners and conversation brought very many young men to the place. He was a character, and his fame had begun to spread soon after his arrival in the metropolis, and especially after his daughter's marriage. He was great in his conversation to the friend for the time being (who was the neighbour drinking by his side), about "me daughther." He told of her marriage, and of the events previous and subsequent to that ceremony; of the carriages she kept; of Mirabel's adoration for her and for him; of the hundther pounds which he was at perfect liberty to draw from his son-in-law, whenever necessity urged him. And having stated that it was his firm intention to "dthraw next Sathurday, I give ye me secred word and honour next Sathurday, the fourteenth, when ye'll see the money will be handed over to me at Coutts's, the very instant I present the cheque," the Captain would not unfrequently propose to borrow a half-crown of his friend until the arrival of that day of Greek Calends, when, on the honour of an officer and gentleman, he would repee the thrifling obligetion.

Sir Charles Mirabel had not that enthusiastic attachment to his father-in-law, of which the latter sometimes boasted (although in other stages of emotion Cos would inveigh, with tears in his eyes, against the ingratitude of the child of his bosom, and the stinginess of the wealthy old man who had married her); but the pair had acted not unkindly towards Costigan; had settled a small pension on him, which was paid regularly, and forestalled with even more regularity by poor Cos; and the period of the payments was always well known by his friend at the Fielding's Head, whither the honest Captain took care to repair, bank-notes in hand, calling loudly for change in the midst of the full harmonic meeting. "I think ye'll find that note won't be refused at the Bank of England, Cutts, my boy," Captain Costigan would say. "Bows, have a glass? Ye needn't stint yourself to-night, anyhow; and a glass of punch will make ye play con spirito." For he was lavishly free with his money when it came to him, and was scarcely known to button his breeches pocket, except when the coin was gone, or sometimes, indeed, when a creditor came by.

It was in one of these moments of exultation that Pen found his old friend swaggering at the singers' table at the Back Kitchen of the Fielding's Head, and ordering glasses of brandy-and-water for any of his acquaintances who made their appearance in the apartment. Warrington, who was on confidential terms with the bass singer, made his way up to this quarter of the room, and Pen walked at his friend's heels.

Pen started and blushed to see Costigan. He had just come from Lady Whiston's party, where he had met and spoken with the Captain's daughter again for the first time after very old old days. He came up with outstretched hand, very kindly and warmly to greet the old man; still retaining a strong remembrance of the time when Costigan's daughter had been everything in the world to him. For though this young gentleman may have been somewhat capricious in his attachments, and occasionally have transferred his affections from one woman to another, yet he always respected the place where Love had dwelt, and, like the Sultan of Turkey, desired that honours should be paid to the lady towards whom he had once thrown the royal pocket-handkerchief. The tipsy Captain returning the clasp of Pen's hand with all the strength of a palm which had become very shaky by the constant lifting up of weights of brandy-and-water, looked hard in Pen's face, and said, "Grecious Heavens, is it possible? Me dear boy, me dear fellow, me dear friend;" and then with a look of muddled curiosity, fairly broke down with, "I know your face, me dear dear friend, but, bedad, I've forgot your name." Five years of constant punch had passed since Pen and Costigan met. Arthur was a good deal changed, and the Captain may surly be excused for forgetting him; when a man at the actual moment sees things double, we may expect that his view of the past will be rather muzzy.

Pen saw his condition and laughed, although, perhaps, he was somewhat mortified. "Don't you remember me, Captain?" he said. "I am Pendennis—Arthur Pendennis, of Chatteris."

The sound of the young man's friendly voice recalled and steadied Cos's tipsy remembrance, and he saluted Arthur, as soon as he knew him, with a loud volley of friendly greetings. Pen was his dearest boy, his gallant young friend, his noble collagian, whom he had held in his inmost heart ever since they had parted—how was his fawther, no, his mother, and his guardian, the General, the Major? "I preshoom, from your apparance, you've come into your prawpertee; and, bedad, yee'll spend it like a man of spirit—I'll go bail for that. No? not yet come into your estete? If ye want any thrifle, heark ye, there's poor old Jack Costigan has got a guinea or two in his pocket—and, be heavens! you shall never want, Awthur, me dear boy. What'll ye have? John, come hither, and look aloive; give this gentleman a glass of punch, and I'll pay for't.—Your friend? I've seen him before. Permit me to have the honour of making meself known to ye, sir, and requesting ye'll take a glass of punch."

"I don't envy Sir Charles Mirabel his father-in-law," thought Pendennis. "And how is my old friend, Mr. Bows, Captain? Have you any news of him, and do you see him still?"

"No doubt he's very well," said the Captain, jingling his money, and whistling the air of a song

Arthur Pendennis stood before the mirror in his tiny bedroom, admiring the reflection of a young man who had recently begun to resemble a ghost. His revels were over, or at least, the funds to sustain them had evaporated. The awful sword of impecuniosity hung over him, sharp and inevitable. His money was gone; his club subscription had swallowed a third of his estate, and the furniture of his little sanctuary had been paid for with the last of his reserves. He was down to the final five-pound note in his pocket-book, staring at it with the horror of a prince suddenly stripped of his crown.

His companion, Warrington, knew nothing of this precipice. To Warrington, Pen appeared as a young aristocrat in the making, bred like a child in arms fed by a doting mother. He saw the gold chains and the dressing-case fit for a lord, and he assumed the habits of an aristocrat were merely a matter of style, not substance. Pen dined on porter and beef with perfect contentment, yet he possessed a terrifying inability to economize. He could not give twopence to a waiter without feeling insulted; he could not resist a cab, rain or shine, and invariably overpaid the driver. He scorned cleaned gloves and minor frugalties. If he had been born to ten thousand a year, he would have been indistinguishable from a spendthrift; if he were a beggar, he would still be generous to a fault, unable to resist handing money to a sad story or a pair of piteous-faced children. It was a sumptuous nature, perhaps, that could not regard money, a natural kindness mixed with a petty vanity that loved the praise of waiters and cabmen.

Warrington, however, had not yet been subjected to the full audit of Pen's finances. He knew Pen had been wild at college, but the extent of the extravagance versus the mother's dwindling means remained a secret. That secret finally spilled out while Pen grimly surveyed the change for his last five-pound note lying on the public-house tray beside Warrington's pot of ale.

"It is the last rose of summer," Pen declared, his voice heavy with the weight of his situation. "Its blooming companions have gone long ago; and behold, the last one of the garland has shed its leaves." He recounted the whole saga: his mother's limited means, his own follies, and Laura's generous attempts to prop him up. Warrington smoked his pipe, listening intently.

"Impecuniosity will do you good," Warrington said, tapping ash from his pipe. "For an honest man, it is the most wholesome medicine. It keeps your moral man in a perpetual state of excitement, like a rider facing a fence or an opponent's single-stick. A little necessity brings out your pluck. You'll discover what you can do without when you have no money for new gloves, varnished boots, eau de Cologne, or cabs. You've been bred a molly-coddle, Pen, spoilt by women. A single man with health and brains who can't find a livelihood doesn't deserve to stay in it. Pay your last halfpenny and jump over Waterloo Bridge. Steal a leg of mutton, get transported, and leave the country. *Dixi*; I have spoken. Give us another pull at the pale ale."

"But how is one to live?" Pen asked, despairing. "There is beef and bread in plenty in England, but you must pay for it with work or money. And who will take my work? What work can I do?"

Warrington burst into laughter. "Suppose we advertise in the *Times*," he suggested. "For an usher's place at a classical and commercial academy: *A gentleman, B.A. of St. Boniface College, and who was plucked for his degree—Wishes to give lessons in classics and mathematics, and the rudiments of the French language; he can cut hair, attend to the younger pupils, and play a second on the piano with the daughters of the principal. Address A. P., Lamb Court, Temple.*"

"Confound you!" cried Pen.

"Men take to all sorts of professions," Warrington continued, undeterred. "Your friend Bloundell is a professional blackleg, traveling the Continent to fleece young gentlemen of fashion. There is Bob O'Toole, who drives the Ballynafad mail. I know a doctor's son who, upon reaching his last five-pound note, let his mustachios grow, went to a provincial town, announced himself as Professor Spineto, chiropodist to the Emperor of All the Russians, and established a practice by operating on the local newspaper editor. He lives respectably now and has succeeded to his father's gallypots."

"Hang gallypots," Pen retorted. "I can't drive a coach, cut corns, or cheat at cards. There's nothing else you propose."

"Yes; there's our own correspondent," Warrington said, his tone shifting. "Before you told me your story, I thought you a man of fortune. Clearly, you must not touch your mother's income anymore. You can't go on spunging on the women. You must pay off that trump of a girl—Laura, is her name? Here is your health, Laura!—and carry a hod rather than ask for a shilling from home."

"But how earn one?" Pen asked.

"How do I live, think you?" Warrington replied, his countenance falling slightly. "On my younger brother's allowance, Pendennis? I have secrets of my own. I made away with that allowance five years ago; if I had made away with myself a little time before, it would have been better. I have played off my own bat ever since. I don't want much money. When my purse is out, I go to work and fill it, then lie idle like a serpent until I have digested the mass. Look, I begin to feel empty." He held up a long, lean purse containing but a few sovereigns.

"I write," Warrington admitted, blushing. "I don't tell the world I do so. I don't wish it said that George Warrington writes for bread. But I write in the *Law Reviews*. Look here." He turned over some sheets. "I write in a newspaper now and then, too." He fetched a file of the *Dawn* and pointed silently to a few articles. Pen read them with delight, recognizing the strong thoughts, curt periods, satire, and scholarship.

"I am not up to this," Pen admitted with genuine admiration. "I know very little about politics or history, Warrington; and have but a smattering of letters. I can't fly upon such a wing as yours."

"But you can on your own, my boy, which is lighter, and soars higher, perhaps," Warrington said kindly. "Those little scraps and verses I have seen show a natural gift, rare in these days. You needn't blush, you conceited young jackanapes. You have the sacred flame—a little of the real poetical fire. You are a poet, Pen, my boy." He stretched out his broad hand and clapped Pen on the shoulder.

Arthur was so delighted that tears came into his eyes. "How kind you are to me, Warrington!" he exclaimed.

"I like you, old boy," Warrington said. "I was devilish lonely in chambers and wanted somebody. The sight of your honest face somehow pleased me. I liked the way you laughed at Lowton—that poor good little snob. In fine, the reason why I cannot tell—but so it is, young 'un. I'm alone in the world, sir; and I wanted someone to keep me company." A glance of extreme kindness and melancholy passed from Warrington's dark eyes.

Pen was too pleased with his own thoughts to perceive the sadness of the friend complimenting him. "Thank you, Warrington," he said. "Thank you for your friendship and what you say about me. I have often thought I was a poet. I will be one—I think I am one, as you say so, though the world mayn't. Is it the *Ariadne in Naxos* you liked, or the Prize Poem?"

Warrington burst into a roar of laughter. "Why, young goose," he yelled, "of all the miserable weak rubbish I ever tried, *Ariadne in Naxos* is the most mawkish and disgusting. The Prize Poem is so pompous and feeble that I'm surprised it didn't get the medal. You don't suppose you are a serious poet, do you? Are you setting up to be a Pindar, you absurd little tom-tit, fancying you have the strength of the Theban eagle? No, my boy, I think you can write a magazine article and turn a pretty copy of verses; that's what I think of you."

"By Jove!" said Pen, bouncing up and stamping his foot. "I'll show you that I am a better man than you think for."

Warrington only laughed the more, blowing twenty-four puffs rapidly from his pipe.

Opportunity soon presented itself. Mr. Bacon, the eminent publisher of Paternoster Row, owned the *Legal Review* and other periodicals. Every year he presented a beautiful gilt volume called the *Spring Annual*, edited by Lady Violet Lebas, featuring the most fashionable poets of the time. Young Lord Dodo, the Honourable Percy Popjoy, and others had their works fast given to the world in this miscellany, which had since perished like other vernal blossoms. The book was daintily illustrated with pictures of reigning beauties. As these plates were prepared long beforehand, the eminent poets had to write to the plates, not the other way around.

One day, just as the volume was on the eve of publication, Warrington called on Mr. Hack, Bacon's reader and general manager. He found Hack with a bundle of proof plates and sheets. Percy Popjoy had written verses for a picture called *The Church Porch*: a Spanish damsel hastening to church with a large prayer-book, watched by a youth in a cloak hidden in a niche. The picture was pretty, but Popjoy's genius had deserted him; he had made the most execrable verses ever perpetrated by a young nobleman.

Warrington burst out laughing as he read the poem, and Mr. Hack laughed too, with a rueful face. "It won't do," he said. "The public won't stand it. Bungay's people are bringing out a very good book and have set up Miss Bunyan against Lady Violet. We have most titles, but the verses are too bad. Lady Violet herself owns it; she's busy with her own poem. What's to be done? We can't lose the plate. The governor gave sixty pounds for it."

"I know a fellow who would do some verses, I think," Warrington said. "Let me take the plate home in my pocket and send to my chambers in the morning for the verses. You'll pay well, of course."

"Of course," said Mr. Hack. Warrington went home to Pen, plate in hand.

"Now, boy, here's a chance for you. Turn me off a copy of verses to this."

"What's this? A Church Porch—A lady entering it, and a youth out of a wine-shop window ogling her.—What the deuce am I to do with it?"

"Try," said Warrington. "Earn your livelihood for once, you who long so to do it."

"Well, I will try," said Pen.

"And I'll go out to dinner," Warrington said, leaving Pen in a brown study.

When Warrington returned that night, the verses were done. "There they are," said Pen. "I've screwed 'em out at last. I think they'll do."

"They will," Warrington said after reading them. They ran as follows:

> **The Church Porch**
>
> Although I enter not,
> Yet round about the spot
>     Sometimes I hover,
> And at the sacred gate,
> With longing eyes I wait,
>     Expectant of her.
>
> The Minster bell tolls out
> Above the city's rout
>     And noise and humming
> They've stopp'd the chiming bell,
> I hear the organ's swell
> She's coming, she's coming!
>
> My lady comes at last,
> Timid and stepping fast,
>     And hastening hither,
> With modest eyes downcast.
> She comes—she's here—she's past.
>     May Heaven go with her!
>
> Kneel undisturb'd, fair saint,
> Pour out your praise or plaint
>     Meekly and duly.
> I will not enter there,
> To sully your pure prayer
>     With thoughts unruly.
>
> But suffer me to pace
> Round the forbidden place,
>     Lingering a minute,
> Like outcast spirits, who wait
> And see through Heaven's gate
>     Angels within it.

"Have you got any more, young fellow?" asked Warrington. "We must make them give you a couple of guineas a page; and if the verses are liked, you'll get an entree into Bacon's magazines and may turn a decent penny."

Pen examined his portfolio and found another ballad he thought might figure with advantage in the *Spring Annual*. Consigning these two precious documents to Warrington, the pair walked from the Temple to Paternoster Row.

Bacon's shop was an ancient low-browed building, with a few books displayed under a bust of Lord Verulam. Exactly opposite was Mr. Bungay's house, newly painted and elaborately decorated in the seventeenth-century style, looking as if Mr. Evelyn or Mr. Pepys might step out. Warrington went into Bacon's shop, but Pen stayed outside. It was agreed that his ambassador should act for him entirely. The young fellow paced up and down the street in a nervous condition, waiting

The Captain, a man of theatrical flair even in captivity, stood before his audience—a motley crew of publishers and aspiring writers—and delivered a manifesto that sounded less like a plea for justice and more like a press release for a revolutionary uprising. "Gentlemen of England!" he declaimed, his voice booming off the damp prison walls. "The hour has struck! Our glorious order is besieged by foreign revolutions, by the treachery of mill-owners, and by the gullible masses they have led astray! The ancient monarchy is insulted by a rabble of republicans; the Church is undermined by stealthy infidels. We are left defenseless, our institutions exposed to the plunder of men who believe in nothing holy, who know no history, and who break every law strong enough to command."

Mr. Shandon, the Captain's co-conspirator, leaned in to amplify the drama. "It was because the French kings distrusted their gentlemen that Saint Louis fell," he noted, eyeing the room with a conspiratorial glint. "But here, the people still believe in their gentlemen! It was because we were led by them that the Eagles retreated before us from the Donro to the Garonne. A gentleman broke the line at Trafalgar; a gentleman swept the plains of Waterloo."

At the mention of Waterloo, Mr. Bungay, the publisher, nodded sagely, winking at the Captain as if sharing a secret joke. Warrington, however, couldn't hold back; he burst into laughter.

"You see how our venerable friend Bungay is affected," Shandon whispered slyly to Warrington, feigning innocence while looking down at his papers. "That is the true test. I have used the Duke of Wellington a hundred times, and the Duke never fails to inspire such... enthusiasm."

The Captain then confessed, with a candour that bordered on the absurd, that the gentlemen of England had long neglected their political duties, leaving them to professional proctors and advocates. "No more!" he cried. "The time for hired hands is gone. Our enemies are brave, strong, and numerous. We must meet them in the field, not in the pages of Grub Street gazettes. That is a dig at Bacon's people, Mr. Bungay," Shandon added, turning to the publisher. Bungay clapped his stick on the floor with a sound like a gunshot. "Hang him! Pitch into him, Captain!" he roared, wagging his dull head at Warrington. "For a slashing article, there is nobody like the Captain—nobody!"

The prospectus continued, promising a journal dedicated solely to the interests of the "Gentlemen of England." Some subscribers, whose names were withheld for "obvious reasons," would back this venture. "They are loyal to their Sovereign," the Captain flourished, "and love their Church. They would keep England the greatest and freest nation in the world." He paused dramatically for effect. "As the names of some of them are appended to the deed that secured our liberties at Runnymede—"

"What's that?" asked Mr. Bungay, genuinely curious.

"An ancestor of mine sealed it with his sword-hilt," Pen said with grave dignity.

"It's the Habeas Corpus, Mr. Bungay," Warrington corrected him. The publisher yawned, though he muttered, "Go on, Captain."

The Captain rallied the crowd, declaring that the *Pall Mall Gazette* would be written by gentlemen for gentlemen, addressing the highest circles of society. He spoke of literary departments conducted by men famous at the Universities, a claim that made Pen blush and laugh simultaneously. He urged the nobility, the clergy, and the matrons of England to rally to the cause. At the conclusion, Mr. Bungay woke from a second snooze, nodded approvingly, and pocketed the manuscript with the satisfaction of a man who had just acquired a weapon. He didn't just pay for the MS.; he called little Mary over and gave her a penny as he departed.

Once the reading concluded, the group turned to general conversation. Shandon, dressed in a tattered dressing-gown, spoke with the ease of a man who lived in the heart of the "beau monde." He recounted anecdotes of the Earl of X and dinners at great houses, telling tales with such glib familiarity that Mrs. Shandon listened with fond admiration, believing every word. Pen found it amusing to hear the shabby prisoner talk so confidently of the great.

Eventually, Mr. Bungay rose to leave, followed by Warrington and Pen. Shandon, despite a deprecatory look from his wife, insisted on walking them to the prison gate. In his shabby slippers, he shuffled ahead, his hand already fiddling with his waistcoat pocket where Bungay's five-pound note rested. He handed the note to one follower, sent a leg of mutton to a prisoner, paid a tavern bill, and lost several half-crowns at cards with friends. By the time he emerged from the prison gates, he was as poor as he had been in the morning.

Mrs. Shandon watched him run off toward the tavern, her heart heavy. She had hoped to share a meal of meat and salad with her husband and little Mary, but now she would be alone. She made herself tea instead, a ritual that seemed to comfort countless women in similar straits. Soon, Mr. Jack Finucane arrived, bringing sweets for the child and news of Shandon's latest exploits. Jack, a townsman of the Captain, treated him as a genius and a patron, ready to run errands and manage debts for the embarrassed Irish gentleman.

Outside, Bungay and his companions discussed the Captain's finances. "He won't have that five-pound note very long," Bungay predicted. And he was right; Mrs. Shandon later found only a couple of shillings and a few halfpence left in his pockets.

Later that evening, at the Black Kitchen, Pen and Warrington debated the nature of genius and the tyranny of publishers. Pen defended Shandon, arguing that misfortune drove men to bad company and that one must deal kindly with the eccentricities of genius. Warrington, a severe moralist, countered that genius was not a shield against debt. "If a lawyer or a soldier goes to gaol for owing money, an author must too," he argued. "Rags are not proof of genius; capital is the master."

Despite their philosophical squabble, both men agreed on one thing: they pitied Shandon. "His genius sings within his prison bars," Pen mused. Warrington nodded bitterly. "He accommodates himself to a cage pretty well. He might have a high place, but he can't, so he drinks with Tom and Jack. He might provide for his family, but they have a bottle of brandy they want him to taste. Let us pity the misfortunes of genius and conspire against the publishing tyrants."

"Are you going to have another glass of brandy-and-water?" Pen asked with a humorous look. Warrington ordered a fresh pot with sugar, and the pair retired to their chambers.

There, they found two notes from Mr. Bungay inviting them to dinner. Pen, reflecting on the day's events, lay awake gazing at a moonlit sketch of Fairoaks drawn by Laura. He wondered if he was finally going to earn his bread with his pen, to honor his mother and prove himself to Laura. He prayed for honesty, for truth, and for the courage to stand firm against flattery and prejudice. Overcome with emotion, he knelt by his bed, repeating the words his mother used to say before tucking him in, his eyes streaming with tears of hope and submission.

The next morning, a large brown-paper parcel arrived, addressed to Warrington. Inside were new books—travels, novels, and poems—sent specifically for review. "Catch!" Warrington shouted, flinging the package at Pen's head. Pen caught it, trembling with delight. "Sport the oak, Pidgeon," he declared, refusing visitors. He sat in his easy chair, barely drinking his tea, eager to dive into the pages and begin his work as a reviewer.

Captain Shandon, usually a man of action rather than administration, had been gently steered by his formidable wife into a new venture: appointing John Finucane, Esquire, as the sub-editor of the *Pall Mall Gazette*. This honor fell to Finucane, a man of the Upper Temple who harbored a deep, almost theatrical affection for the Captain and his family. Their bond was forged in the fires of financial ruin; Finucane's chambers had once served as Shandon's sanctuary when bailiffs were abroad, a refuge so well-known that the sheriff's officers eventually began waiting for the Captain on Finucane's staircase as regularly as at his own door. It was here, amidst the scent of old paper and tea, that Mrs. Shandon often came to pour out her sorrows, seeking rescue plans for her adored husband. Finucane, proud to host such a distinguished lady, would watch her descend the stairs, veil drawn low, with a mixture of genuine concern and a secret, boyish thrill. He hoped, perhaps foolishly, that some rogue might waylay her, giving him the glorious opportunity to rush forward and break the villain's bones—a fantasy that brought her sincere delight.

With the arrangement made, Finucane became the Captain's aide-de-camp in the newspaper world. He spent his evenings tucking little Mary into her crib, offering a sympathetic "Amen" to her prayers for her father, despite his own Roman Catholic faith. But duty called. At six o'clock, he departed for a dinner with Mr. Bungay, the publisher, dressed in his finest, albeit cheaply bought, garb of brilliant colors. In his pocket sat four pounds and two shillings—his weekly salary minus the cost of a new pair of gloves.

At Dick's Coffee-house, Finucane executed a masterclass in salesmanship. He "cut his mutton" with Mr. Bungay and his reader, Mr. Trotter, outlining a vision for the *Pall Mall Gazette* that dazzled the slow-witted Bungay. Finucane spoke with the authority of a man who knew the weekly earnings of every press worker and possessed a thousand economic dodges to save capital. He assigned roles for markets, turf, church intelligence, and chit-chat, presenting himself as the finest sub-editor London had to offer. His rapid calculations on paper left Bungay mystified and impressed.

Yet, beneath the professional veneer, Finucane's true agenda burned bright: the liberation of Captain Shandon. He knew the exact debt against the Captain at Fleet Prison, though no man in England truly knew their own debts. He argued that Shandon worked better in freedom, a claim Bungay initially dismissed, noting that a locked-up Captain was easier to find than a free one. But Finucane played the emotional card, describing Mrs. Shandon's pining and the child's sickness. Moved by this tableau, Bungay promised to consider the matter the next morning. The evening ended with rounds of brandy-and-water; Bungay refused to let Finucane pay the bill, handing the waiter the funds himself. Thus, Finucane retired to his Temple chambers with his full week's salary intact.

That night, he winked a knowing, joyful signal to Mrs. Shandon. She hurriedly gathered her bonnet and shawl, and Finucane offered to take them for a walk. Little Mary, ever delighted by Finucane's gifts and shows, skipped with joy.

"May I go, Charley?" Mrs. Shandon asked hesitantly. "He's got a headache, Mr. Finucane. I persuaded him to stay in bed."

"Go along with you, and Polly," Shandon replied cheerfully from his desk, where he was quoting Greek and Latin from his *Burton's Anatomy*. "Hand me over the book and leave me to my abominable devices."

Finucane escorted the mother and child through the prison gates into the fresh air. Their destination was Paternoster Row, specifically the shop of Mr. Bungay. There, Mrs. Bungay was arriving, dressed in a flamboyant shot-silk gown of red and purple, a yellow shawl, and a parasol of brilliant blue. She carried a parcel and a manuscript bound in red.

Mrs. Shandon, clad in an old black watered silk, looked every bit the lady despite her faded attire. The two women curtsied. Mrs. Bungay, with a tragic look, asked if Mrs. Shandon could leave little Mary with her while she attended to business in the counting-house.

"I want to stay with mamma," Mary cried, burying her face in her mother's dress.

"Go with this lady, Mary, my dear," Mrs. Shandon said gently.

Mrs. Bungay, adopting the voice of an ogress, promised to show Mary "pretty pictures" and opened her parcel to reveal choice sweet biscuits. The treat charmed the child, and she followed Mrs. Bungay into the private entrance. However, when the moment of separation approached, Mary's courage faltered. Seeing Mrs. Bungay's disappointed expression, Mrs. Shandon kindly offered to accompany them upstairs for a few minutes. A second biscuit sealed the deal, and Mary soon prattled away with perfect confidence.

Meanwhile, in the counting-house, Finucane faced a sterner reality. The previous night's generosity had evaporated. Mrs. Bungay had rebuked her husband, declaring Shandon a good-for-nothing who needed no money and criticizing the entire plan of the *Pall Mall Gazette*. She insisted Shandon remain in prison, the best place for him. Finucane's pleas fell on deaf ears; the publisher was inexorable.

But upstairs, the dynamic shifted. Mrs. Shandon's artless sweetness and winning frankness began to melt the fierce Mrs. Bungay. Describing her husband's virtues and the child's failing health, she painted a picture of a clergyman's daughter from the West of England. Mrs. Bungay, though as grim as Lady Macbeth, softened. She recalled her own daughter, Bessy, who had died eighteen years prior, and saw in little Mary a reflection of her lost angel.

"The Capting is an Irishman," Mrs. Bungay admitted, her eyes twinkling. "I couldn't abide them. But his wife is a lady... and a West of England woman, like myself."

She summoned her husband. "What's in the wind now, my dear?" Mr. Bungay asked, surprised by her change of heart.

"The little 'un said, 'Why don't you take Par out of prizn?' I did feel it, Flora, upon my honour I did," Mr. Bungay confessed, admitting he had felt the weight of the child's question.

The result was a heavy, clumsy speech from the publisher. Hearing that sixty-five pounds would secure the Captain's release, he offered to advance the sum, deducting it from future salaries, on the condition that Mrs. Shandon personally settled with the creditors.

"It was the happiest day Mrs. Shandon and Mr. Finucane had seen in a long time," the narrative notes. Finucane roared in a powerful brogue, "Bedad, Bungay, you're a trump!" He tossed Mary into the air with frantic joy.

Mrs. Bungay, looking out at a one-horse carriage, offered to drive them. With Mary nestled between the two women and Finucane on the back seat, they drove triumphantly away from Paternoster Row, while Mr. Bungay, returning to his desk, wondered how women remembered such things. He noted that the Captain's debt was only one hundred and thirty pounds; half would free him, and they would pay the rest later.

Mrs. Shandon's mission was a resounding success. Armed with the promise of payment, she mollified the creditors with a bribe of ten shillings in the pound. The following Sunday marked the last time, for some time, that the Captain spent his days behind bars.

On the appointed day, our two friends made their appearance at Mr. Bungay's door in Paternoster Row. They did not enter through the public throng, where bookboys lugged sacks of Bungay's volumes and timid aspirants clutched virgin manuscripts hoping for a sale to Sultan Bungay. Instead, they sought the private entrance, the secret portal from whence the splendid Mrs. Bungay emerged to mount her chaise, settle onto her cushions, and cast looks of defiant superiority at Mrs. Bacon's opposite windows.

Mrs. Bacon, currently a chaiseless woman, was a woman of considerable spirit. On such occasions, when wroth at her sister-in-law's ostentation, she would fling up the sash of her drawing-room window and gaze out at the chaise with her four children. Her message was clear: *"Behold these four darlings! Flora Bungay! This is precisely why I cannot drive in a carriage; you would give a coach-and-four just to have the same excuse."* It was with these verbal arrows that Emma Bacon shot at Flora Bungay, who sat in her chariot, envying the motherhood she lacked.

As Pen and Warrington approached Bungay's door, a carriage and cab pulled up to Bacon's residence. Old Dr. Slocum descended heavily from the first vehicle; his equipage was as ponderous as his style, yet both possessed a fine, sonorous effect upon the publishers lining the Row. From the cab stepped a couple of dazzling white waistcoats.

Warrington laughed. "You see, Bacon has his dinner-party too. That is Dr. Slocum, author of 'Memoirs of the Poisoners.' You would hardly recognize our friend Hoolan in that gallant white waistcoat. Doolan is one of Bungay's men, and faith, here he comes." Indeed, Messrs. Hoolan and Doolan had arrived from the Strand in the same cab, tossing a coin to decide who should pay the shilling. Mr. Doolan stepped from the other side of the road, arrayed in black with a large pair of white gloves spread wide on his hands, which he regarded with distinct pleasure.

The house porter, dressed in an evening coat, along with gentlemen sporting gloves as large as Doolan's but made of the famous Berlin web, stood on the passage of Mr. Bungay's house to receive hats and coats and bawl names up the stairs. Some guests had already arrived; however, only Mrs. Bungay, clad in red satin and a turban, represented her own charming sex. She curtsied to each newcomer as he entered the drawing-room, though her mind was evidently preoccupied by extraneous thoughts. The fact was, Mrs. Bacon's dinner-party was disturbing her. As soon as she received each member of her own company, Flora Bungay would fly back to the window embrasure to rake the carriages of Emma Bacon's friends as they rattled up the Row. The sight of Dr. Slocum's large carriage, with its gaunt job-horses, crushed Flora; none but hack cabs had driven up to her own door that day.

They were all literary gentlemen, though unknown to Pen. There was Mr. Bole, the real editor of the magazine (Mr. Wagg being merely the nominal chief); Mr. Trotter, who, having burst onto the world as a poet of a tragic and suicidal cast, had now subsided into one of Mr. Bungay's back shops as a reader; and Captain Sumph, an ex-beau reader about town, related in some indistinct manner to Literature and the Peerage. He was said to have written a book, to have been a friend of Lord Byron, and to be related to Lord Sumphington. In fact, anecdotes of Byron formed his staple. He seldom spoke without invoking that poet or his contemporaries: *"I remember poor Shelley, at school, being sent up for good for a copy of verses, every line of which I wrote, by Jove!"* or, *"I recollect, when I was at Missolonghi with Byron, offering to bet gamba,"* and so forth. This gentleman, Pen remarked, was listened to with great attention by Mrs. Bungay. His anecdotes of the aristocracy, of which he was a middle-aged member, delighted the publisher's lady. Had he but come in his own carriage, Mrs. Bungay would have made Mr. Bungay purchase any given volume from his pen.

Mr. Bungay went about to his guests as they arrived, doing the honours of his house with much cordiality. "How are you, sir? Fine day, sir. Glad to see you year, sir. Flora, my love, let me have the honour of introducing Mr. Warrington to you. Mr. Warrington, Mrs. Bungay; Mr. Pendennis, Mrs. Bungay. Hope you've brought good appetites with you, gentlemen. You, Doolan, I know have, for you've always had a deuce of a twist."

"Lor, Bungay!" said Mrs. Bungay.

"Faith, a man must be hard to please, Bungay, who can't eat a good dinner in this house," Doolan said, winking and stroking his lean chops with his large gloves, making appeals of friendship to Mrs. Bungay which that honest woman refused with scorn. "She couldn't abide that Doolan," she said in confidence to her friends. Indeed, all his flatteries failed to win her.

As they talked, Mrs. Bungay surveying mankind from her window, a magnificent vision of an enormous grey cab-horse appeared and neared rapidly.
