A pair of white reins, held by small white gloves, were visible behind it; a face pale but richly decorated with a chin-tuft, the head of an exiguous groom bobbing over the cab-head—these bright things were revealed to the delighted Mrs. Bungay. "The Honourable Percy Popjoy's quite punctual, I declare," she said, sailing to the door to be in waiting at the nobleman's arrival.

"It's Percy Popjoy," said Pen, looking out the window and seeing an individual in extremely lacquered boots descend from the swinging cab. Indeed, it was that young nobleman, Lord Falconet's eldest son, who was come to dine with the publisher—his publisher of the Row.

"He was my fag at Eton," Warrington said. "I ought to have licked him a little more." He and Pen had had some bouts at the Oxbridge Union debates, in which Pen had had very much the better of Percy, who presently appeared with his hat under his arm and a look of indescribable good humour and fatuity in his round, dimpled face. Upon this face, Nature had burst out with a chin-tuft but, exhausted with the effort, had left the rest of the countenance bare of hair.

The temporary groom of the chambers bawled out, "The Honourable Percy Popjoy," much to that gentleman's discomposure at hearing his titles announced.

"What did the man want to take away my hat for, Bungay?" he asked of the publisher. "Can't do without my hat—want it to make my bow to Mrs. Bungay. How well you look, Mrs. Bungay, to-day. Haven't seen your carriage in the Park: why haven't you been there? I missed you; indeed, I did."

"I'm afraid you're a sad quiz," said Mrs. Bungay.

"Quiz! Never made a joke in my—hullo! who's here? How d'ye do, Pendennis? How d'ye do, Warrington? These are old friends of mine, Mrs. Bungay. I say, how the doose did you come here?" he asked of the two young men, turning his lacquered heels upon Mrs. Bungay, who respected her husband's two young guests now that she found they were intimate with a lord's son.

"What! do they know him?" she asked rapidly of Mr. B.

"High fellers, I tell you—the young one related to all the nobility," said the publisher, and both ran forward, smiling and bowing, to greet almost as great personages as the young lord—no less characters, indeed, than the great Mr. Wenham and the great Mr. Wagg, who were now announced.

Mr. Wenham entered wearing the usual demure look and stealthy smile with which he commonly surveyed the tips of his neat little shining boots, a look he but seldom brought to bear upon the person who addressed him. Wagg's white waistcoat, on the contrary, spread out with profuse brilliancy; his burly, red face shone resplendent over it, lighted up with the thoughts of good jokes and a good dinner. He liked to make his entry into a drawing-room with a laugh, and, when he went away at night, to leave a joke exploding behind him. No personal calamities or distresses could altogether keep his humour down. Whatever his griefs might be, the thought of a dinner rallied his great soul; and when he saw a lord, he saluted him with a pun.

Wenham went up, then, with a smug smile and whisper, to Mrs. Bungay, looked at her from under his eyes, and showed her the tips of his shoes. Wagg said she looked charming and pushed on straight at the young nobleman, whom he called Pop, to whom he instantly related a funny story seasoned with what the French call *gros sel*. He was delighted to see Pen, too, shaking his hand and slapping him on the back cordially, for he was full of spirits and good humour. He talked in a loud voice about their last place and occasion of meeting at Baymouth, asked how their friends of Clavering Park were, whether Sir Francis was not coming to London for the season, and whether Pen had been to see Lady Rockminster, who had arrived—a fine old lady, Lady Rockminster! These remarks Wagg made not for Pen's ear so much as for the edification of the company, whom he was glad to inform that he paid visits to gentlemen's country seats and was on intimate terms with the nobility.

Wenham also shook hands with our young friend—all of which scenes Mrs. Bungay remarked with respectful pleasure, communicating her ideas to Bungay afterwards regarding the importance of Mr. Pendennis—ideas by which Pen profited much more than he was aware.

Pen, who had read and rather admired some of her works (and expected to find in Miss Bunion a person somewhat resembling her own description of herself in the 'Passion-Flower,' in which she stated that her youth resembled—"A violet, shrinking meanly / When blows the March wind keenly; / A timid fawn, on wild-wood lawn, / Where oak-boughs rustle greenly"—and that her maturer beauty was something very different, certainly, to the artless loveliness of her prime, but still exceedingly captivating and striking), beheld, rather to his surprise and amusement, a large and bony woman in a crumpled satin dress who came creaking into the room with a step as heavy as a grenadier's. Wagg instantly noted the straw which she brought in at the rumpled skirt of her dress and would have stooped to pick it up; but Miss Bunion disarmed all criticism by observing this ornament herself. Putting her own large foot upon it, so as to separate it from her robe, she stooped and picked up the straw, saying to Mrs. Bungay that she was very sorry to be a little late, but that the omnibus was very slow and what a comfort it was to get a ride all the way from Brompton for sixpence. Nobody laughed at the poetess's speech; it was uttered so simply. Indeed, the worthy woman had not the least notion of being ashamed of an action incidental upon her poverty.

"Is that 'Passion-Flowers?'" Pen said to Wenham, by whom he was standing. "Why, her picture in the volume represents her as a very well-looking young woman."

"You know passion-flowers, like all others, will run to seed," Wenham said. "Miss Bunion's portrait was probably painted some years ago."

"Well, I like her for not being ashamed of her poverty."

"So do I," said Mr. Wenham, who would have starved rather than have come to dinner in an omnibus, "but I don't think that she need flourish the straw about, do you, Mr. Pendennis? My dear Miss Bunion, how do you do? I was in a great lady's drawing-room this morning, and everybody was charmed with your new volume. Those lines on the christening of Lady Fanny Fantail brought tears into the Duchess's eyes. I said that I thought I should have the pleasure of meeting you to-day, and she begged me to thank you, and say how greatly she was pleased."

This history, told in a bland smiling manner of a Duchess whom Wenham had met that very morning, quite put poor Wagg's dowager and baronet out of court and placed Wenham beyond Wagg as a man of fashion. Wenham kept this inestimable advantage, having the conversation to himself, and ran on with a number of anecdotes regarding the aristocracy. He tried to bring Mr. Popjoy into the conversation by making appeals to him, saying, "I was telling your father this morning," or, "I think you were present at W. house the other night when the Duke said so-and-so," but Mr. Popjoy would not gratify him by joining in the talk, preferring to fall back into the window recess with Mrs. Bungay and watch the cabs that drove up to the opposite door. At least, if he would not talk, the hostess hoped that those odious Bacons would see how she had secured the noble Percy Popjoy for her party.

And now the bell of Saint Paul's tolled half an hour later than that for which Mr. Bungay had invited his party, and it was complete with the exception of two guests, who at last made their appearance, and in whom Pen was pleased to recognise Captain and Mrs. Shandon.

When these two had made their greetings to the master and mistress of the house and exchanged nods of more or less recognition with most of the people

The new journal, *The Pall Mall Gazette*, launched with a bang that echoed through the literary circles of London. Rumors swirled like dust in a sunbeam: an influential political party stood behind the paper, and its columns boasted the names of titans. Whether these whispers were true remained a mystery, but the truth was far more theatrical. A fiery article on foreign policy, widely attributed to a high-born Lord with deep ties to the Foreign Office, was actually penned by Captain Shandon in the smoky parlor of the Bear and Staff public-house near Whitehall Stairs. There, tracked down by a printer's boy and temporarily residing alongside a literary ally named Mr. Bludyer, the Captain had crafted the piece with the ease of a man comfortable in his own skin. Similarly, a string of papers on finance, universally believed to be the work of a great Statesman of the House of Commons, were the brainchild of Mr. George Warrington of the Upper Temple.

There was likely some secret handshake between the *Gazette* and that powerful party. Percy Popjoy, whose father, Lord Falconet, belonged to the faction, could frequently be seen climbing the stairs to Warrington's chambers. The paper also published peculiar information that seemed to come from nowhere, alongside several poems signed "P.P."—feeble in thought but roaring with expression. These verses, along with outrageously enthusiastic praise for Popjoy's novel, filled the pages.

While Mr. Pen kept his distance from the political machinations, he became the paper's most energetic literary soul. The *Gazette* operated out of Catherine Street in the Strand, a hub where Pen would arrive with manuscripts tucked in his pockets, buzzing with the electric thrill of a debutant. To see his words in print felt like a novel sensation; he delighted in the idea that his writings were stirring the world.

It was here that Mr. Jack Finucane, the sub-editor, performed his magic with paste and scissors. As the supervisor of the Journal, Finucane possessed an eagle eye for the world of fashion. He ensured that no death or dinner party among the aristocracy escaped his notice, fishing out astonishing gossip from obscure provincial prints and distant Scottish and Irish papers. It was a comical sight for any philosopher to witness: Jack Finucane, Esquire, sitting on a stool with a plate of cheap meat and a glass of porter, recounting the grand feasts of the elite as if he had been seated at the head table. Dressed in tattered trousers and a dingy shirt, he cheerfully arranged the most brilliant fêtes of the world, his appearance a stark contrast to the high society he described. This incongruity amused Pen, who knew that Jack had rarely seen anything beyond the tavern parlors of his native village, yet his writing made him sound like a regular at White's. While errors occasionally slipped through, the *Ballinafad Sentinel*, where Jack served as correspondent, suffered more from them than the London *Gazette*, where the editors preferred his scissors to his pen.

Pen poured his heart into his reviews. Armed with a desultory but eager reading habit cultivated in his youth, he possessed a keen sense of fun. His articles delighted both his chief and the public, earning him pride and a paycheck. The *Gazette* was devoured at Fairoaks, enjoyed by the two ladies there, and welcomed at Clavering Park, where a young lady of literary tastes awaited it. Even old Doctor Portman, to whom the widow sent the paper after memorizing her son's articles, offered his seal of approval. "The lad has spirit, taste, and fancy," the doctor declared. "He writes, if not like a scholar, at least like a gentleman."

What truly astonished Major Pendennis, however, was hearing his clubmates at the Regent dissecting the latest issue. Among them were Wenham, Lord Falconet, and other gentlemen of fashion, all laughing uproariously over an article that savaged a recent book by the Countess of Muffborough. The book, a *Travels in Spain and Italy*, was a masterpiece of linguistic confusion, written indifferently in French and English. Pen's critique, signed anonymously, pounced on the blunders with delightful mischief. He danced around his subject with jocularity, exposing the noble lady's faults with mock gravity. Every word was polite, yet the Countess was thoroughly scarified. Wenham's bilious face puckered with malignant pleasure; Lady Muffborough hadn't invited him to her parties in a year. Lord Falconet giggled heartily, remembering his rivalry with Lord Muffborough. They all turned to Major Pendennis, praising his nephew. Until then, the Major had dismissed hints from Fairoaks about "Arthur's constant and severe literary occupations" as beneath his dignity, but now, seeing the acclaim, he changed his tune.

"When Wenham praises the boy," the Major mused, "and Lord Falconet approves of his genius, and even Lord Steyne laughs and calls it capital, saying the Muffborough will writhe like a whale under a harpoon..." The Major's voice trembled with pleasure. "By gad, the young rascal has some stuff in him! He will do something!" Trembling with joy, he sat down to write to the widow at Fairoaks, relaying the great folks' praise, and then addressed a letter directly to the young man. He invited Pen to eat a chop with him and announced he was commissioned to take him to dinner at Gaunt House, for Lord Steyne appreciated anyone who could entertain him, whether by folly, wit, or dullness. Pen tossed the letter to Warrington, perhaps expecting a reaction that didn't quite come.

Young critics possess prodigious courage. They climb the judgment seat and deliver verdicts on the most intricate works without hesitation. If Macaulay's *History* or Herschel's *Astronomy* had been placed before Pen at this stage, he would have glanced through them, smoked a cigar, and issued an august approval as if he were their superior patron. With the aid of the *Biographie Universelle* or the British Museum, he could summarize historical periods and allude to dates and facts with masterly ease, astonishing his mother at home who wondered where such knowledge came from. Only when reading his articles months later, having forgotten the sources, would he realize the extent of his bluffing. At twenty-four, Pen would not have hesitated to judge the greatest scholars or critique the *Encyclopaedia*. Fortunately, Warrington kept him in check with constant, wholesome ridicule. Without it, Pen might have become conceited beyond endurance. Shandon, the veteran, relished the dash and flippancy of his young aide-de-camp, preferring Pen's light flashes to the heavier metal of his elder coadjutor.

Yet, despite his impertinence and premature judgments, Pen was a perfectly honest critic—too candid for Mr. Bungay's liking. A dispute arose between Pen and his chief, Captain Shandon. "In the name of common-sense, Mr. Pendennis," Shandon asked, "what have you been doing—praising one of Mr. Bacon's books? Bungay was in a fury this morning seeing a laudatory article on the works of that odious firm."

Pen's eyes widened. "Do you mean to say," he asked, "that we are to praise no books Bacon publishes? Or that, if they are good, we must call them bad?"

"My good young friend," Shandon inquired, "for what do you suppose a benevolent publisher undertakes a critical journal, to benefit his rival?"

"To benefit himself certainly, but to tell the truth too," Pen insisted. "*Ruat coelum*, to tell the truth."

"And my prospectus," Shandon laughed, sneering. "Do you consider that was a work of mathematical accuracy?"

"Pardon me," Pen replied. "I had qualms of conscience about that prospectus and debated it with Warrington. We agreed, however, that because the prospectus was declamatory and poetical, and the giant painted on the show-board was larger than the original inside the caravan, we need not be too scrupulous about this trifling inaccuracy. We are the fiddlers, playing our tunes; you are the showman."

"And leader of the van," Shandon added. "Well, I am glad your conscience gave you leave to play for us."

"Yes, but," Pen said, asserting the dignity of his position, "we are all party men in England. I will stick to my party like a Briton. A fool quarrels with his own nest; I will hit the enemy as hard as you like—but with fair play, Captain. One can't tell all the truth, I suppose, but one can tell nothing but the truth. I would rather starve, by Jove, and never earn another penny by my pen—this redoubted instrument, now six weeks in use, which I hold with vast enthusiasm—than strike an opponent an unfair blow or rank him below his honest desert."

"Well, Mr. Pendennis, when we want Bacon smashed, we must get some other hammer," Shandon said with fatal good-nature, perhaps thinking, "A few years hence, perhaps the young gentleman won't be so squeamish." The veteran Condottiere was no longer scrupulous; remorse had long left him after fighting and killing on so many sides. "Gad," he muttered, "you've a tender conscience, Mr. Pendennis. It's the luxury of all novices, and I may have had one once. But that bloom wears off with the rubbing of the world. I'm not going to the trouble of putting on an artificial complexion like our pious friend Wenham or our model of virtue, Wagg."

"I don't know whether some people's hypocrisy is not better, Captain, than others' cynicism."

"It's more profitable, at any rate," said the Captain, biting his nails. "That Wenham is as dull a quack as ever quacked. You see the carriage in which he drove to dinner. Faith, it'll be a long time before Mrs. Shandon takes a drive in her own chariot. God help her, poor thing!"

Pen departed from his chief, pondering the Captain's tale. He saw a man stored with genius, wit, learning, and a hundred good natural gifts, yet wrecked by paltering with honesty and forgetting self-respect. *Wilt thou remember thyself, O Pen?* he thought. *Thou art conceited enough! Wilt thou sell thy honour for a bottle? No, by heaven's grace, we will be honest, whatever befalls, and our mouths shall only speak the truth when they open.*

A punishment, or at least a trial, awaited Pen. In the very next number of the *Pall Mall Gazette*, Warrington read aloud an article that failed to amuse Arthur Pendennis, who was busy drafting his own critique for the following week. In this piece, the Spring Annual was ferociously maltreated by an unknown writer. The person most cruelly mauled was Pen himself. His verses had not appeared under his own name in the Annual but under an assumed signature. Having refused to review the book, Shandon had handed it to Mr. Bludyer with instructions to dispose of it. Bludyer, a man of considerable talent belonging to a race extinct in modern journalism, possessed a reputation for savage humor. He smashed and trampled the poor spring flowers with no more mercy than a bull on a parterre. Having cut up the volume to his heart's content, he sold it at a bookstall and purchased a pint of brandy with the proceeds.

Let us, with a generous wave of the hand, skip over a few months of Mr. Arthur Pendennis's life. During this interval, events undoubtedly occurred that thrilled the protagonist to his marrow but would likely bore the reader of these memoirs to tears. We last saw him fully embarked on his profession as a literary hack—a title Mr. Warrington, his bosom friend, deems entirely appropriate. Now, we must confront the grim reality of the hack's existence, whether in a curacy, a marching regiment, or behind a merchant's desk. Life is a monotonous grind; one day's labor mirrors the next with tedious precision. A writer, much like any other daily toiler, often battles for his bread against time, against his own will, against failing health, or against a profound indolence. He writes in spite of his repugnance for the subject, just as a bricklayer lays stones he despises. When you must monetize Pegasus—the poetic muse—farewell to soaring flights and celestial inspiration. Pegasus now trots in harness over the stony pavement of the city, pulling a cart or a cab, rising only at advertised intervals when the spectator's money has already been paid. He pants, his sides heaving, his knees trembling, and frequently receives a sharp cut of the whip from his driver.

Yet, let us not be too prodigal of our pity for Pegasus. There is no divine exemption for the muse; he suffers from labor, illness, and decay just like any other creature of God's creation. If he gets the whip, he often deserves it. I am quite ready to protest against the sentimental doctrine that letters and genius are exempt from the prose duties of daily life, tax-paying, and bread-winning. Genius must work and pay like its neighbors.

Well, the *Pall Mall Gazette* was duly established, and Arthur Pendennis's merits as a flippant, witty, and amusing critic were recognized. He worked hard every week, preparing reviews with a certain flippancy, yet with honesty and to the best of his power. Occasionally, a historian of seventy, who had spent twenty-five years composing a tome that our young gentleman disposed of in two days' reading at the British Museum, felt unfairly treated. Or a poet, elaborating sublime sonnets for fame, was annoyed by two dozen pert lines in Mr. Pen's review, where the critic acted as a lord on the bench and the author a trembling suitor. The actors complained wofully, too; he was likely too hard on them. But little harm was done. It was a different era; there were so few great historians, poets, or actors in Pen's time that scarce any came up for judgment before his critical desk. Those who received a little whipping got what was good for them. Not that the judge was wiser than the sentenced, nor did he fancy himself so. Pen possessed a strong sense of humor and justice, lacking an overweening respect for his own works. Furthermore, he had his friend Warrington at his elbow—a terrible critic if Pen grew conceited, and more savage over Pen than over anyone else he tried at his literary assize.

Through these critical labors and occasional contributions to leading articles, Mr. Pendennis earned four pounds four shillings weekly, with no small pains. He also furnished magazines and reviews, and is believed to have been the London correspondent of the *Chatteris Champion*, which at the time featured brilliant letters from the metropolis. These labors enabled the fortunate youth to earn nearly four hundred pounds a year. On the second Christmas after arriving in London, he actually brought a hundred pounds to his mother as a dividend upon the debt he owed to Laura. Mrs. Pendennis read every word of her son's works, considering him the profoundest thinker and most elegant writer of the day. She viewed his retribution of the hundred pounds as an act of angelic virtue. She feared he was ruining his health and was delighted by his accounts of society and the great men he met. Readers who have witnessed son-worship among mothers understand this charming simplicity of love, where a mother in Somersetshire delights in hearing if John has held a brief, if Tom was invited to a ball, or if George met a famous man. In the second winter, Pen came for a very brief space, cheering the widow's heart and lightening the lonely house at Fairoaks. Helen had her son all to herself; Laura was visiting old Lady Rockminster; the folks of Clavering Park were absent. The few old friends, led by Doctor Portman, treated him with marked respect. Between mother and son, it was pure fondness, confidence, and affection. It was the happiest fortnight of the widow's life, perhaps of both theirs. The holiday vanished too quickly, and Pen returned to the busy world, leaving the gentle widow alone again. She sent Arthur's money to Laura; I do not know why this young lady chose to leave home when Pen was coming, nor whether he was more piqued or relieved by her absence.

By this time, through his own merits and his uncle's introductions, Pen was well-introduced into London, known in both literary and polite circles. His fashionable reputation stood him in good stead; he was considered a gentleman of good present means and better expectations, who wrote for his pleasure—the ultimate recommendation for a young aspirant. Bacon, Bungay, and Co. were proud to accept his articles; Mr. Wenham asked him to dinner; Mr. Wagg looked upon him favorably. They reported how they met him at fashionable houses, where he was welcome because his appearance and address were good, and because he had a character for being clever. Finally, he was asked to one house because he was seen at another. Thus, no small variety of London life presented itself to the young man. He became familiar with everyone from Paternoster Row to Pimlico, feeling as much at home at Mayfair dining-tables as at the tavern boards where his companions of the pen assembled.

Full of high spirits and curiosity, easily adapting himself to all he met, the young fellow pleased himself in this strange jumble of men. He would breakfast at Mr. Plover's in the morning alongside a Peer, a Bishop, a parliamentary orator, two blue ladies of fashion, a popular preacher, the author of the latest novel, and the newest lion imported from Egypt or America. Then, he would quit this distinguished society for the back room of the newspaper office, where pens, ink, and wet proofs awaited him. Here sat Finucane, the sub-editor, with the latest news from the Row. Soon Shandon would arrive, nodding to Pen, and begin scribbling his leading article at the other end of the table, flanked by a pint of sherry that the attendant boy silently brought whenever he appeared. Or Mr. Bludyer's roaring voice would be heard in the front room, where that truculent critic would impound books on the counter despite the timid remonstrances of Mr. Midge, the publisher. After looking through the volumes, Bludyer would sell them at his bookstall, drink and dine upon the produce in a tavern box, and then call for ink and paper to "smash" the author of his dinner and the novel. Towards evening, Mr. Pen would stroll toward his club to pick up Warrington for a constitutional walk. This exercise freed the lungs and created an appetite for dinner, after which Pen had the privilege of bowing at pleasant houses or exploring the town for amusement. There was the Opera, the Eagle Tavern, a ball in Mayfair, or a quiet night with a cigar, a book, and a long talk with Warrington. Or perhaps a wonderful new song at the Back Kitchen. At this stage of his life, Mr. Pen beheld all sorts of places and men, and very likely did not realize how much he enjoyed himself until long after, when balls gave him no pleasure, farces made him not laugh, tavern jokes produced no excitement, and the loveliest dancer never stirred him from his chair after dinner. At his present mature age, all these pleasures are over; the times have passed away. Bludyer will no more bully authors or cheat landlords. Shandon, the learned and thriftless, sleeps his last sleep. They buried honest Doolan the other day; never will he cringe or flatter, never pull a long-bow or empty a whisky-noggin again.

The London season was blooming in full vigor. Fashionable newspapers abounded with information regarding grand banquets, routs, and balls enlivening the polite world. Our gracious Sovereign held levees and drawing-rooms at St. James's. The bow-windows of the clubs were crowded with the heads of respectable, red-faced, newspaper-reading gentlemen. Thousands of carriages trailed along the Serpentine. Squadrons of dandy horsemen trampled over Rotten Row. Everybody was in town, and of course, Major Arthur Pendennis, who was somebody, was not absent.

With his head tied up in a smart bandana handkerchief and his meagre carcass enveloped in a brilliant Turkish dressing-gown, the worthy gentleman sat by his fireside on a certain morning, letting his feet gently simmer in a bath while he took his early cup of tea and perused the *Morning Post*. He could not have faced the day without his two hours' toilet, his early tea, or his *Morning Post*. I suppose nobody in the world, not even Morgan, knew how feeble and ancient the Major was growing, or what numberless little comforts he required.

If men sneer at the artifices of an old beauty—her paint, perfumes, ringlets, and stratagems to reconstruct the charms bereft by time—they should presume that ladies are not ignorant that men are vain as well. How does old Blushington keep that constant rose-tint on his cheeks? Where does old Blondel get the preparation that makes his silver hair pass for golden? Have you ever seen Lord Hotspur get off his horse when he thinks nobody is looking? Taken out of his stirrups, his shiny boots can hardly totter up the steps of Hotspur House. He is a dashing young nobleman on Rotten Row, but on foot, what an old, old fellow! Did you ever form an idea of Dick Lacy in a natural state, without his stays? All these men are objects the observer of human life may contemplate with profit, much like the most elderly Belgravian Venus or inveterate Mayfair Jezebel. An old reprobate daddy-longlegs who has never said his prayers (except perhaps in public) for fifty years; an old buck who still clings to the habits of youth as his feeble grasp of health allows; who has given up the bottle but sits with young fellows over it telling naughty stories upon toast-and-water; who has given up beauty but still talks about it wickedly as the youngest roue in company—such an old fellow, if any parson in Pimlico or St. James's were to order the beadles to bring him into the middle aisle and preach about him, could be turned to a wholesome use for once in his life. But we are wandering from our text: the honest Major sits with his feet cooling in the bath. Morgan takes them out of that place of purification, dries them daintily, and proceeds to set the old gentleman on his legs with waistband, wig, starched cravat, and spotless boots and gloves.

It was during these hours of the toilet that Morgan and his employer had their confidential conversations, for they did not meet much at other times—the Major abhorring the society of his own chairs and tables in his lodgings. Morgan, his master's toilet over and letters delivered, had his time very much on his own hands. He bestowed this spare time among the valets and butlers of the nobility, his acquaintances. Morgan Pendennis, as he was styled (for gentlemen's gentlemen are called by such compound names in private circles), was a frequent and welcome guest at some of the very highest tables in town. A member of two influential clubs in Mayfair and Pimlico, he knew the whole gossip of the town and could entertain his master agreeably during the two hours of toilet conversation. He knew a hundred tales and legends regarding persons of the very highest ton, whose valets canvass their august secrets just as our parlour-maids and dependants discuss our characters, stinginess, generosity, pecuniary means, and domestic tiffs. If I leave this manuscript open on my table, I have not the slightest doubt Betty will read it, and they will talk it over in the lower regions tonight. Tomorrow she will bring in my breakfast with a face of entire imperturbable innocence, that no mortal could suppose her guilty of playing the spy. If you and the Captain have high words, the circumstances of the quarrel will be discussed with impartial eloquence over the kitchen tea-table. Nothing is secret. Take it as a rule that John knows everything. As in our humble world so in the greatest: a duke is no more a hero to his valet-de-chambre than you or I. His Grace's Man at his club, in company with other Men of equal social rank, talks over his master's character and affairs with the ingenuous truthfulness which befits gentlemen met in confidence. Who is a niggard and screws up his money-boxes? Who is in the hands of money-lenders? Who is intimate with whose wife? Who wants whom to marry her daughter, and which he won't, no not at any price? All these facts gentlemen's confidential gentlemen discuss confidentially, known and examined by every person who claims rank in genteel society. In a word, if old Pendennis himself was said to know everything, being admirably scandalous and delightfully discreet, it is but justice to Morgan to say that a great deal of his master's information was supplied by his valet, who went out and foraged knowledge for him. Indeed, what more effectual plan is there to get a knowledge of London society than to begin at the foundation—that is, at the kitchen floor?

So Mr. Morgan and his employer conversed as the latter's toilet proceeded. There had been a drawing-room the previous day, and the Major read among the presentations that of Lady Clavering by Lady Rockminster, and of Miss Amory by her mother Lady Clavering. In a further part of the paper, their dresses were described with a precision and jargon that will puzzle and amuse the antiquary of future generations. The sight of these

Better minds than Morgan, the valet, possessed a clearer understanding of Lady Clavering's actual wealth, yet the London rumor mill spun a tale of staggering opulence. Upon her arrival in the capital, the gossip claimed her fortune was infinite: indigo factories, opium clippers, banks swollen with rupees, and diamonds from native princes. The story went that these princes owed her father vast sums, paying interest that filled her London account with so many zeros it seemed to multiply the letter 'O' in every admirer's gasp. Even an envoy from an Indian Prince, the extraordinary Colonel Altamont—a man rumored to have embraced Islam after surviving a thousand perils—was currently in town, attempting to sell the Nawab of Lucknow's famous nose-ring, "the Light of the Dewan," to the Begum Clavering.

Before Lady Clavering even descended upon the city, her fame, branded as the Begum, had already swept through polite society. It was the boast of Delolme and Blackstone that the British Constitution admits merit of every kind, allowing the lowliest-born to wear the robes of a peer if they deserve it. So, too, should good society boast: though haughty and jealous of its privileges, it instantly removes all barriers for the wealthy. If an individual possesses enough coin, the gates fly open; they eat his dinners, dance at his balls, marry his daughters, or gift their own lovely girls to his sons with the affability of a commoner. This, they argued, proved British independence and honesty; the higher orders were not the haughty aristocrats the ignorant claimed, but rather, if a man had money, they held out their hands, eager to serve.

Chevalier Strong, having overseen the country mansion, now applied his taste to the fashionable London upholsterers preparing the town house for the Claverings. In decorating this elegant abode, honest Strong's soul rejoiced as if he were the proprietor himself. He hung and re-hung pictures, studied sofa placements, and interviewed wine merchants. Simultaneously, the Baronet's factotum furnished his own chambers and stocked his snug cellar. Friends complimented the neatness of Strong's quarters, while select guests sharing his cutlets now enjoyed excellent claret. The Chevalier was "in clover," residing in comfortable rooms at Shepherd's Inn, served by a former Spanish Legionary comrade he had rescued from a fort breach in Spain and elevated to body-servant. This loyal man was none other than the Nawab's favorite, the valiant Colonel Altamont.

Ned Strong, however, was a man of few curiosities and great discretion. He avoided inquiring into the mysterious connection established shortly after their meeting at Baymouth between Sir Francis Clavering and the Nawab's envoy. The Colonel clearly held some secret leverage over Clavering, a hold Strong suspected stemmed from the Baronet's irregular early life and lackluster career in India. Strong knew Clavering was a man weak in purpose, principle, and intellect—a moral and physical poltroon.

After their initial meeting, Strong witnessed several private interviews between Clavering and His Excellency, the nature of which Clavering never disclosed. Yet, Clavering sent letters via Altamont, who acted as his ambassador in all affairs. On one occasion, the envoy was in a foul mood, crushing the letter in his hand and declaring, "A hundred, be hanged. I'll have no more letters nor no more shilly-shally. Tell Clavering I'll have a thousand, or by Jove I'll split, and burst him all to atoms. Let him give me a thousand and I'll go abroad, and I give you my honour as a gentleman, I'll not ask him for no more for a year. Give him that message from me, Strong, my boy; and tell him if the money ain't here next Friday at twelve o'clock, as sure as my name's what it is, I'll have a paragraph in the newspaper on Saturday, and next week I'll blow up the whole concern."

Strong relayed this threat to his principal. The effect was immediate; on the appointed day and hour, the Chevalier appeared at Altamont's hotel at Baymouth with the required sum. Altamont behaved like a gentleman, paid his bill, and departed on a foreign tour, embarking at Dover. Strong concluded it must be forgery at the very least, noting that the Colonel had secured the bill.

However, within the year, the Colonel returned. A "confounded run on the red" had ruined him at Baden Baden; no gentleman could withstand a color rising fourteen times. Forced to draw upon Sir Francis Clavering for his return, Clavering, though pressed for funds due to election expenses and furnishing costs, reluctantly accepted the bill. He cursed the Colonel internally, wishing he were locked in a German debtor's goal for life, yet he paid. These sums were raised without Lady Clavering's knowledge. Though she was sumptuous in her own spending and inherited a knack for business from her father, Snell, she gave her husband only a handsome allowance, demanding accurate accounts for any extra payments. Regarding the subsidies to the Colonel, Clavering frankly told Strong he could not speak to his wife.

Strong's business involved procuring these funds. In his Shepherd's Inn apartments, negotiations flourished between moneyed gentlemen and Sir Francis Clavering, with valuable banknotes and stamped paper changing hands. A man accustomed to debt from youth rarely finds permanent fortune; the money-lender soon returns. Clavering found it more convenient to meet these creditors at Strong's lodgings. The Chevalier, though possessing not a shilling of his own, drew almost all the bills of exchange Clavering accepted. He discounted them in the City, parleyed with holders when due, and exchanged instalments for fresh acceptances. Like the troops at Comorn, who danced and played despite the threat of assault and the gallows, hundreds of gallant spirits in this town walked in good spirits, dining in gaiety and sleeping comfortably, with a bailiff nearby and a rope of debt around their necks. Ned Strong, the old soldier, bore these trifling inconveniences with ease.

Meanwhile, Lady Clavering and her friends were kept waiting at the doorsteps of Grosvenor Place. They entered a gorgeous dining room, fitted up in a middle-aged style, which Lady Clavering laughed off, saying, "Unless, said her good-natured ladyship, 'because me and Clavering are middle-aged people.'" They were offered the copious remains of the luncheon they had just shared. Our little Sylphide, who ate scarcely more than six grains of rice at dinner, was most active with her knife and fork, consuming a substantial portion of mutton cutlets—a hypocrisy she shared with other young ladies of fashion. Pen and his uncle declined the food but admired the room, pronouncing it "very chaste," the proper phrase. Indeed, there were high-backed Dutch chairs of the seventeenth century, a sculptured carved buffet of the sixteenth, a sideboard robbed from a Low Country church, and a large brass cathedral lamp over the round oak table. Old family portraits from Wardour Street, French tapestry, bits of armor, double-handed swords and battle-axes of carton-pierre, looking-glasses, saint statuettes, and Dresden china filled the space. Nothing could be chaster.

Behind lay the library, fitted with busts and books of uniform size, wonderful easy-chairs, and solemn bronzes in the severe classic style. Here, guarded by double doors, Sir Francis smoked cigars, read *Bell's Life in London*, and slept after dinner, unless he was smoking over the billiard table at his clubs or punting at Saint James's gambling-houses.

What could equal the chaste splendour of the drawing-rooms? The carpets were so magnificently fluffy that one's foot made no more noise than a shadow. On their white grounds bloomed roses and tulips as large as warming-pans. High and low chairs, bandy-legged seats so attenuated only a sylph could sit upon them, marquetterie-tables covered with gimcracks, china ornaments of all ages, bronzes, gilt daggers, *Books of Beauty*, yataghans, Turkish papooshes, and boxes of Parisian bonbons adorned the room. Everywhere sat Dresden shepherds and shepherdesses; light blue porcelain poodles, ducks, cocks, and hens; nymphs by Boucher and shepherdesses by Greuze, very chaste indeed; muslin and brocade curtains; gilt cages with parroquets and love-birds, two squealing cockatoos out-chattering each other; a clock singing tunes on a console-table, and another booming the hours like Great Tom on the mantelpiece. In a word, everything comfort could desire and elegant taste could devise. A London drawing-room fitted up without regard to expense is surely one of the noblest sights of the day. The Romans of the Lower Empire and the Marchionesses of Louis XV could scarcely have exhibited finer taste than our modern folks. Everyone who saw Lady Clavering's reception rooms confessed them most elegant; even Lady Harley Quin's, Lady Hanway Wardour's, or Mrs. Hodge-Podgson's rooms lacked such consummate "chastity."

Poor Lady Clavering, meanwhile, knew little of these things and showed little respect for the splendours around her. "I only know they cost a precious deal of money, Major," she said to her guest, "and that I don't advise you to try one of them gossamer gilt chairs: I came down on one the night we gave our second dinner-party. Why didn't you come and see us before? We'd have asked you to it."

"You would have liked to see Mamma break a chair, wouldn't you, Mr. Pendennis?" dear Blanche said with a sneer, angry because Pen was laughing with Mamma, who had made blunders describing the house.

"I should like to have been by to give Lady Clavering my arm if she had need of it," Pen answered, bowing and blushing.

"Quel preux Chevalier!" cried the Sylphide, tossing up her little head.

"I have a fellow-feeling with those who fall, remember," Pen said. "I suffered myself very much from doing so once."

"And you went home to Laura to console you," said Miss Amory. Pen winced. He disliked the memory of Laura's consolation and was displeased that his rebuff was known; so, turning his attention to the furniture, he praised Lady Clavering's taste with all his might.

"No, don't praise me," said honest Lady Clavering. "It's all the upholsterer's doings and Captain Strong's, they did it all while we was at the Park—and—and—Lady Rockminster has been here and says the salongs are very well," she added with deference.

"My cousin Laura has been staying with her," Pen said.

"It's not the dowager: it is the Lady Rockminster."

"Indeed!" cried Major Pendennis, hearing the great name of fashion. "If you have her ladyship's approval, Lady Clavering, you cannot be far wrong. No, no, you cannot be far wrong. Lady Rockminster, I should say, Arthur, is the very centre of the circle of fashion and taste. The rooms are beautiful indeed!" The Major's voice hushed as he spoke, surveying the apartments awfully and respectfully, as if at church.

"Yes, Lady Rockminster has took us up," said Lady Clavering.

"Taken us up, Mamma," cried Blanche, in a shrill voice.

"Well, taken us up, then," said my lady. "It's very kind of her, and I dare say we shall like it when we git used to it, only at first one don't fancy being took—well, taken up, at all. She is going to give our balls for us; and wants to invite all our dinners. But I won't stand that. I will have my old friends and I won't let her send all the cards out, and sit mum at the head of my own table. You must come to me, Arthur and Major—come, let me see, on the 14th.—It ain't one of our grand dinners, Blanche," she said, looking round at her daughter, who bit her lips and frowned savagely.

The Major, with a smile and a bow, said he would much rather come to a quiet meeting than a grand dinner. He had had enough of large entertainments and preferred the simplicity of the home circle.

"I always think a dinner's the best the second day," said Lady Clavering, trying to mend her speech. "On the 14th we'll be quite a snug little party;" at which blunder, Miss Blanche clasped her hands in despair, saying, "O, mamma, vous etes incorrigible." Major Pendennis vowed he liked snug dinners of all things and confounded her ladyship's impudence for daring to ask such a man as him to a second day's dinner. But he was economical, bethinking himself he could throw over these people if anything better offered, and accepted with the blandest air. As for Pen, not yet a diner-out of thirty years' standing, the idea of a fine feast in a fine house was perfectly welcome.

"What was that pretty little quarrel which engaged itself between your worship and Miss Amory?" the Major asked Pen as they walked away. "I thought you used to au mieux in that quarter."

"Used to be," answered Pen, with a dandified air, "is a vague phrase regarding a woman. Was and is are two very different terms, sir, as regards women's hearts especially."

"Egad, they change as we do," cried the elder. "When we took the Cape of Good Hope, I recollect there was a lady who talked poisoning herself for your humble servant; and, begad, in three months she ran away from her husband with somebody else. Don't get yourself entangled with that Miss Amory. She is forward, affected, and under-bred; and her character is somewhat—never mind what. But don't think of her; ten thousand pound won't do for you. What, my good fellow, is ten thousand pound? I would scarcely

On the appointed day, Major Pendennis and Arthur Pendennis arrived at Sir Francis Clavering's townhouse with the enthusiasm of two men who had absolutely nothing else to do. They found the drawing-room occupied only by Sir Francis, his wife, and Captain Strong. Arthur beamed with delight at the sight of Strong, a man he clearly admired, while his uncle, the Major, glared at him with the sour expression of a man forced to dine with a house steward. "Clavering's d—— house-steward," the Major muttered, though he quickly softened his features when Mr. Welbore Welbore, a dull but wealthy country gentleman, breezed in. The Major felt instantly safe in the presence of such solid, ancient respectability.

The company swelled with the Dowager Lady Rockminster, Lady Agnes Foker, and her son Harry, alongside the inevitable Miss Blanche Amory. Blanche arrived in a white silk dress that seemed to glow with its own internal light, displaying shoulders that Arthur deemed "pearly" and "killing." She was, in short, a stunner. She greeted Arthur with an affectionate simplicity, asking after his mother and Laura, and expressing a longing for the country that was so genuine it bordered on artlessness.

Harry Foker, usually a bit shy around ladies, found his tongue loosened by Blanche's charm. He spoke with a liveliness that surprised his own mother, who watched him from afar with wide eyes. As the party moved downstairs, Harry hoped to escort Blanche, but fate—or social protocol—assigned him to Mrs. Welbore Welbore, leaving Blanche to accompany the Major. However, Harry's ambition was not crushed; he maneuvered himself to sit directly beside her at the dinner table, flattered that he had secured such a prime spot, though it may have been arranged by someone else entirely.

At the table, Blanche found herself sandwiched between the two young men, each vying for her attention. Harry, eager to impress, launched into a monologue about Taglioni and *La Sylphide*, humming the famous air and asking if she had seen the dancer. "Miss Amory is a sylph herself," the Major interjected smoothly. Blanche, charmed, praised Harry's tenor voice and expressed a desire to sing with him. The Major, remembering similar advances from years past, felt a twinge of jealousy but masked it with a tender compliment, claiming he preferred her voice to Laura's contralto. He declared himself tired of loud professionals and London's blase atmosphere, insisting he only cared for the songs of his youth.

"I like English music best," Blanche agreed, turning to Harry to order a saddle of mutton. "Sing me one of the old songs after dinner," the Major pleaded. Blanche, however, turned her gaze to Harry, promising to sing if he would come up to tea immediately. Harry, a simple soul, accepted with a grin, explaining his modest drinking habits and ordering jelly with cherries inside, baffling the Major with his question on how the cherries got inside. Blanche listened to Harry's chatter with inexhaustible patience, dropping her gloves near him and her handkerchief near the Major, treating both with a kindness that made them both feel special.

When the ladies retired to the drawing-room, Blanche left a final, bright glance that swept over Captain Strong's white waistcoat and landed squarely on Harry. The door closed, leaving Harry sighing and swallowing a bumper of claret.

The dinner concluded early, allowing the ladies to retreat to the piano while the gentlemen lingered over wine. Through the open windows, the street scene unfolded: children peering at the fruit on the table, carriages rattling past, and the policeman patrolling the pavement. Among the passersby was a man gorgeously attired in chains, shiny boots, and a wig that defied sobriety. He was Captain Strong, laughing loudly through the window, recounting a joke that made a policeman pause in confusion.

Suddenly, the man outside began to squabble with the policeman, shouting that he knew everyone in the room, including Sir Francis and "that d—— Altamont." Inside, Sir Francis recognized the voice and the intruder. It was Colonel Altamont, a deserter from the Horse Artillery currently serving the Nawab of Lucknow. Strong rushed out to manage the situation, dragging the intoxicated Colonel into the house.

Altamont, furious at the indignity of being stopped at the door, demanded wine. Sir Francis, pale and agitated, managed to usher him into the dining room. The Colonel, reeling with triumph, scanned the room, glaring at the footman Jeames before sinking into a chair. He began drinking voraciously, making encomiastic remarks about the vintage.

"Very singular man," Strong noted gravely. "Indian habits."
"Very," the Major replied dryly.

Harry Foker whispered to the Major that this was the man who had sung the Malay song at the Back Kitchen. The Colonel, however, was in no mood for subtlety. Spotting the Major, he roared, "I know you, too, young fellow! Baymouth ball! Wanted to fight the Frenchman!" He laughed wildly, recalling the incident with drunken fervor.

"Mr. Pendennis, you remember Colonel Altamont?" Strong prompted.
The Major bowed stiffly. "He had the pleasure of remembering that circumstance perfectly."
"What's his name?" the Colonel bellowed.
"Pendennis!" the Major corrected sharply. "My name is Pendennis, and I will be obliged to you not to curse it too loudly."

The Colonel paused, his eyes narrowing as he stared at the Major. For a moment, the drunken haze seemed to lift, replaced by a fierce, sober intensity. Then, as if shaken by a sudden realization, he buttoned his coat, stumbled toward the door, and vanished, shouting, "Captain Beak! Captain Beak, by jingo!"

Within twenty minutes, the Colonel had arrived and departed. The remaining guests were bewildered. Sir Francis looked terrified, and the Major, realizing the Colonel was a deserter in foreign service, breathed a sigh of relief.

The evening continued with music. Blanche played the piano, enrapturing Harry, while the Major sat silently, watching her with a newfound appreciation. He later complimented her singing and pronunciation effusively, a stark contrast to his earlier disdain. After the party dispersed, the Major walked Harry Foker home, urging him to visit Grosvenor Place the next day. As they walked down the Strand, Harry couldn't stop talking about Blanche and her music, leaving the Major to wonder at the boy's single-minded devotion.

Since that fateful, delightful night in Grosvenor Place, the heart of Mr. Harry Foker had been in a state of such violent agitation that it would have been impossible to imagine a man of his supposed philosophical depth enduring such turmoil. One must recall the excellent counsel he had previously dispensed to Arthur Pendennis, the early wisdom that marked this gifted youth, and the fact that a life of constant self-indulgence, befitting a gentleman of his means, should theoretically have hardened him into a cynic who cared for no one but himself. Yet, here was Harry Foker, a man who had supposedly mastered the world, suddenly falling victim to the universal plague that strikes us all once or twice in our lives: the disquieting power of a woman. Though early wise, Foker was still merely a man. He could not escape the common lot any more than Achilles, Ajax, Lord Nelson, or Adam himself. Now, his time had arrived, and young Harry became a prisoner to Love, the All-Conqueror.

That night, when he retreated to the Back Kitchen after bidding farewell to Arthur Pendennis at the staircase door in Lamb Court, the gin-twist and deviled turkey held no charm for him. The jokes of his companions fell flat on his ears. Even when Mr. Hodgen, the chronicler of 'The Body Snatcher,' launched into a new chant even more dreadful and humorous than his famous composition, Foker, bound by politeness and his status as a chief of the Back Kitchen, offered a perfunctory "Bravo, Hodgen," yet he failed to hear a single word of the song, later immortalized as 'The Cat in the Cupboard.' Exhausted and late, he slipped into his private apartments, but the fever of his soul kept him awake. The moment he stirred from his agitated sleep, the image of Miss Amory materialized, declaring, "Here I am, your princess and beauty; you have discovered me, and shall care for nothing else henceforth."

Suddenly, his former pursuits and friendships seemed stale and distasteful. Until now, he had rarely associated with women of his own rank, dismissing them as "modest women"—a virtue that, he felt, failed to compensate for the lack of lively qualities found in his relatives. His mother, though tender, did not amuse him; his cousins, the daughters of the Earl of Rosherville, wore him thin. One was a blue geologist, another a cigar-smoking horsewoman, and the third a High Church fanatic who turned her room into an oratory and fasted every Friday. Their paternal home, Drummington, was a place he swore he would rather endure on the treadmill than visit. To Lord Erith, his cousin, Harry was a vulgar low-life; to Harry, Erith was a dull prig, the Speaker's opprobrium, and a dreary philanthropist. The memory of an evening when Harry poked his uncle in the side with a billiard cue, declaring, "Well, old cock, I've seen many a bad stroke, but never such a bad one as that," remained a source of deep shame for the Earl, who shunned his nephew thereafter, though he dared not banish him entirely to spare his sister Agnes's heart.

Furthermore, there was the matter of Lady Ana Milton, the fifth daughter of Lord Gravesend, whom fate had destined for Harry since childhood. From her nursery days, it was ordained that when Harry reached maturity, he would wed Lady Ann. Both young people had acquiesced without protest. When Harry's father declared, "Harry, your uncle and I have agreed you'll marry Lady Ann. She has no money, but she is good blood and looks well. Refuse, and you'll have your mother's jointure and two hundred a year," Harry, trusting his sire implicitly, had replied, "Well, sir, if Ann's agreeable, I say ditto. She's not a bad-looking girl." He had hung a small, insignificant portrait of his cousin in his sitting-room, a poor figure beside the French prints of dancing nymphs and opera stars that adorned his walls.

But now, the instant Foker awoke the morning after Lady Clavering's dinner, Blanche's image glared upon him with clear grey eyes and a winning smile. Her tune rang in his ears: "Yet round about the spot, ofttimes I hover." Opposite him, a French print depicted a Turkish lady and her Greek lover caught by a venerable Ottoman; on another wall, a gentleman and lady rode and kissed at full gallop. The room was a gallery of gauzy nymphs, lovely illustrations, and signed autographs of Miss Calverley in tight pantaloons and Miss Rougemont as Venus. These were the treasures of honest Harry, an idle, jovial fast man about town. Yet, a letter from Miss Calverley, scrawled in a degage style calling him "Hokey-pokey-fokey," lay on his bedside table amidst keys, sovereigns, and a sprig of verbena from Miss Amory. Suddenly, the prospect of dining at the Elephant and Castle with that old woman filled him with dreary disgust.

When his fond mother, Lady Agnes, beheld her son's pallor and gloom, she fretted over his billiards at Spratt's and the Back Kitchen. Harry, however, revealed a perfect confidence to her, bringing home choice anecdotes from clubs and billiard rooms that the simple lady relished. He artfully praised the Claverings' magnificent display of plate and Sir Francis's agreeable nature, selecting a date only a fortnight hence for their invitation to Grosvenor Street. But as Lady Agnes wrote the card, Harry noticed an omission. "You have not asked Miss Whatdyecallem—Miss Amory," he insisted. Reluctantly, his mother added the name. Triumphant, Harry embraced his parent and retired to his ottoman, brooding on schemes to bring Miss Amory under his roof.

His polyglot valet, Anatole, noted the disturbance in his master's spirits. That morning, Harry was impossible to please, cursing his pantaloons and boots. In a moment of blushing hesitation, he asked Anatole if he could dress hair. Anatole twisted Harry's locks until his head was as curly as a negro's. Dressed in splendor, Harry ordered his drag to Pelham Corner, but then hesitated. "Confound her! Put the dinner off—I can't go!" he cried, though he immediately reversed himself, ordering the carriage to Pelham Corner at six.

For what purpose? Why did he need to see Pendennis so urgently? Because, as the narrator notes, when this mishap befalls a young lady, people who were once indifferent become fascinating, while former loves turn insipid. Harry, suddenly valuing Pendennis because he had been near the "fragrant flower of love," rushed to Lamb Court. Warrington informed him that Pen was gone to the printing office. Disconsolate, Foker visited the *Pall Mall Gazette*, only to find Pen had left again. With no courage to face Lady Clavering directly, he delivered two engraved cards to Jeames, who bowed his powdered head and closed the varnished doors.

Heartbroken, Harry ordered his pony to Tattersall's. Just as he mounted the fiery animal, Lady Clavering's splendid barouche drove up. Dodging the carriage, Harry followed it through the Green Park until he saw Lady Clavering enter a shop with Miss Amory, clad in a gossamer dress and pink bonnet. The carriage proceeded to Madame Rigodon's, Mrs. Wolsey's, and finally to Hunter's for ices. Harry, on his pony with a red jacket, dodged them until they entered the Park. He dashed forward, hoping for a nod of recognition across the ditch, but received none.

As Miss Amory returned to her carriage, Harry joined a group of young men in the Park. They mocked the "Indian widow" and speculated on Lady Clavering's debts and gambling habits. When they mentioned Miss Amory's singing, comparing her to a mermaid, Harry turned red with tears in his eyes, protesting his ignorance of classical literature. "I never said I was a good scholar," he stammered. Alone, he lamented his wasted youth, realizing he was a "poor, ignorant little beggar" fit only for Foker's Entire, while she could compose music as easily as he drank beer.

As he cantered out of Rotten Row, he nearly collided with a large family carriage. A cheery voice cried, "Harry, Harry!" It was his aunt, Lady Rosherville, and her daughters, including his betrothed, Lady Ann. Harry started back, pale and scared, the truth crashing over him: his fate sat in the back seat. "What is the matter, Harry?" Lady Ann asked. He muttered a greeting, waved a hurried goodbye, and fled on his pony as the great carriage rolled away, leaving him behind in the dust.

Carries the Reader both to Richmond and Greenwich

Poor Foker found the dinner at Richmond to be the most dreary entertainment upon which ever mortal man wasted his guineas. "I wonder how the deuce I could ever have liked these people," he thought in his own mind. "Why, I can see the crow's-feet under Rougemont's eyes, and the paint on her cheeks is laid on as thick as Clown's in a pantomime! The way in which that Calverley talks slang, is quite disgusting. I hate chaff in a woman. And old Colchicum! that old Col, coming down here in his brougham, with his coronet on it, and sitting bodkin between Mademoiselle Coralie and her mother! It's too bad. An English peer, and a horse-rider of Franconi's!—It won't do; by Jove, it won't do. I ain't proud; but it will not do!"

"Twopence-halfpenny for your thoughts, Fokey!" cried out Miss Rougemont, taking her cigar from her truly vermilion lips, as she beheld the young fellow lost in thought, seated at the head of his table, amidst melting ices, and cut pineapples, and bottles full and empty, and cigar-ashes scattered on fruit, and the ruins of a dessert which had no pleasure for him.

"Does Foker ever think?" drawled out Mr. Poyntz. "Foker, here is a considerable sum of money offered by a fair capitalist at this end of the table for the present emanations of your valuable and acute intellect, old boy!"

"What the deuce is that Poyntz a talking about?" Miss Calverley asked of her neighbour. "I hate him. He's a drawlin', sneerin' beast."

"What a droll of a little man is that little Fokare, my lor'," Mademoiselle Coralie said, in her own language, and with the rich twang of that sunny Gascony in which her swarthy cheeks and bright black eyes had got their fire. "What a droll of a man! He does not look to have twenty years."

"I wish I were of his age," said the venerable Colchicum, with a sigh, as he inclined his purple face towards a large goblet of claret.

"C'te Jeunesse. Peuh! je m'en fiche" said Madame Brack, Coralie's mamma, taking a great pinch out of Lord Colchicum's delicate gold snuff-box. "Je m'aime que les hommes faits, moi. Comme milor. Coralie! n'est-ce pas que tu n'aimes que les hommes faits, ma bichette?"

My lord said, with a grin, "You flatter me, Madame Brack."

"Taisez-vous, Maman, vous n'etes qu'une bete," Coralie cried, with a shrug of her robust shoulders; upon which, my lord said that she did not flatter at any rate; and pocketed his snuff-box, not desirous that Madame Brack's dubious fingers should plunge too frequently into his Mackabaw.

There is no need to give a prolonged detail of the animated conversation which ensued during the rest of the banquet; a conversation which would not much edify the reader. And it is scarcely necessary to say, that all ladies of the corps de dance are not like Miss Calverley, any more than that all peers resemble that illustrious member of their order, the late lamented Viscount Colchicum. But there have been such in our memories who have loved the society of riotous youth better than the company of men of their own age and rank, and have given the young ones the precious benefit of their experience and example; and there have been very respectable men too who have not objected so much to the kind of entertainment as to the publicity of it. I am sure, for instance, that our friend Major Pendennis would have made no sort of objection to join a party of pleasure, provided that it were en petit comite, and that such men as my Lord Steyne and my Lord Colchicum were of the society. "Give the young men their pleasures," this worthy guardian said to Pen more than once. "I'm not one of your strait-laced moralists, but an old man of the world, begad; and I know that as long as it lasts young men will be young men." And there were some young men to whom this estimable philosopher accorded about seventy years as the proper period for sowing their wild oats: but they were men of fashion.

Mr. Foker drove his lovely guests home to Brompton in the drag that night; but he was quite thoughtful and gloomy during the whole of the little journey from Richmond; neither listening to the jokes of the friends behind him and on the box by his side nor enlivening them as was his wont, by his own facetious sallies. And when the ladies whom he had conveyed alighted at the door of their house, and asked their accomplished coachman whether he would not step in and take something to drink, he declined with so melancholy an air, that they supposed that the Governor and he had had a difference or that some calamity had befallen him; and he did not tell these people what the cause of his grief was, but left Mesdames Rougemont and Calverley, unheeding the cries of the latter, who hung over her balcony like Jezebel, and called out to him to ask him to give another party soon.

He sent the drag home under the guidance of one of the grooms, and went on foot himself; his hands in his pockets, plunged in thought. The stars and moon shining tranquilly overhead, looked down upon Mr. Foker that night, as he in his turn sentimentally regarded them. And he went and gazed upwards at the house in Grosvenor Place, and at the windows which he supposed to be those of the beloved object; and he moaned and he sighed in a way piteous and surprising to witness, which Policeman X did, who informed Sir Francis Clavering's people, as they took the refreshment of beer on the coach-box at the neighbouring public-house, after bringing home their lady from the French play, that there had been another chap hanging about the premises that evening—a little chap, dressed like a swell.

And now with that perspicuity and ingenuity and enterprise which only belongs to a certain passion, Mr. Foker began to dodge Miss Amory through London, and to appear wherever he could meet her. If Lady Clavering went to the French play, where her ladyship had a box, Mr. Foker, whose knowledge of the language, as we have heard, was not conspicuous, appeared in a stall. He found out where her engagements were (it is possible that Anatole, his man, was acquainted with Sir Francis Clavering's gentleman, and so got a sight of her ladyship's engagement-book), and at many of these evening parties Mr. Foker made his appearance—to the surprise of the world, and of his mother especially, whom he ordered to apply for cards to these parties, for which until now he had shown a supreme contempt. He told the pleased and unsuspicious lady that he went to parties because it was right for him to see the world: he told her that he went to the French play because he wanted to perfect himself in the language, and there was no such good lesson as a comedy or vaudeville,—and when one night the astonished Lady Agnes saw him stand up and dance, and complimented him upon his elegance and activity, the mendacious little rogue asserted that he had learned to dance in Paris, whereas Anatole knew that his young master used to go off privily to an academy in Brewer Street, and study there for some hours in the morning. The casino of our modern days was not invented, or was in its infancy as yet; and gentlemen of Mr. Foker's time had not the facilities of acquiring the science of dancing which are enjoyed by our present youth.

Old Pendennis seldom missed going to church. He considered it to be his duty as a gentleman to patronise the institution of public worship and that it was quite a correct thing to be seen at church of a Sunday. One day it chanced that he and Arthur went thither together: the latter, who was now in high favour, had been to breakfast with his uncle, from whose lodging they walked across the park to a church not far from Belgrave Square. There was a charity sermon at Saint James's, as the Major knew by the bills posted on the pillars of his parish church, which probably caused him, for he was a thrifty man, to forsake it for that day: besides he had other views for himself and Pen. "We will go to church, sir, across the Park; and then, begad, we will go to the Claverings' house and ask them for lunch in a friendly way. Lady Clavering likes to be asked for lunch, and is uncommonly kind, and monstrous hospitable."

"I met them at dinner last week, at Lady Agnes Foker's, sir," Pen said, "and the Begum was very kind indeed. So she was in the country: so she is everywhere. But I share your opinion about Miss Amory; one of your opinions, that is, uncle, for you were changing the last time we spoke about her."

"And what do you think of her now?" the elder said.

"I think her the most confounded little flirt in London," Pen answered, laughing "She made a tremendous assault upon Harry Foker, who sat next to her; and to whom she gave all the talk, though I took her down."

"Bah! Henry Foker is engaged to his cousin all the world knows it: not a bad coup of Lady Rosherville's, that. I should say, that the young man at his father's death, and old Foker's life's devilish bad: you know he had a fit at Arthur's, last year: I should say, that young Foker won't have less than fourteen thousand a year from the brewery, besides Logwood and Norfolk property. I've no pride about me, Pen. I like a man of birth certainly, but dammy, I like a brewery which brings in a man fourteen thousand a year; hey, Pen? Ha, ha, that's the sort of man for me. And I recommend you now that you are lanced in the world, to stick to fellows of that sort, to fellows who have a stake in the country, begad."

"Foker sticks to me, sir," Arthur answered. "He has been at our chambers several times lately. He has asked me to dinner. We are almost as great friends, as we used to be in our youth: and his talk is about Blanche Amory from morning till night. I'm sure he's sweet upon her."

"I'm sure he is engaged to his cousin, and that they will keep the young man to his bargain," said the Major. "The marriages in these families are affairs of state. Lady Agnes was made to marry old Foker by the late Lord, although she was notoriously partial to her cousin who was killed at Albuera afterwards, and who saved her life out of the lake at Drummington. I remember Lady Agnes, sir, an exceedingly fine woman. But what did she do?—of course she married her father's man. Why, Mr. Foker sate for Drummington till the Reform Bill, and paid dev'lish well for his seat, too. And you may depend upon this, sir, that Foker senior, who is a parvenu, and loves a great man, as all parvenus do, has ambitious views for his son as well as himself, and that your friend Harry must do as his father bids him. Lord bless you! I've known a hundred cases of love in young men and women: hey, Master Arthur, do you take me? They kick, sir, they resist, they make a deuce of a riot and that sort of thing, but they end by listening to reason, begad."

"Blanche is a dangerous girl, sir," Pen said. "I was smitten with her myself once, and very far gone, too," he added; "but that is years ago."

"Were you? How far did it go? Did she return it?" asked the Major, looking hard at Pen.

Pen, with a laugh, said "that at one time he did think he was pretty well in Miss Amory's good graces. But my mother did not like her, and the affair went off." Pen did not think it fit to tell his uncle all the particulars of that courtship which had passed between himself and the young lady.

"A man might go farther and fare worse, Arthur," the Major said, still looking queerly at Pen.

"Her birth, sir; her father was the mate of a ship, they say: and she has not money enough," objected Pen, in a dandified manner. "What's ten thousand pound and a girl bred up like her?"

"You use my own words, and it is all very well. But, I tell you in confidence, Pen,—in strict honour, mind,—that it's my belief she has a devilish deal more than ten thousand pound: and from what I saw of her the other day, and—and have heard of her—I should say she was a devilish accomplished, clever girl: and would make a good wife with a sensible husband."

"How do you know about her money?" Pen asked, smiling. "You seem to have information about everybody, and to know about all the town."

"I do know a few things, sir, and I don't tell all I know. Mark that," the uncle replied. "And as for that charming Miss Amory,—for charming, begad! she is,—if I saw her Mrs. Arthur Pendennis, I should neither be sorry nor surprised, begad! and if you object to ten thousand pound, what would you say, sir, to thirty, or forty, or fifty?" and the Major looked still more knowingly, and still harder at Pen.

"Well, sir," he said to his godfather and namesake, "make her Mrs. Arthur Pendennis. You can do it as well as I."

"Psha! you are laughing at me, sir," the other replied rather peevishly, "and you ought not

Mr. Pen's life, much like a merchant's ledger, had specific columns for different kinds of transactions. One column was for procuring money, another for securing social standing, and a third, far more volatile, was for the procurement of literary fame. It was in this third column that he currently found himself, staring down at a manuscript that had lain dormant in a dusty chest for nearly half a decade.

The chest itself was a museum of his past: it held his old Oxbridge scribbling-books, a battered academic gown, a surplice that smelled of mothballs, and several shooting jackets that had seen better decades. But the centerpiece was the novel. Written in the feverish twilight of his youth, fueled by the Byronic despair of a defeated student and the Wertherian melancholy of a heartbroken lover, the book was titled *Leaves from the Life-book of Walter Lorraine*.

One morning, Pen dragged the volume onto the table, the movement causing his tea to slosh violently and the milk in the jug to dance a frantic jig. He threw his hands up, a bitter laugh escaping him. "By Jove!" he declared to Warrington, who sat calmly puffing his pipe. "When I think these were written but a few years ago, I am ashamed of my memory. I wrote this believing myself eternally in love with that little coquette, Miss Amory. I used to carve verses into the hollow of trees and dedicate them 'Amori.'"

"That was a sweet little play on words," Warrington remarked, taking a thoughtful puff. "'Amory' to 'Amori.' It showed proof of scholarship. Let us hear a bit of the rubbish."

With the casual authority of a man handling a live coal, Warrington snatched the manuscript with his fire-tongs. "False as thou art beautiful! heartless as thou art fair! mockery of Passion!' Walter cried, addressing Leonora..."

"Cut that part!" Pen lunged, but Warrington held firm. "Well, don't read it out at any rate. That's about my other flame, my first—Lady Mirabel. I saw her last night at Lady Whiston's. She asked me to a party, saying that as old friends we ought to meet oftener. She has been seeing me for two years and never invited me until she saw Wenham talking to me, and Monsieur Dubois, the French literary man who might have passed for a Marshal of France. The Claverings are there too. Won't it be exciting to meet one's two flames at the same table?"

"Two flames!—two heaps of burnt-out cinders," Warrington scoffed. "Are both the beauties in this book?"

"Both, or something like them," Pen admitted. "Leonora, who marries the Duke, is the Fotheringay. I drew the Duke from Magnus Charters, with whom I was at Oxford; it's a little like him; and Miss Amory is Neaera. By gad, that first woman! I thought of her as I walked home from Lady Whiston's in the moonlight; and the whole early scenes came back to me as if they had been yesterday."

"And when I got home," Pen continued, his voice rising with the ghost of his former passion, "I pulled out the story which I wrote about her and the other three years ago: do you know, outrageous as it is, it has some good stuff in it, and if Bungay won't publish it, I think Bacon will."

"That's the way of poets," Warrington said, returning the manuscript to the table with a dismissive flick. "They fall in love, jilt, or are jilted; they suffer and they cry out that they suffer more than any other mortals: and when they have experienced feelings enough they note them down in a book, and take the book to market. All poets are humbugs, all literary men are humbugs; directly a man begins to sell his feelings for money he's a humbug. If a poet gets a pain in his side from too good a dinner, he bellows Ai Ai louder than Prometheus."

"I suppose a poet has a greater sensibility than another man," Pen countered, his spirit flaring. "That is what makes him a poet. You speak eagerly enough in your leading articles when you espy a false argument in an opponent, or detect a quack in the House. Paley, who does not care for anything else in the world, will talk for an hour about a question of law. Give another the privilege which you take yourself, and the free use of his faculty, and let him be what nature has made him. Why should not a man sell his sentimental thoughts as well as you your political ideas, or Paley his legal knowledge? Each alike is a matter of experience and practice. It is not money which causes you to perceive a fallacy, or Paley to argue a point; but a natural or acquired aptitude for that kind of truth: and a poet sets down his thoughts and experiences upon paper as a painter does a landscape or a face upon canvas, to the best of his ability, and according to his particular gift. If ever I think I have the stuff in me to write an epic, by Jove I will try. If I only feel that I am good enough to crack a joke or tell a story, I will do that."

"Not a bad speech, young one," Warrington conceded, though his eyes remained skeptical. "But that does not prevent all poets from being humbugs."

"What—Homer, Aeschylus, Shakspeare and all?"

"Their names are not to be breathed in the same sense with you pigmies," Mr. Warrington snapped. "There are men and men, sir."

"Well, Shakspeare was a man who wrote for money, just as you and I do," Pen answered, at which Warrington confounded his impudence and resumed his pipe and his manuscript.

There was not the slightest doubt then that this document contained a great deal of Pen's personal experiences, and that *Leaves from the Life-book of Walter Lorraine* would never have been written but for Arthur Pendennis's own private griefs, passions, and follies. Now, though he had kept it in his box for nearly half of the period during which, according to the Horatian maxim, a work of art ought to lie ripening, Mr. Pen had not buried his novel for this time in order that the work might improve, but because he did not know where else to bestow it, or had no particular desire to see it.
