A man who thinks of putting away a composition for ten years before he shall give it to the world, or exercise his own maturer judgment upon it, had best be very sure of the original strength and durability of the work; otherwise on withdrawing it from its crypt he may find, that like small wine it has lost what flavour it once had, and is only tasteless when opened. There are works of all tastes and smacks, the small and the strong, those that improve by age, and those that won't bear keeping at all, but are pleasant at the first draught, when they refresh and sparkle.

Now Pen had never any notion, even in the time of his youthful inexperience and fervour of imagination, that the story he was writing was a masterpiece of composition, or that he was the equal of the great authors whom he admired; and when he now reviewed his little performance, he was keenly enough alive to its faults, and pretty modest regarding its merits. It was not very good, he thought; but it was as good as most books of the kind that had the run of circulating libraries and the career of the season. He had critically examined more than one fashionable novel by the authors of the day then popular, and he thought that his intellect was as good as theirs and that he could write the English language as well as those ladies or gentlemen; and as he now ran over his early performance, he was pleased to find here and there passages exhibiting both fancy and vigour, and traits, if not of genius, of genuine passion and feeling. This, too, was Warrington's verdict, when that severe critic, after half an hour's perusal of the manuscript, and the consumption of a couple of pipes of tobacco, laid Pen's book down, yawning portentously. "I can't read any more of that balderdash now," he said; "but it seems to me there is some good stuff in it, Pen, my boy. There's a certain greenness and freshness in it which I like somehow. The bloom disappears off the face of poetry after you begin to shave. You can't get up that naturalness and artless rosy tint in after days. Your cheeks are pale, and have got faded by exposure to evening parties, and you are obliged to take curling-irons, and macassar, and the deuce-knows-what to your whiskers; they curl ambrosially, and you are very grand and genteel, and so forth; but, ah! Pen, the spring-time was the best."

"What the deuce have my whiskers to do with the subject in hand?" Pen said (who, perhaps, may have been nettled by Warrington's allusion to those ornaments, which, to say the truth, the young man coaxed, and curled, and oiled, and perfumed, and petted, in rather an absurd manner). "Do you think we can do anything with 'Walter Lorraine'? Shall we take him to the publishers, or make an auto-da-fe of him?"

"I don't see what is the good of incremation," Warrington said, "though I have a great mind to put him into the fire, to punish your atrocious humbug and hypocrisy. Shall I burn him indeed? You have much too great a value for him to hurt a hair of his head."

"Have I? Here goes," said Pen, and 'Walter Lorraine' went off the table, and was flung on to the coals. But the fire having done its duty of boiling the young man's breakfast-kettle, had given up work for the day, and had gone out, as Pen knew very well; Warrington with a scornful smile, once more took up the manuscript with the tongs from out of the harmless cinders.

"Oh, Pen, what a humbug you are!" Warrington said; "and what is worst of all, sir, a clumsy humbug. I saw you look to see that the fire was out before you sent 'Walter Lorraine' behind the bars. No, we won't burn him: we will carry him to the Egyptians, and sell him. We will exchange him away for money, yea, for silver and gold, and for beef and for liquors, and for tobacco and for raiment. This youth will fetch some price in the market; for he is a comely lad, though not over strong; but we will fatten him up and give him the bath, and curl his hair, and we will sell him for a hundred piasters to Bacon or to Bungay. The rubbish is saleable enough, sir; and my advice to you is this: the next time you go home for a holiday, take 'Walter Lorraine' in your carpet-bag—give him a more modern air, prune away, though sparingly, some of the green passages, and add a little comedy, and cheerfulness, and satire, and that sort of thing, and then we'll take him to market, and sell him. The book is not a wonder of wonders, but it will do very well."

"Do you think so, Warrington?" said Pen, delighted, for this was great praise from his cynical friend.

"You silly young fool! I think it's uncommonly clever," Warrington said in a kind voice. "So do you, sir." And with the manuscript which he held in his hand he playfully struck Pen on the cheek. That part of Pen's countenance turned as red as it had ever done in the earliest days of his blushes: he grasped the other's hand and said, "Thank you, Warrington," with all his might: and then he retired to his own room with his book, and passed the greater part of the day upon his bed re-reading it; and he did as Warrington had advised, and altered not a little, and added a great deal, until at length he had fashioned 'Walter Lorraine' pretty much into the shape in which, as the respected novel-reader knows, it subsequently appeared.

Whilst he was at work upon this performance, the good-natured Warrington artfully inspired the two gentlemen who "read" for Messrs. Bacon and Bungay with the greatest curiosity regarding 'Walter Lorraine,' and pointed out the peculiar merits of its distinguished author. It was at the period when the novel, called 'The Fashionable,' was in vogue among us; and Warrington did not fail to point out, as before, how Pen was a man of the very first fashion himself, and received at the houses of some of the greatest personages in the land. The simple and kind-hearted Percy Popjoy was brought to bear upon Mrs. Bungay, whom he informed that his friend Pendennis was occupied upon a work of the most exciting nature; a work that the whole town would run after, full of wit, genius, satire, pathos, and every conceivable good quality. We have said before, that Bungay knew no more about novels than he did about Hebrew or Algebra, and neither read nor understood any of the books which he published and paid for; but he took his opinions from his professional advisers and from Mrs. B., and, evidently with a view to a commercial transaction, asked Pendennis and Warrington to dinner again.

Bacon, when he found that Bungay was about to treat, of course, began to be anxious and curious, and desired to outbid his rival. Was anything settled between Mr. Pendennis and the odious house "over the way" about the new book? Mr. Hack, the confidential reader, was told to make inquiries, and see if any thing was to be done, and the result of the inquiries of that diplomatist was, that one morning, Bacon himself toiled up the staircase of Lamb Court and to the door on which the names of Mr. Warrington, and Mr. Pendennis, were painted.

For a

If you've ever wondered where the real magic of London hides, look no further than Shepherd's Inn. Nestled right next to Lincoln's-Inn Fields and the Temple, tucked behind the grimy gables and soot-blackened chimneys of Wych Street and Holywell Street, lies a quadrangle that seems to have forgotten how to let the sun in. You find it through winding, smoky alleyways where the light barely dares to tread. Here, the air is thick with the scent of slop-sellers, brandy-ball vendors, and peddlers of cheap theatrical prints for the young. The shops sell dingy furniture that promises comfort but delivers only dreams of sleeplessness.

The doors are ringed with bells, and the steps are crowded with dirty children forming endless, noisy groups. Around the shellfish dealers' trays, the damp pavement echoes with the clatter of pattens, stained perpetually by mud. Ballad singers drone out satirical songs against the Whigs, the bishops, and even the German relatives of royalty, while Punch sets up his tiny theatre, confident of an audience and a halfpenny. Women scream after loitering kids or, worse, at husbands stumbling out of gin-shops. It is a ceaseless, chaotic din—a perfect contrast to the quiet, old-fashioned peace of the Inn's central courtyard. In the middle of a patchy grass plot stands the statue of Shepherd, guarded by iron rails from the inevitable assault of boys. The Inn's hall, painted with the founder's arms, lines one side, while tall, ancient chambers wrap around the others, opening onto Oldcastle Street and the great London thoroughfare beyond.

Long ago, this place belonged to lawyers, but the law has largely moved on. Today, the ground floor houses the offices of the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum Copper Mines and the Registry of Patent Inventions. The only gentleman of note listed in the "Law List" is Mr. Campion, a man with mustachios who arrives in his cab two or three times a week. In his West End offices on Curzon Street, he is Mr. Somerset Campion, lending money to nobility; here, in Shepherd's Inn, he is simply "Campion and Co." The same tuft of hair that decorates his chin sprouts from the lower lip of his entire firm. It is a spectacle to see his cab-horse's harness blazing with heraldic bearings as the vehicle stops; the horse froths at the mouth, chafing under the shiny bit, while the groom's white breeches and reins gleam like a sudden burst of sunshine in the gloom.

Our old friend, Captain Costigan, spends his afternoons inspecting Campion's cab and horse. Dressed in carpet slippers and a dressing-gown, with his old hat cocked over one eye, he trails about the court like a ghost of a bygone era. He suns himself there after breakfast, visiting the porter's lodge to pat the children's heads and chat with Mrs. Bolton about the theatres and her daughter, Leedy Mirabel. Mrs. Bolton, once a dancer at the Wells as the thirteenth of Mr. Serle's pupils, knows a thing or two about the stage.

Costigan lives on the third floor of No. 4, in the rooms formerly belonging to Mr. Podmore—whose name still hangs above the door, though almost every door in Shepherd's Inn bears someone else's name. After the charming tenor singer Charley Podmore married and moved to Lambeth, he handed over his chambers to Mr. Bows and Captain Costigan, who share them. You can often hear Mr. Bows's piano tinkling when the windows are open, either for amusement or to teach Fanny Bolton, the porteress's daughter. Fanny, with her good voice and pretty figure, dreams of emulating her mother's glory. In exchange for cleaning and cooking for the two gentlemen, Mr. Bows teaches her music. She thinks the Captain is the finest gentleman in the world, ignoring his unfortunate tendency toward liquor, believing all fashionable men indulge in excess. She loves Mr. Bows too, and this shy, eccentric old man returns the affection with a fatherly warmth, for his heart is full of kindness and he cannot be at ease unless he loves someone.

Captain Costigan has certainly hosted distinguished visitors before, and in the mornings, he speaks with such grandeur that you'd imagine Sir Charles and Lady Mirabel drop by daily to visit "the honest old half-pay Captain, poor old Jack." But the truth is far less romantic. Lady Mirabel left her husband's card in the little mirror over the mantelpiece months ago and hasn't visited her father in person recently. Upon marrying Sir Charles, she settled a small pension on him, allowing him occasional access to their table. At first, Cos's behavior in high society was merely absurd; he dressed in his best and spoke in the longest, richest words he could muster. "Was your Leedyship in the Pork to dee?" he would ask. "I looked for your equipage in veen!" He would claim to have attended dozens of Levees at Dublin Castle and threaten to leave his yard upon Apsley House. He was magnificent in his courtesy, addressing not just the masters and guests but the servants, who struggled to maintain their gravity while serving Captain Costigan.

However, after a few visits, Cos's caution vanished. He would maintain sobriety until he reached the Back Kitchen, where he would brag about his son-in-law's wines until his speech failed him over his sixth tumbler of whisky-punch. Familiarity bred disaster; he prematurely inebriated himself at Sir Charles's table, leading to a carriage being called and the hospitable door shutting in his face. He spoke sadly to his friends at the Kitchen about being a "thankless child" and a "pore worn-out lonely old man," drowning his sorrows in punch.

When his credit ran dry, he began begging money from his daughter, sometimes making statements that weren't entirely truthful. Once, a bailiff was about to take him to prison; he wrote a desperate letter asking for three pounds and five shillings to "liberate a poor man's grey hairs from gaol." Lady Mirabel sent the money with a stern warning to be more economical. Another time, he broke a plate-glass window in the Strand. Again, the money came, delivered by her servant to the Captain's "aide-de-camp"—a poorer Irish gentleman sent to run errands. If the servant had followed, he would have seen the Captain at the Roscius Head, Harlequin Yard, Drury Lane, in pawn for rum-and-water, surrounded by his staff. On a third occasion, claiming illness, he asked for funds for a physician. Alarmed, Lady Mirabel drove to Shepherd's Inn. The porteress pointed to No. 4, third floor, "name of Podmore over the door." Inside, Cos opened the door in his shirt-sleeves, ready with a gridiron to cook mutton-chops Mrs. Bolton had bought.

It was also unpleasant for Sir Charles Mirabel to receive constant letters stating the Captain was waiting in the hall, or to rush out of his brougham at the Travellers' Club to avoid his father-in-law seizing him. Sir Charles, a weak, elderly man with many infirmities, cried to his wife about the ordeal, declaring he must move abroad or to the country, fearing death or another fit if he saw that man again. Only after a second stern visit, where Lady Mirabel threatened to withdraw his allowance entirely if he continued to plague her husband with letters or street encounters, did she manage to keep her papa in order. She also rebuked Mr. Bows for failing to watch the Captain, forbidding him to drink in taverns and telling the owners to refuse him credit. "Papa's conduct is bringing me to the grave," she declared, though she looked perfectly healthy. "And you, as an old man... ought to be ashamed of abetting him." Honest Bows received these thanks for his lifelong devotion, though one wonders if the old philosopher was much better off than many others.

Next door, at No. 3, live two other acquaintances: Colonel Altamont, agent to the Nawaub of Lucknow, and Captain Chevalier Edward Strong. Their door bears no name. The Captain prefers anonymity, listing a Jermyn Street hotel on his cards, while the Colonel claims to be on a confidential mission independent of the East India Company. "In fact," Strong explains, "Colonel Altamont's object is financial; he wishes to sell some of the principal diamonds and rubies of the Lucknow crown to private capitalists, avoiding the India House."

Since the arrival of Sir Francis Clavering, Strong's chambers have been comfortably furnished. The Chevalier boasts that few retired captains are more snugly quartered than he. Below are three rooms: an office where Strong conducts his mysterious business, a bedroom, and a sitting-room. A private stair leads to two upper apartments—one for the Colonel, the other a kitchen and bedroom for Mr. Grady, the attendant. These rooms are on the same level as Bows and Costigan's at No. 4, connected by leads over which Grady can reach the mignonette box blooming in Bows's window.

From Grady's kitchen often wafted fragrant odors. The three old soldiers guarding No. 3 were culinary experts. Grady mastered Irish stew; the Colonel was famous for pilau and curry; and Strong could cook anything. French dishes, Spanish stews, fricassees, and omelettes came from his hands to perfection. When his purse was full or his credit good, he was the most hospitable man in England, offering friends a good dinner, a good glass of wine, and a good song. Poor Cos often heard with envy the roar of Strong's choruses and the musical clinking of glasses from his distant room, separated yet near to those festivities. It wasn't expedient to invite Costigan always; his drunkenness was lamentable, and he bored Strong's guests with stories when sober and maudlin tears when drunk.

They were a strange, motley set, these friends of the Chevalier. Though Major Pendennis wouldn't have relished their company, Arthur and Warrington enjoyed it, and Pen found it as amusing as the society of the finest gentlemen in the finest houses. Every man had a history, a tide of luck and bad fortune. Jack Holt, once in Don Carlos's army, was now organizing a tobacco smuggling scheme promising thirty thousand a year for a fifteen hundred-pound bribe. Tom Diver, from the Mexican navy, knew of a sunken specie-ship carrying three hundred and eighty thousand dollars and gold bars, offering eighteen hundred pounds to dive for it and return to Parliament. Keightley, manager of the underwater copper mines, had a Smyrna Sponge Company and a quicksilver operation in view. Filby had been everything: a dragoon, field-preacher, missionary, and Greenwich fair actor, inheriting a property from his deceased father whose location remained a mystery. Adding to this mix was Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., who liked their society despite lacking their convivial powers. Made much of for his wealth and position, he told little stories and sang a few songs with great affability, having seen the inside of more prisons than one and signed many stamped papers in his past.

When Altamont first returned from Paris, having communicated with Sir Francis Clavering from a hotel in a denuded state despite his entrusted wealth, Strong was sent by the Baronet to pay his bills and invited to stay at the chambers. To negotiate with Altamont was one thing, but having such a person settled in his rooms and burdened with his society did not suit the Chevalier's taste. He grumbled to his principal.

"I wish you would put this bear into somebody else's cage," he told Clavering. "The fellow's no gentleman. I don't like walking with him. He dresses himself like a nigger on a holiday. I took him to the play the other night; and, by Jove, sir, he abused the actor playing the villain and swore so that the people in the boxes wanted to turn him out. The after-piece was 'The Brigand,' where Wallack comes in wounded and dies. When he died, Altamont began to cry like a child, saying it was a d——d shame, crying and swearing so that there was another row and everybody laughing. Then I had to take him away because he wanted to take his coat off to one fellow who laughed at him and bellowed to him to stand up like a man. Who is he? Where the deuce does he come from? You had best tell me the whole story, Frank. You and he have robbed a church together, that's my belief. You had better get it off your mind at once, Clavering, and tell me what this Altamont is, and what hold he has over you."

"Hang him! I wish he was dead!" was the Baronet's only reply. His countenance grew so gloomy that Strong dared not question him further, though he resolved to discover for himself the secret tie between Altamont and Clavering if need be.

The morning sun had barely warmed the dust of Shepherd's Inn when Colonel Altamont burst into Captain Strong's sitting room, a whirlwind of dyed whiskers and desperate energy. Inside, the Chevalier sat comfortably in his easy chair, a newspaper draped over one knee and a cigar glowing in the other hand. Strong was a man who made a home anywhere, and by the time Altamont arrived, he had already demolished a breakfast of fried eggs and broiled rashers prepared with military precision by Grady. He possessed a temperament as resilient as his digestion; whether facing battle or the Fleet, he slept soundly and woke with a hearty appetite, his rosy cheeks untouched by the cares of the night.

Altamont's first act was to bellow for a pint of pale ale. Grady poured it into a pewter flagon, which the Colonel then transferred directly to his lips. He drained the tankard, wiped his mouth with his dressing gown, and let out a long, satisfied breath.

"Nothing beats that beer when the coppers are hot," Altamont declared, his voice booming. "I've drunk a dozen pints of Bass at Calcutta, and—"

"And at Lucknow, I suppose?" Strong laughed, lighting his cigar. "I got the beer for you specifically; I knew you'd need it after last night."

The Colonel began to recount his adventures of the previous evening, beating his head with his large hand. "I'm a madman once the liquor hits me," he confessed. "I can't trust myself with a spirit bottle. I start drinking and can't stop until it's empty, and then Lord knows what I say. I dined quietly at home, Grady gave me just two tumblers, and I intended to stay sober at the Black and Red like a parson. But why did you leave that sample bottle of Hollands in the cupboard? Grady had to leave me the kettle boiling, and I couldn't resist it. I washed it all down, by Jove, and probably had more at that thieves' den afterward."

"You were there too?" Strong asked. "Before you reached Grosvenor Place? That was early for a clear-out."

"Early hours for a drunkard, eh? Yes, like a great fool, I went there. Found Blackland and young Moss dining with two or three other rogues. If we'd gone to Rouge et Noir, I'd have won. But we tried hazard on the dining table. I dropped all the money you gave me in the morning. That set me wild. I went off thinking to get more from Clavering, but then... I don't remember much until I woke up this morning to hear old Bows playing the piano at No. 4."

Strong mused as he lit his cigar. "I'd like to know how you always extract money from Clavering, Colonel."

Altamont burst into laughter. "Ha, ha! He owes it to me!"

"I don't see that as a reason for Frank to pay," Strong replied dryly. "He owes plenty to others."

"Well, he pays because he's fond of me," Altamont grinned with a sneer. "He loves me like a brother. You know he does, Captain. —No? Well, perhaps he doesn't. But if you ask no questions, I'll tell no lies. Put that in your pipe and smoke it, my boy."

"I'll give up that brandy bottle," Altamont continued after a pause. "I must, or it will ruin me."

"It makes you say queer things," Strong said, staring him hard in the face. "Remember what you said last night at Clavering's table."

"Say? What did I say?" Altamont asked hastily. "Did I split anything? Dammy, Strong, did I split anything?"

"Ask me no questions, and I'll tell you no lies," the Chevalier replied. Strong recalled Altamont's abrupt departure from the Baronet's dining room upon recognizing Major Pendennis, but he resolved to seek an explanation elsewhere. "No," he said gently, "you didn't 'split,' Colonel. It was merely a trap to see if you'd speak. But you were too far gone to say a word anyone could understand."

Relieved, Altamont heaved a great sigh. "Yes, I own to my faults," he continued. "There are three things I can't resist: a bottle of brandy, a box of dice, and a beautiful woman. No man of pluck or spirit has ever escaped them. From the age of fifteen, when I ran away to be a cabin-boy on an Indiaman, until now at fifty, women have been my ruin. One of them, with black eyes and jewels and ermine like a duchess, swept off the best part of a thousand pounds I had in Paris. I was cautious at first, living like a gentleman at Meurice's hotel, never playing except at public tables. Then I met a swell fellow named Bloundell-Bloundell, with white kid gloves and a tuft to his chin. He took me to Madame the Countess de Foljambe's soirées. Such a woman, Strong! Her eye, her hand at the piano! She'd sing until she warbled your soul out of your body. She invited me to her Tuesday parties, and I gave her dinners and opera boxes. But it wasn't the dinners that drained Clavering's money; it was the tables."

"One night at the Countess's," Altamont recounted, "we had supper with Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, the Honourable Deuceace, and the Marquis de la Tour de Force. Champagne flowed, then brandy. The Countess mixed punch for me, and we played cards until I didn't know what I was doing. I was taken to bed, woke with a roaring headache, and found the Honourable Deuceace waiting. 'How are you, Colonel?' he asked. 'How long did you stay after I left? The play got too high, and I lost enough to you for one night.'

"'To me?' I asked. 'How's that, my dear feller?' He told me he'd borrowed thirty louis at vingt-et-un and produced an I.O.U. I pulled out my card-case—the Countess had worked it for me—and there was the note. He paid me thirty louis in gold. I was a gentleman, so I asked if he wanted anything else. But Deuceace didn't drink in the morning and left.

"Shortly after, Bloundell-Bloundell and the Marquis arrived. 'Bong jour, Marky,' I said. 'Good morning—no headache?' They declared I showed no signs of intoxication, taking my liquor as grave as a judge.

"'So,' said the Marquis, 'Deuceace has been with you. Has he settled? Get it while you can; he's a slippery card.'

"'He has paid me,' I lied. 'But I knew no more than the dead he owed me.'

"Their smiles grew sharper. Bloundell said, 'Colonel, you're a queer feller. No man could suppose you'd tasted anything stronger than tea and yet forget things. Come, tell that to the marines.'

"'En effet,' the Marquis twiddled his mustachios. 'Affairs of honour are best settled at once. Perhaps it won't be inconvenient to arrange our little matters of last night.'

"'What little matters?' I asked. 'Do you owe me money, Marky?'

"'Bah!' he snapped. 'I have your note for three hundred and forty louis.' Bloundell added, 'And mine for two hundred and ten.'

"I sprang out of bed in a rage. 'Are you here to make a fool of me? I don't owe you two hundred or two thousand louis! I won't pay a farthing. I laugh at your notes and at you. I believe you're a couple of——'

"'A couple of what?' Bloundell asked. 'We are men of honour, Colonel Altamont, and not here to trifle. Pay us, or we expose you as a cheat and chastise you as one.'

"'Oui, parbleu,' the Marquis agreed. I could have thrown him out the window, but Bloundell was a large man, weighing three stones more than me and standing six inches taller.

"'Monsieur will pay, or give me the reason why,' Bloundell insisted. 'You're little better than a polisson.'

"I was in the thick of the row when a friend I'd met at Boulogne entered. He hadn't played and had warned me against Bloundell. He told me the story: the Countess had entreated me to stop, the gentlemen had offered to intervene, but I insisted on large stakes. He charged dead against me. 'I left the Countess crying,' he said. 'She hates these two men. She warned you repeatedly. Now she's in hysterics lest the Marquis puts a bullet through your head. I believe,' he added, 'that woman is distractedly in love with you.'

"'Do you think so?' I asked. My friend told me she had gone down on her knees to him, shouting, 'Save Colonel Altamont!'

"As soon as I was dressed, I called on the lovely woman. She shrieked and nearly fainted. She called me Ferdinand—I'm blest if she didn't. 'I thought your name was Jack,' Strong laughed later, causing Altamont to blush furiously behind his dyed whiskers.

'"A man may have more names than one,' Altamont retorted. 'When with a lady, I like a good one. She cried fit to break your heart. I told her I wouldn't touch a farthing of her jewellery.

'"Then they'll shoot you,' she said. 'They'll kill my Ferdinand.'

'"They'll kill my Jack wouldn't have sounded well in French,' Strong noted.

'"Never mind names,' Altamont said sulkily. 'A man of honour may take any name he chooses.'

"She said they would kill me. I told her, 'No, I won't let that scamp of a Marquis send me out of the world; if he lays a hand on me, I'll brain him, Marquis as he is.'

"At this, the Countess shrank back. 'Do I understand aright?' she asked. 'That a British officer refuses to meet any person who provokes him to the field of honour?'

'"Field of honour be hanged, Countess,' I said. 'You wouldn't have me be a target for that little scoundrel's pistol practice.'

'"Colonel Altamont,' she said, 'I thought you were a man of honour. Good-bye, sir.' She swept out, her voice choking in her handkerchief.

'"Countess!' I rushed after her, seizing her hand.

'"Leave me, Monsieur le Colonel,' she shook me off. 'My father was a General of the Grand Army. A soldier should know how to pay all his debts of honour.'

"What could I do? Everyone was against me. Caroline said I'd lost the money, though I remembered nothing. I had taken Deuceace's money too, but he'd offered it. Every one of these chaps was a man of fashion and honour; the Marky and the Countess were of the first families in France. Rather than offend her, I paid up five hundred and sixty gold Napoleons, plus three hundred I lost trying to get my revenge. I can't tell you now if I was done or not. Sometimes I think I was, but then Caroline was so fond of me. That woman would never have seen me done."

Their conversation was interrupted by a knock at the door. Grady admitted no less a person than Sir Francis Clavering.

"The Governor, by Jove!" Strong exclaimed, surprised.

"What's brought you here?" Altamont growled, looking sternly at the Baronet. "It's no good, I warrant."

Whenever Clavering visited Shepherd's Inn, it was for money. He was a man who never faced his debts squarely; as long as he could renew a bill, his mind was easy. He was made to be ruined, a victim of sharper wits, niggardly and reckless, a dupe because he was too mean to be a successful knave. He had told more lies and endured more baseness to stave off small debts than a braver rogue would need to make a fortune. He was abject and a shuffler even in prosperity. Strong knew him well; they were not friends, but employer and factotum.

Clavering's pale face expressed extreme anger. "So, sir," he addressed Altamont, "you've been at your old tricks."

"Which of 'em?" Altamont sneered.

"You were at the Rouge et Noir last night!" Clavering cried.

"How do you know? Were you there?" Altamont asked. "I was at the Club, but I didn't play on the colours—ask the Captain. I played with the bones. It was hazard, on my word and honour."

"What the deuce do I care how you lose your money, whether at hazard or roulette?" Clavering screamed, his voice rising. "What I will not have is that you should use my name or couple it with yours! Damn him, Strong, why don't you keep him in better order? He's drawn a bill upon me and lost the money on the table. I can't stand it! Flesh and blood won't bear it! Do you know how much I've paid for you?"

"This was only a very little one, Sir Francis—only fifteen pounds, Captain Strong. They wouldn't stand another. It oughtn't to anger you, Governor. Why, it's so trifling I didn't even mention it to Strong. All on account of that confounded liquor I took."

"Liquor or no liquor, sir, it is no business of mine. I don't care what you drink, only it shan't be in my house. And I will not have you breaking into my house at night, intruding yourself on my company. How dared you show yourself in Grosvenor Place last night? What do you suppose my friends must think

Every morning after the festivities at Grosvenor Place and Greenwich, Major Pendennis's friendship for the Clavering family seemed to deepen with the rising sun. His visits became frequent, his attentions to the lady of the house relentless. As an elderly gentleman of the town, he possessed the rare luck of being welcomed into households where a woman of Lady Clavering's distinction was expected. "Would Her Ladyship not delight in the grand entertainment at Gaunt House?" he would inquire. "There is to be a delightful breakfast ball at Viscount Marrowfat's in Fulham. Everyone will be there—including august figures of the highest rank—and there will be a Watteau quadrille in which Miss Amory will surely look enchanting." To these and other diversions, the obsequious old gentleman kindly offered to escort Lady Clavering, while remaining equally ready to assist the Baronet in any manner agreeable to him.

Despite his current station and fortune, the world maintained a chilly demeanor toward Clavering, trailing strange, suspicious rumors in his wake. He was blackballed from two clubs in succession. In the House of Commons, he conversed only with a select few of the most disreputable members, possessing a happy knack for choosing bad society and adapting to it as others do to the company of their superiors. To name all the senators with whom Clavering consorted would be invidious; we need only mention a few. There was Captain Raff, the honorable member for Epsom, who retired after the last Goodwood races, having accepted, as Mr. Hotspur, the whip of the party, claimed, a mission to the Levant; there was Hustingson, the patriotic member for Islington, whose voice is now silent in denouncing corruption since his appointment as Governor of Coventry Island; and there was Bob Freeny of the Booterstown Freenys, a dead shot whom we wish to speak of with every respect. Of all these gentlemen, with whom Mr. Hotspur had to confer in his professional duty, none inspired in him more thorough contempt and dislike than Sir Francis Clavering, the representative of an ancient race who had sat for their own borough of Clavering time out of mind.

"If that man is wanted for a division," Hotspur declared, "ten to one he is to be found in a hell. He was educated in the Fleet, and he has not heard the end of Newgate yet, take my word for it. He'll muddle away the Begum's fortune at thimble-rig, be caught picking pockets, and finish on board the hulks." And if the high-born Hotspur, holding such an opinion of Clavering, could yet remain civil to him for professional reasons, why should not Major Pendennis also have his own motives for attending to this unlucky gentleman?

"He has a very good cellar and a very good cook," the Major remarked. "As long as he is silent, he is not offensive, and he very seldom speaks. If he chooses to frequent gambling tables and lose his money to blacklegs, what matters to me? Don't look too curiously into any man's affairs, Pen, my boy; every fellow has some cupboard in his house, begad, which he would not like you and me to peep into. Why should we try, when the rest of the house is open to us? And a devilish good house, too, as you and I know. And if the man of the family is not all one could wish, the women are excellent. The Begum is not over-refined, but as kind a woman as ever lived, and devilish clever too; and as for the little Blanche, you know my opinion about her, you rogue; you know my belief is that she is sweet on you, and would have you for the asking. But you are growing such a great man, that I suppose you won't be content under a Duke's daughter—Hey, sir? I recommend you to ask one of them, and try."

Perhaps Pen was somewhat intoxicated by his success in the world; and it may also have entered into the young man's mind (his uncle's perpetual hints serving not a little to encourage the notion) that Miss Amory was tolerably well disposed to renew the little flirtation which had been carried on in the early days of both of them, by the banks of the rural Brawl. But he was little disposed to marriage at that moment. Adopting some of his uncle's worldly tone, he spoke rather contemptuously of the institution and in favor of a bachelor life.

"You are very happy, sir," he said, "and you get on very well alone, and so do I. With a wife at my side, I should lose my place in society; and I don't, for my part, much fancy retiring into the country with a Mrs. Pendennis; or taking my wife into lodgings to be waited upon by the servant-of-all-work. The period of my little illusions is over. You cured me of my first love, who, certainly, was a fool, and would have had a fool for her husband, and a very sulky discontented husband too if she had taken me. We young fellows live fast, sir; and I feel as old at five-and-twenty as many of the old fellows—the old bachelors—whom I see in the bow-window at Bays's. Don't look offended, I only mean that I am blasé about love matters, and that I could no more fan myself into a flame for Miss Amory now, than I could adore Lady Mirabel over again. I wish I could; I rather like old Mirabel for his infatuation about her, and think his passion is the most respectable part of his life."

"Sir Charles Mirabel was always a theatrical man, sir," the Major said, annoyed that his nephew should speak flippantly of any person of Sir Charles's rank and station. "He has been occupied with theatricals since his early days. He acted at Carlton House when he was Page to the Prince; he has been mixed up with that sort of thing: he could afford to marry whom he chooses; and Lady Mirabel is a most respectable woman, received everywhere—everywhere, mind. The Duchess of Connaught receives her, Lady Rockminster receives her—it doesn't become young fellows to speak lightly of people in that station. There's not a more respectable woman in England than Lady Mirabel:—and the old fogies, as you call them, at Bays's, are some of the first gentlemen in England, of whom you youngsters had best learn a little manners, and a little breeding, and a little modesty." And the Major began to think that Pen was growing exceedingly pert and conceited, and that the world made a great deal too much of him.

The Major's anger amused Pen. He studied his uncle's peculiarities with a constant relish and was always in a good humor with his worldly old mentor. "I am a youngster of fifteen years' standing, sir," he said adroitly, "and if you think that we are disrespectful, you should see those of the present generation. A protégé of yours came to breakfast with me the other day. You told me to ask him, and I did it to please you. We had a day's sights together, and dined at the club, and went to the play. He said the wine at the Polyanthus was not so good as Ellis's wine at Richmond, smoked Warrington's cavendish after breakfast, and when I gave him a sovereign as a farewell token, said he had plenty of them, but would take it to show he wasn't proud."

"Did he?—did you ask young Clavering?" cried the Major, appeased at once—"fine boy, rather wild, but a fine boy—parents like that sort of attention, and you can't do better than pay it to our worthy friends of Grosvenor Place. And so you took him to the play and tipped him? That was right, sir, that was right:" with which mentor quitted Telemachus, thinking that the young men were not so very bad, and that he should make something of that fellow yet.

As Master Clavering grew into years and stature, he became too strong for the authority of his fond parents and governess; and rather governed them than permitted himself to be led by their orders. With his papa he was silent and sulky, seldom making his appearance, however, in the neighborhood of that gentleman; with his mamma he roared and fought when any contest between them arose as to the gratification of his appetite, or other wish of his heart; and in his disputes with his governess over his book, he kicked that quiet creature's shins so fiercely that she was entirely overmastered and subdued by him. And he would have so treated his sister Blanche, too, and did on one or two occasions attempt to prevail over her; but she showed an immense resolution and spirit on her part, and boxed his ears so soundly that he forbore from molesting Miss Amory, as he did the governess and his mamma, and his mamma's maid.

At length, when the family came to London, Sir Francis gave forth his opinion that "the little beggar had best be sent to school." Accordingly, the young son and heir of the house of Clavering was despatched to the Rev. Otto Rose's establishment at Twickenham, where young noblemen and gentlemen were received preparatory to their introduction to the great English public schools.

It is not our intention to follow Master Clavering in his scholastic career; the paths to the Temple of Learning were made more easy to him than they were to some of us of earlier generations. He advanced towards that fane in a carriage-and-four, so to speak, and might halt and take refreshment almost whenever he pleased. He wore varnished boots from the earliest period of youth, and had cambric handkerchiefs and lemon-colored kid gloves, of the smallest size ever manufactured by Privat. They dressed regularly at Mr. Rose's to come down to dinner; the young gentlemen had shawl dressing-gowns, fires in their bedrooms, horse and carriage exercise occasionally, and oil for their hair. Corporal punishment was altogether dispensed with by the Principal, who thought that moral discipline was entirely sufficient to lead youth; and the boys were so rapidly advanced in many branches of learning that they acquired the art of drinking spirits and smoking cigars even before they were old enough to enter a public school. Young Frank Clavering stole his father's Havannahs and conveyed them to school, or smoked them in the stables at a surprisingly early period of life, and at ten years old drank his champagne almost as stoutly as any whiskered cornet of dragoons could do.

When this interesting youth came home for his vacations, Major Pendennis was as laboriously civil and gracious to him as he was to the rest of the family; although the boy had rather a contempt for old Wigsby, as the Major was denominated, mimicked him behind his back as the polite Major bowed and smirked with Lady Clavering or Miss Amory; and drew rude caricatures, such as are designed by ingenious youths, in which the Major's wig, his nose, his tie, etc., were represented with artless exaggeration. Untiring in his efforts to be agreeable, the Major wished that Pen, too, should take particular notice of this child; inciting Arthur to invite him to his chambers, to give him a dinner at the club, to take him to Madame Tussaud's, the Tower, the play, and so forth, and to tip him, as the phrase is, at the end of the day's pleasures. Arthur, who was good-natured and fond of children, went through all these ceremonies one day; had the boy to breakfast at the Temple, where he made the most contemptuous remarks regarding the furniture, the crockery, and the tattered state of Warrington's dressing-gown; and smoked a short pipe, recounting the history of a fight between Tuffy and Long Biggings at Rose's, greatly to the edification of the two gentlemen his hosts.

As the Major rightly predicted, Lady Clavering was very grateful for Arthur's attention to the boy; more grateful than the lad himself, who took attentions as a matter of course, and very likely had more sovereigns in his pocket than poor Pen, who generously gave him one of his own slender stock of those coins.

The Major, with the sharp eyes with which Nature endowed him, and with the glasses of age and experience, watched this boy and surveyed his position in the family without seeming to be rudely curious about their affairs. But, as a country neighbor, one who had many family obligations to the Claverings, an old man of the world, he took occasion to find out what Lady Clavering's means were, how her capital was disposed, and what the boy was to inherit. Setting himself to work—for what purposes will appear, no doubt, ulteriorly—he soon gained a pretty accurate knowledge of Lady Clavering's affairs and fortune, and of the prospects of her daughter and son. The daughter was to have but a slender provision; the bulk of the property was, as before has been said, to go to the son—his father did not care for him or anybody else; his mother was dotingly fond of him as the child of her latter days; his sister disliked him. Such may be stated in round numbers to be the result of the information which Major Pendennis gathered.

"Ah! my dear madam," he would say, patting the head of the boy, "this boy may wear a baron's coronet on his head on some future coronation, if matters are but managed rightly, and if Sir Francis Clavering would but play his cards well."

At this the widow Amory heaved a deep sigh. "He plays only too much of his cards, Major, I'm afraid," she said. The Major owned that he knew as much; did not disguise that he had heard of Sir Francis Clavering's unfortunate propensity to play; pitied Lady Clavering sincerely; but spoke with such genuine sentiment and sense that her ladyship, glad to find a person of experience to whom she could confide her grief and her condition, talked about them pretty unreservedly to Major Pendennis, and was eager to have his advice and consolation. Major Pendennis became the Begum's confidante and house-friend, and as a mother, a wife,

Noble Henry Foker, whom we had briefly lost sight of, had been diligently engaged in the pursuit of his all-consuming passion. It is a curious economic fact that the pursuit of love is one of the most expensive hobbies a man can indulge in, a truth that young gentlemen and ladies of "ill-regulated minds" might do well to calculate. One sacrifices precious sleep, arrives late to breakfast with a mind already wandering, performs one's daily duties with the enthusiasm of a zombie, neglects friends and family, and finds the blandishments of home utterly unbearable. Life itself becomes intolerable except when one is actively hunting one's flame.

Mr. Foker, having been bred to indulge every propensity, had surrendered completely to this one with his usual selfish enthusiasm. Nor should anyone be surprised that a man who had previously preached sound advice to his friend Arthur Pendennis should now become a slave to passion himself. Who among us has not preached virtue while secretly practicing vice? We reserve our sympathy for the humble and the stumbling; the serene, perfect beings who never fall are simply lonely, and we cannot reach up to talk familiarly with such potentates. So, we leave the angels alone and focus on the mortals who struggle, stumble, and get up again.

The young person in question was not currently in London, and it was certainly not for her that Mr. Foker's mind was currently agitated. But what matters a few failings? Do we need to be angels to be worshipped? Let us admire the diversity of human taste. History is full of magnificent passions: the love of a transported pickpocket for a female convict, the flame of a Polish dwarf for a Dresden baroness, or the recent escapade of Cornet Tozer and Mrs. Battersby. Are we ashamed that the highest-placed Ajax may one day prostrate himself before the pattens of his kitchen maid? There is no poverty, shame, or crime that a woman's perverse fidelity and admirable constant folly will not support, hugging it closer than virtue ever could.

Thus, Henry Foker, Esquire, longed for his love and cursed the fate that separated them. While Lord Gravesend's family had retreated to the country, Harry remained in London, lingering on certainly not to the sorrow of Lady Ann, to whom he was affianced and who did not miss him in the least. Wherever Miss Clavering went, this infatuated young fellow followed, forced to keep his passion a secret lest it explode and collapse him.

A grand entertainment had taken place at Gaunt House on a beautiful June evening. The journals the next day listed the nobility and gentry in dense columns. Among the guests were Sir Francis and Lady Clavering, Miss Amory (secured by the indefatigable Major Pendennis), and our two young friends, Arthur and Harry. Both youths exerted themselves, dancing extensively with Miss Blanche. The worthy Major assumed charge of Lady Clavering, guiding her to the refreshment room—a department where her ladyship particularly distinguished herself. Surrounded by pictures of Titian and Vandyke, enormous salvers, pyramids of flowers, and constellations of wax candles, a supper was served all night. The Major, with admirable patience, administered to Lady Clavering's various wants, calling to solemn male attendants and lovely servant-maids, while suffering silently as he followed the honest woman about.

Mr. Wagg, counting up the dishes Lady Clavering consumed (though his powers of calculation were compromised by his own free consumption of champagne), recommended Mr. Honeyman, Lady Steyne's physician, to check on the Begum the following day.

Sir Francis Clavering appeared, skulked about the magnificent rooms, found the company and splendour distasteful, tossed off a tumbler or two at the buffet, and departed for Jermyn Street to join his friends Loder, Punter, little Moss Abrams, and Captain Skewball at their familiar green table. In the rattle of the box and their agreeable conversation, Sir Francis's spirits rose to their accustomed point of feeble hilarity.

Mr. Pynsent, who had requested Miss Amory's hand, approached to claim her, but having already exchanged scowls of recognition with Arthur Pendennis in the dancing-room, Arthur suddenly rose and claimed Miss Amory as his partner for the present dance. Mr. Pynsent, biting his lips and scowling savagely, withdrew with a profound bow, surrendering his claim. There are some men who are always falling in one's way in life. Pynsent and Pen held a mutual view of each other; each regarded the other accordingly.

"What a confounded conceited provincial fool that is!" thought one. "Because he has written a twopenny novel, his absurd head is turned, and a kicking would take his conceit out of him."

"What an impertinent idiot that man is!" remarked the other to his partner. "His soul is in Downing Street; his neckcloth is foolscap; his hair is sand; his legs are rulers; his vitals are tape and sealing-wax; he was a prig in his cradle; and never laughed since he was born, except three times at the same joke of his chief. I have the same liking for that man, Miss Amory, as I have for that cold boiled veal."

Upon which Blanche, of course, remarked that Mr. Pendennis was wicked, mechant, and perfectly abominable, wondering what he would say when her back was turned.

"Say!—Say that you have the most beautiful figure, and the slimmest waist in the world, Blanche—Miss Amory, I mean. I beg your pardon. Another turn; this music would make an alderman dance."

"And you have left off tumbling when you waltz now?" Blanche asked, archly looking up at her partner's face.

"One falls and one gets up again in life, Blanche; you know I used to call you so in old times, and it is the prettiest name in the world. Besides, I have practised since then."

"And with a great number of partners, I'm afraid," Blanche said, with a little sham sigh and a shrug of the shoulders. And so in truth Mr. Pen had practised a good deal in this life; and had undoubtedly arrived at being able to dance better.

If Pendennis was impertinent in his talk, Foker, on the other hand, so bland and communicative on most occasions, was entirely mum and melancholy when he danced with Miss Amory. To clasp her slender waist was a rapture; to whirl round the room with her was a delirium; but to speak to her, what could he say that was worthy of her? What pearl of conversation could he bring fit for the acceptance of such a Queen of love and wit as Blanche? It was she who made the talk when she was in the company of this love-stricken partner. It was she who asked him how that dear little pony was, and looked at him and thanked him with such a tender kindness and regret, and refused the dear little pony with such a delicate sigh when he offered it. "I have nobody to ride with in London," she said. "Mamma is timid, and her figure is not pretty on horseback. Sir Francis never goes out with me. He loves me like—like a stepdaughter. Oh, how delightful it must be to have a father—a father, Mr. Foker!"

"Oh, uncommon," said Mr. Harry, who enjoyed that blessing very calmly. Upon which, and forgetting the sentimental air which she had just before assumed, Blanche's grey eyes gazed at Foker with such an arch twinkle that both of them burst out laughing, and Harry, enraptured and at his ease, began to entertain her with a variety of innocent prattle—good kind simple Foker talk, flavoured with many expressions by no means to be discovered in dictionaries, and relating to the personal history of himself or horses, or other things dear and important to him, or to persons in the ballroom then passing before them, and about whose appearance or character Mr. Harry spoke with artless freedom, and a considerable dash of humour.

And it was Blanche who, when the conversation flagged, and the youth's modesty came rushing back and overpowering him, knew how to reanimate her companion: asking him questions about Logwood, and whether it was a pretty place? Whether he was a hunting man, and whether he liked women to hunt? (in which case she was prepared to say that she adored hunting)—but Mr. Foker expressing his opinion against sporting females, and pointing out Lady Bullfinch, who happened to pass by, as a horse-godmother, whom he had seen at cover with a cigar in her face, Blanche too expressed her detestation of the sports of the field, and said it would make her shudder to think of a dear sweet little fox being killed, on which Foker laughed and waltzed with renewed vigour and grace.

And at the end of the waltz,—the last waltz they had on that night,—Blanche asked him about Drummington, and whether it was a fine house. His cousins, she had heard, were very accomplished: Lord Erith she had met, and which of his cousins was his favourite? Was it not Lady Ann? Yes, she was sure it was she; sure by his looks and his blushes. She was tired of dancing; it was getting very late; she must go to mamma;—and, without another word, she sprang away from Harry Foker's arm, and seized upon Pen's, who was swaggering about the dancing-room, and again said, "Mamma, mamma!—take me to mamma, dear, Mr. Pendennis!" transfixing Harry with a Parthian shot, as she fled from him.

My Lord Steyne, with garter and ribbon, with a bald head and shining eyes, and a collar of red whiskers round his face, always looked grand upon an occasion of state; and made a great effect upon Lady Clavering, when he introduced himself to her at the request of the obsequious Major Pendennis. With his own white and royal hand, he handed to her ladyship a glass of wine, said he had heard of her charming daughter, and begged to be presented to her; and, at this very juncture, Mr. Arthur Pendennis came up with the young lady on his arm.

The peer made a profound bow, and Blanche the deepest curtsy that ever was seen. His lordship gave Mr. Arthur Pendennis his hand to shake; said he had read his book, which was very wicked and clever; asked Miss Blanche if she had read it,—at which Pen blushed and winced. Why, Blanche was one of the heroines of the novel. Blanche, in black ringlets and a little altered, was the Neaera of 'Walter Lorraine.'

Blanche had read it: the language of the eyes expressed her admiration and rapture at the performance. This little play being achieved, the Marquis of Steyne made other two profound bows to Lady Clavering and her daughter, and passed on to some other of his guests at the splendid entertainment.

Mamma and daughter were loud in their expressions of admiration of the noble Marquis so soon as his broad back was turned upon them. "He said they make a very nice couple," whispered Major Pendennis to Lady Clavering. Did he now, really? Mamma thought they would; Mamma was so flustered with the honour which had just been shown to her, and with other intoxicating events of the evening, that her good-humour knew no bounds. She laughed, she winked, and nodded knowingly at Pen; she tapped him on the arm with her fan; she tapped Blanche; she tapped the Major;—her contentment was boundless, and her method of showing her joy equally expansive.

As the party went down the great staircase of Gaunt House, the morning had risen stark and clear over the black trees of the square; the skies were tinged with pink; and the cheeks of some of the people at the ball,—ah, how ghastly they looked! That admirable and devoted Major above all,—who had been for hours by Lady Clavering's side, ministering to her and feeding her body with everything that was nice, and her ear with everything that was sweet and flattering,—oh! what an object he was! The rings round his eyes were of the colour of bistre; those orbs themselves were like the plovers' eggs whereof Lady Clavering and Blanche had each tasted; the wrinkles in his old face were furrowed in deep gashes; and a silver stubble, like an elderly morning dew, was glittering on his chin, and alongside the dyed whiskers now limp and out of curl.

There he stood, with admirable patience, enduring, uncomplainingly, a silent agony; knowing that people could see the state of his face (for could he not himself perceive the condition of others, males and females, of his own age?)—longing to go to rest for hours past; aware that suppers disagreed with him, and yet having eaten a little so as to keep his friend, Lady Clavering, in good-humour; with twinges of rheumatism in the back and knees; with weary feet burning in his varnished boots,—so tired, oh, so tired and longing for bed! If a man, struggling with hardship and bravely overcoming it, is an object of admiration for the gods, that Power in whose chapels the old Major was a faithful worshipper must have looked upwards approvingly upon the constancy of Pendennis's martyrdom. There are sufferers in that cause as in the other: the negroes in the service of Mumbo Jumbo tattoo and drill themselves with burning skewers with great fortitude; and we read that the priests in the service of Baal gashed themselves and bled freely. You who can smash the idols, do so with a good courage; but do not be too fierce with the idolaters,—they worship the best thing they know.

The Pendennises, the elder and the younger, waited with Lady Clavering and her daughter until her ladyship's carriage was announced, when the elder's martyrdom may be said to have come to an end, for the good-natured Begum insisted upon

The exertions of the previous night at Gaunt House had nearly broken Major Pendennis. As soon as his weary frame could move with safety, he groaned his way to Buxton, seeking relief in the healing waters. Parliament had adjourned, Sir Francis Clavering and his family had departed, and the specific affairs we have tracked had stalled in the brief interlude between chapters. The town itself had emptied out.

The season was concluding. Pen's lawyer neighbors had vanished upon circuit; his fashionable friends had secured passports for the Continent or fled to the Scotch moors for health and excitement. The bow-windows of the Clubs were deserted, the Pall Mall pavement solitary. The red coats had vanished from before the Palace gate; the tradesmen of St. James's were abroad enjoying themselves; the tailors had grown mustaches and headed up the Rhine; the bootmakers were at Ems or Baden, blushing when meeting customers or punting beside creditors at gambling tables. The clergy of St. James's preached to half a congregation containing no distinguished sinners. The band in Kensington Gardens had packed away their brass and silver trumpets. Only two or three old fly-carts crawled by the Serpentine banks. Clarence Bulbul, retained in town by his arduous duties as a Treasury clerk, compared the loneliness of Rotten Row to the vastness of the Arabian desert, likening himself to a Bedouin wending his way through dusty solitude. Warrington had stowed a quantity of Cavendish tobacco in his carpet-bag and retreated to his brother's house in Norfolk. Pen was left alone in his chambers for a while, for this man of fashion could not quit the metropolis when he chose, currently detained by the affairs of his newspaper, the *Pall Mall Gazette*, of which he acted as editor and *chargé d'affaires* during the temporary absence of the chief, Captain Shandon, who was with his family at the salutary watering-place of Boulogne-sur-Mer.

Although Mr. Pen had pronounced himself for years past to be a man perfectly blasé and wearied of life, the truth was that he was an exceedingly healthy young fellow still. He possessed a fine appetite, which he satisfied with the greatest relish at least once a day, and a constant desire for society that showed him to be anything but misanthropical. If he could not get a good dinner, he sat down to a bad one with perfect contentment; if he could not procure the company of witty or great or beautiful persons, he put up with any society that came to hand. He was perfectly satisfied in a tavern-parlour, on board a Greenwich steamboat, in a jaunt to Hampstead with Mr. Finucane, his colleague at the *Gazette*, or in a visit to the summer theatres across the river. He was also friendly with the great Simpson at the Royal Gardens of Vauxhall, where he shook the principal comic singer of the lovely equestrian of the arena by the hand. While he could watch the grimaces or graces of these performers with a satiric humor not deprived of sympathy, he could look on with an eye of kindness at the lookers-on too: at the roystering youth bent upon enjoyment, at the honest parents with delighted children laughing and clapping, at the poor outcasts whose laughter was less innocent though perhaps louder, bringing their shame and youth there to dance and be merry till dawn to get bread and drown care. Of this sympathy with all conditions of men, Arthur often boasted, saying he was pleased to possess it and hoped to retain it to the last. As another man has an ardour for art or music, Mr. Pen said that anthropology was his favorite pursuit; his eyes were always eagerly open to its infinite varieties and beauties, contemplating with unfailing delight all specimens in all places to which he resorted, whether it was the coquetting of a wrinkled dowager in a ballroom or a high-bred young beauty blushing in her prime; whether it was a hulking guardsman coaxing a servant-girl in the Park, or innocent little Tommy feeding the ducks whilst the nurse listened. Indeed, a man whose heart is pretty clean can indulge in this pursuit with an enjoyment that never ceases, only perhaps the more keen because it is secret and has a touch of sadness in it, because he is of his mood and humour lonely, and apart although not alone.

Yes, Pen used to brag and talk in his impetuous way to Warrington. "I was in love so fiercely in my youth," he declared, "that I have burned out that flame forever, I think, and if ever I marry, it will be a marriage of reason that I will make, with a well-bred, good-tempered, good-looking person who has a little money, and so forth, that will cushion our carriage in its course through life. As for romance, it is all done; I have spent that out, and am old before my time—I'm proud of it."

"Stuff!" growled the other. "You fancied you were getting bald the other day, and bragged about it as you do about everything. But you began to use the bear's-grease pot directly the hairdresser told you; and are scented like a barber ever since."

"You are Diogenes," the other answered, "and you want every man to live in a tub like yourself. Violets smell better than stale tobacco, you grizzly old cynic." But Mr. Pen was blushing whilst he made this reply to his unromantic friend, and indeed cared a great deal more about himself still than such a philosopher perhaps should have done. Indeed, considering that he was careless about the world, Mr. Pen ornamented his person with no small pains in order to make himself agreeable to it, and for a weary pilgrim as he was, wore very tight boots and bright varnish.

It was in this dull season of the year, then, on a shining Friday night in autumn, that Mr. Pendennis, having completed at his newspaper office a brilliant leading article—such as Captain Shandon himself might have written, had the Captain been in good-humour and inclined to work, which he never would do except under compulsion—that Mr. Arthur Pendennis having written his article, and reviewed it approvingly as it lay before him in its wet proof-sheet at the office of the paper, bethought him that he would cross the water and regale himself with the fireworks and other amusements of Vauxhall. So he affably put in his pocket the order which admitted "Editor of Pall Mall Gazette and friend" to that place of recreation, and paid with the coin of the realm a sufficient sum to enable him to cross Waterloo Bridge. The walk thence to the Gardens was pleasant; the stars were shining in the skies above, looking down upon the royal property, whence the rockets and Roman candles had not yet ascended to outshine the stars.

Before you enter the enchanted ground, where twenty thousand additional lamps are burned every night as usual, most of us have passed through the black and dreary passage and wickets which hide the splendours of Vauxhall from uninitiated men. In the walls of this passage are two holes strongly illuminated, in the midst of which you see two gentlemen at desks, where they will take either your money as a private individual, or your order of admission if you are provided with that passport to the Gardens. Pen went to exhibit his ticket at the last-named orifice, where, however, a gentleman and two ladies were already in parley before him.

The gentleman, whose hat was very much on one side, and who wore a short and shabby cloak in an excessively smart manner, was crying out in a voice which Pen at once recognized.

"Bedad, sir, if ye doubt me honour, will ye obleege me by stipping out of that box, and——"

"Lor, Capting!" cried the elder lady.

"Don't bother me," said the man in the box.

"And ask Mr. Hodgen himself, who's in the gyardens, to let these leedies pass. Don't be froightened, me dear madam, I'm not going to quarl with this gintleman, at anyreet before leedies. Will ye go, sir, and desoire Mr. Hodgen (whose orther I keem in with, and he's me most intemate friend, and I know he's goan to sing the 'Body Snatcher' here to-noight), with Captain Costigan's compliments, to stip out and let in the leedies—for meself, sir, I've seen Vauxhall, and I scawrun any interfayrance on moi account: but for these leedies, one of them has never been there, and of should think ye'd harly take advantage of me misfartune in losing the ticket, to deproive her of her pleasure."

"It ain't no use, Captain. I can't go about your business," the check-taker said; on which the Captain swore an oath, and the elder lady said, "Lor, ow provokin!"

As for the young one, she looked up at the Captain and said, "Never mind, Captain Costigan, I'm sure I don't want to go at all. Come away, mamma." And with this, although she did not want to go at all, her feelings overcame her, and she began to cry.

"Me poor child!" the Captain said. "Can ye see that, sir, and will ye not let this innocent creature in?"

"It ain't my business," cried the doorkeeper, peevishly, out of the illuminated box. And at this minute Arthur came up, and recognizing Costigan, said, "Don't you know me, Captain? Pendennis!" And he took off his hat and made a bow to the two ladies. "Me dear boy! Me dear friend!" cried the Captain, extending towards Pendennis the grasp of friendship; and he rapidly explained to the other what he called "a most unluckee conthratong." He had an order for Vauxhall, admitting two, from Mr. Hodgen, then within the Gardens, and singing (as he did at the Back Kitchen and the nobility's concerts, the 'Body Snatcher,' the 'Death of General Wolfe,' the 'Banner of Blood,' and other favourite melodies); and, having this order for the admission of two persons, he thought that it would admit three, and had come accordingly to the Gardens with his friends. But, on his way, Captain Costigan had lost the paper of admission—it was not forthcoming at all; and the leedies must go back again, to the great disappointment of one of them, as Pendennis saw.

Arthur had a great deal of good-nature for everybody, and sympathized with the misfortunes of all sorts of people: how could he refuse his sympathy in such a case as this? He had seen the innocent face as it looked up to the Captain, the appealing look of the girl, the piteous quiver of the mouth, and the final outburst of tears. If it had been his last guinea in the world, he must have paid it to have given the poor little thing pleasure. She turned the sad imploring eyes away directly they lighted upon a stranger, and began to wipe them with her handkerchief. Arthur looked very handsome and kind as he stood before the women, with his hat off, blushing, bowing, generous, a gentleman. "Who are they?" he asked of himself. He thought he had seen the elder lady before.

"If I can be of any service to you, Captain Costigan," the young man said, "I hope you will command me; is there any difficulty about taking these ladies into the garden? Will you kindly make use of my purse? And—and I have a ticket myself which will admit two—I hope, ma'am, you will permit me?"

The first impulse of the Prince of Fairoaks was to pay for the whole party, and to make away with his newspaper order as poor Costigan had done with his own ticket. But his instinct, and the appearance of the two women, told him that they would be better pleased if he did not give himself the airs of a grand seigneur, and he handed his purse to Costigan, and laughingly pulled out his ticket with one hand, as he offered the other to the elder of the ladies—ladies was not the word—they had bonnets and shawls, and collars and ribbons, and the youngest showed a pretty little foot and boot under her modest grey gown, but his Highness of Fairoaks was courteous to every person who wore a petticoat whatever its texture was, and the humbler the wearer, only the more stately and polite in his demeanour.

"Fanny, take the gentleman's arm," the elder said; "Since you will be so very kind—I've seen you often come in at our gate, sir, and go in to Captain Strong's at No. 3."

Fanny made a little curtsey, and put her hand under Arthur's arm. It had on a shabby little glove, but it was pretty and small. She was not a child, but she was scarcely a woman as yet; her tears had dried up, and her cheek mantled with youthful blushes, and her eyes glistened with pleasure and gratitude, as she looked up into Arthur's kind face.

Arthur, in a protecting way, put his other hand upon the little one resting on his arm. "Fanny's a very pretty little name," he said, "and so you know me, do you?"

"We keep the lodge, sir, at Shepherd's Inn," Fanny said with a curtsey; "and I've never been at Vauxhall, sir, and Papa didn't like me to go—and—and—O—O—law, how beautiful!" She shrank back as she spoke, starting with wonder and delight as she saw the Royal Gardens blaze before her with a hundred million of lamps, with a splendour such as the finest fairy tale, the finest pantomime she had ever witnessed at the theatre, had never realized. Pen was pleased with her pleasure, and pressed to his side the little hand which clung so kindly to him. "What would I not give for a little of this pleasure?" said the blasé young man.

"Your purse, Pendennis, me dear boy," said the Captain's voice behind him

Costigan left Pen to his dreams, and no urgent dispatch from Mr. Huxter shattered the peace of the morning. When Arthur Pendennis finally opened his eyes, he found himself refreshed by a vitality that the weary Londoner rarely knows. While the City man wakes to the grim arithmetic of 'Change and the counting-house, and the lawyer rises to the weight of pending briefs, Pendennis woke to a different currency: the lingering, intoxicating residue of the previous night's adventure. It was a peculiar kind of strengthener, that brief respite between the thrill of the event and the drudgery of the day, allowing him to face the sun with a heart lighter than usual.

Yet, Pendennis's labor was of a fleeting nature. The electric spark of literary creation burns out quickly in the hired writer, much like a candle in a draft. Once Pegasus is harnessed to the daily grind of the *Pall Mall Gazette*, the noble steed becomes merely another hack, requiring a whip or a ration of corn to move. Since his novelistic success had bumped up his salary, Arthur performed his duties with a competent, if unenthusiastic, diligence. He was a literary sprinter, brilliant in bursts but prone to the prose of the treadmill.

Society offered him neither comfort nor amusement. He was too young to be taken seriously by the established men, whose conversations he could only eavesdrop upon, and too old for the frivolous youth of his own station. He was destined, as fate often decrees, to be a man of solitude, though he would undoubtedly complain about it later. "What a lovely, artless creature that was," he mused, the memory of Fanny Bolton fresh in his mind. "How much sweeter her natural manner than the calculated coquetry of the ballroom belles! Who would have guessed such a rose blooming in a porter's lodge? Does she learn to sing from old Bows? If her voice is as sweet as her speech, it must be divine. I liked those low, veiled tones. 'What would you like me to call you?' Poor little Fanny! I almost adopted the grand air and told her to call me 'Sir.' But no Faust and Margaret nonsense for me. That old Bows, though—he's a dear. A gentleman in rags, a philosopher with a kind heart. He taught her to sing, didn't he? He's had his sorrows, too. I must cultivate him. I'm tired of genteel society anyway. Besides, there's nobody in town. Yes, I'll visit Bows and Costigan. What a rich character Costigan is! Begad, I'll study him for a book."

Thus, our young anthropologist reasoned, and since Saturday was the holiday of the week, leaving the *Gazette* contributors with nothing but ink and idle brains, Pendennis decided to pay a call at Shepherd's Inn.

If Arthur Pendennis had been the most cunning rogue in history, he could hardly have devised a more effective strategy to captivate poor Fanny Bolton than the one he had employed the night before. His dandified air, his conceited generosity, and his genuine honesty—all the very qualities that had allowed him to resist her trembling advances—combined to make her adore him. If one could peek into Fanny's bed, squeezed into a cupboard alongside her two sisters, one would find her tossing restlessly, disturbed by her companions' movements, replaying the delights of the night. Arthur was her hero, the living embodiment of the dashing villains and heroes she had devoured in the greasy volumes of the circulating library. She had been enchanted by the fineness of his linen, the brilliance of his studs, the elegance of his cambric handkerchief, and the jet-black shine of his boots. The Prince had conquered the handmaid. His image haunted her dreams; the blue light of his eyes, the protective smile, the frank laughter—it was all etched into her memory. She remembered his arm around her, the champagne glass he held, and the snide remarks of her friends: Emma Baker, proud of her cheesemonger fiancé, and Betsy Rodgers, fussing over her attorney's clerk.

At two o'clock, after the Boltons had finished dinner (while Mr. B., the porter, was away with the Countess of Estrich's hearse), a figure in a white hat and white trousers appeared under the Inn archway. Fanny was not surprised; she was simply overwhelmed with delight. She knew it was Him. She called to her mother, who was busy upstairs, and rushed to the wicket, pushing aside the other children. How she blushed as she offered her hand! How affably Arthur removed his hat, acknowledging the staring children. He asked Mrs. Bolton if she had slept well after the night's fatigue and hoped she had no headache, adding that since he was passing that way, he couldn't leave without checking on his little partner.

Mrs. Bolton, perhaps initially shy and suspicious, was quickly melted by Pen's inexhaustible good humor. He searched for a seat, finding none free save for a dish-cover or a workbox, so he perched himself on a child's chair. The children erupted in laughter, Fanny the loudest, amazed by His Royal Highness's condescension. To sit in a child's chair! Many years later, Fanny would look at that very chair with a fondness reserved for objects imbued with memory, wondering if the furniture in her own rooms might one day hold similar sweet, smiling faces.

Pen talked volubly, asking about the undertaking business, the number of mutes accompanying Lady Estrich, and the Inn itself. He expressed interest in Mr. Campion's cab, mentioned meeting him in society, and asked if Mrs. Bolton held shares in the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum. He even inquired about Captain Strong and Colonel Altamont. "Wasn't he a stout man?" Pen asked, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper regarding the Colonel's jewelry and wig. "Very black whiskers—in fact, blue black; that is to say, a rich greenish purple!" His waggishness provoked hysterical giggles from the ladies.

"Oh, we know," the ladies replied. "Sir F—— is Sir Francis Clavering; he's often here. Two or three times a week with the Captain. My little boy has been out fetching bill-stamps for him." Mrs. Bolton, thoroughly charmed, blurted out, "O Lor! I beg pardon, I shouldn't have mentioned no secrets," before admitting, "But we know you to be a gentleman, Mr. Pendennis, for I'm sure you have behaved as such. Hasn't Mr. Pendennis, Fanny?"

Fanny loved her mother for this declaration. Casting up her dark eyes to the low ceiling, she affirmed, "Oh, that he has, I'm sure, Ma," with a voice full of emotion.

Curious about the bill-stamps and the transactions in Strong's chambers, Pen asked further questions, which were artlessly answered by Mrs. Bolton. When Colonel Altamont arrived to join the Chevalier, Pen questioned him about his identity and connections, driven by private motives regarding Sir Francis Clavering. These inquiries, cleverly phrased, were met with honest ignorance.

Realizing he was running out of topics, Pen invoked his privilege as a member of the Press. "Would you like any orders for the play?" he asked. The theater was the universal delight of theatrical families. When Mr. Bolton was away on professional duties—which lately involved drinking heavily and becoming unpleasant to his family—they longed to slip out to the theatre, with little Barney keeping the lodge. Pendennis's generous offer of tickets was received with boundless gratitude.

Fanny clapped her hands, her face beaming. She nodded and laughed at her mother, who reciprocated the joy. Mrs. Bolton, far from being too old for admiration, was now Pen's enthusiastic partisan. When two women unite in liking a man, they fuel each other's enthusiasm until the second becomes as eager as the first.

The offer of tickets and other pleasantries put everyone in a perfect mood, except for a brief interruption when a younger child, hearing the name 'Astley's,' declared she wanted to go too. "Don't bother!" Fanny said sharply. "Git-long, Betsy-Jane, do now, and play in the court," her mother added. Thus, the two little ones, Betsy-Jane and Amelia-Ann, departed in their innocent pinafores to disport themselves on the smooth gravel around the statue of Shepherd the Great.

While Pen entertained the ladies, laughing delightedly at his sallies, an old gentleman emerged from the Inn square and peered into the lodge.
