He wore a very blank and rueful expression upon seeing Mr. Arthur seated on a table, looking like Macheath in the play, in easy discourse with Mrs. Bolton and her daughter.

"What! Mr. Bows? How d'you do, Bows?" Pen cried out in a cheery, loud voice. "I was coming to see you, and was asking your address of these ladies."

"You were coming to see me, were you, sir?" Bows said, entering with a sad face and shaking Arthur's hand. "Plague on that old man!" someone thought in the room; and perhaps, someone else besides her.

Arthur Pendennis spotted Mr. Bows and immediately switched on his "loud, cheery" social mode, offering a handshake that was as dashing as it was perfunctory. Yet, beneath the surface of this performance, a distinct blush bloomed on Arthur's cheeks, mirrored instantly by a fluttering crimson signal on Fanny's face. After the initial pleasantries—where Arthur ironically confirmed his intention to visit Mr. Costigan's chambers—the atmosphere in the room curdled into a heavy, guilty silence. To break the tension, Pendennis attempted to recount the previous night's escapades, mimicking Costigan's futile arguments with the Vauxhall check-taker. It was a disaster. No one laughed. Mrs. Bolton was utterly confused by the sketch, Fanny wore an expression of terrified alarm, and old Mr. Bows sat as glum as a musician forced to play a difficult concerto on a broken piano. Realizing his comedy had failed, Pendennis's voice sputtered and died, leaving the room shrouded in darkness once more.

"You were coming to see me, sir," Mr. Bows interjected, his voice dripping with practiced melancholy. "Won't you honor my chambers? They are high up, but—"

"Oh! I live in a garret myself," Pendennis interrupted, seizing the chance to deflect. "And Shepherd's Inn is twice as cheerful as Lamb Court."

"I knew you had third-floor apartments," Bows countered, adopting a theatrical pose with a trembling finger and a hat in hand. "I merely meant the air up three flights of stairs is wholesomer for gentlemen than the stagnant air of a porter's lodge."

"Sir!" Pendennis snapped, his candle flame flaring in sudden indignation. "Will you permit me to choose my society without—"

"You were so polite to mention your visit," Bows said, ignoring the interruption with the smooth cadence of a man who has spent a lifetime listening to orators on stage. "Shall I show you the way? Mrs. Bolton, Mr. Pendennis and I are old friends; we crossed paths at the dawn of his life."

With a final, melodramatic bow, Bows ushered Pendennis out, promising to remember the theater tickets and pressing Fanny's hand before vanishing into the London fog.

"What a nice young man, to be sure!" Mrs. Bolton exclaimed, watching them ascend the court.

"D'you think so, ma?" Fanny asked, her eyes fixed on the retreating figures.

Mrs. Bolton, however, was already lost in a different reverie, comparing Pendennis to a young gentleman she'd known years prior—a city sugar-baker with black whiskers who had driven a dancer named Emly Budd in a tilbury. She recounted their private wedding at St. Bartholomew's and Emly's subsequent rise to becoming a patroness of the Manshing-House Ball, contrasting the commoner's fate with the professional glamour of Lady Mirabel, Costigan's daughter. As she finished her tale, folding the dinner cloth with Fanny, the two women came close. Mrs. Bolton lifted Fanny's chin and kissed her. This time, the blush on Fanny's cheek wasn't born of alarm, but of a sweet, confusing pleasure. *What is love?* she wondered, the question tasting both sweet and bitter.

Once Mrs. Bolton departed on her market errand, Fanny retreated to the window overlooking Bows's door, her gaze locked onto that specific patch of pavement. Inside, her sisters Betsy-Jane and Amelia-Ann were pretending to read a grave tract upside down, oblivious to Fanny's turmoil. Finally, a movement caught her eye. Bows had emerged. He passed the door, but Pendennis followed him. They bowed under the archway together, but Bows did not stop to speak. Minutes later, Bows returned alone, entering the porter's room with a gingerbread cake for the girls. He sat by the door, ostensibly guarding the entrance, waiting for a guest who never appeared. When Mrs. Bolton returned from the market, she found Bows there instead of the man she expected.

The meeting in Bows's attic room was far from satisfactory. Pendennis was sulky, unwilling to engage with Costigan, who had moved from his bedroom only moments before, smelling distinctly of otto-of-whisky. Costigan, however, was in high spirits, shaking a wobbly hand that still managed to hold a razor. He regaled Pendennis with tales of Baden-Baden and Dublin Castle, speaking with the grandiose confidence of a man who believes his own legends. For a moment, the old memories of Chatteris and the bridge returned, softening Pendennis's heart. He shook hands warmly with both men, forgetting entirely about Fanny until Bows began hobbling down the stairs, clearly intent on following him out.

This persistence was the final straw. Pendennis's patience snapped. "Confound him," he thought, "what does he mean by dogging me?" He burst out laughing in the Strand, a sound that was less honest than it was self-mocking. He tried to drown his thoughts in the Panorama, where Care followed him to the top; in the Club, writing a sarcastic letter home; and in the library, where even the serious Mr. Jawkins failed to hold his attention. Finally, passing a Comedy Theatre advertising "Roars of Laughter," he entered the pit. There, dressed in a man's suit, was the lovely Mrs. Leary. But the sight produced no effect. He laughed again, a bitter, hollow sound, remembering the spell she had cast upon him just weeks prior. The magic was gone, leaving only the sharp edge of reality.

In or near the Temple Garden

Fashion had long since abandoned the green and pretty Temple Garden. In Shakespeare's time, it was the stage for York and Lancaster plucking the innocent white and red roses that would later stain their bloody wars; and the learned author of the *Handbook of London* noted that "the commonest and hardiest kind of rose has long ceased to put forth a bud" in that smoky air. Few of the current occupants of the surrounding buildings cared whether roses still grew there, passing the old gate only on their way to chambers. Attorneys' clerks did not carry flowers in their bags or posies under their arms as they dashed to counsel's offices; the few lawyers who took constitutional walks thought little of York and Lancaster, especially since the railroad business was over. Only antiquarians and literary amateurs cared to look at the gardens with interest, imagining Sir Roger de Coverley and Mr. Spectator pacing the road, or Oliver Goldsmith in the summer-house, perhaps meditating on his next 'Citizen of the World,' his new suit from Mr. Filby, or the dunning letter from Mr. Newbery.

Treading heavily on the gravel, rolling majestically in a snuff-coloured suit and a wig sadly lacking the barber's powder and irons, one might see the Great Doctor step up to him, his Scotch lackey trailing at his heels, a little worse for the port wine they'd shared at the Mitre. Mr. Johnson would invite Goldsmith home for tea with Miss Williams. Kind faith of Fancy! Sir Roger and Mr. Spectator were as real to us now as the two doctors and the boozy, faithful Scotchman. The poetical figures lived in our memory just as much as the real personages. And since Mr. Arthur Pendennis was of a romantic and literary turn, by no means addicted to the legal pursuits common in the neighbourhood, we may presume he was cherishing such reflections when, upon the evening after the events of the last chapter, the young gentleman chose the Temple Gardens for exercise and meditation.

On Sunday evenings, the Temple is commonly calm. The chambers are mostly vacant; the great lawyers are hosting grand dinner-parties in the Belgravian or Tyburnian districts; the agreeable young barristers are absent, paying respects to Mr. Kewsy's excellent claret or Mr. Justice Ermine's accomplished daughters. The uninvited entertain themselves in the Club-room with Circuit jokes and points of law. Nobody is in chambers except poor Mr. Cockle, ill and being fed gruel by his laundress; Mr. Toodle, an amateur flutist, piping solitarily from his second-floor window; or young Tiger, the student, whose open windows gush with cigar smoke and whose door is piled with dishes bearing the insignia of Dicks' or the Cock. But stop! Whither does Fancy lead us? It is vacation time, and with the exception of Pendennis, nobody is in Chambers at all.

Perhaps it was solitude that drove Pen into the garden. Although he had never before passed the gate, looking rather carelessly at the flower-beds and groups of citizens on the trim lawn, this evening, as he returned home to his chambers after dining alone at a nearby tavern, he took a fancy to walk in the gardens and enjoy the fresh air and the sight of the shining Thames. After walking briefly, looking at the peaceful groups around him, he grew tired and seated himself modestly in one of the summer-houses flanking the main walk.

What were his cogitations? The evening was delightfully bright and calm; the sky was cloudless; the chimneys on the opposite bank were not smoking; the wharf warehouses looked rosy in the sunshine, clear as if washed for the holiday. Steamers rushed rapidly up and down the stream, laden with holiday passengers. The bells of the multitudinous city churches rang to evening prayers—such peaceful Sabbath evenings as Pen might have remembered in his early days, pacing with his arm round his mother's waist on the terrace before the lawn at home. The sun lit up the little Brawl as well as the broad Thames, sinking majestically behind the Clavering elms and the tower of the familiar village church. Was it thoughts of these, or the sunset merely, that caused the blush in the young man's face? He beat time on the bench to the chorus of the bells, flicked dust from his shining boots with his pocket-handkerchief, started up, stamped his foot, and declared, "No, by Jove, I'll go home." With this resolution, indicating a struggle regarding the propriety of staying or leaving, he stepped out of the summer-house.

He nearly knocked down two little children, who did not reach much higher than his knee, trotting along the gravel-walk with long blue shadows slanting toward the east. One cried out "Oh!" the other began to laugh, saying with a knowing infantile chuckle, "Missa Pendennis!" Arthur looked down and saw his two little friends of the day before, Mesdemoiselles Ameliar-Ann and Betsy-Jane. He blushed furiously, seized the one he had nearly upset, jumped her into the air, and kissed her. At this sudden assault, Ameliar-Ann began to cry in great alarm.

This cry instantly summoned two ladies in clean collars, new ribbons, and grand shawls: Mrs. Bolton in a rich scarlet Caledonian Cashmere and black silk dress, and Miss F. Bolton with a yellow scarf, sweet sprigged muslin, and a parasol—quite the lady. Fanny did not say a word; her eyes flashed a welcome, shining as brightly as the most blazing windows in Paper Buildings. Mrs. Bolton, after admonishing Betsy-Jane, exclaimed, "Lor sir—how very odd that we should meet you year! I ope you ave your ealth well, sir.—Ain't it odd, Fanny, that we should meet Mr. Pendennis?"

"What do you mean by sniggering, Mesdames?" Arthur retorted. "When young Croesus has been staying at a country-house, have you never, by any singular coincidence, been walking with your Fanny in the shrubberies? Have you and your Fanny never happened to be listening to the band of the Heavies at Brighton, when young De Boots and Captain Padmore came clinking down the Pier? Have you and your darling Frances never chanced to be visiting old widow Wheezy at the cottage on the common, when the young curate has stepped in with a tract adapted to the rheumatism? Do you suppose that, if singular coincidences occur at the Hall, they don't also happen at the Lodge?"

It was a coincidence, no doubt: that was all. On the previous day, Mr. Pendennis had simply replied to Miss Bolton's question that while some courts were gloomy, parts of the Temple were cheerful, especially the chambers looking on the river and around the gardens, and that the gardens were a pleasant Sunday walk frequented by many people. Here, by mere chance, all their acquaintances met together, just like so many people in genteel life. What could be more artless, good-natured, or natural?

Pen looked very grave, pompous, and dandified. He was unusually smart and brilliant in his costume: white duck trousers, white hat, multi-colored neckcloth, light waistcoat, gold chains, and shirt-studs gave him the air of a prince of the blood. How his splendour became his figure! Was anybody ever like him? someone thought. He blushed—how his blushes became him! The same individual said to herself. The children, seeing him the day before, had been so struck that after he left, they played at him. Ameliar-Ann, sticking her little chubby fingers into the arm-holes of her pinafore as Pen did with his waistcoat, had said, "Now, Bessy-Jane, I'll be Missa Pendennis." Fanny had laughed till she cried, smothering her sister with kisses for that feat. How happy she was to see Arthur embrace the child!

If Arthur was red, Fanny, on the contrary, was very worn and pale. Arthur remarked it, asking kindly why she looked so fatigued.

"I was awake all night," said Fanny, beginning to blush.

"I put out her candle, and hordered her to go to sleep and leave off readin'," interposed the fond mother.

"You were reading! And what was it that interested you so?" asked Pen, amused.

"Oh, it's so beautiful!" Fanny sighed. "'Walter Lorraine.' How I do hate that Neaera—Neaera—I don't know the pronunciation. And I love Leonora, and Walter, oh, how dear he is!"

How had Fanny discovered the novel of 'Walter Lorraine' and that Pen was the author? This little person remembered every word Mr. Pendennis had spoken the night before, noting how he wrote in books and newspapers. What books? She was so eager to know she almost considered being civil to old Bows, who was suffering under her displeasure since yesterday, but decided first to apply to Costigan. She began by coaxing the Captain, smiling in her most winning way as she arranged his dinner and set his humble apartment in order. She was sure his linen wanted mending (and indeed the Captain's linen-closet contained curious specimens of manufactured flax and cotton). She would mend his shirts—all his shirts. What horrid holes—what funny holes! She put her little face through one of them, laughing at the old warrior in the most winning manner. She would have made a funny little picture looking through the holes. Then she daintily removed Costigan's dinner things, tripping about the room as dancers do at the play; she danced to the Captain's cupboard, produced his whisky-bottle, mixed a tumbler, and must taste a drop—a little drop; and the Captain must sing her one of his songs, his dear songs, and teach it to her. When he sang an Irish melody in his rich quavering voice, fancying it was he who fascinated the little siren, she put her little question about Arthur Pendennis and his novel. Having got an answer, she cared for nothing more, left the Captain at the piano about to sing another song, the dinner-tray on the passage, and the shirts on the chair, and ran downstairs quickening her pace.

Captain Costigan, as he said, was not a literary character, nor had he yet found time to peruse his young friend's elegant performance, though he intended to take an early opportunity of purchasing a copy of his work. But he knew the name of Pen's novel from the fact that Messrs. Finucane, Bludyer, and other frequenters of the Back Kitchen spoke of Mr. Pendennis (and not all with great friendship; for Bludyer called him a confounded coxcomb, and Hoolan wondered that Doolan did not kick him etc.) by the sobriquet of Walter Lorraine. Hence he was able to give Fanny the information she required.

"And she went and ast for it at the libery," Mrs. Bolton said, "—several liberies—and some ad it and it was bout, and some adn't it. And one of the liberies as ad it wouldn't let er ave it without a sovering: and she adn't one, and she came back a-cryin to me—didn't you, Fanny?—and I gave her a sovering."

"And, oh, I was in such a fright lest any one should have come to the libery and took it while I was away," Fanny said, her cheeks and eyes glowing. "And, oh, I do like it so!"

Arthur was touched by this artless sympathy, immensely flattered and moved. "Do you like it?" he said. "If you will come up to my chambers I will—No, I will bring you one—no, I will send you one. Good night. Thank you, Fanny. God bless you. I mustn't stay with you. Good-bye, good-bye." Pressing her hand once, and nodding to her mother and the other children, he strode out of the gardens.

He quickened his pace as he went from them, running out of the gate talking to himself. "Dear, dear little thing," he said, "darling little Fanny! You are worth them all. I wish to heaven Shandon was back. I'd go home to my mother. I mustn't see her. I won't. I won't, so help me——"

As he talked thus and ran, passers-by turning to look at him, he ran against a little old man and perceived it was Mr. Bows.

"Your very umble servant, sir," said Mr. Bows, making a sarcastic bow and lifting his old hat from his forehead.

"I wish you a good day," Arthur answered sulkily. "Don't let me detain you, or give you the trouble to follow me again. I am in a hurry, sir. Good evening."

Bows thought Pen had some reason for hurrying to his rooms. "Where are they?" exclaimed the old gentleman. "You know whom I mean. They're not in your rooms, sir, are they? They told Bolton they were going to church at the Temple, they weren't there. They are in your chambers: they mustn't stay in your chambers, Mr. Pendennis."

"Damn it, sir!" cried out Pendennis, fiercely. "Come and see if they are in my chambers: here's the court and the door—come in and see." And Bows, taking off his hat and bowing first, followed the young man.

They were not in Pen's chambers, as we know. But when the gardens were closed, the two women, who had had but a melancholy evening's amusement, walked away sadly with the children. They entered Lamb Court and stood under the lamp-post which cheerfully ornamented the centre of that quadrangle, looking up to the third floor of the house where Pendennis's chambers were, where they saw a light presently kindled. Then this couple of fools went away, the children dragging wearily after them, and returned to Mr. Bolton, who was immersed in rum-and-water at his lodge in Shepherd's Inn.

Mr. Bows looked

Let us revisit the little town of Clavering, that cheerful hamlet nestled near Pen's ancestral home of Fairoaks. While the society there was hardly a source of amusement, it did possess its own peculiar brand of theatricality. Among its most spirited inhabitants was Mr. Samuel Huxter, a man whose vacation visits to the village transformed dull tea-tables into stages for the wit of Bartholomew's and the gossip of high London society.

Huxter's circle included Mr. Hobnell, the young gentleman Pen had once thrashed during the Fotheringay affair. At the time, Hobnell was a Grammar School pupil who knew exactly how to navigate Mrs. Huxter's tea-tables and the local surgery's tamarind-pots, often scenting his handkerchiefs with rose-water to impress. It was during these formative years that Hobnell fell for Miss Sophy Huxter. Upon his father's death, he married her and moved them to the Warren, a few miles from Clavering.

The Hobnells had long been yeomen, but Mr. Hobnell Sr. had a vision. He demolished the old farmhouse, erected a glaring white mansion with capacious stables, installed a piano in the drawing-room, and acquired a pack of harriers. Thus, the family ascended from farming to county gentry. When the elder Hobnell passed, his son inherited the title of Squire. Sam Huxter, back in London, took great pride in boasting to his comrades at Bartholomew's about his brother-in-law's hounds, horses, and hospitality.

Every year, when Mrs. Hobnell was too busy with her nursery duties to travel, Squire Hobnell would descend upon London for a "lark." He secured rooms at the Tavistock and, alongside his learned brother-in-law Sam, indulged in the city's pleasures. They toured Ascot, the theatres, Vauxhall, and the convivial taverns of Covent Garden. As Squire Hobnell declared, "When I'm in London, I like to do as London does, and 'go it a bit.'" Upon returning to the West Country, he would present Mrs. Hobnell with a new bonnet and shawl, trading elegant metropolitan amusements for country sports for the next eleven months.

Sam and Hobnell maintained a lively correspondence. Sam sent news of the metropolis in exchange for baskets of hares, partridges, and clouted cream from the Warren. To the Huxters, Sam was the life and soul of the household; his songs and jokes kept the Warren in a perpetual roar. He had even once saved their eldest child's life by extracting a fishbone from her throat. He was, simply put, the delight of their circle.

However, fate, that mischievous playwright, arranged for Pen to encounter Mr. Huxter just three days after their altercation at Vauxhall. True to his vow, Pen had not visited little Fanny. He was attempting to drown his thoughts of her in relentless work, laboring incessantly in his rooms and delivering savage critiques of poems and romances for the *Pall Mall Gazette*. Having slain these literary foes, he retreated to the lonely club of the Polyanthus, where the vast silence only deepened his gloom. He sought relief in the theaters, but the roaring laughter of the crowds only highlighted the ignoble farces he despised, leaving him with a dismal face that would have dampened even the buffoon on stage.

Exhausted from walking twenty miles over Hampstead Common and Hendon lanes in search of rest, Pen decided to visit the Back Kitchen, his old haunt with Warrington. He wasn't sleepy yet, but the thought of seeing Bows offered a comfort. Bows was presiding calmly over the old piano while tremendous comic songs cracked the room with laughter. To Pen, these songs seemed alien; amidst the merriment, he could only focus on Bows. It was a marvel, he mused, that a breast he claimed was an extinct volcano could still feel such a flame. Two days of indulgence had kindled it; two days of abstinence had set it burning in fury.

Drinking glass after glass, Arthur's gaze fell upon Mr. Huxter, who had attended the theater with two or three comrades. Huxter whispered something to his friends that clearly annoyed Pen. Arthur sensed he was the subject of conversation. Huxter then worked his way through the room, followed by his cronies, until he reached Pen's table. He nodded familiarly and extended a dirty hand for a shake.

Pen accepted the handshake, feeling a pang of regret for his savagery the previous night. Huxter, however, was in perfect good humor with himself and the world; the idea that he could be disagreeable never crossed his mind. The "chaff" of Vauxhall was a triviality he barely registered.

The group ordered four stouts and began searching for a topic that would amuse Pen, landing precisely on the one most painful to him.

"A jolly night at Vauxhall, wasn't it?" Huxter asked, winking with knowing familiarity.

"I'm glad you liked it," groaned Pen in spirit.

"I was dev'lish cut—uncommonly so," Huxter continued, feigning offense. "I've been dining with some chaps at Greenwich. That was a pretty bit of muslin hanging on your arm—who was she?"

The question broke Pen. "Have I asked you any questions about yourself, Mr. Huxter?"

"I didn't mean any offence—beg pardon—hang it, you cut up quite savage," Huxter exclaimed, astonished.

"Do you remember what took place between us the other night?" Pen asked, his wrath gathering. "You forget? Very probably. You were tipsy, as you observed just now, and very rude."

"Hang it, sir, I asked your pardon," Huxter said, turning red.

"You did certainly, and it was granted with all my heart," Pen replied sharply. "But if you recollect, I begged that you would omit me from the list of your acquaintance for the future. And when we meet in public, that you would not take the trouble to recognize me. Will you please remember this henceforth? And since the song is beginning, permit me to leave you to your unrestrained enjoyment of the music."

Taking his hat and bowing to the amazed Mr. Huxter, Pen departed. As Huxter's comrades paused in wonder, they erupted into a roar of laughter at Huxter's expense, prompting the room's president to bawl, "Silence, gentlemen; do have silence for the Body Snatcher!" as the popular song began. Pen flattered himself that he had commanded his temper perfectly. He actually wished Huxter had been pugnacious; he would have preferred to fight someone. He went home, but the day's work, the dinner, the play, the whisky-and-water, and the quarrel offered no solace. He slept no better than the previous night.

A few days later, Mr. Sam Huxter wrote home to Mr. Hobnell, making Arthur Pendennis the principal subject. Sam described Arthur's London pursuits and his "confounded insolence" toward old friends. He labeled him an abandoned criminal, a regular Don Juan, and a fellow who, if he ever returned to the country, ought to be barred from honest households. He claimed to have seen Arthur dancing with an innocent girl from the lower ranks, making her a victim. Huxter had identified the girl through an Irish gentleman in the army and intended to warn her father. The letter concluded with thanks for the last parcel of game and a hint of readiness for further favors.

Once a year, a christening occurred at the Warren. This ceremony took place the day after Hobnell received Sam's letter. The infant, a darling little girl, was named Myra Lucretia after her godmothers, Miss Portman and Mrs. Pybus of Clavering. Naturally, Hobnell showed the letter to his wife, and Mrs. Hobnell, in turn, imparted its horrid contents to her gossips.

By evening, the story of Arthur's alleged depravity was circulating through every corner of Clavering society. Myra was too shocked to speak, but Mrs. Pybus had no such reserve. She discussed the matter with Mrs. Portman, the Honorable Mrs. Simcoe, Mrs. Glanders (ordering her daughters out of the room), Madame Fribsby, and everyone else. Madame Fribsby, glancing furtively at her picture of a dragoon and inwardly at her own wounded memories, remarked that men were deceivers by nature and quoted lines from *Marmion* about where deceiving lovers should rest. Mrs. Pybus, however, possessed words of hatred and horror strong enough for such a villain. She attributed the behavior to early indulgence, insolence, extravagance, and aristocratic airs—specifically noting that Pen had refused to drink tea with her—and the corrupt parties of modern Babylon.

Mrs. Portman feared that her own fatal partiality had spoiled the boy, that his literary success had turned his head and his passions made him forget the principles Doctor Portman had instilled. Captain Glanders, the atrocious Dragoon, whistled and made jocular allusions to the affair at dinner, earning a rebuke from Mrs. Glanders and another order to remove the girls. Mr. Simcoe remained calm, merely confirming his low opinion of the "wretched young man" without having read a single line of his "dangerous and poisonous works."

Pen became the subject of a second sermon at the Clavering chapel-of-ease, where the dangers of London and the crime of writing novels were preached to a large congregation. They did not wait to hear if he was guilty; they took his wickedness for granted and eagerly threw stones at poor Pen.

The next day, Mrs. Pendennis, alone and nearly fainting from emotion and fatigue, ran to Dr. Portman's house. She had received an anonymous letter—a "Christian" thought it their duty to stab the good soul who had never harmed anyone. The letter referenced Scripture, pointed out the doom of sinners, and detailed Arthur's crime. Two hours of this agony had aged her visibly. In her first moment of agitation, she dropped the letter, and Laura read it. Laura blushed, her whole frame trembling with anger. "The cowards," she said. "It isn't true. No, mother, it isn't true."

"It is true, and you've done it, Laura!" cried Helen fiercely. "Why did you refuse him when he asked you? Why did you break my heart? It is you who led him into crime. It is you who flung him into the arms of this—this woman. Don't speak to me. Don't answer me. I will never forgive you, never. Martha, bring me my bonnet and shawl. I'll go out. I won't have you come with me. Go away. Leave me, cruel girl; why have you brought this shame on me?"

Bidding her daughter and servants away, she ran down the road to Clavering.

Dr. Portman glanced at the letter, recognized the handwriting, and knew the charge against poor Pen. Against his own conscience, perhaps (for the worthy Doctor, like most, had a natural aptitude for believing reports unfavorable to his neighbors), he strove to console Helen. He pointed out that the slander came from an anonymous quarter and must be the work of a rascal; that the charge was likely untrue; that Pen must be heard before condemnation; that the son of such a mother was unlikely to commit such a crime.

Helen saw through his feint immediately. "You think he has done it," she said. "You know you think he has done it. Oh, why did I ever leave him, Doctor Portman, or suffer him away from me? But he can't be dishonest—pray God, not dishonest—you don't think that, do you? Remember his conduct about that other person—how madly he was attached to her. He was an honest boy then—he is now. And I thank God—yes, I fall down on my knees and thank God he paid Laura. You said he was good—you did yourself. And now, if this woman loves him—and you know they must—if he has taken her from her home, or she tempted him, which is most likely—why still, she must be his wife and my daughter. And he must leave the dreadful world and come back to me—to his mother, Doctor Portman. Let us go away and bring him back—yes—bring him back—and there shall be joy for the sinner that repenteth. Let us go now, directly, dear friend—this very—"

Helen could say no more. She fell back and fainted. She was carried to a bed in the Doctor's house, and the surgeon was summoned. She lay all night in an alarming state. Laura came to the rectory, though Helen refused to see her. Doctor Portman, still beseeching her to be tranquil and growing bolder in his confidence of Arthur's innocence as he witnessed the terrible grief of the poor mother, wrote a letter to Pen. He warned him of the rumors against him and earnestly prayed that he would break off and repent of a connection so fatal to his best interests and his soul's welfare.

And what of Laura? Was her heart not wrung by the thought of Arthur's crime and Helen's estrangement? Was it not a bitter blow for the innocent girl to realize that in one stroke she might lose all the love she cared for in the world?

Doctor Portman's letter was dispatched to London, and the good clergyman busied himself with soothing Mrs. Pendennis into a semblance of composure, hoping for a reply that would vindicate her son's morality. Helen, however, deemed it impractical to travel to London immediately to confront her son with the truth of his wickedness. The apothecary forbade her even a journey to Fairoaks for the first day, and it was not until the following morning that she returned to her sofa at home, tended by the faithful, silent Laura.

Unfortunately for everyone involved, Pen never read Doctor Portman's homily until weeks later. Day after day, the widow waited for her son's response to the charges, her own health deteriorating with every delay. For Laura, the burden was heavy: to witness her dearest friend's suffering, to endure Helen's estrangement, and to bear the pain of that averted affection. Yet, she fulfilled her duty with a grace that defied noise; her prayers, the true source of her strength, were whispered in her chamber, invisible to the world. Like a flower blooming in secret, her virtue remained unseen, yet undeniably present—a testament to the fact that good women are the fairest flowers under heaven, often bowed low by grief or consumed by disease, yet purified by Heaven's chastening trials.

Pen, meanwhile, never received the letter. It was duly posted, delivered to his box in Lamb Court, and carried by the laundress to his writing table, joining the pile of correspondence that included a picture of him entering his room just as Mrs. Flanagan was drinking his gin.

Those who have tracked Mr. Arthur's career have likely identified his greatest enemy: himself. A scoundrel who indulged him all his life, this internal rogue had grown insolent, reacting with frantic rudeness to any attempt at self-denial. Unlike Laura, who sacrificed pleasure easily, Pen, unaccustomed to restraint, suffered woundily when asked to forgo his desires. He resolved not to see Fanny, attempting to drown her image in work, exercise, and dissipation. He worked too hard, walked too much, and consumed too many cigars and punches. Yet, none of this could banish Fanny from his inflamed brain. By the end of a week of this futile discipline, our young gentleman lay in bed with a fever.

Let the reader who has never suffered a fever in chambers pity the wretch bound to such a fate. One might imagine a Cruikshank illustrating the horrors of bachelor life: the lonely breakfast with a black kettle in a midsummer fire, or the return from a festive holiday to find only Christmas bills waiting on the table. But the true tragedy is illness in chambers—long nights of pain, the absence of a kind hand, and the company of one's own sickening fancies. This dismal lot befell Arthur Pendennis.

One night he went to bed ill; the next, he awoke worse. His only visitors were the laundress and a printer's devil from the *Pall Mall Gazette*, whose demands for copy only exacerbated his fever. With Shandon away at Boulogne and Warrington absent, the political columns of the Gazette looked blank until Mr. Finucane rushed to Pen's chambers. The good-natured Irishman stepped in, producing a series of political compositions filled with praise for Ireland and its genius. Shandon, reviewing the paper from the coast, recognized the hand of his friend and chuckled, "Look here, Mary, here is Jack at work again." Jack, a warm partisan, even offered to forgo his holidays to nurse Arthur, though the invalid insisted on bearing his malady alone.

On a Friday night, after finishing the paper, Finucane informed Captain Costigan of Arthur's condition. Two days later, the Captain visited Lamb Court. He found Mrs. Flanagan in tears, her grief so overwhelming she resorted to brandy to sustain her. Her intoxicated attentions became intolerable to the invalid, who begged her to leave, prompting further tears and recourse to the bottle. The Captain reprimanded her soundly for her intemperance.

Inside, Pen, in a fevered state, was delighted by the Captain's arrival. He called eagerly, thanking him and begging him to talk. The Captain felt the young man's pulse—fierce and rapid—and noted the haggard, hot face and bloodshot eyes. Pen tossed in his comfortless bed, trying to discuss the Back Kitchen, Vauxhall, and Fanny. "How is little Fanny?" he asked.

Indeed, how was she? Fanny had gone home sadly the previous Sunday, watching Arthur light his lamp after his meeting with Bows. That night, she lay restless, unable to read *Walter Lorraine* aloud in fear of waking her father. She clutched the book under her pillow, stirring with the dawn. When she finally saw Bows, the good-hearted musician, she demanded to know if he would speak ill of Arthur.

"I've been fond of you ever since we came to live here," Bows said gently. "You used to like me until three or four days ago."

"And now you're going to say ill of him?" Fanny cried, pacing the room, her cheeks flushed with defiance. "Do, Mr. Bows—that will make me like you better."

"Indeed I shall do no such thing," Bows replied. "I think he is a very good and honest young man."

"But if you said a word against him, I would never speak to you again!" she cried, her frame trembling with love, anger, and despair.

Bows explained that he knew Pen years ago, when he had a romantic attachment for Lady Mirabel, the Captain's daughter. "He wanted to marry her, but their ages and ranks were disproportionate," Bows said. "She acted wisely in refusing him. Mr. Pendennis has his way to make in the world; he must marry a lady of his own rank. An honest girl won't ruin a man's prospects or lead him into poverty for her gratification."

Fanny's defiance turned to dismay. "What do I know about marrying, Bows?" she sobbed. "It wasn't my doing... He came and rescued us... Yesterday Ma and I walked in the Temple Gardens, and—and"—she broke into tears—"Oh! I wish I was dead! I wish I was laid in my grave and had never seen him!"

"He said as much himself," Bows said softly. "He cannot and must not think of you as a wife fitting for him. You must strive to forget him."

Leaving the musician, convinced but still longing, Fanny told her mother everything. She bewailed the inequality of their conditions, citing *The Lady of Lyons* and Pauline as examples of true love transcending class. "Not that Mr. Arthur loves me—Oh no! I ain't worthy of him," she confessed. "Only a princess is worthy of such a gentleman. If I might, oh, how I'd serve him, work for him, slave for him... I'd sit up all night and learn to make myself worthy of him."

Her head fell on her mother's shoulder as she wept. Mrs. Bolton, of course, joined her, but when Mr. Bolton entered for his evening meal, the talk ceased instantly. With practiced hypocrisy, the mother dried her daughter's tears, praising the undertaker's aide-de-camp and feigning ignorance of Fanny's gathering emotion, hiding her daughter's turmoil behind a mask of demure quiet.

Thus, fate seemed determined to inflame the poor child's passion. Her mother encouraged it; Bows's words only augmented the fever. Pen was not a wicked seducer; he was high-minded, wishing to avoid a marriage with an ill-educated girl whose relations he could not acknowledge. Society would say he was right to crush such a fondness, to let the passion pass over and cure itself. Yet, perhaps there is something to be said for the blind, unreasoning passion that makes a man ready to stake his all.

Fanny waited a whole week for Arthur to come, only to hear from Costigan of his illness on the final day. That very evening, Arthur's uncle, the excellent Major, arrived in town from Buxton. He sent his valet, Morgan, to inquire for Arthur and arrange a breakfast. Morgan returned with a long face: Mr. Arthur was very bad, in bed with a fever. A doctor was needed. The Major, vexed but unable to attend due to his own ailments, despatched Morgan with a note to Doctor Goodenough.

The Major went to bed disconsolate. When Doctor Goodenough visited the next day, he found the patient in a high fever and ordered immediate bleeding. The Major wrote to Mrs. Pendennis with alacrity, declaring that if he could be of use, he would rush to the chambers, but his own health required him to stay in the country. He tried to satisfy his conscience by deputy and post, traveling by railway to Stillbrook to dine with the Marquis of Steyne, though he remained gloomy and feverish with worry.

The next morning, he received a note from Mr. Bows saying Arthur had had a tolerable night. But shortly after, as the company waited for carriages on the terrace, a fly drove up. A grey-headed, shabby old gentleman jumped out and asked for Major Pendennis. It was Mr. Bows. Most gentlemen saw the alarm on the Major's face.

"Hullo! What's the matter, Pendennis?" cried Lord Steyne.

"It's—it's—my boy that's dead," the Major sobbed, overcome.

"Not dead, my Lord; but very ill when I left London," Bows corrected in a low voice.

Just then, a britzka arrived. Lord Steyne checked his watch. "You've twenty minutes to catch the mail-train. Jump in, Pendennis; and drive like h——, sir, do you hear?"

The carriage sped away. The Major drove rapidly to the Temple, finding a travelling carriage blocking the lane. Two ladies emerged, asking their way. The Major glanced at the panel and saw the worn-out crest of the Eagle looking at the Sun, with the motto "Nec tenui penna." It was his brother's old carriage. It was Helen and Laura, asking their way to Pen's room.

He ran up, hastily clasping his sister's arm and kissing her hand. The three entered Lamb Court and mounted the long, gloomy stair. They knocked gently at the door, on which Arthur's name was written. It was opened by Fanny Bolton.

Fanny stood frozen, her breath hitching in her throat as she took in the scene before her. Two women blocked her path, and the elder one's face was a mask of such terrifying, inscrutable alarm that Fanny's heart hammered against her ribs. She knew instantly: this was Pen's mother. There was a haunting resemblance between the widow's haggard, sunken eyes and the fever-dream gaze Arthur had worn while tossing in his bed. Fanny cast a desperate, wistful glance at Mrs. Pendennis and then at Laura. Laura's face was as unyielding and expressionless as a block of granite. Hard-heartedness and a suffocating gloom clung to both newcomers; neither offered even a flicker of mercy or sympathy for the trembling girl. Fanny's eyes darted wildly to the Major standing behind them. Old Pendennis lowered his eyelids, peering up from beneath them with a stealthy, worried look directed at Arthur's poor little nurse.

"I—I wrote to you yesterday, if you please, ma'am," Fanny stammered, her limbs trembling violently as she spoke. She was as pale as Laura, whose sad, menacing visage loomed over Mrs. Pendennis's shoulder.

"Did you, madam?" Mrs. Pendennis replied, her tone crisp and cold. "I suppose I may now relieve you from nursing my son. I am his mother, you understand."

"Yes, ma'am. I—this is the way to his—" Fanny began, but she was cut short. "Oh, wait a minute!" she cried out, panic rising in her chest. "I must prepare you for his—"

The widow, whose face had been hopelessly cruel and ruthless just moments before, suddenly recoiled with a gasp and a stifled cry.

"He's been so since yesterday," Fanny whispered, her teeth chattering uncontrollably.

From the open door of Pen's room erupted a horrid shriek of laughter. After several raucous shouts, the poor wretch began to sing a college drinking-song, then hurrahed and shouted as if he were in the midst of a wild wine-party, thumping his fist against the wainscot. He was completely delirious.

"He does not know me, ma'am," Fanny said, her voice breaking.

"Indeed. Perhaps he will know his mother; let me pass, if you please, and go in to him." The widow hurriedly shoved past little Fanny and swept through the dark passage leading into Pen's sitting-room. Laura sailed by Fanny without a word, and Major Pendennis followed them. Fanny collapsed onto a bench in the passage, sobbing and praying as best she could. She would have died for him, yet they hated her. They offered not a single word of thanks or kindness, those fine ladies. She sat there in the gloomy corridor, unsure of how long she remained. They never came out to speak to her. She stayed until Doctor Goodenough arrived for his second visit of the day, finding the poor little thing huddled at the door.

"What, nurse? How's your patient?" asked the good-natured Doctor, his voice warm. "Has he had any rest?"

"Go and ask them. They're inside," Fanny answered, pointing vaguely toward the room.

"Who? His mother?"

Fanny nodded silently, unable to speak.

"You must go to bed yourself, my poor little maid," the Doctor said gently. "You will be ill, too, if you don't."

"Oh, mayn't I come and see him? Mayn't I come and see him! I—I love him so," the little girl pleaded. As she spoke, she fell to her knees, clutching the Doctor's hand in such an agony of devotion that it melted the kind physician's heart, causing a mist to cloud his spectacles.

"Pooh, pooh! Nonsense! Nurse, has he taken his draught? Has he had any rest? Of course you must come and see him. So must I."

"They'll let me sit here, won't they, sir? I'll never make no noise. I only ask to stop here," Fanny begged. The Doctor called her a stupid little thing, tapped her pale cheek with his finger, and bustled into the farther room.

Inside, Mrs. Pendennis was ensconced pale and solemn in a great chair by Pen's bedside. Her watch lay on the bed-table beside his medicines. Her bonnet and cloaks were laid out in the window. She held her Bible in her lap, a constant companion she never traveled without. Her first move upon seeing her son had been to take Fanny's shawl and bonnet from his drawers, bring them out, and drop them onto his study-table. She had closed the door on Major Pendennis and Laura, effectively taking possession of her son.

She had harbored a great terror that Arthur would not recognize her, but that pang was spared her in part. Pen knew his mother quite well; he smiled and nodded familiarly. When she entered, he instantly fancied they were back home at Fairoaks. He began to talk, chatter, and laugh in a rambling, wild way. Laura could hear him from outside. His laughter shot shafts of poison into her heart. It was true, then. He had been guilty—an intrigue with a servant-maid, and she had loved him—and he was dying, most likely raving and unrepentant. The Major occasionally hummed out a word of remark or consolation, which Laura barely heard.

It was a dismal sitting for everyone. When Goodenough appeared, he seemed like an angel descending into the room. It is not only for the sick man that the Doctor comes; it is for the sick man's friends. His presence is often as good for them as for the patient. We have all watched for him! What an emotion the thrill of his carriage wheels in the street, and finally at the door, has made us feel! How we hang upon his words, seeking comfort in a smile or two if he can vouchsafe that sunshine to lighten our darkness! Who hasn't seen the mother prying into his face to know if there is hope for the silent infant battling fever? Ah, how she looks into his eyes! What thanks if there is light there; what grief and pain if he casts them down, daring not say "hope!" Or it is the house-father who is stricken. The terrified wife looks on while the Physician feels his patient's wrist, smothering her agonies as the children are called to stay their play. Over the fever-stricken patient, the expectant wife, and the unconscious children, the Doctor stands as if he were Fate, the dispenser of life and death. He must let the patient off this time; the woman prays so for his respite! One can imagine how awful the responsibility must be to a conscientious man: how cruel the feeling that he gave the wrong remedy, or that he might have done better; how harassing the sympathy with survivors if the case is unfortunate—how immense the delight of victory!

After a hasty ceremony of introduction to the new arrivals, of whose arrival he had been made aware by the heartbroken little nurse waiting outside, the Doctor proceeded to examine the patient. The condition of high fever was undeniable, and he deemed it necessary to exercise the strongest antiphlogistic remedies in his power. He consoled the unfortunate mother as best he might, offering the most comfortable assurances he could venture—that there was no reason to despair yet, that everything might still be hoped for from his youth, the strength of his constitution, and so forth. Having done his utmost to allay the horrors of the alarmed matron, he took the elder Pendennis aside into the vacant room (Warrington's bedroom) for a little consultation.

The case was very critical. The fever, if not stopped, might and would carry off the young fellow; he must be bled forthwith; the mother must be informed of this necessity. "Why was that other young lady brought with her?" the Doctor asked. "She was out of place in a sick-room."

"And there was another woman still, be hanged to it!" the Major said. "The—the little person who opened the door." His sister-in-law had brought the poor little devil's bonnet and shawl out, flinging them upon the study-table. "Did Goodenough know anything about the—the little person?" the Major asked. "I just caught a glimpse of her as we passed in," the Major continued, "and begad she was uncommonly nice-looking." The Doctor looked queer; the Doctor smiled. In the very gravest moments, with life and death pending, such strange contrasts and occasions of humor arise, and such smiles pass to satirize the gloom, as it were, and to make it more gloomy!

"I have it," he said at last, re-entering the study. He wrote a couple of notes hastily at the table and sealed one. Then, taking up poor Fanny's shawl and bonnet along with the notes, he went out into the passage to that poor little messenger. "Quick, nurse; you must carry this to the surgeon, and bid him come instantly; and then go to my house, and ask for my servant Harbottle, and tell him to get this prescription prepared, and wait until I—until it is ready. It may take a little in preparation."

So poor Fanny trudged away with her two notes, found the apothecary who lived in the Strand hard by, and who came straightway, his lancet in his pocket, to operate on his patient. Then Fanny made for the Doctor's house in Hanover Square.

The Doctor was at home again before the prescription was made up, which took Harbottle, his servant, such a long time in compounding. During the remainder of Arthur's illness, poor Fanny never made her appearance in the quality of nurse at his chambers any more. But for that day and the next, a little figure might be seen lurking about Pen's staircase—a sad, sad little face looked at and interrogated the apothecary, the apothecary's boy, the laundress, and the kind physician himself as they passed out of the chambers of the sick man. And on the third day, the kind Doctor's chariot stopped at Shepherd's Inn, and the good, honest, and benevolent man went into the porter's lodge and tended a little patient whom he had there. For the best remedy he found was on the day when he was enabled to tell Fanny Bolton that the crisis was over, and that there was at length every hope for Arthur Pendennis.

J. Costigan, Esquire, late of Her Majesty's service, saw the Doctor's carriage and criticized its horses and appointments. "Green liveries, bedad!" the General said. "And as foin a pair of high-stepping bee horses as ever a gentleman need sit behind, let alone a doctor. There's no end to the pride and arrogance of them doctors nowadays—not but that is a good one, and a scientific character, and a right good fellow, bedad; and he's brought the poor little girl well through her fever, Bows, me boy;" and so pleased was Mr. Costigan with the Doctor's behavior and skill that, whenever he met Dr. Goodenough's carriage in future, he made a point of saluting it and the physician inside in as courteous and magnificent a manner as if Dr. Goodenough had been the Lord Lieutenant himself, and Captain Costigan had been in his glory in Phoenix Park.

The widow's gratitude to the physician knew no bounds—or scarcely any bounds, at least. The kind gentleman laughed at the idea of taking a fee from a literary man, or the widow of a brother practitioner; and she determined when she got to Fairoaks that she would send Goodenough the silver-gilt vase, the jewel of the house, and the glory of the late John Pendennis, preserved in green baize, and presented to him at Bath, by the Lady Elizabeth Firebrace, on the recovery of her son, the late Sir Anthony Firebrace, from the scarlet fever. Hippocrates, Hygeia, King Bladud, and a wreath of serpents surmount the cup to this day; which was executed in their finest manner by Messrs. Abednego, of Milsom Street; and the inscription was by Mr. Birch, tutor to the young baronet.

This priceless gem of art the widow determined to devote to Goodenough, the preserver of her son; and there was scarcely any other favor which her gratitude would not have conferred upon him, except one, which he desired most, and which was that she should think a little charitably and kindly of poor Fanny, of whose artless, sad story he had got something during his interviews with her, and of whom he was induced to think very kindly—not being disposed, indeed, to give much credit to Pen for his conduct in the affair, or not knowing what that conduct had been. He knew enough, however, to be aware that the poor infatuated little girl was without stain as yet; that while she had been in Pen's room it was to see the last of him, as she thought, and that Arthur was scarcely aware of her presence; and that she suffered under the deepest and most pitiful grief, at the idea of losing him, dead or living.

But on the one or two occasions when Goodenough alluded to Fanny, the widow's countenance, always soft and gentle, assumed an expression so cruel and inexorable that the Doctor saw it was in vain to ask her for justice or pity, and he broke off all entreaties, and ceased making any further allusions regarding his little client. There is a complaint which neither poppy, nor mandragora, nor all the drowsy syrups of the East could allay, in the men in his time, as we are informed by a popular poet of the days of Elizabeth; and which, when exhibited in women, no medical discoveries or practice subsequent—neither homoeopathy, nor hydropathy, nor mesmerism, nor Dr. Simpson, nor Dr. Locock can cure, and that is—we won't call it jealousy, but rather gently denominate rivalry and emulation in ladies.

Some of those mischievous and prosaic people who carp and calculate at every detail of the romancer, and want to know, for instance, how, when the

The duty of the chronicler now falls to a rather embarrassing admission. We must record a fact concerning Pendennis that, while shameful and disgraceful for the chief protagonist and godfather of a novel, must nonetheless be laid bare for the public reading his veritable memoirs. After succumbing to a fever, suffering the torments of love, and enduring the full regimen of the doctor—bleeding, blistering, shaving, and medicating—Pendennis rallied from his physical ailment. With it came a miraculous cure for his mental malady. He was no longer in love with Fanny Bolton. He was as indifferent to her as any wise or moral observer who knows better than to let their hearts wander after porters' daughters.

Lying on his pillow, Pendennis laughed at himself, marveling at this second cure. He couldn't fathom how he had ever cared for her. He performed a post-mortem on his dead passion, dissecting his defunct sensations for the benefit of his poor little nurse. What had made him so hot and eager weeks prior? Not her wit, breeding, or beauty; there were hundreds of women far superior. The passion had simply evaporated from within him; it never truly resided in her. She remained unchanged, but the eyes that saw her had shifted. Alas, that was the tragedy. He felt a friendly disposition toward the "little thing," but the violent personal regard of weeks past had fled, vanquished by the pill and lancet that had cured his body. It was a source of immense comfort and gratitude to Pendennis—that he had resisted temptation when the danger was greatest—and he felt no reproach for his conduct. Yet, as he reviewed the Fanny Bolton snare from which he had escaped, he wasn't entirely sure he wasn't ashamed of the satisfaction he felt. It is pleasant, perhaps, but humiliating to admit that you love no more.

Meanwhile, the kind smiles and tender watchfulness of his mother at his bedside filled the young man with peace. Seeing health return was all the unwearied nurse demanded; executing his caprices was her chief joy. He felt enveloped by her love, grateful almost as he had been in childhood when weak and helpless.

There were some misty notions in Pen's mind regarding the first part of his illness and Fanny's role as nurse, but they were so dim he couldn't distinguish them from fever-delirium hallucinations. Consequently, just as he had avoided mentioning Fanny to his mother before, he could not now confide in her. It was an unlucky lack of confidence on both sides; a few words in time might have spared the good lady and her circle much pain.

Seeing Miss Bolton installed as nurse, Mrs. Pendennis had put the worst possible construction on the intimacy. She settled firmly in her mind that the accusations against Arthur were true. Why hadn't she stopped to inquire? Stories to a man's disadvantage are always most eagerly believed by the women who love him most. Isn't a wife often the first to be jealous? Poor Pen received a stock of this suspicious, twisted love from his nurse. The kind, pure creature believed her boy had suffered a malady far more awful and debasing than mere physical fever; she thought he was stained by crime as well as weakened by illness. She bore this consciousness silently, masking her doubt and despair with a cheerful facade.

At Boulogne, Captain Shandon, reading the *Pall Mall Gazette*, remarked to Mrs. Shandon that Jack Finucane's hand was no longer visible in the leading articles and that Mr. Warrington must be at work again. "I know the crack of his whip," Shandon observed, using a dreadful metaphor about a butcher mangling a subject. "Mr. Warrington finishes a man, laying his cuts neat and regular, straight down the back, drawing blood every line." Mrs. Shandon protested, claiming Mr. Warrington was a kind gentleman to the children. Shandon agreed he was kind to the children but savage to the men, advising his wife not to understand newspaper politics, noting the comfort of Boulogne where wine was plentiful and brandy cost only two francs a bottle.

In a word, Warrington went to work with all his might in place of his prostrate friend, doing Pen's portion of the *Gazette* "with a vengeance." He wrote articles, attended theaters, and discoursed on them with savage energy. His hand was too strong for such small subjects, yet he delighted in telling Arthur's mother, uncle, and Laura that no hand in the land was more graceful or elegant than Arthur's. "The people in this country don't understand style," he told Mrs. Pendennis. "I call him ours, for I bred him. Bating a little wilfulness and dandification, I don't know a more honest or loyal creature. His pen is wicked sometimes, but he is as kind as a young lady."

Helen sighed deeply, and Laura was sadly wounded, yet both were thankful for Warrington's good opinion of Arthur and loved him for his attachment to their Pen. Major Pendennis was loud in his praises of Mr. Warrington, calling him a gentleman of the highest caliber, a descendant of the Suffolk Warringtons and friends of the Prince of Wales. Laura blushed as the Major praised Arthur's hero, speculating internally that Warrington must be a victim of an unhappy attachment.

Warrington secured chambers near Grenier's in Flag Court. After executing Pen's tasks in the morning, his afternoon delight was sitting with the sick man's company in sunny autumn evenings. He frequently gave Miss Bell his arm for walks in the Temple Gardens. When the frank Laura asked Helen's permission, the Major eagerly approved, comparing the gardens to the countryside and assuring her of the safety of the beadles. If the great arbiter of morals didn't object, why should simple Helen? She was glad her girl had fresh air and saw her return with heightened color and spirits.

Laura and Helen had reached a little explanation. When news of Pen's alarming illness arrived, Laura insisted on accompanying the terrified mother to London. When Helen initially refused, and then refused again more sternly as the lad's life seemed despaired of and his conduct rendered union hopeless, Laura, with many tears, confessed her secret love. She could not marry him, but she was denied the consolation of owning how fondly she had loved him. Their mingled tears appeased the agony of their grief, and the sorrows of their journey were mitigated by sharing them.

What could Fanny expect when suddenly brought before such judges? Swift condemnation, awful punishment, merciless dismissal! Women are cruel critics in such cases; they have their own friends to keep watch and tear apart any who stray. The narrator, a Turk at heart wearing a turban and beard, protests he is all for the sack practice, but urges caution. Be very sure of the fact before ordering the barge out; don't pop your subject into the Bosphorus until certain she deserves it. If she is guilty, down with her, but be sure.

The Major did not object to Warrington's promenades with Miss Laura; indeed, he encouraged the intimacy. Were there exhibitions? He was for Warrington conducting her. If Warrington proposed Vauxhall, the complaisant Major would see no harm. Between two persons of spotless honor—Warrington, seeing in intimacy a pure, high-minded woman for the first time, and Laura, thrown into the society of a gentleman of great natural parts—their interaction was harmless. In Warrington's uncouthness lay a refinement that Pen's finery lacked. In his energy, respect, and hearty laughter, there was a difference to Sultan Pen's yawning sovereignty. What had made Pen a dandy and despot? The women had spoiled him, cloying him with obedience until he grew weary of the slaves waiting upon him. Abroad, he was brisk and impassioned, but here he was languid.

How came it that Laura, despite Pen's indifference, held such a rapturous regard for him? As soon as Helen finished one sobbing story about the dear fellow, Laura began another, equally ornamented with tears. She recounted how heroically he had a tooth pulled, how daringly he robbed a bird's nest, how magnanimously he spared it, how he gave a shilling to an old woman, or went without bread for a beggar-boy. One to another, the sobbing women sang laments upon their hero, who was no more a hero than any of us. Why should a sensible girl be so fond? Because she saw scarcely anyone else at Fairoaks except Doctor Portman and Captain Glanders, because his mother constantly praised Arthur, because he was gentlemanlike and witty, and because, above all, it was of her nature to like somebody. Having received this image into her heart, she tenderly nursed it, brooding over it in her solitudes. When she came to London and became intimate with Mr. George Warrington, what prevented her from thinking him odd, original, agreeable, and pleasing?

Long afterwards, when Fate had disposed of the persons assembled in the dingy building in Lamb Court, perhaps they looked back on how happy the time was. The Major developed a favorable opinion of September in London, declaring the dead season pleasant. He returned home to his lodgings in Bury Street wondering how quickly the evening had passed. He appeared at the Temple constantly, tugging up the long black staircase with benevolent activity. He made interest with the chef at Bays's to prepare jellies, clear soups, and aspics for the invalid. With Doctor Goodenough's permission for Pen to drink sherry, the Major declared that the Marquis of Steyne, passing through London, had ordered any quantity of his precious Amontillado—a gift from King Ferdinand—to be placed at Arthur's disposal. The widow and Laura tasted it with respect, though disliking the bitter flavor, while the invalid was greatly invigorated. Warrington pronounced it superlatively good and proposed the Major's health in a mock speech.

Major Pendennis returned thanks with utmost gravity, using the phrase "the present occasion" repeatedly. Pen cheered with his feeble voice. Warrington taught Miss Laura to cry "Hear! hear!" and tapped the table with his knuckles. Pidgeon grinned, and Doctor Goodenough found the party merrily engaged.

Warrington knew Sibwright, who lived below. In reply to a letter informing him of the use of his apartment, Sibwright wrote a polite, flowery letter of acquiescence, placing his chambers, bed, and carpets at their disposal. Everybody was kindly disposed toward the sick man and his family. Pen's heart melted at the thought of such good-nature.

There was a piano in Mr. Sibwright's chamber, and at this "music-box," as Warrington called it, Laura, trembling and blushing, played and sang simple airs and old songs of home. Her voice was a rich contralto. Warrington, who knew but one tune—a discordant imitation of 'God save the King'—sat rapt in delight, watching with growing enthusiasm the pure and tender creature who made the music.

One wonders how the poor pale little girl in the black bonnet, who stood at the lamp-post in Lamb Court listening to the music, liked to hear it? When Pen's bedtime came, the songs were hushed. Lights appeared in the upper room; the widow conducted him there. Then the Major, Mr. Warrington, and sometimes Miss Laura would play ecarte or backgammon, or she would work a pair of worsted slippers—one of the three men present would have given anything for them.

While this went on, a shabby old gentleman would lead away the pale girl in the black bonnet, who had no right to be abroad at night. The temple porters and amateur listeners disappeared. Just before ten o'clock, the chimes of St. Clement's played a cheerful psalm before ringing the ten fatal strokes. As they rang, Laura folded the slippers. Martha from Fairoaks appeared with a bed-candle and a constant smile. The Major exclaimed, "God bless my soul, is it so late?" Warrington and he left their unfinished game, shaking hands with Miss Bell. Martha lighted them out, and as they descended, they heard her bolting and locking "the sporting door" behind them. If there had been danger, grinning Martha said she would have fetched the Damascus scimitar with the names of the prophet engraved on the blade, which Percy Sibwright brought back from the Levant. It had once entangled Miss Kewsey's train at Lady Mullingar's ball. Good night, Laura and Fairoaks Martha. Sleep well and wake happy.

Sometimes after these evenings, Warrington walked a little way with Major Pendennis—just as far as the Temple gate, the Strand, Charing Cross, or the Club. They talked about Laura all the way. It was wonderful how the Major, who used to dislike her, had grown enthusiastic—"Dev'lish fine girl, begad. Dev'lish well-mannered girl." He noted her natural blush and bouquet of hawthorn, remarking that London girls would give many a guinea for such flowers. In all these opinions, Warrington agreed. Though he laughed as he shook hands with the Major, his face fell as he left his veteran companion. He strode back to chambers, smoked pipe after pipe long into the night, and wrote articles more savage than ever in lieu of his disabled friend.

It was a happy time for almost all parties. Pen mended daily. Sleeping and eating were his constant occupations. His appetite was frightful; he was ashamed to exhibit it before Laura, and his mother laughed and applauded him. As the roast chicken of his dinner went away, he eyed the departing food with sad longing, beginning to crave jelly or tea. He was like an ogre devouring. The Doctor cried stop, but Pen would not. Nature called out louder than the physician, who handed him over with good grace.

Here, let us speak tenderly of an event he never liked to allude to. During his delirium, the ruthless Goodenough ordered ice to his head and all his lovely hair to be cut. It was done in the presence of the other nurse, who left every single hair in a paper for the widow to count. She never believed she hadn't taken some away, for women are suspicious on such matters.

When this direful loss

Since Arthur's illness, Helen had effectively seized command of her son's private quarters, turning his drawers and closets into a fortress of domestic vigilance. She managed his shirts, mended his stockings, and, with a heavy heart, handled his correspondence. While Mrs. Pendennis hoped to find answers to the terrifying mystery of Fanny Bolton within these papers, she found nothing incriminating. Instead, she discovered a mountain of unpaid bills from bootmakers and hatters, a financial burden that threatened to crush her son's already fragile spirit. To shield him from the distress of these demands, Helen had removed the brass knocker from his door, ensuring that the postman's rattling knock would never disturb his rest. She intercepted every letter, paying off debts herself with her own meager savings, a sacrifice she deemed a worthy test of a son's honor.

Yet, the shadow of Fanny Bolton loomed large. When Helen sought counsel from her brother-in-law, Major Pendennis, at their leisurely spot on the Richmond terrace, the old general's expression shifted between amusement and feigned concern. He dismissed the scandal with a wave of his hand, advising her to ignore the "dreadful sin" entirely. "Young men will be young men," he declared, trying to soothe her hysterical fears that her honorable boy had fallen from grace. When Helen tearfully insisted that Arthur's silence confirmed his guilt, the Major gently chided her for suppressing letters, warning that such interference could sever the bond between mother and son.

The tension broke when Helen produced a single, small letter. It was written in a trembling hand, filled with poor spelling and desperate pleas for Arthur's health. The Major, initially feigning shock, quickly pivoted to a defense of Arthur's innocence. He spun a tale of inquiries made by his servant Morgan and Dr. Goodenough, painting a picture of a hapless girl named Fanny, whose father was a drunken brute and whose mother was a hysterical liar. He argued that the girl had merely visited Arthur while he was delirious, acting as a nurse, and that there was no evidence of anything more than a fleeting, innocent attachment. "The boy is innocent," the Major proclaimed, kissing Helen's hand with theatrical relief. "He has told you nothing because he is a man of honor, and you have no right to doubt him based on the word of an anonymous scoundrel."

Convinced by her brother's logic, Helen wept with gratitude, convinced that her son was blameless. The Major, victorious in his diplomatic maneuvering, retired to a nap on the sofa, dreaming of his triumph. Meanwhile, Arthur and his friend Warrington lounged on the terrace, discussing Arthur's ambitious plans for new novels and tragedies. The atmosphere was light until Miss Bell arrived, bringing game and flowers. She expressed deep concern for Helen's health, noting how worn and anxious her mother appeared. The Major, seizing the opportunity to steer the conversation toward recovery, proposed a grand tour abroad to the Rhine. He envisioned a trip where Helen could regain her strength and Arthur could forget his troubles in a new environment, perhaps accompanied by Warrington.

Arthur, eager to escape the stifling atmosphere of Fairoaks, enthusiastically agreed to the plan. However, Warrington hesitated. Citing a lack of funds and pressing engagements at home, he politely declined, though his reluctance was palpable. The Major, undeterred, offered to act as Warrington's banker, promising to cover any expenses incurred during the journey. He framed the invitation as a duty to his old friend's family, insisting that Arthur needed a companion who was "stronger and gayer" than the aging Major himself.

The situation reached a climax at the evening tea. With Helen momentarily absent, the Major pressed Warrington again, calling his refusal "ungallant" and "unfriendly." Miss Bell, looking down at her teacup with long, dark lashes, remained silent. Warrington blushed furiously but said nothing. Encouraged by the Major, Miss Bell finally spoke, asking if Warrington would join them. In a moment of sudden, painful sincerity, Warrington confessed that he would go anywhere she asked. The Major erupted in delight, shouting "Bravo!" and shaking hands with the young couple, sealing the bargain. Just as the Major announced the agreement to the returning Helen, her pale, frightened face illuminated by the candlelight, Miss Bell pulled her hand away, leaving the room in a tense silence.

Helen Pendennis watched her son with a mother's sharp, silent vigilance. Though she never spoke a word of the young woman, Fanny, and though she pretended to ignore her existence entirely, her eyes tracked every movement of Master Arthur. Under the guise of his fragile health, she kept him under her gaze, determined to spare him the trouble of correspondence for the present. Arthur, sitting at the family table, felt the weight of her scrutiny even when her eyes were fixed on her teacup. He waited for a letter that never came. Each day, the silence grew heavier. Was he annoyed that Fanny had parted from him without a single tear, without a "Help, Arthur?"? Or was he relieved? Self-love and pride are stubborn things; we rarely accept sudden bankruptcy gracefully. We pawn our jewels to keep the show going a little longer. But eventually, the shutters must go up. As for Arthur Pendennis, the reader will soon decide whether he belongs to the class of men who sink or those who swim.

While Arthur was too weak to walk, Helen could not stop Mr. Warrington from going to London. Indeed, she suspected his errand before he left: he was there to inquire about Fanny. Arthur had confessed his feelings to Warrington with a gravity that worried the older man. "It is hard to struggle, Arthur, and it is easy to fall," Warrington warned. "The best courage is to fly from danger." When Arthur pressed him for the story of Warrington's own ruined fortunes, the older man deflected with a warning: "Take the moral without the fable."

Meanwhile, in the chaotic world of London's lower classes, Mr. Huxter was making a name for himself. He was a man of mimicry, a disciple of Saint Bartholomew's who thrived on the Back Kitchen club. There, he delighted in imitating an Irish officer of distinction, Captain Costigan. Huxter would draw the General out with brandy-and-water, coaxing stories of his daughter's ingratitude and his own military exploits until the old man was a blur of laughter and tears. The club members loved the General's eccentricities, and the landlord tolerated his foibles because the General brought in the young swells who drank his liquor.

Costigan, however, was a man of secrets. He lived in a state of perpetual flux, moving from garret to garret, often avoiding questions about his lodgings. Legends sprang up that he slept in watch-boxes or under the Duke of York's Column. But on one brilliant September morning, the legend ended in reality. Huxter, fresh from Vauxhall and nursing a few coins, spotted the General reeling down Henrietta Street, pursued by a gang of blackguard boys. The old man, glazed-eyed and drunk, stumbled blindly toward home.

Just as he reached the gate of Shepherd's Inn, a piece of orange peel sent him tumbling backward. Huxter rushed to his aid. Despite the General's refusal to admit his location, the young surgeon insisted on accompanying him. They reached the ancient gate of the inn, where the porter, Mr. Bolton, scowled at the intrusion. Inside, Huxter helped the disoriented Captain to his room, applying compresses to a torn knee and shin. The old warrior lay ill for days, plagued by fever and pain, while Huxter visited him with cheerful chatter and medical skill.

It was during these days of convalescence that Huxter became intimately acquainted with the household of Shepherd's Inn. He bought toys for the children, brought apples, and once frightened them with a mask, causing a rare smile on the face of the pale, wan Fanny Bolton. Her head was bowed, her face ghostly, her heart heavy with the sorrow of the last three weeks. Huxter's kindness was a balm, and he soon learned from Mrs. Bolton the full extent of Arthur's "misconduct" and Fanny's suffering.

Huxter wrote to his brother-in-law, Clavering, reporting Arthur's narrow escape from fever and his current occupation with a compound fracture, but he omitted any mention of Fanny. However, his true feelings were reserved for the musician, Mr. Bows. In a heated conversation, Huxter labeled Arthur a "conceited blackguard" and vowed to punch his head. But Bows, the old fiddler, had a different story. He recounted how Arthur had acted with manliness, how Mrs. Pendennis had been an "aristocratic" brute in turning Fanny out, and how Fanny herself was the one who lacked a heart, now seeking solace in the arms of a new doctor from Saint Bartholomew's.

When Warrington arrived at Shepherd's Inn to deliver Arthur's thanks to Bows, he found the musician in a foul mood. Bows, irritable and bitter, confirmed what Huxter had hinted: Fanny was indeed consoling herself with a new suitor, a "sawbones" who grinned through a horse-collar for her sisters. Warrington, witnessing this scene from the doorway, saw Fanny laughing languidly at Huxter's farces. A bitter thought crossed his mind: "Are all women like that?"

Later, at Piccadilly, Warrington encountered Major Pendennis. The Major, a man of philosophy, agreed with Warrington's observation that "all women are the same," citing the myth of Calypso. He was delighted to hear that Fanny had found a new lover and predicted that Arthur would be furious. "We must break it to him gently," the Major advised, promising to handle the delicate matter with his wife.

Back at the inn, Warrington finally delivered the news to Arthur. Using the metaphor of Ariadne abandoned on Naxos, he revealed the truth. Arthur started up, his face turning red, shouting, "It's a lie!" But Warrington gently pushed him back into his seat. "Sit down, stupid," he said. "It's better for you as it is." The young man, who had hoped for a different ending, was forced to confront the reality of his situation.

Major Pendennis kept his word to Warrington, at least enough to satisfy his own conscience and to offer Helen some relief regarding her son. He assured her that Arthur's connection with the detestable gatekeeper was severed forever, sparing her further anxiety about an imprudent attachment or a humiliating marriage for Pen. For Arthur, the relief came after the initial sting to his vanity faded; he reasoned that Miss Fanny would not die of love for him and that the brief, unfortunate liaison would bear no ugly fruit.

With these matters settled, the party was finally free to execute their planned journey to the Continent. Arthur Pendennis, *rentier* and *voyageant* alongside Madame Pendennis and Mademoiselle Bell, joined George Warrington, *particulier*, age thirty-two, six feet tall (English), ordinary features, black hair and beard, etc. They secured passports from the Belgian consul at Dover and crossed the channel to Ostend. From there, they leisurely toured Bruges and Ghent on their way to Brussels and the Rhine. It is not our purpose to chronicle this well-worn tour, nor Laura's delight in the tranquil, ancient cities she saw for the first time, nor Helen's wonder at the Beguine convents. Yet, the sight of black-veiled nuns kneeling with outstretched arms before illuminated altars, and the strange pomp of Catholic worship, filled her with almost terror.
