Barefoot friars in the streets, crowned images of saints defying written law, priests in gorgeous robes, and theatres open on Sundays—all these new sights bewildered the simple country lady.

When the young men returned from their evening drives, they found their mother's books of devotion laid out. Upon entering, Laura would often cease reading the psalms, the sacred pages Helen loved most. The recent events had cruelly shaken Helen; Laura watched her friend with intense, hidden anxiety. Poor Pen was most constant and affectionate in his attentions, yet a secret hung between them. Helen's wounded bosom yearned for her son, though she harbored a secret anguish, almost rage, at the thought of being dispossessed of his heart. She missed the sacred days of boyhood when Arthur's heart held no secrets, when she was his all in all, when he poured his hopes and childish vanities into her tender embrace. Now, fate and selfishness had driven him forth on wayward wings to seek his own mate. Watching this devouring care in her friend, Laura once confessed, "If Pen had loved me as you wished, I should have gained him, but I should have lost you, mamma. I like you to love me best. Men do not know what it is to love as we do." Helen sighed, agreeing to this sentiment while protesting the rest. For my part, I suppose Miss Laura was right: love is an hour with us, but all night and all day with a woman. Damon has his taxes and parades; Delia has only Damon. Is it not your nature to creep about his feet and twine round his trunk, while he stands like a British man with his hands in his breeches pockets?

Old Pendennis had seen his friends off to the water's edge, leaving Warrington in charge of the expedition. He himself was bound for a brief visit to a great man and friend, after which he intended to join his sister-in-law at the German watering-place. The Major felt his long attentions to his sick family had earned him relaxation. Though the best partridges were thinned, the pheasants remained at Stillbrook. He betook himself to the hospitable mansion of the Marquis of Steyne, where he disported himself with great comfort. A royal duke, foreign notables, and illustrious statesmen visited; it did the old fellow's heart good to see his name in the *Morning Post* among the distinguished company. He was invaluable in a country-house, entertaining young men with queer anecdotes and grivoise stories in the smoking-room, walking the ladies through the gardens, and pointing out where the timber was to be felled or where old Lord Lynx discovered Sir Phelim O'Neal. He knew the number of domestics in the housekeeper's room and had a word for everybody. Richly meriting his vacation, he transferred the command of the family expedition to Warrington, keeping him in the service of the ladies—a servitude George willingly underwent for his friend's sake and the delightful society he found.

Warrington, a good German scholar, gave Miss Laura lessons, while Pen was too weak or lazy to resume his studies. Warrington acted as courier, interpreter, and manager of money matters, finding the English church and walking decorously with the ladies. He walked by Mrs. Pendennis's donkey during evening excursions, procured *Galignani* for her, and devised comfortable seats under the lime-trees. Many a whiskered Prussian or French dandy cast longing glances toward the pretty, fresh-coloured English girl accompanying the pale widow, wishing to dance with her. But Laura rarely appeared in the ballroom unless Pen vouchsafed to walk with her. As for Warrington, the rough diamond lacked the polish of a dancing-master and could not waltz, though he would have liked to learn if he could have had such a partner. Such a partner! *Psha*, what had a stiff bachelor to do with partners? Yet he stayed on. One might have thought him the widow's son, watching her with constant care, or an adventurer seeking her fortune. Very likely he did seek some benefit, for this is a selfish story, and almost everyone is occupied about Number One. Warrington selfishly devoted himself to Helen, who selfishly devoted herself to Pen, who selfishly devoted himself to himself, save for his mother's health, which caused him serious disquiet. Though they sat together, they spoke little; the cloud remained between them.

Every day Laura looked for Warrington, welcoming him with frank eagerness. He found himself talking to her as he never knew he could, performing acts of gallantry that astounded him afterward. He stared blankly in the glass at the crow's feet around his eyes and the streaks of white in his hair, envying the young bucks, the tight-waisted Germans, and the capering Frenchmen for their youth and good looks. Every night he quitted the little circle with greater reluctance, retreating to his lodging feeling lonelier and unhappier. The widow could not miss his attachment. She understood now why Major Pendennis, always a tacit enemy of her darling project, had been so eager for Warrington to join them. Laura openly owned her enthusiastic regard for him; Arthur made no move to intervene, choosing not to see what was happening or to prevent it. Helen was in torture, secretly feuding with her son, in doubt about Laura, and averse to Warrington, the good and generous. No wonder the healing waters of Rosenbad did her good, nor that Doctor von Glauber found no progress in her recovery. Meanwhile, Pen recovered rapidly, sleeping twelve hours a day and eating huge meals, regaining his strength in a couple of months.

After fifteen days at their resting place, a letter arrived from Major Pendennis announcing his speedy arrival at Rosenbad. Soon after, the Major appeared, accompanied by Morgan, his faithful valet. When traveling, the Major wore a jaunty, juvenile costume; to see his back, one might have taken him for one of the young fellows Warrington was beginning to envy. It was not until he moved that the observer noticed Time had weakened his ancient knees and impeded the action of his natty, varnished boots. Magnates of our own country and foreign nations were present that autumn. The elder Pendennis read the strangers' list with gratification, pleased to find acquaintances among the great folks, and intended to present his nephew to a German Grand Duchess, a Russian Princess, and an English Marquis. That very evening, leaning on his nephew's arm, the old gentleman appeared in the halls of the Kursaal, losing or winning a napoleon or two at *Trente-et-quarante*. He claimed he did not play to win or lose, but simply as other folks did. He pointed out Russians and Spaniards gambling for heaps of gold, denouncing their eagerness as sordid and barbarous, while asserting that an English gentleman should play where the fashion is play but not elate or depress himself at the sport. He recounted how his friend, the Marquis of Steyne, had lost eighteen thousand at a sitting and broken the bank three nights running in Paris without showing emotion. "And that's what I call being an English gentleman, Pen, my dear boy," the old gentleman said, warming as he prattled. "The great manner only remains with us and a few families in France."

As Russian Princesses passed him, whose reputations had long ceased to be doubtful, and damaged English ladies accompanied by their faithful attendants, the old Major, with eager garrulity, told his nephew wonderful particulars regarding their lives, diverting the young man with scandals. Egad, he felt quite young again, remarking to Pen as the Princess Obstropski, rouged and grinning, smiled and recognized him. He remembered her in '14 as an actress of the Paris Boulevard, married to the Emperor Alexander's aide-de-camp. He courteously asked leave to call upon the Princess and present his nephew, pointing out a half-dozen other famous personages. What would poor Helen have thought, could she have heard those tales or known to what kind of people her brother-in-law was presenting her son? Only once, leaning on Arthur's arm, had she passed through the room where the green tables were prepared, shrinking terrified from the pandemonium of the croaking croupiers shouting *Rouge gagne* and *Couleur perd*. She had imploringly begged Pen to promise, on his word of honor, that he would never play at those tables. The scene that frightened the simple widow only amused the worldly old veteran, making him feel young again. He could breathe the air cheerfully which stifled her. Her right was not his right; his food was her poison. To the credit of Mr. Pen, he kept honestly the promise made to his mother and stoutly told his uncle of his intention to abide by it.

When the Major arrived, his presence cast a damp upon at least three members of the party: Laura, who had no respect for him; Warrington, whose manner showed involuntary haughtiness; and the timid widow, who dreaded interference with her darling projects. Indeed, the Major, unknown to himself, was the bearer of tidings destined to bring about a catastrophe. Pen and his two ladies had apartments in Rosenbad; Warrington lodged nearby; the Major, befitting his dignity, took quarters at one of the great hotels, the Roman Emperor or the Four Seasons, where hundreds of gamblers and pleasure-seekers sat daily. On the morning after the Major's arrival, Pen went dutifully to pay his respects, finding the sitting-room arranged by Morgan with hats brushed, coats laid out, and despatch-boxes ready. Everything was trim, from the medicine-bottle to the prayer-book, without which the old fellow never traveled.

The elder had just finished a bath for which Rosenbad is famous. His after-bath toilet was not yet complete when Pen arrived. The elder called out cheerily from the inner apartment, and the valet brought a little packet to Pen's address—letters and papers from London, chiefly numbers of the *Pall Mall Gazette* that Mr. Finucane thought his collaborateur would like. Amongst the letters was a little note addressed to "Arther Pendennis, Esquire," which Arthur opened with a start and a blush, reading it with a keen pang of interest and sorrow. Fanny Bolton had come to his house, found him gone to Germany without a word, and prayed but for one word of kindness or the books he had promised. She said she would not reproach those who had found her at his bedside when he was in the fever and turned her away without a word. She thought she would have died of that, but Doctor Goodenough had kindly tended her. She forgave everybody and would pray for Arthur forever. She had kept one little lock of his hair when they cut it off and asked if she might still keep it, or if his mother would order it given up. She was willing to obey him in all things and remembered how kind he once was.

When Major Pendennis, fresh and smirking from his toilet, came out, he found Arthur with the note before him, his face set in savage anger, which surprised the elder. "What news from London, my boy?" he asked faintly. "Are the duns at you that you look so glum?"

"Do you know anything about this letter, sir?" Arthur asked.

"What letter, my good sir?" the other replied dryly.

"You know what I mean—about Miss... about Fanny Bolton," Arthur broke out. "When she was in my room? Was she there when I was delirious? Who sent her out of my chambers? Who intercepted her letters? Who dared to do it? Did you do it, uncle?"

"It's not my practice to tamper with gentlemen's letters or answer damned impertinent questions," Major Pendennis cried out in a tremor of indignation. "There was a girl in your rooms when I came up at great personal inconvenience, and to meet with a return of this kind for my affection to you is not pleasant, by Gad, sir."

"That's not the question, sir," Arthur said hotly. "Did you say anything harsh to this poor girl? Did you send her away from me?"

"I never spoke a word to the girl," the uncle said testily. "Let's change the subject."

"Then it's my mother that did it," Arthur broke out. "Did my mother send that poor child away?"

"I repeat I know nothing about it, sir," the elder said. "Let's change the subject."

"I'll never forgive the person who did it," Arthur said, bouncing up and seizing his hat.

"Stop, Arthur, for God's sake, stop!" cried the Major, but Arthur had rushed out, and the next minute the Major saw him striding rapidly down the street toward his home.

"Get breakfast!" said the old fellow to Morgan, wagging his head and sighing. "Poor Helen—poor soul! There'll be a row.

The "Fairoaks to let" sign stood as a grim monument to the evening's tragedy, though inside the house, the true eviction was taking place in the hearts of those gathered around the dying fire. Major Pendennis, ever the pragmatist, had organized a modest feast to welcome his son, but the table was a stage for a silent drama. Helen, too frail to eat, had sent word that she could not join them, leaving Laura to bear her mother's company in the shadows. While the Major chattered cheerfully, oblivious to the heavy silence that pressed against the two women, the atmosphere thickened as evening fell.

When Helen finally emerged into the sitting-room, she leaned heavily on Laura, her back turned to the fading light, hiding the pallor of her face from Arthur's view. As she approached Pen, placing her trembling arms around his shoulders in a gesture of desperate affection, Laura stepped back, unable to watch. Arthur felt the cold clamminess of her hand against his forehead and the violent tremor of her voice. Instead of comfort, her misery ignited a savage wrath within him. He saw not a mother seeking forgiveness, but a persecutor playing the martyr. "She persecutes me," he thought, his heart hardening against her plea. "She comes to me with the air of a martyr!"

"You look very ill, my child," she whispered, her voice breaking. "I don't like to see you look in that way." She sank onto the sofa, clutching his hand with fingers that felt like ice.

"I have had much to annoy me, mother," Pen replied, his chest heaving with suppressed rage.

The words hung in the air like a detonator. Helen's heart hammered so violently she sat frozen, terrified of what he would say. Warrington, Laura, and the Major held their breath, sensing the storm about to break.

"I have had letters from London," Arthur continued, his voice rising. "One has given me more pain than I ever felt in my life. It tells me that former letters of mine have been intercepted and stolen—that a young creature who showed me the greatest love has been cruelly used by... by you, mother."

"For God's sake stop!" cried Warrington, seeing Helen's terror. "She's ill—don't you see she is ill?"

"Let him go on," Helen whispered faintly.

"Let him go on and kill her!" Laura shouted, rushing to her mother's side. "Speak on, sir, and see her die."

"It is you who are cruel!" Pen roared, his soft nature revolting against the injustice laid at his feet. "It is you who attribute all this pain to me! You with your wicked reproaches, your wicked doubts, your wicked persecutions of those who love me—yes, those who brave everything for me, whom you despise and trample upon because they are of lower degree! Shall I tell you what I will do? Now that I know your conduct, I will go back to that poor girl you turned out of my doors and ask her to share my home. I'll defy the pride that persecutes her and the suspicion that insults us both!"

"Do you mean, Pen, that you—" Helen began, reaching out with eager, outstretched hands.

"Silence, hush, dear mother," Laura cried, stopping her.

Savagely as he spoke, Pen's words only fueled Helen's desperate need to hear him out. "Go on, Arthur, go on," she gasped, nearly swooning.

"By Gad, I say he shan't go on, or I won't hear him!" the Major bellowed, trembling with wrath. "If you choose, after all we've done for you, to insult your mother and disgrace the name of Pendennis by allying yourself with a low-born kitchen-girl, go and do it! But let us have no more to do with him. I wash my hands of you, sir! I'm an old fellow, ain't long for this world, and I come of an ancient family. I hoped, before I went off the hooks, that the fellow I'd liked and nursed would show me our name wasn't dishonoured. But if he won't, dammy, I say amen. Both my father and brother Jack were the proudest men in England, and I never thought there'd come this disgrace. I'm ashamed it's Arthur Pendennis."

The old man's voice broke into a sob, the second time Arthur had drawn tears from those wrinkled lids. The sound instantly checked Pen's anger; he stopped pacing, stunned by the vulnerability of the man who had raised him.

In the twilight, George Warrington's deep voice broke the tension. "Will you let me tell you something about myself, my kind friends?" he asked gently. "You have been so good to me, ma'am, so kind to Laura... My dear Pen and I have been such friends that I wanted to tell you my story. It is sad, contains another's secret, but it may divert you from this fatal misconception causing so much pain. May I speak, Mrs. Pendennis?"

"Pray speak," Helen said, though her mind was already racing with a terrifying hope.

Warrington filled a bumper of wine and drained it. "You see me," he began, "a man without ambition, careless of reputation, living in a garret from hand to mouth. I shall likely die there alone. I nailed myself to that doom in early life. Years ago, what interested me about Arthur was a story from Oxford gossip about his early affair with the Chatteris actress—a woman who, but for the Major's generalship, might have been your daughter-in-law. I can't see Pen blush, but I dare say Miss Bell does. And Major Pendennis, I dare say, laughs as he ought to—for he won. What would Arthur's lot have been tied at nineteen to an illiterate woman older than himself, with no equality, no love? He would have been miserable. When he threatened a similar union just now, it was anger, very natural after meeting unkind suspicion from friends. He has complained of unfair treatment toward an innocent person."

Helen tried to rise, but Warrington continued. "I liked him because of that early history, and because I like a man who shows unreasonable attachment for a woman. That is why we are friends. Pen has been a great comfort to a lonely man. I am not complaining; up in my garret with my old books and pipe for a wife, I am contented, occasionally envying men who can solace their ill fortune with the affection of a woman or child."

A sigh came from near Warrington, and a hand reached out to him, only to be instantly withdrawn by female prudery. Warrington resumed his confession. "My fate is such as I made it. Before college, at eighteen, I was at a private tutor's and became attached to a woman of lower degree and greater age. You shrink from me..."

"No, I don't," Laura said resolutely, laying her hand firmly in his. She had divined his story.

"She was a yeoman's daughter," Warrington faltered. "Her parents knew my father and encouraged me with coarse artifices. To do her justice, she never cared for me; she was forced by her family's threats. I thought I loved her, but I was married to a boor. Her dulness palled until I loathed it. Later, I found letters showing her heart had never been mine, but belonged to a man of her own degree. At my father's death, I paid my debts and settled my remaining shillings in an annuity on those who bore my name, on condition they hide themselves and not assume it. They kept the condition for more money. If I had made a name, they would have claimed it. I entered life at twenty, hopeless and ruined. I was a boyish victim of vulgar cheats. Beware how you marry out of your degree. I was made for a better lot, but God awarded me this one. So, I look on and see others happy with a heart as little bitter as possible."

"By Gad, sir!" cried the Major, suddenly in high good humour. "I intended you to marry Miss Laura here."

"And, by Gad, Master Shallow, I owe you a thousand pounds," Warrington said.

"How d'ye mean a thousand? It was only a pony, sir," the Major replied simply.

Helen, overwhelmed with delight, started up. "God bless you—God for ever bless you, Mr. Warrington!" She kissed his hands, ran to Pen, and fell into his arms.

"Yes, dearest mother," Pen said, embracing her with noble tenderness. "I am innocent, and my dear mother has done me a wrong."

"Oh yes, my child, I have wronged you!" Helen whispered. "Come away, Arthur—not here. I want to ask my child to forgive me—and my God, to forgive me; and to bless you, my son."

She led her tottering son into her room, closing the door as the three spectators watched the reconciliation in pleased silence. Ever after, the tender accents of that voice, the look of sacred eyes, and the quiver of fond lips smiling mournfully would be remembered by the young man. In his best moments and hours of trial, his mother's face would look down upon him, blessing him with a gaze of pity and purity, appearing as an angel transfigured with love.

The moon rose, lighting up Helen's sweet, pale face. Their talk was more tender and confidential than in years. Arthur told her the story of his struggle against temptation and his thankfulness for overcoming it. He promised never to wrong the girl again, nor wound his mother's pure heart. The threat to return was a momentary exasperation he repented of; he would never see her again. But his mother said yes, he should; she admitted her pride and culpability, begged his pardon for opening the letter, and promised to write to the young girl if she had time. Was it not natural she should love her Arthur? Again she kissed him and blessed him.

The clock struck nine. Helen reminded him of how, when he was a boy, she used to hear him say the *Our Father* at that hour. Once more, the young man fell to his mother's sacred knees and sobbed out the prayer echoed by millions of humbled men. As he spoke the last words, "for ever and ever" and "Amen," Helen's head fell on his shoulder, her arms closing round him.

A quarter of an hour later, Laura heard Arthur call, "Laura! Laura!" She rushed in to find him still on his knees, holding his mother's hand. Helen's head was sunk back, her face quite pale. Pen looked round, scared with ghastly terror. "Help, Laura, help! She's fainted—she's—"

Laura screamed and fell beside Helen. The shriek brought Warrington, the Major, and the servants running. The sainted woman was dead. Her last emotion was joy to be henceforth unchequered and eternal. Her tender heart beat no more; it had no more pangs, doubts, or griefs. Its last throb was love; Helen's last breath was a benediction.

The melancholy party hurried homewards. Helen was laid by her husband's side at Clavering, in the old church where she had prayed so often. For a while, Laura stayed with Dr. Portman, who read the service over his dear departed sister amidst his own sobs. Few cared for her when she was gone; scarcely more than a nun in a cloister did people know of that pious lady. A few words among the cottagers, a little talk at Clavering about the amount of property left, and speculation on whether Arthur would let Fairoaks—that was all. Except with one or two who cherished her, the kind soul was forgotten by the next market-day.

The great blank she left was in Laura's heart, to whom her love had been everything. "I am glad she gave me her blessing before she went away," Warrington said to Pen. Arthur, with humble wonder, hardly dared ask Heaven to make him worthy of it, feeling a saint was now interceding for him.

All the lady's affairs were found in perfect order. Papers showed she had long known her heart complaint and knew it would suddenly remove her; a prayer in her handwriting asked that her end might be in the arms of her son. Laura and Arthur talked over her sayings, packing up the vase destined for Dr. Goodenough, sending the silver coffee-pot to Portman, and giving a diamond ring with her hair to Warrington.

It was a hard day for Laura visiting Fairoaks, standing in the widow's blank chamber where those two had passed beloved hours. There were the clothes, the cushion, the chair, the glass that was no more to reflect her dear sad face. Pen knocked, led her downstairs, made her drink wine, and said, "God bless you." "Nothing shall ever be changed in your room," he said. "It is always your room—it is always my sister's room. Shall it not be so, Laura?" And Laura said, "Yes!"

Among the papers was a packet marked "Letters from Laura's father," which Arthur gave to her. The ink was faded, the tears dried out, the grief healed. Laura learned fully for the first time the tie that bound her so tenderly to Helen: how faithfully her more-than-mother had cherished her father's memory, how truly she had loved him, how meekly she had resigned him.

One legacy Pen remembered, unknown to Laura: Helen's wish to make a present to Fanny Bolton. Pen wrote to her, enclosing the letter under an envelope for Mr. Bows to read first. "Dear Fanny," Pen wrote, acknowledging her letters and thanking her for nursing him during his fever. "The last words of my dear mother who is no more were words of goodwill and gratitude to you... she said she would like to ask your pardon if she had harshly treated you—and that she would beg you to show your forgiveness by accepting some token of friendship." He concluded by stating that his friend, George Warrington, Esq., was trustee of a little sum of money, the interest of which would be paid to Fanny until she came of age or changed her name. The

Old Friends

The great English festival arrived, turning Epsom Downs into a sea of holiday-makers. Among the throngs of personages assembled to witness the Derby, a comfortable open carriage, drawn by a pair of spirited horses, carried Mrs. Bungay of Paternoster Row. She was attired in all the glory of Solomon, flanked by her modest friend, Mrs. Shandon. While the worthy publisher, Bungay, had recovered from a copious luncheon to engage in the mad sport of shying at the sticks nearby, Mrs. Shandon ambled among the drinking tenants and gipsies. Finucane remained in constant attendance on the ladies, intercepting gentlemen connected with the publishing house.

Mr. Archer approached to pay his respects, delighting Mrs. Bungay with a tour of the aristocracy. He pointed out the Prime Minister, the Chinese Ambassador surrounded by Mandarins, and even recounted a scandalous tale of a Chinese cook who had skinned the Ambassador's beloved spaniel and served it stuffed with forced-meat. Mrs. Bungay listened with wide-eyed astonishment as Archer detailed the Ambassador's failed attempt to woo Lady Flamingo, a woman currently squeezing her feet to fit the exotic standard of her future husband.

As the conversation turned to the younger generation, Mrs. Bungay inquired about Mr. Pendennis. Archer revealed that the young man possessed a substantial fortune—eighteen hundred a year in land and twenty-two thousand five hundred in government bonds. He explained that he had been consulted regarding the late Mrs. Pendennis's will, ensuring that the Major's potential extravagance was checked. Archer then identified several other notable figures, including Lord Rochester, Lord John, and the Duke of Hampshire, who boasted of attending sixty-five Derbies since childhood.

While the elite mingled in the carriages, a different scene unfolded on the battered roof of Wheeler's drag, a stage-coach carrying a crew of shabby spirits from Bow Street. As the famous horse Podasokus won the race, one of the passengers leaped onto the roof in triumph, declaring supper for ten and demanding that the expense be covered. The crowd below eyed the winner with suspicion, fearing a loan shark, while the driver and his companions celebrated the victory.

Nearby, cab No. 2002 transported a gentleman and two ladies. The young lady, Fanny Bolton, sat on the box with her mother, enjoying lobster salad and bitter ale. Her companion, Mr. Sam Huxter, a young surgeon, kept the atmosphere light with jokes and a collection of street trophies, including a gypsy fortune-teller who ominously warned Fanny to beware of a false fair man.

Suddenly, Arthur Pendennis approached the cab. Fanny's heart hammered; her mother turned a shade of red that rivaled the lobster she had consumed; and Mr. Huxter's demeanor shifted instantly from jovial to fiercely suspicious. Arthur, however, detected the tension and, recalling his own recent illness and poor temper, offered a conciliatory toast to Fanny's health. He extended his lavender kid glove to Huxter, a gesture of peace that disarmed the surgeon's clenched fist. Huxter, feeling the heat of his own hand and the black mark it left on Pen's glove, struggled with the urge to fight, but the moment passed. Pen drank the frothy, lukewarm beer cordially, and the immediate hostility dissolved into polite socializing.

As they talked, a young lady in a dove-colored dress passed by, leaning on the arm of a man with a military mustache. Fanny's gaze followed her, sparking a romantic fantasy in her mind involving love, betrayal, and a tragic ending where Sam Huxter was not the hero. After Pen departed, the mood in the cab shifted. The sun seemed dimmer, the beer hotter, and the world changed for Huxter. He spent the ride home with peas and a tin pea-shooter in his pocket, eventually bursting into a sardonic laugh when another wag fired a pea-shooter at his face. Upon arriving home, Fanny's charm returned; she coaxed and smiled, leading to an emotional reunion where Huxter confessed his jealousy and unworthiness, nearly kissing her before her mother intervened.

Meanwhile, Arthur Pendennis visited Lady Clavering's carriage. The carriage was besieged by dandies and military bucks courting Miss Amory, Lady Clavering's daughter. Pen pushed through the crowd to reach her, engaging in a playful exchange about the "monster" Huxter, whom Miss Amory described as someone who would kill Pen if he attended to her when sick. Pen, amused by her dramatic French, noted that Huxter had merely bruised his glove in a handshake and was a young surgeon from Clavering.

As the day wound down, the carriage returned to Grosvenor Place. The Major observed the heavy betting habits of Sir Francis Clavering, fearing the family fortune would be drained by the turf. Inside the carriage, young Hopeful Clavering confessed to having bet on Podasokus despite his father's promises, revealing that his father had lost seven thousand pounds backing the favorite. The news shattered the Begum's spirits; she wept, vowing to separate from her ungrateful husband. As the carriage drove away, the Major resolved to help Miss Blanche, who lamented her status as a beggar due to her father's deceit. The party dispersed, leaving the Major and his companions to ponder the financial ruin awaiting the Clavering house.

It has been nearly a year since the last time we saw Arthur Pendennis in his black coat. Now, that somber garment is being swapped for a blue one, signaling a transformation far more pleasing than mere clothing. His wig has been retired to the attic, allowing his hair—though slightly thinned by the rigors of life—to reclaim its public stage. Most significantly, Arthur has earned the honor of appearing at Court in the uniform of a Cornet of the Clavering troop of the Yeomanry Cavalry, presented to the Sovereign by the flamboyant Marquis of Steyne.

This promotion was a measure vigorously, almost tearfully, advocated by Arthur's uncle, the Major. The old gentleman refused to let a single year pass without this ceremonial rite of passage into gentlemanhood. He believed his nephew deserved a club far more exclusive than the Megatherium and loudly proclaimed his disappointment that Arthur's income had settled at a mere fifteen hundred a year.

Such is the official valuation of Pendennis in the world—a figure that has surprisingly elevated his standing among publishers and even softened the edges of maternal disapproval. While the dazzling Myra might snag an Earl, leaving the slightly asymmetrical Beatrice to dangle after a boor, logic dictates that Mr. Pendennis should be her support. Indeed, in the first winter following his mother's inheritance, Mrs. Hawxby arranged for Beatrice to learn billiards from Arthur and insisted he drive her pony carriage, citing their shared "literary" nature. She publicly declared that Arthur, under the instigation of his "horrid old uncle," had behaved infamously by trifling with Beatrice's feelings. The truth, however, was that the Major, knowing Mrs. Hawxby's predatory nature, had spirited Arthur away from her claws at a country house, saving him from her immediate grasp if not her tongue. Arthur had declined the Major's invitation to spend Christmas at Clavering, finding the proximity to the haunted memories of Fairoaks too painful to bear.

Meanwhile, the Clavering family had vanished from our view until their reappearance at the Epsom racecourse. Their past year had been a trial by fire for Lady Clavering, a woman of immense good nature whose appetite and temper had been sorely tested by domestic chaos. "I'd rather eat a turnip for dessert than that pineapple," she lamented to her faithful friend, Strong, regarding the fruit laid before her. "If only I could eat it in peace! How much happier I was when I was a widow!"

The Claverings had indeed made a disastrous start. Their London debut was shaky, followed by a notorious failure. The town whispered that human patience could not withstand Sir Francis Clavering, describing him as hopelessly low, dull, and disreputable, casting a taint over the entire household. Questions abounded: Where did the Begum, with her money but missing 'h's, come from? Was the daughter's Gallicized affectation fit for English breeding? Sir Francis was a gambler surrounded by blacklegs; his son, Harry Foker, was accused of cheating at cards and showing the white feather. Consequently, Lady Rockminster dumped Lady Clavering, the great ladies barred their daughters from her parties, and the young men attending behaved with odious familiarity. Poor Lady Clavering was forced to admit she had to open her parlour to "the canal"—the lower strata of society—because the "tip-tops" refused to come.

Yet, Lady Clavering bore this rejection without pride or ill-will. She had simply followed the social godmother's rule: know whom they know, ask whom they ask. It is easier to leave a mistress than to be left by her; society can be abandoned with relief, but when society abandons you, the mortification is severe.

Among the faithless, one faithful figure emerged: Harry Foker. However, his lack of prudence turned his secret passion for Pen into a town-wide scandal, reaching the ears of his weak mother and ultimately the bald, inflexible Foker Senior. The result was a violent confrontation that banished young Henry Foker from England for a year. Ordered to return and marry his cousin or retire on a paltry three hundred a year, the exiled gentleman found even the Parisian Boulevard and the Italian sky bleak under his crape-covered eyes.

For Sir Francis Clavering, that same year was a catastrophe. As our sporting readers recall, Lord Harrowhill's horse, Podasokus, won the Derby, baffling the experts who had backed the non-existent Borax. Sir Francis, relying on "valuable information" from rascals on the turf, had heavily backed the favorite. The result, as his son noted to Lady Clavering, was a staggering loss of seven thousand pounds.

This blow crushed the widow, who had already paid her husband's debts countless times, funded his houses, and silenced his creditors. Now, she faced the instant demand for this enormous sum.

The Major Pendennis, acting as the family's adviser, had previously inspected every room of the Clavering estate, including the closet where the family skeleton was locked. While the Baronet hid his financial entanglement in a web of lies, the Major knew Lady Clavering's situation intimately. He understood the vast sacrifices the widow Amory had made for her second marriage and openly suggested that Miss Blanche deserved a better provision.

The Major revealed his knowledge of Lady Clavering's past, including her separation from her first husband in India, pointing out the Calcutta newspaper that detailed Amory's trial. He admitted his interest was motivated by a desire to marry his nephew to the daughter.

"My dear lady," the Major explained, "my boy has only five hundred a year. A wife with ten thousand pounds would barely improve his lot. But Arthur is a shrewd, ambitious fellow who has sown his wild oats. With a good start and a partner who can help him advance, he could make a name his sons would be proud of. Why keep a useless seat in Parliament for Sir Francis when Arthur could be a distinguished son-in-law? It is a bargain for both."

Lady Clavering, the good-natured Begum, agreed readily. She spoke warmly of Arthur, noting how much she missed his late mother, and confessed that while she wished Laura Pen (favored by Lady Rockminster) were to marry, her own daughter Blanche had not been a comfort. "Laura ought to marry," she said.

"Marry on five hundred a year?" the Major exclaimed. "You are mad! Consult me on all affairs with your unhappy husband. Old Pendennis is always your friend."

He had also preached these practical truths to Miss Amory, arguing that mutual convenience was the only foundation for a lasting marriage. Love matches, he claimed, inevitably led to quarrels and desertion, whereas a sensible union allowed the lady to bring the means and the gentleman to win the plate. "A couple like that have something to talk about," he insisted. "Even Cupid and Psyche would yawn after a few evenings if they had nothing but sentiment to discuss."

Miss Amory, content with Pen as long as no better option existed, accepted his presence in the house without engagement. They moved with extreme familiarity but little sentiment, meeting and parting in good humor. Arthur reflected that he was the same man who had once harbored a grand passion and raged over Briseis, yet time had brought its ordinary consolations.

We alter very little. When we lament changes in our friends, we fail to realize that circumstance merely reveals latent qualities. The selfish languor of today is the consequence of yesterday's selfish ardor; the scorn of the successful parvenu is the continuation of the needy struggler's career. Our mental changes are like grey hairs or wrinkles—the fulfillment of mortal growth. That which is now calm weariness was once fierce ambition; that which is sluggish obesity was once boisterous health.

Lucky is the one who can bear failure generously, giving up his broken sword to Fate with a humble heart. Consider, reader, how you stand in the crowd today. Perhaps you are the lucky captain riding into port, bowing from the deck with a care hidden under a star on your breast. Or perhaps you are the wrecked sailor lashed to a solitary spar, drowning out of sight. Yet, both the sinking man and the successful captain are likely thinking of home, remembering the time before the voyage began. You are the same person you were in childhood, having passed through struggles of defeat, success, crime, and remorse, loving and growing cold, weeping and laughing again. Alone on the spar, or alone in the applause, you remain the same.

Our good-natured Begum, Lady Clavering, was initially so incensed by her husband's latest display of duplicity and financial folly that she flatly refused to assist Sir Francis Clavering in meeting his debts of honor. She declared her intention to separate from him, leaving him to face the dire consequences of his incorrigible weakness and wastefulness. Following the disastrous transactions at the Derby, the unlucky gambler found himself in such a state of mind that he wished to avoid everyone. This included his turf associates, whom he owed money to and feared he could not pay, and his wife, his long-suffering banker, whom he now doubted would allow him to draw further funds.

The next morning, when Lady Clavering inquired if Sir Francis was home, she was informed that he had not returned. Instead, he had sent a messenger to his valet, ordering him to forward clothes and letters by the bearer. Strong knew that he would receive a visit or message from his principal within the day. Accordingly, he drafted a note beseeching him to call upon his "distracted friend" F.C. at Short Hotel, Blackfriars, and ask for Mr. Francis. The Baronet possessed a peculiar mental trait: he would rather tell a lie than admit the truth, and he invariably began a contest with fortune by running away and hiding himself.

The Boots of Mr. Short's establishment, who carried Clavering's message to Grosvenor Place and returned with his carpet-bag, instantly recognized the owner. He whispered this intelligence to the footman setting the breakfast table, who carried the news to the servants' hall, which reached Mrs. Bonner, the housekeeper and confidential maid, who finally delivered it to my lady. Thus, every single person in the Grosvenor Place establishment knew that Sir Francis was in hiding, under the alias of Francis, at an inn in the Blackfriars Road. Furthermore, Sir Francis's coachman shared the news with other gentlemen's coachmen, who spread it to their masters and to the neighboring Tattersall's, where gloomy anticipations arose that Sir Francis Clavering was about to embark on a tour of the Levant.

That day, the number of letters addressed to Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., which found their way to his hall-table, was quite remarkable. The French cook submitted his account to my lady; tradesmen supplying her table, Messrs. Finer and Gimcrack (the mercers and ornamental dealers), and Madame Crinoline (the eminent milliner) forwarded their bills in company with Miss Amory's private, and by no means inconsiderable, accounts from each establishment.

In the afternoon of the day after the Derby, Strong, after a colloquy with his principal at Short's Hotel (whom he found crying and drinking Curacoa), called to transact business at Grosvenor Place. He found all these suspicious documents ranged in the Baronet's study and began to open them with a rueful countenance.

Mrs. Bonner, my lady's maid and housekeeper, descended upon him while he was occupied. Mrs. Bonner, a part of the family as necessary to her mistress as the Chevalier was to Sir Francis, was naturally on Lady Clavering's side in the dispute and, bound by duty, even angrier than her ladyship herself.

"She won't pay if she takes my advice," Mrs. Bonner declared. "You'll please to go back to Sir Francis, Captain—and he's lurking about in a low public-house and doesn't dare to face his wife like a man!—and say that we won't pay his debts no longer. We made a man of him; we took him out of gaol (and other folks too, perhaps); we've paid his debts over and over again; we set him up in Parliament, gave him a house in town and country. Where he doesn't dare show his face, the shabby sneak! We've given him the horse he rides, the dinner he eats, and the very clothes he has on his back; and we will give him no more. Our fortune, such as is left of it, is left to ourselves, and we won't waste any more of it on this ungrateful man. We'll give him enough to live upon and leave him, that's what we'll do: and that's what you may tell him from Susan Bonner."

Susan Bonner's mistress, hearing of Strong's arrival, sent for him immediately. The Chevalier went up to her ladyship not without hopes that he would find her more tractable than her factotum, Mrs. Bonner. Many times before he had pleaded his client's cause with Lady Clavering and caused her good nature to relent. He tried once more, painting in dismal colors the situation in which he had found Sir Francis and refusing to guarantee any consequences if he could not find means to meet his engagements.

"Kill hisself," laughed Mrs. Bonner. "Kill hisself, will he? Dying's the best thing he could do." Strong vowed that he had found him with razors on the table; but at this, in her turn, Lady Clavering laughed bitterly. "He'll do himself no harm as long as there's a shilling left of which he can rob a poor woman. His life's quite safe, Captain: you may depend upon that. Ah! it was a bad day that ever I set eyes on him."

"He's worse than the first man," cried out my lady's aide-de-camp. "He was a man, he was—a wild devil, but he had the courage of a man—whereas this fellow... what's the use of my lady paying his bills, selling her diamonds, and forgiving him? He'll be as bad again next year. The very next chance he has he'll be cheating her and robbing her; and her money will go to keep a pack of rogues and swindlers—I don't mean you, Captain—you've been a good friend to us enough, bating we wish we'd never set eyes on you."

The Chevalier saw from the words Mrs. Bonner had let slip regarding the diamonds that the kind Begum was disposed to relent once more, and that there were still hopes for his principal.

"Upon my word, ma'am," he said, with a real feeling of sympathy for Lady Clavering's troubles and admiration for her untiring good nature, along with a show of enthusiasm that advanced his graceless patron's cause—"anything you say against Clavering, or Mrs. Bonner here cries out against me, is no better than we deserve, both of us, and it was an unlucky day for you when you saw either. He has behaved cruelly to you, and if you were not the most generous and forgiving woman in the world, I know there would be no chance for him. But you can't let the father of your son be a disgraced man and send little Frank into the world with such a stain upon him. Tie him down; bind him by any promises you like: I vouch for him that he will subscribe them."

"And break 'em," said Mrs. Bonner.

"And keep 'em this time," cried out Strong. "He must keep them. If you could have seen how he wept, ma'am! 'Oh, Strong,' he said to me, 'it's not for myself I feel now: it's for my boy—it's for the best woman in England, whom I have treated basely—I know I have.' He didn't intend to bet upon this race, ma'am—indeed he didn't. He was cheated into it: all the ring was taken in. He thought he might make the bet quite safely, without the least risk. And it will be a lesson to him for all his life long. To see a man cry—oh, it's dreadful."

"He don't think much of making my dear missus cry," said Mrs. Bonner—"poor dear soul!—look if he does, Captain."

"If you've the soul of a man, Clavering," Strong said to his principal when he recounted this scene to him, "you'll keep your promise this time: and, so help me Heaven! if you break word with her, I'll turn against you, and tell all."

"What all?" cried Mr. Francis, to whom his ambassador brought the news back at Short's Hotel, where Strong found the Baronet crying and drinking curacoa.

"Psha! Do you suppose I am a fool?" burst out Strong. "Do you suppose I could have lived so long in the world, Frank Clavering, without having my eyes about me? You know I have but to speak and you are a beggar tomorrow. And I am not the only man who knows your secret."

"Who else does?" gasped Clavering.

"Old Pendennis does, or I am very much mistaken. He recognised the man the first night he saw him, when he came drunk into your house."

"He knows it, does he?" shrieked out Clavering. "Damn him—kill him."

"You'd like to kill us all, wouldn't you, old boy?" said Strong, with a sneer, puffing his cigar.

The Baronet dashed his weak hand against his forehead; perhaps the other had interpreted his wish rightly. "Oh, Strong!" he cried, "if I dared, I'd put an end to myself, for I'm the d——est miserable dog in all England. It's that that makes me so wild and reckless. It's that which makes me take to drink" (and he drank, with a trembling hand, a bumper of his fortifier—the curacoa), "and to live about with these thieves. I know they're thieves, every one of 'em, d——d thieves. And—and how can I help it?—and I didn't know it, you know—and, by Gad, I'm innocent—and until I saw the d——d scoundrel first, I knew no more about it than the dead—and I'll fly, and I'll go abroad out of the reach of the confounded hells, and I'll bury myself in a forest, by Gad! and hang myself up to a tree—and, oh—I'm the most miserable beggar in all England!" And so, with more tears, shrieks, and curses, the impotent wretch vented his grief and deplored his unhappy fate; and, in the midst of groans and despair and blasphemy, vowed his miserable repentance.

The honored proverb which declares that to be an ill wind which blows good to nobody was verified in the case of Sir Francis Clavering, and another of the occupants of Mr. Strong's chambers in Shepherd's Inn. The man was "good," by a lucky hap, with whom Colonel Altamont made his bet; and on the settling day of the Derby—as Captain Clinker, who was appointed to settle Sir Francis Clavering's book for him (for Lady Clavering, by the advice of Major Pendennis, would not allow the Baronet to liquidate his own money transactions), paid over the notes to the Baronet's many creditors—Colonel Altamont had the satisfaction of receiving the odds of thirty to one in fifties, which he had taken against the winning horse of the day.

Numbers of the Colonel's friends were present on the occasion to congratulate him on his luck—all Altamont's own set, and the gents who met in the private parlour of the convivial Wheeler, my host of the Harlequin's Head, came to witness their comrade's good fortune and would have liked, with a generous sympathy for success, to share in it. "Now was the time," Tom Driver had suggested to the Colonel, "to have up the specie ship that was sunk in the Gulf of Mexico, with the three hundred and eighty thousand dollars on board, besides bars and doubloons." "The Tredyddlums were very low—to be bought for an old song—never was such an opportunity for buying shares," Mr. Keightley insinuated; and Jack Holt pressed forward his tobacco-smuggling scheme, the audacity of which pleased the Colonel more than any other of the speculations proposed to him. Then of the Harlequin's Head boys: there was Jack Rackstraw, who knew of a pair of horses the Colonel must buy; Tom Fleet, whose satirical paper, *The Swell*, wanted but two hundred pounds of capital to be worth a thousand a year to any man—"with such a power and influence, Colonel, you rogue, and the entrée of the green-rooms in London," Tom urged; whilst little Moss Abrams entreated the Colonel not to listen to these absurd fellows with their humbugging speculations, but to invest his money in some good bills which Moss could get for him, and which would return him fifty per cent as safe as the Bank of England.

Each and all of these worthies came round the Colonel with their various blandishments; but he had courage enough to resist them, and to button up his notes in the pocket of his coat, and go home to Strong, and "sport" the outer door of the chambers. Honest Strong had given his fellow-lodger good advice about all his acquaintances; and though, when pressed, he did not mind frankly taking twenty pounds himself out of the Colonel's winnings, Strong was a great deal too upright to let others cheat him.

He was not a bad fellow when in good fortune, this Altamont. He ordered a smart livery for Grady, and made poor old Costigan shed tears of quickly dried gratitude by giving him a five-pound note after a snug dinner at the Back Kitchen, and he bought a green shawl for Mrs. Bolton, and a yellow one for Fanny: the most brilliant "sacrifices" of a Regent Street haberdasher's window. And a short time after this, upon her birthday, which happened in the month of June, Miss Amory received from "a friend" a parcel containing an enormous brass inlaid writing-desk, in which there was a set of amethysts, the most hideous eyes ever looked upon,—a musical snuff-box, and two Keepsakes of the year before last, and accompanied with a couple of gown pieces of the most astounding colours, the receipt of which goods made the Sylphide laugh and wonder immoderately. Now it is a fact that Colonel Altamont had made a purchase of cigars

Shortly after Colonel Altamont's stroke of luck at Epsom, he executed his plan for a grand foreign tour. The chroniclers of high society, those who descend to London Bridge to bid farewell to the fashionable exiles, reported that the *Soho* bound for Antwerp carried "Sir Robert, Lady, and the Misses Hodge; Mr. Serjeant Kewsy, and Mrs. and Miss Kewsy; Colonel Altamont, Major Coddy, etc." The Colonel traveled in full state, dressed in a rich traveling costume, drinking brandy-and-water with gusto, remaining sober while others succumbed to seasickness, and accompanied by his faithful Irish body-servant, the very legionary who had waited on him and Captain Strong in their Shepherd's Inn chambers.

Before departing, the Chevalier shared a copious dinner with his friend the Colonel at Blackwall. Over many toasts to Altamont's health, the Colonel, a generous soul, offered Strong a loan. "Strong, old boy," he said, "if you need a bit of cash, now is your time. I'm your man. You've been a good feller to me, and a twenty-pound note makes no difference to me." But Strong politely declined. "No, thank you," he replied, feigning flushness. "I'm quite flush—well, not flush enough to repay your last loan, Altamont, but sufficient to carry on for a while." Thus, with a cordial but slightly strained handshake, they parted.

Was money truly making Altamont more honest, or merely making him appear so in Strong's eyes? Perhaps wealth had improved him; perhaps Strong simply admired the beauty of riches. Inside himself, Strong reasoned that this "unlucky outcast," a returned convict, was ten times better than his friend Sir Francis Clavering. Altamont possessed pluck and a strange form of honesty; he stuck to friends and faced enemies. Clavering, conversely, lacked the courage to do either. "What cloud hangs over this poor devil?" Strong mused. "He was only a little wild, signing his father-in-law's name. Many have done worse and held their heads high. Clavering does not hold his head up; he never did, not even in his best days." Strong perhaps regretted the lie he'd told the open-handed Colonel—that he wasn't in want of money. It was a lie born of honesty, and the Chevalier, despite his desperate situation, could not bring himself to borrow again from his outlawed friend. Besides, he had hopes. Clavering had promised money, and though the Baronet's promises were notoriously unreliable, the Chevalier was hopeful. He trusted in the chances of intercepting stray remittances, a task for which Mr. Strong was ideally suited.

Life in the Shepherd's Inn chambers felt glum without the Colonel. The solitary existence grated on Strong's social soul, compounded by his own extravagant habits. With a servant at his beck and call to run errands, arrange his toilet, and cook his meals, it became a grand and touching sight to see the portly gentleman painting his own boots and broiling his own mutton chop. The Chevalier had a wife, a Spanish lady from Vittoria, who had returned to her friends after a few months of marriage, having broken his head with a dish. Now, melancholy settled over him, or as he termed it, he was "down on his luck." These moments of depression are the lot of every hero: Marius at Minturnae, Charles Edward in the Highlands, Napoleon before Elba. What great man has not faced evil fortune?

Supplies from Clavering were nonexistent. The twenty-five pounds, or "pony," the exemplary Baronet had received from Altamont, had vanished as swiftly as previous ponies. Clavering had been down the river with a party of sporting gentlemen, dodging the police to land in Essex, where they staged a fight between Billy Bluck and Dick the cabman. The Baronet backed the cabman, who won thirteen rounds until an unlucky blow to the windpipe killed him. "It's always my luck, Strong," Sir Francis lamented. "The betting was three to one on the cabman, and I thought I was as sure of thirty pounds as if it were in my pocket. Dammit, I owe my man Lightfoot fourteen pounds now, which he lent and paid for me. He duns me—the confounded impudent blackguard! I wish to Heaven I knew how to get a bill drawn, or squeeze something from my lady! I'll give you half, Ned, upon my soul and honor, I'll give you half if you can get anyone to do us a fifty."

"Ned" responded sternly that he had given his word of honor as a gentleman to be no party to any future bill transactions involving her husband (who had also given his word). The Chevalier vowed to keep his promise, promising to black his own boots all his life rather than break it. Furthermore, he declared he would advise Lady Clavering that Sir Francis was about to break his faith toward her the moment he suspected such an intention.

Upon receiving this intelligence, Sir Francis Clavering, according to custom, cried and cursed volubly. He spoke of death as his only resource, beseeching his dear Strong, his best friend, his dear old Ned, not to abandon him. Yet, as he descended the stairs of Shepherd's Inn, he swore and blasphemed at Ned as the most infernal villain, traitor, blackguard, and coward under the sun, wishing him in his grave and a worse place, only wishing the ruffian would live long enough for Frank Clavering to have his revenge.

In Strong's chambers, the Baronet met a gentleman whose visits had become frequent: Mr. Samuel Huxter, of Clavering. This young fellow, who had once poached walnuts in Clavering Park and watched the Baronet drive through the street with four horses, possessed immense respect for his Member and a prodigious delight in making his acquaintance. He introduced himself with blushing trepidation as a "Clavering man"—son of Mr. Huxter of the marketplace, whose father had attended Sir Francis's keeper, Coxwood, when his gun burst and took off three fingers. Proud to make Sir Francis's acquaintance, he spoke affably. Honest Huxter talked about Sir Francis to the chaps at Bartholomew's and told Fanny in the lodge that, after all, there was nothing like a thoroughbred un, a regular good old English gentleman of the olden time. Fanny replied that she thought Sir Francis was an "ojous" creature—she didn't know why—but she couldn't abide him; she was sure he was wicked, low, and mean. When Sam countered that Sir Francis was very affable and had borrowed half a sovereign of him quite kindly, Fanny burst into laughter, pulled Sam's long hair (which was not yet of irreproachable cleanliness), patted his chin, and called him a "stoopid, stoopid, old foolish stoopid," noting that Sir Francis was always borrowing money from everybody, and that Mar had actually refused him twice, forcing him to wait three months to get seven shillings.

"Don't say 'er' but 'her,' 'borrer' but 'borrow,' 'actially' but 'actually,' Fanny," Mr. Huxter replied—not correcting her argument, but her grammar.

"Well then, 'her,' and 'borrow,' and 'hactually'—there then, you stoopid," said the other. The scholar made such a pretty face that the grammar master was quickly appeased and would have willingly given her a hundred more lessons on the spot at the price he charged for that one.

Of course, Mrs. Bolton was present, and Fanny and Dr. Sam were on exceedingly familiar and confidential terms. Time had brought Fanny certain consolations and soothed certain regrets, which are deucedly bitter when they occur but are, no more than tooth-pulling or any other pang, eternal.

As you sit, surrounded by respect and affection; happy, honored, and flattered in your old age; your foibles gently indulged; your least words kindly cherished; your garrulous old stories received for the hundredth time with dutiful forbearance and never-failing hypocritical smiles; the women of your house constant in their flatteries; the young men hushed and attentive when you begin to speak; the servants awestricken; the tenants cap in hand, ready to act in the place of your worship's horses when your honor takes a drive—it has often struck you, O thoughtful Dives! that this respect and these glories are for the main part transferred, with your fee simple, to your successor. The servants will bow, and the tenants shout, for your son as for you; the butler will fetch him the wine (improved by a little keeping) that's now in your cellar. When your night comes, and the light of your life is gone down, as sure as the morning rises after you and without you, the sun of prosperity and flattery shines on your heir. Men come and bask in the halo of consols and acres that beams round about him. The reverence is transferred with the estate; of which, with all its advantages, pleasures, respect, and goodwill, he becomes the life-tenant. How long do you wish or expect that your people will regret you? How much time does a man devote to grief before he begins to enjoy? A great man must keep his heir at his feast like a living *memento mori*. If he holds very much by life, the presence of the other must be a constant sting and warning. "Make ready to go," says the successor to your honor; "I am waiting: and I could hold it as well as you."

What has this reference to the possible reader to do with any of the characters of this history? Do we wish to apologize for Pen because he has got a white hat, and because his mourning for his mother is fainter? All the lapse of years, all the career of fortune, all the events of life, however strongly they may move or eagerly excite him, can never remove that sainted image from his heart, or banish that blessed love from its sanctuary. If he yields to wrong, the dear eyes will look sadly upon him when he dares to meet them; if he does well, endures pain, or conquers temptation, the ever-present love will greet him, he knows, with approval and pity; if he falls, plead for him; if he suffers, cheer him—be with him and accompany him always until death is past, and sorrow and sin are no more. Is this mere dreaming, or, on the part of an idle story-teller, useless moralizing? May not the man of the world take his moment, too, to be grave and thoughtful? Ask of your own hearts and memories, brother and sister, if we do not live in the dead; and (to speak reverently) prove God by love.

Of these matters, Pen and Warrington often spoke in many a solemn and friendly converse in after days, and Pendennis's mother was worshipped in his memory and canonized there, as such a saint ought to be. Lucky he in life who knows a few such women! A kind provision of Heaven it was that sent us such; and gave us to admire that touching and wonderful spectacle of innocence, love, and beauty.

But as it is certain that if, in the course of these sentimental conversations, any outer stranger, Major Pendennis for instance, had walked into Pen's chambers, Arthur and Warrington would have stopped their talk, chosen another subject, and discoursed about the Opera, or the last debate in Parliament, or Miss Jones's marriage with Captain Smith, or what not—so, let us imagine that the public steps in at this juncture, stops the confidential talk between author and reader, and begs us to resume our remarks about this world, with which both are certainly better acquainted than with that other one into which we have just been peeping.

On coming into his property, Arthur Pendennis at first comported himself with a modesty and equanimity which obtained his friend Warrington's praises, though Arthur's uncle was a little inclined to quarrel with his nephew's meanness of spirit for not assuming greater state and pretensions now that he had entered on the enjoyment of his kingdom. He would have had Arthur installed in handsome quarters, riding on showy park hacks, or in well-built cabriolets, every day. "I am too absent," Arthur said, with a laugh, "to drive a cab in London; the omnibus would cut me in two, or I should send my horse's head into the ladies' carriage-windows; and you wouldn't have me driven about by my servant like an apothecary, uncle?" No, Major Pendennis would on no account have his nephew appear like an apothecary; the august representative of the house of Pendennis must not so demean himself. And when Arthur, pursuing his banter, said, "And yet, I dare say, sir, my father was proud enough when he first set up his gig," the old Major hemmed and ha'd, and his wrinkled face reddened with a blush as he answered, "You know what Buonaparte said, sir, 'Il faut laver son linge sale en famille.' There is no need, sir, for you to brag that your father was a—a medical man. He came of a most ancient but fallen house, and was obliged to reconstruct the family fortunes as many a man of good family has done before him. You are like the fellow in Sterne, sir—the Marquis who came to demand his sword again. Your father got back yours for you. You are a man of landed estate, by Gad, sir, and a gentleman—never forget you are a gentleman."

Then Arthur slily turned on his uncle the argument which he had heard the old gentleman often use regarding himself. "In the society which I have the honor of frequenting through your introduction, who cares to ask about my paltry means or my humble gentility, uncle?" he asked. "It would be absurd of me to attempt to compete with the great folks; and all that they can ask from us is, that we should have a decent address and good manners."

"But for all that, sir, I should belong to a better Club

The information Major Pendennis had gathered about the Clavering family—courtesy of Strong and his own meddling as a self-appointed guardian—was enough to make the old gentleman pause in his tracks. The idea of marrying his nephew, Arthur, to a woman with two fathers-in-law like the current occupants of Clavering Hall seemed less like a blessing and more like a trap. One was a convict who had recently taken a hatchet to an officer; the other was a man who would be strung up if caught. No sensible guardian would hand his ward a wife whose parents were currently being hunted by the law. Yet, Major Pendennis reasoned that Altamont, with the gallows looming so large, would surely avoid recognition, while Clavering, terrified of exposure, would become a willing slave to anyone holding the secret.

But there was a catch. The Begum, who had paid Clavering's debts a dozen times over, would soon find her treasury empty, spent entirely on this irreclaimable reprobate. Her heirs would inherit nothing but an empty vault and a pedigree tarnished by the "sus. per coll." attached to the last male of her line. Miss Amory, instead of bringing a fortune and a seat in Parliament, would bring only her person and a scandalous footnote to Arthur's name.

However, the old schemer in Pendennis's mind had another card to play. To understand it, one must witness a conversation that soon took place between the Major and the Honourable Baronet, the Member for Clavering.

When a man falls on hard times and vanishes from his usual circle, he tends to resurface in the strangest places. I have known a dandy of Pall Mall vanish from the clubs only to be found laughing and drinking at a pennyworth in Billingsgate; a scholar of great wit, fleeing the constable, sending his address to a pub called "The Fox under the Hill" in a dark archway of the Strand. Such men, when misfortune strikes, are rarely in their own homes. Only the faithful know where to find them.

Since his quarrels with his wife and the subsequent financial ruin, finding Sir Francis Clavering at home was impossible. "Ever since I lent him my book for fourteen pounds, he doesn't come home till three," Mr. Lightfoot told his friend Morgan. "He pretends to sleep when I bring his water in the morning and dodges out when I'm downstairs." Lightfoot planned to move to the house and marry his old woman. Similarly, after his fallout with Strong, the Baronet fled to other haunts, escaping the Chevalier's reproaches and, perhaps, his own conscience.

Thus, despite the Major's ulterior motives, it took considerable effort to corner the shy Baronet. Since the Major had become the family friend, Clavering treated him like a lawyer or an agent: avoiding him unless money was involved. The Major made several futile attempts. Once, he invited the Baronet to dinner at Greenwich to meet "friends"; the Baronet accepted, suspected a trap, and didn't show, leaving the Major to eat his whitebait alone. Another time, the Major requested ten minutes of talk; the Baronet agreed to meet at four o'clock the next day at Bays's, carefully underlining the word "precisely." Four o'clock arrived, as destiny decreed, but no Clavering appeared. If he had borrowed twenty pounds from Pendennis, he couldn't have been more timid.

Just as Strong's patron had been showering the Chevalier with blessings in front of his face and curses behind his back, Sir Francis Clavering, having sworn to his wife's advisers to sign no more bills, managed to sign a piece of stamped paper carried off by his friend, Mr. Moss Abrams. Strong, overhearing the transaction in the back-parlour of Mr. Santiago's cigar shop near St. James's Street, learned the truth.

"He's at his old work again," Mr. Santiago told a customer. "He and Moss Abrams were in my parlour. Moss sent my boy for a stamp. It must have been a fifty-pound bill. He told Moss to date it two months back. He'll pretend it's an old bill he forgot about during his settlement with his wife. I dare say they'll give him more money now he's clear." A man who signs "promises to pay" at six months knows his signature is circulating among London's worst knaves.

As the Major walked daintily toward his lodgings, Strong, smelling of Havana tobacco and burning with wrath, strode past him. "Confound these young men," the Major thought, eyeing the cigar and mustache. "Every fellow who smokes and wears mustachios is a low fellow."

"Good evening, Mr. Strong?" the Major called out, making a dignified bow before entering his house.

"My news is bad, sir," Strong replied. "It concerns our friends at Tunbridge Wells. Clavering is at his old tricks again."

"Indeed! Come into my lodging," the Major cried, ushering the Chevalier into his drawing-room.

Strong unburdened himself of his indignation. "No promises bind him, sir," he said. "Remember when we met with the lawyer? He wouldn't be satisfied with his honour; he wanted to swear on his knees, ring for a Bible, and swear perdition on his soul if he gave another bill. He signed one today. He will sign as many as you please for ready money. He'll deceive his wife, his child, or any old friend who has backed him a hundred times. There's a bill of his and mine due next week."

"I thought we had paid all."

"Not that one," Strong blushed. "He asked me not to mention it. I had half the money for that, Major. They'll be down on me. But I don't care; I'm used to it. It's Lady Clavering that riles me. It's a shame that good-natured woman, who has paid him out of gaol a score of times, should be ruined by his heartlessness. A parcel of bill-stealing boxers get his money, and he doesn't scruple to throw an honest fellow over. Would you believe it, sir, he took money of Altamont?"

"Indeed? Of that singular man who came tipsy once to Sir Francis's house?" Pendennis asked, his face impenetrable. "Who is Altamont, Mr. Strong?"

"I don't know, if you don't," Strong answered, suspicious.

"To tell you frankly," said the Major, "I have my suspicions—mind, I only suppose—that in our friend Clavering's loose life, there are queer secrets he'd rather keep buried. Very likely this Altamont knows a story against Clavering and holds him to ransom. I know many men of the best families paying through the nose in that way. But their private affairs are no business of mine. And so with Clavering, I am most interested for his wife's sake and her daughter's, who is a charming creature. When her ladyship asked me, I looked into her affairs and tried to set them straight. And if I am called upon—and by the way, this Mr. Altamont? How is he? Are you acquainted with him? Is he in town?"

"I don't know that I am called upon to know where he is, Major Pendennis," Strong said, rising in dudgeon, offended by the Major's patronizing caution.

Pendennis's manner shifted instantly from hauteur to knowing good humor. "Ah, Captain Strong, you are cautious too, I see; and quite right. We don't know what ears walls may have. As a man of the world and an old soldier, you know there is no use throwing away your fire. You may have your ideas, and I may put two and two together. But there are things many men had better not know, eh, Captain? And I, for one, won't know them until I have reason. That seems to be your maxim too. Regarding the Baronet, I think it advisable he be checked. I strongly reprehend any departure from his word that causes pain to his family. That is my full and frank opinion, and I am sure it is yours."

"Certainly," Strong said dryly.

"I am delighted," Pendennis said, extending a hand. "Delighted that an old brother soldier agrees with me. Good evening. Thank you. Morgan, show the door to Captain Strong."

Strong, preceded by the valet, took his leave, puzzled by the Major's sudden reticence. Morgan, however, had stood close to the door, overhearing much of the talk.

"Who is that Altamont?" Morgan asked Lightfoot later at the Club.

"Strong's man of business," Lightfoot replied. "He draws the Governor's bills and indorses them. That kite-flying always takes two or three to set the paper going. Altamont put the pot on at the Derby and won a good bit. I wish the Governor could get some somewhere, and I could get my book paid up."

"Do you think my Lady would pay his debts again?" Morgan asked. "Find out for me, Lightfoot, and I'll make it worth your while."

Major Pendennis often joked that his valet Morgan was richer than himself. Indeed, through careful speculation, the silent attendant had amassed a considerable sum. When Arthur Pendennis finally inherited his property, Morgan surprised him by offering to lend him fifty or a hundred pounds for investment. Arthur, knowing nothing of finance, politely declined, but mentioned the offer to his uncle.

"It was then," the Major told Arthur, "that I believed Morgan had been getting devilish rich for a devilish long time. In fact, he bought the house in Bury Street where I lodge and made a considerable sum by buying up as many of the Baronet's acceptances as he could raise money to purchase. Of these transactions, I knew no more than most gentlemen know of their servants."

"So he offered to lend you money, did he?" the Major remarked to his nephew. "He's a devilish sly fellow and a devilish rich fellow. Many a nobleman would like such a valet. He ain't a bit changed. He does his work just as well as ever—he's always ready to my bell, steals about the room like a cat, and is so devilishly attached to me."

On the day of Strong's visit, the Major bethought himself of Pen's story and rallied the valet regarding his wealth.

"I hear you have got some money to invest, Morgan," the Major said.

"It's Mr. Arthur has been telling, hang him," the valet thought.

"I'm glad my place is such a good one."

"Thank you, sir—I've no reason to complain," Morgan replied demurely.

"You're a good fellow. I believe you are attached to me. I'm glad you get on well. And I hope you'll be prudent, not taking a public-house or that kind of thing."

A public-house? Me in a public-house! The old fool! Dammy, if I was ten years younger, I'd set in Parliament before I died.

"No, thank you kindly, sir. I don't think of the public line. And I've got my little savings pretty well put out, sir."

"You do a little in the discounting way, eh, Morgan?"

"Yes, sir, a very little—I—I beg your pardon, sir—might I be so free as to ask a question?"

"Speak on, my good fellow," the Major said graciously.

"About Sir Francis Clavering's paper, sir? Do you think he's any longer any good? Will my Lady pay on 'em any more, sir?"

"What, you've done something in that business already?"

"Yes, sir, a little," Morgan replied, dropping his eyes. "And I don't mind owning, sir, and I hope I may take the liberty of saying, sir, that a little more would make me very comfortable if it turned out as well as the last."

"Why, how much have you netted by him, in Gad's name?" asked the Major.

"I've done a good bit, sir, at it: that I own, sir. Having some information and made acquaintance with the family through your kindness, I put on the pot, sir."

"You did what?"

"I laid my money on, sir—I got all I could, and borrowed, and bought Sir Francis's bills. Many of 'em had his name, and the gentleman's as is just gone out, Edward Strong, Esquire, sir: and of course I know of the blow-hup and shindy as is took place in Grosvenor Place, sir: and as I may as well make my money as another, I'd be very much obleeged to you if you'd tell me whether my Lady will come down any more."

Although the Major was as surprised as if he'd heard Morgan was a disguised Marquis, and indignant at the audacity of the fellow growing rich under his nose, he felt a natural admiration for money and success. He found himself respecting Morgan, and rather afraid of that worthy.

"Well, Morgan," he said, "I mustn't ask how rich you are; and the richer the better for your sake, I'm sure. And if I could give you any information that could serve you, I would speedily help you. But frankly, if Lady Clavering asks me whether she shall pay any more of Sir Francis's debts, I shall advise and I hope she won't, though I fear she will—and that is all I know. And so you are aware that Sir Francis is beginning again in his—eh—reckless and imprudent course?"

"At his old games, sir—can't prevent that gentleman. He will do it."

"Mr. Strong was saying that a Mr. Moss Abrams was the holder of one of Sir Francis Clavering's notes. Do you know anything of this Mr. Abrams; or the amount of the bill?"

"Don't know the bill, know Abrams quite well, sir."

"I wish you would find out about it for me. And I wish you would find out where I can see Sir Francis Clavering, Morgan."

Morgan said, "Thank you, sir, yes, sir, I will, sir," and retired with his usual stealth

Lady Clavering, having survived the tumultuous London season with her husband, retreated to a charming villa nestled on a picturesque common near Tunbridge Wells. Miss Amory, naturally, tagged along with her mother, while Master Clavering returned for his holidays. For Blanche, the arrival of her brother was less a reunion and more a declaration of war; her primary occupation became fighting and quarreling with him. Yet, this domestic skirmishing was merely a pastime. The young schoolboy, far from enjoying home sports, found his true joy in cricket, horses, and a legion of friends at Tunbridge.

The Begum's residence had transformed into a chaotic hub of adolescent revelry. It was filled with a constant stream of young gentlemen, all around thirteen years of age, who consumed tarts and champagne with alarming voracity. They raced horses across the lawn, terrified the fond mother, smoked until the air was thick with haze, and generally rendered the dining room unbearable for Miss Blanche. She simply did not care for the company of thirteen-year-old gentlemen.

For the fair young creature, however, any change was a delight. For a week or two, she would have traded her current comforts for poverty, a cottage, and bread-and-cheese; perhaps even a dungeon with bread-and-water for a night. The move to Tunbridge was, therefore, a welcome escape. She wandered the woods sketching trees and farmhouses, devoured French novels, and drove into the town to catch any play, ball, conjurer, or musician who happened to appear.
