She slept excessively, quarreled with her mother and Frank in the mornings, and attended the local village school with a mischievous flair. First, she would fondle the girls and thwart the mistress; then, she would scold the girls and laugh at the teacher. Of course, she was constant at church.

It was a lovely little church of immense antiquity—a tiny Anglo-Norman jewel, seemingly built the day before yesterday, adorned with painted windows, carved saints' heads, gilt scripture texts, and open pews. Blanche immediately set to work creating a most correct high-church altar-cover for it. She quickly earned a reputation as a saint with the clergyman, whom she coaxed, wheedled, and fondled with such artfulness that poor Mrs. Smirke went nearly mad with jealousy. Mrs. Smirke was the wife of our old friend Smirke, Pen's tutor and former suitor for Helen's hand. Having consoled himself for her refusal with a young lady from Clapham provided by his mother, Smirke's views had hardened daily since her death. He had cut off his coat collar, let his hair grow long over his back, abandoned his forehead curl and necktie, and went without dinner on Fridays. He read the Roman Hours and hinted that he was ready to hear confessions in the vestry.

The most harmless creature in the world, he was denounced as a black and dangerous Jesuit and Papist by Muffin of the Dissenting chapel and Mr. Simeon Knight of the old church. Smirke had built his chapel-of-ease with the money left him by his mother at Clapham. Lord! What would she have said to hear a table called an altar, to see candlesticks upon it, or to receive letters signed on the Feast of Saint So-and-so? All these things the boy of Clapham practiced, his faithful wife following suit.

When Blanche held a conference of nearly two hours in the vestry with Mr. Smirke, Belinda paced up and down on the grass, where there were only two small grave-stones yet. She wished she had a third there, fearing he would soon offer himself to that creature who had infatuated him in a fortnight. No, she decided, she would retire to a convent, profess, and leave him. Such dark thoughts plagued Smirke's wife and neighbors, who suspected him of direct correspondence with the Bishop of Rome and bewailing errors even more odious. Yet, our friend meant no earthly harm. The post-office never brought him letters from the Pope. At first, Blanche seemed to him the most pious, gifted, and fascinating person he had ever met; her singing of the Chants delighted him. But after a while, he grew tired of Miss Amory; her ways and graces grew stale. Then he became doubtful, and finally, she caused a disturbance in his school, lost her temper, and rapped the children's fingers. Blanche inspired this cycle of admiration and satiety in many men. She tried to please them, flinging out all her graces at once, descending upon them with jewels, smiles, cajoleries, and ogles. Then she grew tired of them, dropped them, and the men, in turn, dropped her.

It was a happy night for Belinda when Blanche departed. Her husband, blushing and sighing, admitted he had been deceived, fearing her precious gifts were merely tinsel and that she had made religion an amusement. She had certainly lost her temper with the schoolmistress and beaten Polly Rucker's knuckles cruelly. Belinda flew to his arms; the question of the grave or the veil was settled. He tenderly embraced her on the forehead. "There is none like thee, my Belinda," he said, throwing his fine eyes up to the ceiling, "precious among women!" As for Blanche, from the moment she lost sight of him and Belinda, she never thought or cared about them again.

But when Arthur descended to spend a few days at Tunbridge Wells with the Begum, this stage of indifference had not yet arrived for Miss Blanche or the simple clergyman. Smirke still believed her to be an angel and a wonder of a woman. Such perfection he had never seen; he sat listening to her music in the summer evenings, open-mouthed and rapt, forgetting tea and bread-and-butter. Fascinated as he had heard the music of the opera to be—he had attended only one exhibition, which he recalled with a blush and a sigh, the day he accompanied Helen and her son to the play at Chatteris—he could conceive nothing more delicious or celestial than Miss Amory's music. She was a gifted being with a precious soul and the most remarkable talents, possessing, to all outward seeming, the most heavenly disposition.

In this state of feverish bewitchment, Smirke discoursed to Arthur about her. Their meeting had been cordial. Arthur loved anyone who loved his mother, and Smirke could speak on that theme with genuine emotion. They shared a hundred stories of their lives. "Arthur will perceive," Smirke said, "that his views on Church matters have developed since our acquaintance." Mrs. Smirke, a most exemplary person, seconded them with all her efforts. Having built the little church on his mother's demise, he had heard of Arthur's reputation. Speaking in the kindest and most saddened tone, with eyelids lowered and head bowed, he expressed his feelings. Arthur was immensely amused by him—by his airs, follies, simplicity, blank stock, and long hair—and by his real goodness and kindness. His praises of Blanche pleased and surprised Arthur, making him regard her with particular favor.

The truth was, Blanche was very glad to see Arthur. One is always glad to see an agreeable man in the country who brings the latest news and stories from the great city, someone who can talk that darling London jargon so dear and indispensable to Londoners, yet so little understood by those outside the world. The first day Pen arrived, he kept Blanche laughing for hours after dinner. She sang her songs with redoubled spirit. She did not scold her mother but fondled and kissed her to the honest Begum's surprise. When bedtime came, she said, "Deja!" with the prettiest air of regret, truly sorry to go to bed, and squeezed Arthur's hand fondly. On his side, he gave her pretty palm a very cordial pressure. Our young gentleman was of such a turn that eyes moderately bright dazzled him.

"She is very much improved," Pen thought, looking out into the night. "Very much. I suppose the Begum won't mind my smoking with the window open. She's a jolly good old woman, and Blanche is immensely improved. I liked her manner with her mother tonight. I liked her laughing way with that stupid young cub of a boy, whom they oughtn't to allow to get tipsy. She sang those little verses very prettily; they were devilish pretty verses too, though I say it who shouldn't say it." He hummed a tune Blanche had put to some verses of his own. "Ah! what a fine night! How jolly a cigar is at night! How pretty that little Saxon church looks in the moonlight! I wonder what old Warrington's doing? Yes, she's a devilish nice little thing, as my uncle says."

"Oh, heavenly!" A voice broke out from a clematis-covered casement nearby—a girl's voice, the author of *Mes Larmes*.

Pen burst into laughter. "Don't tell about my smoking," he said, leaning out of his own window.

"Oh! go on! I adore it," cried the lady of *Mes Larmes*. "Heavenly night! heavenly, heavenly moon! but I must shut my window and not talk to you on account of *les moeurs*. How droll they are, *les moeurs*! Adieu." And Pen began to sing the Goodnight to Don Basilio.

The next day, they walked in the fields together, laughing and chattering—the gayest pair of friends. They talked of their youth, and Blanche was prettily sentimental. They discussed Laura, dearest Laura—Blanche had loved her as a sister. Was she happy with that odd Lady Rockminster? Wouldn't she come to stay with them at Tunbridge? Oh, what walks they would take together! What songs they would sing—the old, old songs! Laura's voice was splendid. Did Arthur—she must call him Arthur—remember the songs they sang in the happy old days, now he was grown such a great man with such success? And so on.

The day after, enlivened by a happy ramble through the woods to Penshurst and a sight of that pleasant park and hall, came the conversation with the curate that made our young friend think more and more.

"Is she all this perfection?" he asked himself. "Has she become serious and religious? Does she tend schools and visit the poor? Is she kind to her mother and brother? Yes, I am sure of that; I have seen her." Walking with his old tutor over his little parish and visiting his school, Pen found Blanche seated, instructing the children. With inexpressible delight, he fancied how patient she must be, how good-natured, how ingenuous, how really simple in her tastes, and unspoiled by the world.

"And do you really like the country?" he asked her as they walked together.

"I should like never to see that odious city again. O Arthur—that is, Mr.—well, Arthur, then—one's good thoughts grow up in these sweet woods and calm solitudes, like those flowers which won't bloom in London, you know. The gardener comes and changes our balconies once a week. I don't think I shall bear to look London in the face again—its odious, smoky, brazen face! But, heigho!"

"Why that sigh, Blanche?"

"Never mind why."

"Yes, I do mind why. Tell me, tell me everything."

"I wish you hadn't come down;" and a second edition of *Mes Soupirs* came out.

"You don't want me, Blanche?"

"I don't want you to go away. I don't think this house will be very happy without you, and that's why I wish that you never had come."

*Mes Soupirs* were laid aside, and *Mes Larmes* had begun.

Ah! What answer is given to those in the eyes of a young woman? What is the method employed for drying them? What took place? O ringdoves and roses, O dews and wildflowers, O waving greenwoods and balmy airs of summer! Here were two battered London rakes, taking themselves in for a moment, and fancying that they were in love with each other, like Phillis and Corydon!

When one thinks of country houses and country walks, one wonders that any man is left unmarried.

It was a peculiar irony of Arthur Pendennis's nature that, while he poured out his soul to Warrington regarding the discovery of his old tutor Smirke, the eccentricities of his wife, and his grand departure from Clapham for Rome, he remained mysteriously silent about Blanche. When pressed, Arthur would offer vague, diplomatic responses. "She is a good-natured, clever little thing," he'd say, adding that with proper guidance she might not turn out a disaster, though for the moment, romance was dead and buried. He claimed contentment with his current lot, effectively locking the door on any further discussion of the girl who had captured his imagination.

Yet, the silence was broken by the arrival of satin envelopes at Lamb Court. Superscribed in a neat, looping hand and sealed with a cipher that would have baffled even the sharpest eye, these letters revealed a correspondence between Arthur and a young lady whose initials were B.A. Arthur's replies were a masterclass in gallantry—filled with witty observations, town gossip, and, most dangerously, poetry. Indeed, those verses that later graced the pages of *Roseleaves*, the annual edited by Lady Violet Lebas, were originally penned for Blanche, serving as pre-publication cornets to Pinkney's pictorial garland.

"Verses are all very well," the elder Pendennis remarked one evening at the Club, watching his nephew scratch out another artless effusion. "And letter-writing, if Mamma permits it, is fine between old country friends. But mind, Pen, don't commit yourself. Who knows what the doose may happen? The safest path is to keep your letters safe. I've never written a letter in my life that would bind me, and *demmy*, sir, I have had some experience of women." The Major, growing garrulous with age, launched into a lecture on the evils of indiscretion, recounting how young Spoony had been forced into a marriage with a widow old enough to be his mother after sending too ardent a note, and how Sir John Bird had been duped into a union with Miss Stickney after receiving letters from Miss Salter.

Pen laughed, blushing slightly at his uncle's warnings. "Don't you remember the lesson I learned in Lady Mirabel's? Miss Fotheringay's affair? I am not to be caught again, Uncle." He spoke with mock humility, perhaps because he already was cautious. Instinctively, or perhaps out of a strange honesty, he had refrained in his letters to Miss Blanche from any avowals that might compromise him.

Old Pendennis congratulated himself on his nephew's prudence, unaware that Warrington would have judged the situation differently. Warrington would have argued that the boy's foolish letters were superior to the man's slippery gallantries, that to truly win a woman, one must advance without subterfuge, with no retreat secured. But Pen knew his friend was guilty of the very faults he preached against, so he said nothing.

Meanwhile, the winds of fate were turning violently against Captain Strong. After refusing a loan from Colonel Altamont—a sacrifice to his own conscience that now gnawed at him—Strong found himself besieged. The tradesmen, once confident in his jolly face, now poured in their bills with cowardly unanimity. Tailors, bootmakers, and pastrycooks held levees on his stairs, while young clerks lurked nearby with writs demanding his appearance before the Queen.

Poor Strong, having no guinea in his pocket, retreated into the "Englishman's castle," barring the doors against the creditors. From behind a curtain over his letter-box, the Chevalier peeped out to watch the furious faces of the duns dash against the wood. But he was not entirely alone. His allies, Huxter and Miss Fanny Bolton, maintained a secret supply line. When hostile visitors prowled, Fanny's little sisters would innocently whoop a specific jodel in the court; upon hearing this signal, Fanny and Huxter would arrive, singing the same note, allowing the honest garrison to emerge, provisions and porter in hand, to pass a comfortable night.

Beyond his allies, Strong had secured a vital retreat. It had been noted previously that the Costigan and Bows household lived next door, with a leaden water-pipe connecting their window to Strong's kitchen. Laughingly demonstrated to Altamont, this escape route was now his lifeline. One afternoon, Strong burst into the Costigans' apartment, explaining that the enemy was waiting on his staircase, and he had taken this means to give them the slip. Thus, while the sheriff's aides-de-camp waited in the passage of No. 3, Strong walked down the steps of No. 4, dined at the Albion, attended the play, and returned home at midnight, baffling Mrs. Bolton and Fanny, who could not conceive how he had passed the line of sentries.

For weeks, Strong bore this siege with the spirit of a veteran soldier, though his patience wore thin. What truly riled him was the infernal indifference of Sir Francis Clavering, to whom Strong wrote letter after letter, receiving not a single acknowledgment or remittance. A five-pound note, Strong lamented, would have been a fortune at that time.

Then, salvation arrived. "Yes, if it hadn't been for this good fellow here," Strong declared, his spirits lifted. "For a good fellow you are, Altamont, my boy... I think, Pendennis, it would have been all up with Ned Strong. I was in the fifth week of my imprisonment... my spirit was quite broken... when who should drop down from heaven but Altamont!"

"Heaven ain't exactly the place, Ned," Altamont corrected cheerfully. "I came from Baden-Baden. I'd had a deuced lucky month there."

True to his word, Altamont took up Marks's bill and paid the others. The meeting, held at Richmond in the coffee-room, was a raucous affair of wine and hilarity. Strong, an admirable storyteller, recounted his siege and escapes with great liveliness, describing the talk of the officers at his door and the pretty signals of Fanny. Altamont, flushed with victory and claret, proposed a toast to "every good fellow like Strong finding another good fellow to stand by him," though he admitted he disliked the name Pendennis.

"Why?" Arthur asked.

Strong pressed the Colonel's foot under the table. Altamont, rather excited, filled another bumper, nodded to Pen, and declared, "He was a gentleman, and that was sufficient, and they were all gentlemen."

Altamont then unveiled his grand plan. Having won a good sum at Baden-Baden, he intended to return to the country to fetch his money and Captain Strong. He possessed an "infallible system" for gambling, a method he claimed would make everyone's fortune. "I'll put in for that little Fanny," he announced enthusiastically, noting that she had lent two pounds to Ned Strong. "Let's drink her health."

Arthur pledged the toast with cordiality. As Altamont emptied his bottle, he detailed his plans to Pen: he would form a bank with partners, put every shilling he had into it, and trust Strong to play for him, believing Strong's temper superior to his own or that of Bloundell-Bloundell.

Later, Pen recounted the story to his uncle. "I met that queer fellow Altamont the other day," he said.

"Altamont? What Altamont? There's Lord Westport's son," the Major replied.

"No, no; the fellow who came tipsy into Clavering's dining-room... he said he did not like the name of Pendennis."

"I don't know any man of the name of Altamont," the impenetrable Major insisted. "And as for your acquaintance, I think the less you have to do with him the better, Arthur."

Arthur laughed. "He is going to quit the country and make his fortune by a gambling system. He and my amiable college acquaintance, Bloundell, are partners, and the Colonel takes out Strong with him as aide-de-camp. What is it that binds the Chevalier and Clavering, I wonder?"

"My uncle has talked him over," the Major answered. "Clavering thinks he ain't fit for Parliament. No more he is. What's to prevent him from putting you or anybody else into his place if he likes? Do you think that Government or the Opposition would make any bones about accepting the seat if he offered it to them!"

The Major's logic was sound, though Pen accepted it more because he wished to believe it than because he fully understood the mechanics of political maneuvering.

At his next visit to Tunbridge, Pen amused Miss Blanche with the history of the Chevalier's imprisonment and Altamont's rescue, praising little Fanny's generosity. Blanche, however, was somewhat jealous and piqued by the attention given to Fanny. During their drives and evening walks, Arthur described the drama of the "poor little Ariadne of Shepherd's Inn" with becoming modesty. His moral, drawn in his usual satirical mood, was that women recovered from first loves as easily as men did, transferring their affections to a second object without difficulty. Poor Fanny was offered up as a sacrifice to prove this theory.

"You wicked odious creature," Miss Blanche said, her voice trembling. "I believe that you are enraged with Fanny for being so impudent as to forget you, and that you are actually jealous of Mr. Huxter."

Perhaps she was right. The blush that tingled upon Pendennis's cheek proved he was angry to think he had been superseded by such a rival—a fellow without good looks, good manners, or wit, possessing only ardour. "If Nature had not made that provision for each sex in the credulity of the other," he mused internally, "there would not have been near so much marrying and giving in marriage."

"Jealous or not," Pen said aloud, "I should have liked Fanny to have come to a better end than that. I don't like histories that end in that cynical way... Is a life a compromise, my lady fair, and the end of the battle of love an ignoble surrender? Is the search for the Cupid... to result in Huxter smelling of tobacco and gallypots?"

"And don't you intend to be good and happy, pray, Monsieur le Misanthrope?" Blanche asked, her tone sharp. "Are you very discontented with your lot—and will your marriage be a compromise?"

"Again, I don't say no," Pen replied, looking gloomily at her. "I don't think I have much of what people call heart; but I don't profess it. I made my venture when I was eighteen, and lighted my lamp and went in search of Cupid. And what was my discovery of love?—a vulgar dancing-woman! I failed, as everybody does... only it is luckier to fail before marriage than after."

"Merci du choix, Monsieur," said the Sylphide, making a curtsey.

"Look, my little Blanche," Pen said, taking her hand, "at least I stoop to no flatteries. I tell you no foolish lies, as vulgar men do. Why should you and I, with our experience, ape romance and dissemble passion? I do not believe Miss Blanche Amory to be peerless among the beautiful... But if I don't set you up as a heroine, neither do I offer you your very humble servant as a hero. But I think you are—well, there, I think you are very sufficiently good-looking."

"Merci," Miss Blanche said, with another curtsey.

"I think you sing charmingly. I'm sure you're clever. I hope and believe that you are good-natured, and that you will be companionable."

"And so, provided I bring you a certain sum of money and a seat in Parliament, you condescend to fling to me your royal pocket-handkerchief," Blanche retorted, playing the role of the ambitious suitor. "Que d'honneur! We used to call your Highness the Prince of Fairoaks. What an honour to think that I am to be elevated to the throne, and to bring the seat in Parliament as backsheesh to the sultan!"

"And if thieves are about the house," Pen said grimly, pursuing the simile, "forty besetting thieves in the shape of lurking cares and enemies in ambush and passions in arms, my Morgiana will dance round me with a tambourine, and kill all my rogues and thieves with a smile. Won't she?" But Pen looked as if he did not believe that she would.

"Ah, Blanche," he continued after a pause, "don't be angry; don't be hurt at my truth-telling. Don't you see that I always take you at your word? You say you will be a slave and dance—I say, dance. You say, 'I take you with what you bring:' I say, 'I take you with what you bring.' To the necessary deceits and hypocrisies of our life, why add any that are useless and unnecessary? If I offer myself to you because I think we have a fair chance of being happy together, and because by your help I may get for both of us a good place and a not undistinguished name, why ask me to feign raptures and counterfeit romance, in which neither of us believe?"

"Do you want me to come wooing in a Prince Prettyman's dress from the masquerade warehouse, and to pay you compliments like Sir Charles Grandison? Do you want me to make you verses as in the days when we were—when we were children? I will if you like, and sell them to Bacon and Bungay afterwards. Shall I feed my pretty princess with bonbons?"

"Mais j'adore les bonbons, moi," said the little Sylphide, with a queer, piteous look.

"I can buy a hatful at Fortnum and Mason's for a guinea. And it shall have its bonbons, its pooty little sugar-plums, that it shall," Pen said with a bitter smile. "Nay, my dear,

In Which Pen Begins His Canvass

The great house at Clavering Park, once a monument to melancholy when its bankrupt master fled to foreign shores, had not become significantly cheerier under the tenure of Sir Francis Clavering. The mansion remained largely shuttered, a skeleton of a home where the Baronet occupied only a few ground-floor rooms. Here, his housekeeper and her lodge-gate assistant waited upon the unfortunate gentleman during his forced retreat, cooking portions of the game he spent his dreary mornings shooting. Lightfoot, his former man, had defected to Lady Clavering's service. As Pen learned from a letter from Mr. Smirke—who had officiated at the ceremony—the Baronet had wisely married Mrs. Bonner, Lady Clavering's maid. In her mature years, she was struck by the charms of the youth, endowing him with her savings and her substantial person.

To be both landlord and landlady of the Clavering Arms was the shared ambition of the pair. They agreed to remain in Lady Clavering's employ until quarter-day, when they would take possession of their hotel. Pen graciously promised to hold his election dinner there once the Baronet vacated his seat in the young man's favor. True to his word, Arthur descended upon Clavering Park in September on a visit. The owner, glad to have a companion to relieve his loneliness and perhaps a lender of ready cash, welcomed him warmly.

Pen furnished his host with these desirable supplies two days after Arthur's arrival. No sooner were the small funds in Sir Francis's pocket than the latter found he had business at Chatteris and the neighboring watering-places—of which Shire boasts many—and promptly departed to attend to his affairs, which were transacted, as might be expected, at county race-grounds and billiard-rooms. Arthur could live alone well enough; possessing many mental resources and amusements that did not require company, he walked with the gamekeeper in the mornings and spent evenings with books and cigars, a pastime suitable for a literary genius like Mr. Arthur. In truth, within two or three days, he found Sir Francis Clavering's society perfectly intolerable. With mischievous eagerness and satisfaction, he offered the pecuniary aid the latter customarily solicited, supplying him with the means to flee his own house.

Furthermore, our ingenious friend had to ingratiate himself with the townspeople of Clavering and the voters of the borough he hoped to represent. He set about this task with renewed vigor, remembering how unpopular he had previously been and determined to vanquish the odium he had inspired among the simple folk. His sense of humor delighted him in this endeavor. Naturally reserved and silent in public, he suddenly became as frank, easy, and jovial as Captain Strong. He laughed with everyone willing to exchange a laugh, shook hands with dexterous cordiality, appeared at market-days and farmers' ordinaries, and acted like a consummate hypocrite. Just as gentlemen of the highest birth and most spotless integrity act when wishing to please their constituents for some gain, so did Pen. How is it that we allow ourselves not to be deceived, but are so readily ingratiated by a glib tongue, a ready laugh, and a frank manner? We know, for the most part, that it is false coin, yet we take it; we know it is flattery, costing nothing to distribute to everyone, and we would rather have it than be without it. Friend Pen moved through Clavering, laboriously simple and adroitly pleased, quite a different being from the scornful, sulky young dandy the inhabitants remembered ten years prior.

The Rectory was shut up. Doctor Portman had gone, with his gout and family, to Harrogate—an event Pen deplored deeply in a letter to the Doctor, expressing regret at not seeing his old friend whose advice he needed and whose aid he might require someday. Yet, Pen consoled himself for the Doctor's absence by making acquaintances with Mr. Simcoe, the opposition preacher, and the two partners of the cloth-factory at Chatteris, as well as the Independent preacher there. He met them all at the Clavering Athenaeum, which the Liberal party had established in accordance with the advanced spirit of the age, perhaps in opposition to the aristocratic old reading-room into which the *Edinburgh Review* had scarcely gained admission, and where no tradesmen were allowed entry. He propitiated the younger partner of the cloth-factory by inviting him to dine at the Park; he complimented the Honorable Mrs. Simcoe with hares and partridges from the same quarter and a request to read her husband's last sermon; and when feeling unwell one day, the rascal took advantage of the circumstance to show his tongue to Mr. Huxter, who sent him medicines and called the next morning. How delighted old Pendennis would have been with his pupil! Pen himself was amused by the sport in which he was engaged, and his success inspired him with a wicked good-humor.

Yet, as he walked out of Clavering at night, after "presiding" at an Athenaeum meeting or working through an evening with Mrs. Simcoe—who, along with her husband, was awed by the young Londoner's reputation and had heard of his social successes—as he passed over the old familiar bridge of the rushing Brawl and heard that well-remembered sound of waters beneath, and saw his own cottage of Fairoaks among the trees, their dark outlines clear against the starlit sky, different thoughts undoubtedly came to the young man's mind, awakening pangs of grief and shame. There still used to be a light in the windows of the room he remembered so well, where the Saint who loved him had passed so many hours of care, yearning, and prayer. He turned his gaze away from the faint light which seemed to pursue him with a wan, reproachful stare, as though it was his mother's spirit watching and warning. How clear the night was! How keen the stars shone! How ceaseless the rush of the flowing waters! The old home trees whispered and waved gently their dark heads and branches over the cottage roof. Yonder, in the faint starlight glimmer, was the terrace where, as a boy, he had walked on summer evenings, ardent and trustful, unspotted, untried, ignorant of doubts or passions; sheltered as yet from the world's contamination in the pure and anxious bosom of love. The clock of the near town tolling midnight with a clang disturbed our wanderer's reverie, sending him onward toward his night's resting-place, through the lodge into Clavering Avenue, and under the dark arcades of the rustling limes.

When he saw the cottage the next time, it was smiling in the sunset; those bedroom windows were open where the light had burned the night before. Pen's tenant, Captain Stokes of the Bombay Artillery (whose mother, old Mrs. Stokes, lived in Clavering), received his landlord's visit with great cordiality. He showed him over the grounds and the new pond he had made in the back garden from the stables, talked confidentially about the roof and chimneys, and begged Mr. Pendennis to name a day when he would do himself and Mrs. Stokes the pleasure of visiting. Pen, who had been in the country for a fortnight, excused himself for not calling sooner by frankly admitting he had not the heart to do it. "I understand you, sir," the Captain said. Mrs. Stokes, who had slipped away at the ring of the bell (how odd it seemed to Pen to ring a bell!), came down in her best gown, surrounded by her children. The young ones clambered about Stokes; the boy jumped into an armchair. It was Pen's father's armchair, and Arthur remembered the days when he would as soon have thought of mounting the king's throne as of seating himself in that armchair. He asked if Miss Stokes—she was the very image of her mother—could play? He should like to hear a tune on that piano. She played. He heard the notes of the old piano once more, enfeebled by age, but he did not listen to the player. He was listening to Laura singing as in the days of their youth, and saw his mother bending and beating time over the shoulder of the girl.

The dinner at Fairoaks, given in Pen's honor by his tenant, and attended by old Mrs. Stokes, Captain Glanders, Squire Hobnel, and the clergyman and his lady from Tinckleton, was very stupid and melancholy for Pen. This changed when the waiter from Clavering (who aided the captain's stable-boy and Mrs. Stokes's butler), whom Pen remembered as a street boy and who was now indeed a barber in that place, dropped a plate over Pen's shoulder. Mr. Hobnel, who also employed him, remarked, "I suppose, Hodson, your hands are slippery with bear's-grease. He's always dropping the crockery about, that Hodson is—haw, haw!" On which Hodson blushed and looked so disconcerted that Pen burst out laughing; good-humor and hilarity became the order of the evening. For the second course, there was a hare and partridges, top and bottom. When, after the withdrawal of the servants, Pen said to the Vicar of Tinckleton, "I think, Mr. Stooks, you should have asked Hodson to cut the hare," the joke was taken instantly by the clergyman, who was followed in a few minutes by Captains Stokes and Glanders, and by Mr. Hobnell, who arrived rather late but with an immense guffaw.

While Mr. Pen was engaged in the country with these schemes, it happened that the lady of his choice, if not of his affections, came up to London from the Tunbridge villa bound upon shopping expeditions or important business, accompanied by old Mrs. Bonner, her mother's maid. Mrs. Bonner had lived and quarreled with Blanche many times since she was an infant, and now, being about to quit Lady Clavering's service for the hymeneal state, was anxious like a good soul to bestow some token of respectful kindness upon her old and young mistress before she quitted them altogether to take her post as the wife of Lightfoot and landlady of the Clavering Arms.

The honest woman took the benefit of Miss Amory's taste to make the purchase she intended to offer her ladyship, requesting the fair Blanche to choose something for herself that should be to her liking and remind her of her old nurse who had attended her through many wakeful nights, eventful teething, and childish fevers, and who loved her like a child of her own almost. These purchases were made. As the nurse insisted on buying an immense Bible for Blanche, the young lady suggested that Bonner should purchase a large Johnson's Dictionary for her mother. Each of the two women might certainly profit by the present made to her.

Then Mrs. Bonner invested money in some bargains in linen-drapery, which might be useful at the Clavering Arms, and bought a red and yellow neck-handkerchief, which Blanche could see at once was intended for Mr. Lightfoot. Younger than herself by at least twenty-five years, Mrs. Bonner regarded that youth with a fondness at once parental and conjugal, loving to lavish ornaments on his person, which already glittered with pins, rings, shirt-studs, chains, and seals, purchased at the good creature's expense.

It was in the Strand that Mrs. Bonner made her purchases, aided by Miss Blanche, who liked the fun very well. When the old lady had bought everything she desired and was leaving the shop, Blanche, with a smiling face and a sweet bow to one of the shopmen, said, "Pray, sir, will you have the kindness to show us the way to Shepherd's Inn?"

Shepherd's Inn was but a few score of yards off; Old Castle Street was close by. The elegant young shopman pointed out the turning the young lady was to take, and she and her companion walked off together.

"Shepherd's Inn! What can you want in Shepherd's Inn, Miss Blanche?" Bonner inquired. "Mr. Strong lives there. Do you want to go and see the Captain?"

"I should like to see the Captain very well. I like the Captain; but it is not him I want. I want to see a dear little good girl who was very kind to—to Mr. Arthur when he was so ill last year, and saved his life almost; and I want to thank her and ask her if she would like anything. I looked out several of my dresses on purpose this morning, Bonner!" she said, looking at Bonner as if she had a right to admiration and had performed an act of remarkable virtue. Blanche, indeed, was very fond of sugar-plums; she would have fed the poor upon them when she had had enough, and given a country girl a ball-dress when she had worn it and was tired of it.

"Pretty girl—pretty young woman!" mumbled Mrs. Bonner. "I know I want no pretty young women to come about Lightfoot," and in imagination she peopled the Clavering Arms with a harem of the most hideous chambermaids and barmaids.

Blanche, with pink and blue, feathers, flowers, and trinkets (that wondrous invention, a chatelaine, was not extant yet, or she would have had one, we may be sure), a shot-silk dress, a wonderful mantle, and a charming parasol, presented a vision of elegance and beauty such as bewildered the eyes of Mrs. Bolton, who was scrubbing the lodge-floor of Shepherd's Inn and caused Betsy-Jane and Ameliar-Ann to look with delight.

Blanche looked on them with a smile of ineffable sweetness and protection; like Rowena going to see Rebecca; like Marie Antoinette visiting the poor in the famine; like the Marchioness of Carabas alighting from her carriage-and-four at a pauper-tenant's door and taking from John No II. the packet of Epsom salts for the invalid's benefit, carrying it with her own imperial hand into the sick-room—Blanche felt a queen stepping down from her throne to visit a subject, and enjoyed all the bland consciousness of doing a good action.

"My good woman!

While Pen was busy weaving his own selfish parliamentary tapestry in his county, news arrived that Lady Rockminster had descended upon Baymouth, bringing with her the radiant figure of Laura. The announcement struck Pen with a sudden, stinging guilt. His deepest desire was to stand higher in her esteem than in anyone else's; she was his mother's legacy, the living embodiment of his inheritance. He felt a paralyzing dread at the prospect of facing her. How would she react to the news he carried? How could he possibly explain the ambitious, worldly schemes he was hatching to a soul so spotless and pure? He imagined that neither he nor Blanche could withstand Laura's calm, scrutinizing gaze, nor dare to expose their muddy ambitions to such a perfect judge.

Overwhelmed by panic, Pen penned a letter filled with flowery phrases, feigned affection, and easy satire, all while secretly feeling like a rogue caught in the act of hypocrisy. How could a simple country girl, untouched by the corruption of the world, terrify such an accomplished gentleman? Pen realized that his diplomatic skills and cynical knowledge of human nature crumbled before her purity. His affairs were in such a precarious state that he simply could not tell the truth to that honest soul. Riding from Clavering to Baymouth, he felt as guilty as a schoolboy about to face his teacher without knowing his lesson. Truth was the master, and she held the book.

Meanwhile, Laura, under the watchful, somewhat imperious care of Lady Rockminster, had spent the last year gathering the armor of society. While many girls, bred in excessive tenderness, would have withered under the harsh realities of the world, Laura had blossomed. Though Helen, her mother, had worshipped her with a suffocating affection that had shielded her from the world, Laura had stepped out into the great, careless expanse alone. She had faced rude servants, indifferent relatives, and the sting of exclusion, yet she met it all with a cheerful spirit. She viewed her situation not as a burden, but as a privilege compared to the countless unprotected girls who suffered in silence.

Laura possessed a talisman far more valuable than any diamond: a heart of such innocent, trusting goodness that it disarmed everyone around her. From the irritable old lady to the grumbling housekeeper who had endured forty years of Lady Rockminster's temper, everyone was won over. When Laura fell ill in Paris, the housekeeper, usually hostile, nursed her with fierce devotion, fighting with the maid for medicine. The chef wept over her food; the Swiss major-domo praised her in broken languages; the footmen celebrated her recovery with wild hilarity. Even Lady Diana Pynsent, who had previously disliked her, declared Laura a "trouvaille"—a wonderful find. Laura achieved this not through artifice, but through the simple, magnetic force of being pleased and making others feel pleased.

When Pen finally arrived, he found Laura waiting. Instead of the cold reception he feared, she ran downstairs and embraced him so warmly that the stoic footmen, Calverley and Coldstream, would have watched a head-snipping disaster with impassive indifference. Laura had improved immensely; her eyes beamed with health, her cheeks flushed with beauty. As Pen looked at her artless grace, he felt a profound touch of innocence that moved him deeply.

"How good you are to me, Laura—sister!" Pen exclaimed, feeling unworthy of her kindness.

"Mamma left you to me," Laura replied, brushing his forehead with her lips. "You promised to come to me in trouble or share your joy. Are you happy now, or in trouble?"

Pen confessed his parliamentary plans. Laura's reaction was immediate and devastatingly casual. "All the world knows!" she laughed. "Even the doctor talked about it today. It will be in the papers tomorrow. Sir Francis Clavering is retiring for Mr. Arthur Pendennis, and the beautiful Miss Blanche Amory is—"

"What! That too?" Pen asked, wincing.

"That, too, dear Arthur," Laura continued, her tone light and accepting. "I've heard Blanche speak warmly of you. When is it to be? May I come and live with you?"

"My home is yours," Pen said, trying to deflect the weight of her acceptance. "But nothing is decided. No words have passed between us. I have no heart, I think; only a sober attachment. I want my wife on one side of the fire and my sister on the other."

As he spoke, a strange, jealous anger rose in him. Why did he feel angry at the thought of an invisible "somebody" with the right to take Laura away? He was hurt that she received his news so easily, taking his happiness for granted.

"I will stay at home and be Aunt Laura," she laughed, performing a perfect Parisian curtsey. "I've learned to sing and dance with your money. When you become a minister, Blanche shall present me!"

Just then, Lady Rockminster entered, shaking Pen's finger with a commanding air. "So you are going to marry, sir," she declared bluntly. "And go into Parliament. I wanted my grandson to have the seat! I hope you have a great deal of money with Miss Amory. I wouldn't take her without it."

Pen managed a polite smile, but the old lady was relentless. She criticized Blanche as an "affected little creature" and a "crooked lump of affectation," contrasting her unfavorably with Laura. She ranted about the selfishness and cowardice of modern young men, lamenting that they married only for money.

"You are very glad, and you are very sorry," she chided Pen. "A young man who prefers Miss Amory to Miss Bell has no business to be sorry or glad. Where is your friend Bluebeard? Why doesn't he marry Laura? They all marry for money. You are all selfish and cowards."

Pen defended himself with some heat, asking if she had consulted Laura before offering her to the embassy attaches. Laura, sensing the tension, intervened with a kind smile. "He is going to marry somebody else; and I intend to be very fond of her," she said, taking Pen's hand. "Provided he doesn't ask every bachelor why he doesn't marry me."

With his conscience temporarily appeased, Pen found himself drawn constantly to Baymouth. Lady Rockminster, surprisingly, invited him to dine regularly, claiming she could cure his selfishness. "Come here," she ordered, "and let us teach you to behave properly. You will not have to carve; Hecker will give you as much wine as is good for you."

During these walks and rides, Pen began to truly appreciate Laura's frankness and the purity of her heart. Their conversations often turned to their late mother, Helen. Laura told stories of her mother's silent, absorbing devotion, a love that had followed Pen through life. One day, as they rode past Fairoaks, Pen reflected bitterly on the vanity of human existence. He saw how people kicked away happiness for trivial gains, how the world was selfish and cruel.

"I have been round to the cottages where my mother used to visit," Pen said, his voice hard. "It is scarcely a year since she is gone, and the people she benefited care no more for her death than for Queen Anne's. We are all selfish. There are but a few exceptions, like you, to shine like good deeds in a naughty world."

Laura looked down at the honeysuckle on her breast. "When you gave that to the little boy, you were not selfish," she said gently. "Your heart was kind. If you think humbly of yourself, that kindness is diminished, is it not?"

She challenged his cynicism, suggesting that if the world was wicked, he should leave it to study and write good books. "I would have you bring your wife to Fairoaks," she pleaded. "I would like to see your children playing on the lawn, and us praying in our mother's church again."

Pen scoffed at the idea of Blanche becoming a country wife, fearing he would become a petty gentleman. Then, the topic shifted. Laura blushed, and Pen noticed the fervor in her eyes before she pulled her veil down.

"What does Mr. Warrington say?" she asked softly.

The name brought a sharp pang to Pen. He had never heard George speak of his past with Laura since the day of his misfortune. Now, driven by a morbid curiosity and a sense of guilt, he stopped his horse close to hers.

"Tell me something, Laura," he said. "Would you... would you have given him what you refused me?"

"Yes, Pen," she burst out, tears streaming down her face.

"He deserved you better than I did," Pen groaned, wrapping his arm around her as she sobbed on his shoulder.

In that moment, the truth of Laura's heart was laid bare. During her mother's final journey, she had confessed her lingering feelings for George. It was only when Warrington revealed his own hopeless situation that Laura realized her affections had shifted. She had admired and respected him, but the tender feeling had died, leaving only humility and remorse. She felt guilty for having withdrawn her love from Pen, whom she had secretly cherished, and for entertaining thoughts of another. She judged herself a criminal for a trifling fault, deciding that she must be gentle in judging others' greater temptations.

When the news of Pen's engagement to Blanche finally hit her, the humbled girl accepted it meekly. "He knows the world better than I do," she reasoned. "My duty is to aid him and Blanche." She immediately began calculating how to give Blanche gifts from her small treasury to secure their friendship.

Later, a slim letter awaited Laura in the hall. Trembling slightly, she opened it to find it was from Blanche. The letter detailed a trip to London and a visit to Shepherd's Inn to see Fanny, ending with a request to know when the "horrid canvassing" would be over.

After dinner, while the doctor read the *Times*, he announced the death of John Henry Foker, Esq., of Logwood Hall, at Pau. The news hung in the air, a stark reminder of the fragility of life and the relentless march of fortune, as Pen and Laura sat in the quiet aftermath of their revelations.

To any gentleman who has ever stepped inside the Wheel of Fortune public-house—remembering well that it was here Mr. James Morgan's Club convened, and where Sir Francis Clavering once held court with Major Pendennis—one knows the layout of the ground floor. Beyond the bar, where the landlady presides, lie three distinct realms for guests. There is the public parlour, open to all; a second room reserved for gentlemen in livery; and finally, the third apartment, marked boldly with "Private" on its door, hired exclusively by the "Confidentials," a club of which Messrs. Morgan and Lightfoot were proud members.

It was in this shadowed sanctum that the noiseless Morgan sat, having followed his master from the Major's lodgings to the pub. He had eavesdropped on the Major's earlier conversation with Strong, gathering enough private speculation to fuel a lifetime of curiosity. Now, while Pendennis and Clavering argued heatedly in the adjacent parlour, Morgan occupied a strategic corner of the Confidential room from which the entire discourse drifted in. The tone was angry, the voices high, and Morgan, a man of sharp ears, caught almost every syllable. What he heard only confirmed the suspicions already festering in his mind.

"He knew Altamont the moment he spotted him in Sydney!" Morgan whispered to himself, his eyes widening behind his spectacles. "Clavering isn't married to my Lady at all! Altamont is the man! A convict! Young Harthur is set to enter Parliament, and the Governor promises not to split the party. By Jove, what a sly old rogue that Governor is! No wonder he's pushing the match between Blanche and Harthur. Why, she'll bring a hundred thousand pounds to the table, plus a seat in Parliament for her husband."

Nobody witnessed the transformation on Mr. Morgan's face as this intelligence crystallized, but a physiognomist would have marveled at the shift. "But for my age and the confounded prejudices of society," he muttered, eyeing his reflection in the glass, "damn it, James Morgan, you might marry her yourself." Yet, marriage was out of the question. Morgan quickly pivoted to a more lucrative strategy: the possession of this secret could mend his fortunes in ways far more immediate. Of all those touched by the scandal, the majority would pay dearly to keep it buried. Sir Francis Clavering, whose fortune hung in the balance, would wish silence; Colonel Altamont, whose neck was implicated, would prefer oblivion; and that young upstart, Mr. Harthur—who was as proud as a duke despite being a convict's son trading secrets for a parliamentary seat—would pay any price to hide his origins. As for Lady C., whether she wanted to escape Clavering or protect her frightened son, she would pay. And Miss Blanche? She would certainly reward the man who restored her rightful inheritance.

"Damn it," concluded the valet, calculating the wonders of his windfall, "with such cards, James Morgan, you are a made man. It may be a regular eternity of profit. Everyone must subscribe. With what I've already made, I might cut business, warn my old Governor, turn gentleman, and hire a servant of my own, begad." Despite these feverish calculations that threatened to perturb his spirit, Morgan displayed remarkable self-command, maintaining a calm exterior while his inner world spun with ambition.

Colonel Altamont, however, was currently absent from London. The valet knew of Sir Francis's haunt at Shepherd's Inn and arrived an hour or two after the Baronet and Pendennis had parted, only to find the bird had flown. Altamont had cashed his Derby winnings and departed for the Continent. This absence was a profound vexation to Morgan. "He'll drop all that money at the gambling dens on the Rhine," Morgan fretted. "I might have had a good slice of it. It's confounded annoying he's gone and couldn't wait a few days." Yet, hope deferred or triumphant, victory or ambush, Morgan bore it all with an equable countenance. Until the proper day arrived, the Major's boots remained varnished, his hair curled, his tea brought to his bedside, and his oaths endured with silent, obsequious fidelity. Who would guess, seeing him pack trunks and assist at table at country houses, that Morgan was richer than his employer, knowing secrets of his master and others alike? In his profession, Morgan was revered, his reputation for wealth and wisdom earning him renown at supper tables. The younger gentlemen dismissed him as stupid, a fogey with no ideas, yet none would deny the heartfelt prayer of the serious-minded: "When I die, may I cut up as well as Morgan Pendennis!"

As befitted a man of fashion, Major Pendennis spent the autumn moving from house to house among country friends. If the Duke was abroad, he stayed with the Marquis in Scotland; if not, with Sir John or the plain Squire. Truth be told, the old gentleman's reputation was waning. Many of his contemporaries had died, and the current holders of their titles knew him not, caring little for his tales of "the wild Prince and Poins" or the heroes of a vanished age. It struck the good man with melancholy as he walked past London doors that were rarely opened for him, recalling the banquets and welcomes of a score of years prior. He began to admit he was no longer of the present age, dimly sensing that the young men laughed at him. Such musings plague many a Pall Mall philosopher. "The men are not what they were," he thought. "The old grand manner is gone. What is Castlewood House now compared to the magnificence of the old mansion? The late lord arrived with four postchaises and sixteen horses; the North Road hurried to see him. The present lord travels in a railway carriage with five bagmen, sneaking away in a brougham. The late lord filled Castlewood with guests drinking claret till midnight; the present man hides in a hut on a Scotch mountain or a closet in Paris. What a contrast between Lady Lorraine of the Regent's time—gorgeous, magnificent in diamonds, daring in rouge, with the wits of the world bowing at her feet—and today's Lady Lorraine, a little woman in a black silk gown like a governess, talking of astronomy and laboring classes!"

Abbots-Lorraine, once the noblest house in the county, had become a monastery, a regular La Trappe. They didn't drink two glasses of wine after dinner; every other guest was a country curate discussing Polly Higson's school progress or Widow Watkins's lumbago. "And the other young men, those lounging guardsmen and lazy dandies—sprawling over sofas, stealing off to smoke in bedrooms, reverencing nothing, not even an old gentleman who has known their fathers—what a difference!" thought the Major. "They're replaced by cotton-spinners and utilitarians, and young parsons with hair combed down their backs. I'm getting old; they're getting past me." He was not far wrong. The times and manners he admired were nearly gone; the gay young men larked him irreverently, while the serious youth viewed him with a grave pity that would have been more painful had he known its extent. But the Major was simple; his moral examinations had never been deep. He hadn't realized until recently that he was merely a respectable, rather fortunate man. Was there no old age without reverence? Did youthful folly never jeer at other bald pates? For the past few years, he had sensed his day was ending, and the new reign had begun.

After an unsuccessful autumn, during which Morgan faithfully trailed him, Major Pendennis returned to London at the dismal end of October, when fogs and lawyers descend upon the city. Who hasn't watched those loaded cabs, piled with boxes and children, rattling through the streets on dun October evenings? Yesterday was France and sunshine; today is work and yellow fog. And what a heap of bills lies in the master's study! The clerk brings the lawyer's papers; the printer's boy waits in the passage; Mr. Smith arrives with that particular little account, symbolizing duty, difficulty, and struggle. Old Pendennis had no special labors or bills to fear, for he had money in his desk and little affection at home to soothe him. Being indifferent to others' wants, he rarely felt disturbed by them. Yet, a gentleman may be out of temper without owing a shilling; he must occasionally feel dispirited and lonely. He had suffered twinges of gout in the country; the birds were shy, the walks fatiguing, and the young men had laughed at him. He hadn't played whist, and he was glad to return. In all his dealings with Morgan, he had been exceedingly sulky and discontented. He had sworn at him, abused him for days, scalded his mouth with bad soup at Swindon, and left his umbrella in the railroad carriage—a piece of forgetfulness that drove him to curse Morgan more freely than ever. Both chimneys smoked furiously in his lodgings; when he flung open the windows, he swore so acrimoniously that Morgan was inclined to fling him out through the casement. The valet swore after his master as Pendennis marched down the street to the Club.

Bays's was not pleasant. The house smelled of varnish and turpentine, and a large streak of white paint marred the back of the Major's fur-collared coat. The dinner was poor. The three most odious men in London—old Hawkshaw with his cough, old Colonel Gripley clutching his newspapers, and the irreclaimable bore Jawkins, who described every inn-bill from his foreign tours—contributed to the Major's misery. Even the Club waiter trod on his toe bringing coffee. Never alone do the Immortals appear; the Furies hunt in company. They pursued Pendennis from home to the Club and back.

While the Major was away, Morgan sat in the landlady's parlour, drinking freely of hot brandy-and-water and pouring out his master's abuse onto Mrs. Brixham. Mrs. Brixham was Morgan's slave. He was her landlord; he had bought her lease, secured her son's acceptances, and held a bill of sale on her furniture. Her son, a clerk in an insurance office, was subject to Morgan's whims. Mrs. Brixham, a clergyman's widow, found herself fetching bootjacks and slippers for the man who performed his duties on the first floor. The little black profiles of her son and daughter, the very picture of Tiddlecot Church where she was married, now hung over the mantelpiece of Morgan's back-parlour as his property. Morgan sat in the ex-curate's old horse-hair study-chair, making Mrs. Brixham bring supper and refill his glass.

The liquor was bought with the poor woman's own coin, which made Morgan indulge the more freely. He had eaten his supper and was drinking a third tumbler when old Pendennis returned from the Club and went upstairs. Morgan swore savagely at the bell, finishing his brandy before answering the summons.

He received the ensuing abuse in silence, nor did the Major read the anger in the flushed face and glaring eyes of the man beneath him. The foot-bath waited by the fire; his gown and slippers were ready. Morgan knelt to remove the boots with due subordination, keeping up a growl of maledictions below while the Major raged above. "Confound you, sir, mind that strap—curse you, don't wrench my foot off," cried the Major, while Morgan whispered below a wish to strangle, drown, and punch his head off.

Once the boots were off, the coat had to come off, requiring Morgan to approach very near. Pendennis immediately perceived the scent of brandy and, in that simple, forcible phraseology men use with domestics, informed him he was a drunken beast.

At this, the man broke out, losing all patience and subordination. "I'm drunk, am I? I'm a beast, am I? You infernal old miscreant! Shall I wring your old head off and drown you in that pail of water? Do you think I'm going to bear your confounded old harangue, you old Wigsby? Chatter your old ivory teeth at me, do you, you grinning old baboon! Come on, if you're a man, and can stand to a man. Ha! You coward, knives, knives!"

"If you advance a step, I'll send it into you," said the Major, seizing a knife from the table. "Go downstairs, you drunken brute, and leave the house. Send for your book and wages in the morning, and never let me see your insolent face again. This impertinence has been growing for months. You've grown too rich. You're not fit for service. Get out."

"And where would you wish me to go, pray, out of the house?" asked the man, feigning confusion. "Won't it be equal convenient tomorrow morning? Tootyfay mam's hose, sir, sir?"

"Silence, you beast, and go!" cried the Major.

Morgan began to laugh, a rather sinister sound. "Look here, Pendennis," he said, seating himself. "Since I've been in this room, you've called me beast, brute, dog, and damn me. How do you suppose one man likes that talk from another? How many years have I waited on you, and how many damns and curses have you given me along with my wages? Do you think a man is a dog that you can talk to him this way? If I choose to drink a little, why shouldn't I? I've seen many gentlemen drunk formerly, and perhaps have the habit from them. I'm not going to leave this house, old fellow. Shall I tell you why? The house is my house; every stick of furniture in it is mine, except your old traps, your shower-bath, and your wig-box. I've bought the place with my own industry and perseverance. I can show a hundred pounds where you can show fifty, or your damned supercilious nephew either. I've served you honorably, done everything for you these dozen years

The morning sun filtered through the slats of the shutters, illuminating the scene of a domestic coup. Mr. Morgan, the former valet, stood with the air of a man who had just executed a flawless military maneuver. His face was a mask of grave respect, though his eyes danced with a triumph that only a man who had just seized the keys to the kingdom could muster. He carried the Major's clothes, cans of water, and an array of toilet requisites that seemed to include everything from a toothbrush to a miniature cannon.

"It's you, is it?" grunted the old Major from his bed, a prisoner in his own quarters. "I shan't take you back again, you understand."

"I have not the least wish to be taken back again, Major Pendennis," Mr. Morgan replied, his voice dripping with a dignity that was almost theatrical. "Nor to serve you, nor any man. But as I wish you to be comfortable as long as you stay in my house, I came up to do what is necessary." With a flourish that suggested he was unveiling a new invention rather than polishing a razor, Mr. Morgan laid out the silver dressing-case and strapped the shining blade.

Once these offices were concluded, he turned to the Major with an indescribable solemnity. "Thinking that you would most likely be in want of a respectable person, until you suited yourself, I spoke to a young man last night, who is here."

"Indeed," said the warrior in the tent-bed, his voice thick with sleep and suspicion.

"He has lived in the first families, and I can vouch for his respectability."

"You are monstrous polite," grinned the old Major, a rare smile breaking through his grumpiness. The truth was, after the chaotic events of the previous evening, Morgan had retreated to his own club, the *Wheel of Fortune*. There, finding Frosch—a courier and valet recently returned from a foreign tour with young Lord Cubley, and currently unemployed—he had pitched his pitch. He had told Frosch that he had "a devil of a blow up with his own Governor" and was retiring from the business altogether. He had suggested that if Frosch wanted a temporary job, he might apply in Bury Street.

"You are very polite," said the Major, "and your recommendation, I am sure, will have every weight."

Morgan blushed; he felt his master was 'a-chaffing' of him. "The man has awaited on you before, sir," he said with great dignity. "Lord De la Pole gave him to his nephew, young Lord Cubley. He has been with him on his foreign tour. Not wishing to go to Fitzurse Castle—which Frosch's chest is delicate, and he cannot bear the cold in Scotland—he is free to serve you or not, as you choose."

"I repeat, sir, that you are exceedingly polite," said the Major. "Come in, Frosch—you will do very well—Mr. Morgan, will you have the great kindness to—"

"I shall show him what is necessary, sir, and what is customary for you to wish to have done. Will you please to take breakfast here or at the Club, Major Pendennis?"

"With your kind permission, I will breakfast here, and afterwards we will make our little arrangements."

"If you please, sir."

"Will you now oblige me by leaving the room?"

Morgan withdrew. The excessive politeness of his ex-employer made him almost as angry as the Major's bitterest words. And whilst the old gentleman was making his mysterious toilet, we will also modestly retire.

After breakfast, Major Pendennis and his new aide-de-camp occupied themselves in preparing for their departure. The establishment of the old bachelor was not very complicated. He encumbered himself with no useless wardrobe. A Bible (his mother's), a road book, Pen's novel (calf elegant), and the Duke of Wellington's Despatches, with a few prints, maps, and portraits of that illustrious general, and of various sovereigns and consorts of this country, and of the General under whom Major Pendennis had served in India, formed his literary and artistic collection: he was always ready to march at a few hours' notice, and the cases in which he had brought his property into his lodgings some fifteen years before, were still in the lofts amply sufficient to receive all his goods. These, the young woman who did the work of the house, and who was known by the name of Betty to her mistress, and of "Slavey" to Mr. Morgan, brought down from their resting-place, and obediently dusted and cleaned under the eyes of the terrible Morgan. His demeanour was guarded and solemn; he had spoken no word as yet to Mrs. Brixham respecting his threats of the past night, but he looked as if he would execute them, and the poor widow tremblingly awaited her fate.

Old Pendennis, armed with his cane, superintended the package of his goods and chattels, under the hands of Mr. Frosch, and the Slavey burned such of his papers as he did not care to keep; flung open doors and closets until they were all empty; and now all boxes and chests were closed, except his desk, which was ready to receive the final accounts of Mr. Morgan.

That individual now made his appearance, and brought his books. "As I wish to speak to you in private, perhaps you will have the kindness to request Frosch to step downstairs," he said, on entering.

"Bring a couple of cabs, Frosch, if you please—and wait downstairs until I ring for you," said the Major. Morgan saw Frosch downstairs, watched him go along the street upon his errand, and produced his books and accounts, which were simple and very easily settled.

"And now, sir," said he, having pocketed the cheque which his ex-employer gave him, and signed his name to his book with a flourish, "and now that accounts are closed between us, sir," he said, "I propose to speak to you as one man to another"—(Morgan liked the sound of his own voice; and, as an individual, indulged in public speaking whenever he could get an opportunity, at the Club, or the housekeeper's room)—"and I must tell you, that I'm in possession of certain information."

"And may I inquire of what nature, pray?" asked the Major.

"It's valuable information, Major Pendennis, as you know very well. I know of a marriage as is no marriage—of a honourable Baronet as is no more married than I am; and which his wife is married to somebody else, as you know too, sir."

Pendennis at once understood all. "Ha! this accounts for your behaviour. You have been listening at the door, sir, I suppose," said the Major, looking very haughty; "I forgot to look at the keyhole when I went to that public-house, or I might have suspected what sort of a person was behind it."

"I may have my schemes as you may have yours, I suppose," answered Morgan. "I may get my information, and I may act on that information, and I may find that information valuable as anybody else may. A poor servant may have a bit of luck as well as a gentleman, mayn't he? Don't you be putting on your haughty looks, sir, and coming the aristocrat over me. That's all gammon with me. I'm an Englishman, I am, and as good as you."

"To what the devil does this tend, sir? and how does the secret which you have surprised concern me, I should like to know?" asked Major Pendennis, with great majesty.

"How does it concern me, indeed! how grand we are! How does it concern my nephew, I wonder? How does it concern my nephew's seat in Parliament: and to subornation of bigamy? How does it concern that? What, are you to be the only man to have a secret, and to trade on it? Why shouldn't I go halves, Major Pendennis? I've found it out too. Look here! I ain't going to be unreasonable with you. Make it worth my while, and I'll keep the thing close. Let Mr. Arthur take his seat, and his rich wife, if you like; I don't want to marry her. But I will have my share, as sure as my name's James Morgan. And if I don't——"

"And if you don't, sir—what?" Pendennis asked.

"If I don't, I split, and tell all. I smash Clavering, and have him and his wife up for bigamy—so help me, I will! I smash young Hopeful's marriage, and I show up you and him as making use of this secret, in order to squeeze a seat in Parliament out of Sir Francis, and a fortune out of his wife."

"Mr. Pendennis knows no more of this business than the babe unborn, sir," cried the Major, aghast. "No more than Lady Clavering, than Miss Amory does."

"Tell that to the marines, Major," replied the valet; "that cock won't fight with me."

"Do you doubt my word, you villain?"

"No bad language. I don't care one twopence-a-penny whether your word's true or not. I tell you, I intend this to be a nice little annuity to me, Major: for I have every one of you; and I ain't such a fool as to let you go. I should say that you might make it five hundred a year to me among you, easy. Pay me down the first quarter now and I'm as mum as a mouse. Just give a note for one thousand five hundred. There's your cheque-book on your desk."

"And there's this too, you villain," cried the old gentleman. In the desk to which the valet pointed was a little double-barrelled pistol, which had belonged to Pendennis's old patron; the Indian commander-in-chief, and which had accompanied him in many a campaign. "One more word, you scoundrel and I'll shoot you, like a mad dog. Stop—by Jove, I'll do it now. You'll assault me, will you? You'll strike at an old man, will you, you lying coward? Kneel down and say your prayers, sir, for by the Lord you shall die."

The Major's face glared with rage at his adversary, who looked terrified before him for a moment, and at the next, with a shriek of "Murder!" sprang towards the open window, under which a policeman happened to be on his beat. "Murder! Police!" bellowed Mr. Morgan.

To his surprise, Major Pendennis wheeled away the table and walked to the other window, which was also open. He beckoned the policeman. "Come up here, policeman," he said, and then went and placed himself against the door.

"You miserable sneak," he said to Morgan; "the pistol hasn't been loaded these fifteen years, as you would have known very well, if you had not been such a coward. That policeman is coming, and I will have him up, and have your trunks searched; I have reason to believe that you are a thief, sir. I know you are. I'll swear to the things."

"You gave 'em to me—you gave 'em to me!" cried Morgan.

The Major laughed. "We'll see," he said; and the guilty valet remembered some fine lawn-fronted shirts—a certain gold-headed cane—an opera-glass, which he had forgotten to bring down, and of which he had assumed the use along with certain articles of his master's clothes, which the old dandy neither wore nor asked for.

Policeman X entered; followed by the seared Mrs. Brixham and her maid-of-all-work, who had been at the door and found some difficulty in closing it against the street amateurs, who wished to see the row. The Major began instantly to speak.

"I have had occasion to discharge this drunken scoundrel," he said. "Both last night and this morning he insulted and assaulted me. I am an old man and took up a pistol. You see it is not loaded, and this coward cried out before he was hurt. I am glad you are come. I was charging him with taking my property, and desired to examine his trunks and his room."

"The velvet cloak you ain't worn these three years, nor the weskits, and I thought I might take the shirts, and I—I take my oath I intended to put back the opera-glass," roared Morgan, writhing with rage and terror.

"The man acknowledges that he is a thief," the Major said, calmly. "He has been in my service for years, and I have treated him with every kindness and confidence. We will go upstairs and examine his trunks."

In those trunks Mr. Morgan had things which he would fain keep from public eyes. Mr. Morgan, the bill-discounter, gave goods as well as money to his customers. He provided young spendthrifts with snuff boxes and pins and jewels and pictures and cigars, and of a very doubtful quality those cigars and jewels and pictures were. Their display at a police-office, the discovery of his occult profession, and the exposure of the Major's property, which he had appropriated, indeed, rather than stolen,—would not have added to the reputation of Mr. Morgan. He looked a piteous image of terror and discomfiture.

"He'll smash me, will he?" thought the Major. "I'll crush him now, and finish with him."

But he paused. He looked at poor Mrs. Brixham's scared face; and he thought for a moment to himself that the man brought to bay and in prison might make disclosures which had best be kept secret, and that it was best not to deal too fiercely with a desperate man.

"Stop," he said, "policeman. I'll speak with this man by himself."

"Do you give Mr. Morgan in charge?" said the policeman.

"I have brought no charge as yet," the Major said, with a significant look at his man.

"Thank you, sir," whispered Morgan, very low.

"Go outside the door, and wait there, policeman

Arthur Pendennis had just touched down in London, though his stay was merely a fleeting stopover. After dropping off his fellow travelers at a hotel he'd escorted them to from the West, he hurried straight to the Chambers in Lamb Court. The building was dreary, yet not entirely devoid of comfort, basking in whatever patches of sunlight dared to visit its gloomy corridors. In such chambers, freedom itself serves as the sun; the Templars may grumble, but they eventually settle in to enjoy their ease.

His domestic announced that Warrington was also present. Naturally, Arthur bolted up the stairs to his friend's room. As always, the air was thick with the scent of pipe tobacco, and George was already deep in his newspapers and reviews. The two greeted one another with that specific, rough cordiality reserved for young Englishmen—a facade of rudeness hiding a furnace of warmth and affection.

"Well, young one!" Warrington grinned, pulling his pipe from his mouth.
"How are you, old boy?" Arthur replied, extending a hand.

It was a greeting between friends who hadn't seen each other in months. Imagine Alphonse and Frederic rushing into each other's arms, shrieking *Ce bon cœur!* over one another's shoulders; or Max and Wilhelm exchanging a dozen kisses scented with Havana on their mustaches. But Britons? "Well, young one!" "How are you, old boy!" is all they say, perhaps after saving each other's lives the day before. Tomorrow, the handshakes cease, replaced by head-wags at breakfast. Yet, beneath this stoical decorum lies a bond of absolute trust: they would share their purses, defend each other with the loudest enthusiasm, and yet part with a perfunctory "Good-bye" and meet with a casual "How-d'you-do?" in the interim. It is a curious, modest, strangely stoical form of friendship. "Yes, we aren't demonstrative like those confounded foreigners," Hardman might say, a man who showed no friendship and felt none in his entire life.

"Been in Switzerland?" Pen asked.
"Yes," Warrington confirmed.
"Couldn't find a bit of tobacco fit to smoke until we hit Strasbourg, where I finally got some caporal."

The man's mind was undoubtedly filled with the grand sights and the vast emotions of nature, yet his enthusiasm remained coy, veiled behind a cloud of tobacco smoke. He would save his true confessions for confidential evenings and pour his ardor into his writings instead. His travels would enrich his prose with pregnant allusions and brilliant illustrations, coloring his eloquence and sharpening his wit, even if he never displayed them in conversation.

The elder gave a rapid, gloomy account of his tour: Switzerland, North Italy, the Tyrol, Vienna, Dresden, and the Rhine. He spoke of these places in a sulky voice, as if mentioning them made him miserable. Then the young man began. He had been in the country, bored stiff, canvassing at a glacial pace. He was only in town for a day or two before heading to Tunbridge Wells to visit friends who would be equally slow. How difficult it is for an Englishman to admit he is happy!

"And the seat in Parliament, Pen? Have you made it all right?" Warrington asked.
"All right," Pen said. "As soon as Parliament meets and a new writ is issued, Clavering retires, and I step into his shoes."
"And under which king does Bezonian speak or die?" Warrington pressed. "Are we Liberals, Conservatives, Government men, or flying our own flag?"
"Hem!" Pen sighed. "There are no real politics now; everyone's politics are pretty much the same. I haven't got enough acres to be a Protectionist, nor could I be one even if I owned the county. I'll go pretty much with the Government, though I plan to lead them on some social questions I've been working on during the vacation—don't grin, you old cynic. I've been compiling the Blue Books and intend to be strong on Sanitary and Colonisation issues."
"We reserve the liberty of voting against the Government, though we're generally friendly. We are friends of the people *avant tout*. We give lectures at the Clavering Institute and shake hands with intelligent mechanics. We believe the franchise should be greatly enlarged, yet we are free to accept office once the House has listened to our crack speeches and the Administration sees our merit."
"I am not Moses," Pen said, with his usual touch of melancholy. "I have no laws from Heaven to bring down from the mountain. I don't belong to the mountain, nor do I set myself up as a leader of mankind. My faith isn't strong enough for that, nor my vanity, nor my hypocrisy. I won't tell any lies, George, I promise you; I'll only coincide with those necessary ones that keep circulation flowing. Give a man the advantage of his skeptical turn. If I find a good thing to say in the House, I'll say it; a good measure, I'll support it; a fair place, I'll take it, and be glad of my luck. But I wouldn't flatter a great man more than a mob. Now you know as much about my politics as I do. What call have I to be a Whig? Whiggism isn't a divine institution. Why not vote with the Liberal Conservatives? They've done for the nation what the Whigs never could without them. Who converted both? The Radicals and the country outside. I think the *Morning Post* is often right, and *Punch* is often wrong. I don't profess a calling; I just take advantage of a chance. *Parlons d'autre chose*."

"The next thing at your heart, after ambition, is love, I suppose?" Warrington asked. "How have our young loves prospered? Are we changing conditions and leaving the chambers? Are you going to divorce me, Arthur, and take a wife?"
"I suppose so," Pen said. "She's very good-natured and lively. She sings, and she doesn't mind smoking. She'll have a fair fortune—I don't know exactly how much—but my uncle augurs everything from the Begum's generosity and says she'll come down very handsomely. And I think Blanche is devilish fond of me," Arthur added, sighing.
"That means we accept her caresses and her money."
"Haven't we said before that life is a transaction?" Pendennis countered. "I don't pretend to break my heart over her. I've told her pretty fairly what my feelings are—and I've engaged myself to her. Since I last saw her, especially over the last two months in the country, I think she's grown fonder and fonder. Her letters, and especially hers to Laura, show it. Mine have been simple—no raptures, no vows, you understand—but I've treated it as an *affaire faite*, neither hastening nor deferring the completion."
"And Laura? How is she?" Warrington asked frankly.
"Laura, George," Pen said, staring hard at his friend—"by heaven, Laura is the best, noblest, and dearest girl the sun ever shone upon." His voice faltered; he seemed unable to utter the words. He stretched out his hand to his comrade, who took it and nodded.
"Have you only found out that now, young un?" Warrington asked after a pause.
"Who hasn't learned things too late, George?" Arthur cried, gathering words and emotion in his impetuous rush. "Whose life isn't a disappointment? Who carries his heart entire to the grave without mutilation?
