I never knew anyone who was truly happy, or who hasn't had to ransom themselves from Fate's grasp with the payment of some dearest treasure. Lucky if we're left alone afterwards, having paid our fine, if the tyrant visits us no more. Suppose I've found out I've lost the greatest prize in the world, now that it can't be mine—that for years I had an angel under my tent and let her go? Am I the only one—ah, dear old boy, am I the only one? And do you think my lot is easier to bear because I own that I deserve it? She's gone from us. God's blessing be with her! She might have stayed, and I lost her; it's like Undine, isn't it, George?"
"She was in this room once," George said softly.
He saw her there—he heard the sweet low voice, the kind shining eyes, the face remembered in night-watches, blest and loved always—now gone! A glass that had held a nosegay, a Bible with Helen's handwriting, were all that remained of that brief flower of his life. Tell it was a dream; tell it passes. Better the recollection of a dream than an aimless waking from a blank stupor.

The two sat in silence, each lost in their own thoughts, aware of the other's presence. Pen broke it soon after, saying he must go seek his uncle and report progress. The Major had written in a very bad humor; the Major was getting old. "I should like to see you in Parliament, and snugly settled with a comfortable house and an heir to the name before I make my bow," the Major had written. "Show me these, and then let old Arthur Pendennis make room for the younger fellows; he has walked the Pall Mall pave long enough."
"There is a kindness about the old heathen," Warrington observed. "He cares for somebody besides himself, at least for some other part of himself besides that buttoned into his own coat—for you and your race. He wants to see the progeny of the Pendennises multiplying and increasing, hoping they may inherit the land. The old patriarch blesses you from the Club window of Bays's, and is carried off and buried under the flags of St. James's Church, in sight of Piccadilly, the cabstand, and the carriages going to the levee. It is an edifying ending."
"The new blood I bring into the family," Pen mused, "is rather tainted. If I had chosen, I think my father-in-law Amory wouldn't have been the progenitor I desired for my race; nor my grandfather-in-law Snell; nor our Oriental ancestors. By the way, who was Amory? Amory was lieutenant of an Indiaman. Blanche wrote some verses about him, about the storm, the mountain wave, the seaman's grave, the gallant father, and that sort of thing. Amory was drowned commanding a country ship between Calcutta and Sydney; Amory and the Begum weren't happy together. She has been unlucky in her selection of husbands, the good old lady, for, between ourselves, a more despicable creature than Sir Francis Clavering, of Clavering Park, Baronet, never—"
"Never legislated for his country," Warrington broke in, causing Pen to blush slightly.
"By the way, at Baden," Warrington continued, "I found our friend the Chevalier Strong in great state, wearing his orders. He told me he'd quarreled with Clavering, of whom he seemed to have almost as bad an opinion as you do, and in fact, I think, though I won't be certain, he confided to me that Clavering was an utter scoundrel. That fellow Bloundell, who taught you card-playing at Oxbridge, was with Strong; and time, I think, has brought out his valuable qualities, rendering him a more accomplished rascal than he was during your undergraduateship. But the king of the place was the famous Colonel Altamont, who was carrying all before him, giving flies to the whole society, and breaking the bank, it was said."
"My uncle knows something about that fellow—Clavering knows something about him. There's something *louche* regarding him. But come! I must go to Bury Street, like a dutiful nephew." Taking his hat, Pen prepared to leave.
"I will walk, too," Warrington said. They descended the stairs, stopping briefly at Pen's chambers, which, as the reader knows, were now on the lower story.

Here Pen began sprinkling himself with eau-de-Cologne, carefully scenting his hair and whiskers with that odoriferous water.
"What's the matter? You've not been smoking. Has my pipe poisoned you?" Warrington growled.
"I'm going to call upon some women," Pen said. "I'm—I'm going to dine with 'em. They're passing through town and are at an hotel in Jermyn Street."
Warrington looked with good-natured interest at the young fellow dandifying himself to a pitch of completeness, emerging in a gorgeous shirt-front and neckcloth, fresh gloves, and glistening boots. George wore thick high-lows, his old shirt torn about the breast and ragged at the collar where his blue beard had worn it.
"Well, young un," he said simply, "I like you to be a buck; somehow. When I walk about with you, it's as if I had a rose in my button-hole. And you're still affable. I don't think there's any young fellow in the Temple who turns out like you; and I don't believe you were ever ashamed of walking with me yet."
"Don't laugh at me, George," Pen said.
"I say, Pen," the other continued, sadly, "if you write—if you write to Laura, I wish you would say 'God bless her' from me."
Pen blushed, looked at Warrington, and then—then burst into an uncontrollable fit of laughter.
"I'm going to dine with her," he said. "I brought her and Lady Rockminster up from the country today—made two days of it—slept last night at Bath—I say, George, come and dine, too. I may ask anyone I please, and the old lady is constantly talking about you."
George refused. George had an article to write. George hesitated; and oh, strange to say! at last he agreed. It was settled they would call on the ladies, and they marched away in high spirits to the hotel in Jermyn Street. Once more the dear face

The dinner arrived just as Arthur stumbled back into the room, and Lady Rockminster immediately launched into a tirade about his tardiness. But Laura, catching sight of her cousin's ghastly pallor and terrified expression, cut her imperious aunt short. With a voice trembling with concern, she asked what had happened. Was Arthur ill?

Arthur, however, merely drained a massive bumper of sherry. "I've heard the most extraordinary news," he stammered, eyeing the servants nervously. "I'll tell you later." He was visibly shaking, his nerves frayed.

"Don't trample and beat the floor with your feet under the table!" Lady Rockminster snapped. "You've crushed Fido and knocked over his saucer. Look at Mr. Warrington; he keeps his boots perfectly quiet."

By the time dessert arrived, the meal seemed interminable. Finally, Lady Rockminster declared the dinner a dismal failure. "Something must have happened," she muttered, dismissing the possibility of tea. "I'm going to have a nap. Good night, Mr. Warrington. Come again, when there's no business to discuss." With a toss of her head, the old lady marched out with theatrical dignity.

George and the others rose to follow, and Warrington prepared to depart, bidding Laura a farewell that she received with wide, anxious eyes. Then Arthur stopped him. "Stay, George. You must hear this news and advise me. I'm at a loss."

"It concerns Blanche, Arthur," Laura whispered, her heart hammering against her ribs, her cheeks flushing a color she'd never known.

"Yes," Pen added, his voice tight. "And a scandalous story. When I left you to visit my uncle's lodgings, I found his old servant, Morgan, at the door. He said he and his master had parted that morning; my uncle had fled to this very hotel. When I arrived, he was out to dinner. Morgan insisted I come inside, claiming he had something vital to say. Apparently, the scoundrel has hoarded a fortune while serving my uncle and is now a millionaire. He told me this must remain a secret among us. Blanche's father isn't dead. He's alive. The marriage between Clavering and the Begum was never valid."

"So Blanche is her grandfather's heir?" Warrington asked.

"Perhaps," Pen replied grimly. "But what a father! Amory is an escaped convict—Clavering knows it, my uncle knows it. That's the leverage they used to force Clavering to surrender his borough to me."

"Blanche doesn't know," Laura said softly. "Nor does Lady Clavering?"

"No," Pen confirmed. "Blanche believes her father drowned in New South Wales as a ship's captain, not as a convict. Lady Clavering has hinted they weren't happy and that her husband was a bad character. She once told me, tears in her eyes, that it was hard for a woman to admit she was glad to hear her husband dead, adding that she had chosen poorly twice in her life. Now? If the rascal returns, Morgan will blackmail him. Morgan holds the secret and intends to extort money from us all. He thought I knew, asking for an annuity to keep quiet, threatening to drag Clavering down to the hulks. Good heavens, was my uncle mad to conspire in this? To think my mother's son would trade on such treason!"

"It's Colonel Altamont, of course," Warrington realized. "I see it all now."

"If the rascal comes back," Arthur continued, "Morgan will use it. He proposes to rob us all. The devil assumed I was in on it."

"No!" Warrington's deep voice cracked, filled with a mixture of love and agony. He looked at the two young lovers with a pang of despair. "Our boy can't meddle in this filth. Arthur Pendennis can't marry a convict's daughter and sit in Parliament as a member for the hulks. You must wash your hands of this, Pen. Break it off. Give no explanation, just cite family reasons. It's better if those poor women think you false than that they know the truth. Besides, I can get Clavering to acknowledge your reasons are sufficient to void the union. Don't you agree, Laura?" He couldn't bear to meet her gaze, knowing he was casting away his last shred of hope.

"Her father's shame isn't Blanche's fault, dear Arthur," Laura said, her voice rapid and pale. "If you were married, would you desert her because she's innocent? You're pledged to her. If she's unhappy, wouldn't you comfort her? Our mother would have." As she spoke, she wrapped her arms around him, burying her face in his heart.

"Our mother is an angel with God," Pen sobbed. "And you are the dearest, the best of women. Teach me my duty. Pray for me. God bless you, my sister!"

"Amen," Warrington groaned, burying his face in his hands. "She's right. She can't do wrong. That girl looks like an angel." Indeed, she smiled with a radiant innocence that haunted him for days.

"And now," Laura said, resting her small hand on the table, "we shall have some tea."

Before this mundane conclusion could settle, a servant announced that Major Pendennis had returned to the hotel and was waiting for his nephew. Laura, her face writing a silent plea—*Behave, hold the line, be gentle but firm*—took her leave and retreated to her room. Warrington, who rarely drank tea, grumbled as he watched the empty chair. *Why couldn't old Pendennis have come an hour later?* The inevitable moment had struck. The hand was shaken, the door closed, and the friend was gone. Alone in the smoking room of a neighboring club, surrounded by men brawling about politics and opera girls, Warrington lit a cigar. He bore the sacred secret like a lead weight, sinking deeper into the noise, wondering which window in the hotel held the light of the girl he loved.

Meanwhile, Arthur walked the hotel passages, his anger boiling. He was furious that the old man had turned him into a puppet, compromising his honor. When Arthur finally met the Major, the old gentleman's hand was ice-cold and trembling. He coughed, grumbled over the fire, and cursed Morgan's ingratitude with peevish passion.

"The confounded impudent scoundrel! He challenged me to a fight last night! I nearly drove a knife into him! He's made ten thousand pounds and deserves hanging! But he knew all my ways. When I rang the bell, that thief brought the thing I wanted, unlike that stupid German lout."

"What have you been doing in the country? Been with Lady Rockminster? She's of the old school, *vieille ecole*, *bonne ecole*. They don't make gentlemen like that anymore. In fifty years, you won't know a man from another. But they'll last my time. I'm getting old, Pen. I was packing my library today and found a Bible belonging to my poor mother. I'd like you to keep it. You'll open the box when it's your turn, and the old fellow is laid under the sod."

His age and kindness disarmed Arthur's fury, stirring a pang of guilt. Arthur knew the announcement he was about to make would shatter the old man's hopes.

"I'm off, sir," the Major nodded. "But I'd like to read a speech of yours in the Times before I go—'Mr. Pendennis said, Unaccustomed as I am...' Hey, Arthur? You look healthy. My brother Jack brought the family right. Go west, buy the old estate. *Nec tenui penna*. We'll rise again. You'll be a Baronet before you die."

The words struck Arthur like a blow. *And it is I who am about to demolish this poor old man's castle.*

"I went to your lodgings at Bury Street, though I didn't find you," Arthur began slowly. "I spoke with Morgan, Uncle."

"Indeed!" The Major's face flushed. "The cat's out of the bag, begad!"

"He told me a story that shocked and pained me deeply," Arthur said.

The Major feigned nonchalance. "What? The story about... what-d'ye-call-'em?"

"About Miss Amory's father. About Lady Clavering's first husband, who he is, and what."

"Hem. A devilish awkward affair!" The old man rubbed his nose. "I've been aware of that confounded circumstance for some time."

"I wish I'd known sooner, or not at all," Arthur muttered.

*He is safe,* the Major thought, relieved. *I should have kept it from you and those innocent women.*

"You're right. No reason for the women to know. I'll never tell them, though that villain Morgan might. He wants to trade on the secret and has offered me ransom. It's unpleasant to think I'm engaged to a convict's daughter."

"That's exactly why I kept it from you, my dear boy. But Miss Amory isn't a convict's daughter, do you see? She's the daughter of Lady Clavering, with fifty or sixty thousand pounds. Her father-in-law, a Baronet of high reputation, approves the match and gives up his seat to his son-in-law. What could be simpler?"

"Is it true, sir?"

"Begad, yes. Of course. Amory is dead. The first sign of life he shows, he's dead. We have him at a deadlock, like the fellow in the play 'The Critic.' Monstrously witty man, Sheridan. By Gad, when I was at the Cape, I remember—"

The Major's garrulity, a desperate attempt to distract Arthur, failed. Arthur interrupted. "If you'd told me this sooner, you'd have spared us both pain. I wouldn't be tied to an engagement I can't honorably recede from."

"No, begad, we've fixed you. A man fixed to a seat in Parliament and a pretty girl with two thousand a year is fixed to no bad thing."

"Great Heavens, sir!" Arthur cried. "Are you blind? Can't you see?"

"See what, young gentleman?"

"See that rather than trade on this secret of Amory's, I'd join my father-in-law at the hulks! Rather than take a parliamentary seat bought by Clavering's silence, I'd take the spoons off the table! You've given me a felon's daughter for a wife, doomed me to poverty and shame, cursed my career! We've played a guilty game and been overreached. By marrying this girl for her money and advancement, I degraded myself and prostituted my honor!"

"What in Heaven's name do you mean?" the old man shrieked.

"I mean there's a measure of baseness I can't pass. I have no other words. I've felt for months my conduct was wicked, sordid, worldly. I'm rightly punished by losing both."

"How do you mean you lose either?" the Major shrieked. "Who's to take your fortune or seat away? Clavering shall give them to you. You'll have every shilling of eighty thousand pounds."

"I'll keep my promise to Miss Amory, sir."

"And her parents shall keep theirs to you."

"Not so, please God," Arthur answered. "I have sinned, but Heaven help me, I will sin no more. I will let Clavering off from that bargain made without my knowledge. I will take no money with Blanche but her original fortune. I will try to make her happy. You brought this on me, sir. But you knew no better, and I forgive—"

"Arthur—in God's name—in your father's name, who was the proudest man alive—in mine—for the sake of this broken-down old fellow who's fond of you—don't fling this chance away! It's the making of you. You'll be a Baronet; it's three thousand a year. On my knees, I beg of you, don't do this."

The old man actually sank to his knees, seizing Arthur's hand, looking up with pitiful eyes. It was cruel to see the shaking hands, the wrinkled, quivering face, the weeping, winking old eyes. "Ah, sir," Arthur groaned, "you've brought enough pain on me, spare me this. You wished me to marry Blanche. I marry her. For God's sake, rise! I can't bear it."

"You mean to say you'll take her as a beggar and be one yourself?" the Major gasped, rising and coughing violently.

"I look at her as a person to whom a great calamity has befallen, and to whom I am pledged. She cannot help the misfortune. As she had my word when she was prosperous, I shall not withdraw it now she is poor. I will not take Clavering's seat unless he gives it freely. I will not have a shilling more than her original fortune."

"Have the kindness to ring the bell," the Major said, his voice breaking. "I've done my best. And—and Shakespeare was right—and Cardinal Wolsey—'and had I but served my God as I've served you.' Yes, on my knees, by Jove, to my own nephew—I mightn't have been—Good night, sir. You needn't trouble yourself to call again."

Arthur took the passive, clammy hand the old man left him. He looked aged and broken, as if the contest had shattered him completely.

The next day, Arthur kept to his bed and refused to see his uncle.

The morning sun filtered through the dust motes of Warrington's chambers, illuminating a scene of sudden, quiet vacancy. Pen, still wrapped in the familiar comfort of his dressing gown, climbed the stairs as per custom, expecting the usual symphony of George's boisterous greeting or perhaps the clatter of his boots. Instead, he found only Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress, bustling about with a basket of linens.

"George is gone, sir," she announced, her voice carrying a peculiar, hollow cheerfulness. "He's off to his brother's house in Suffolk. Left a carpet-bag behind, though. And look at this!" She gestured to the table, where packages addressed to the newspaper and review for which George wrote lay waiting, like unsent letters to a ghost. "I found him at the table when I came," she continued, her tone shifting to a mock-sympathetic drama. "Writing at his papers, candle burned out, and hard as his bed is, he wasn't in it all night, sir."

Pen stood frozen. The silence of the room suddenly felt heavy, filled with the phantom echo of George's frantic energy. Indeed, George had spent the previous night at the Club until the brawl became unbearable, then walked home to finish a piece of work he had been tormenting himself over. Now, the labor was done, the night worn away, and the tardy November dawn found the young man slumped over his desk. In the next day's paper, or perhaps a quarter's review later, the world would admire the genius of his illustrations, the fierce vigor of his satire, the depth of his reason. But there would be no hint of the other thoughts that haunted him—a tone more melancholy than usual, a bitterness sharper than his later style. To the few who knew him, his work from this period would reveal a man carrying a weight invisible to the public eye.

Pen threw himself into George's empty chair, the wood creaking under his weight. He noted the relics scattered about: two or three reference books, fragments of torn-up manuscript, drawers standing open, pens and inkstand, lines half-visible on blotting paper, and a bit of sealing-wax twisted and bitten into sundry pieces. His eyes drifted to the bookcase, noting a gap next to the old College Plato and the Boniface Arms. Helen's Bible was missing.

"He's taken that with him," Pen realized, the thought settling in his stomach like a lead weight. He knew why his friend was gone.

"Oh, how much wiser, how much better, how much nobler he is than I," Pen murmured, rubbing his eyes. "Where was such a friend, or such a brave heart? Where shall I ever hear such a frank voice, and kind laughter? Where shall I ever see such a true gentleman?" He looked around the empty room, the absence of George making the space feel vast and cold. "No wonder she loved him. God bless him! What was I compared to him? What could she do else but love him? To the end of our days we will be her brothers, as fate wills that we can be no more. We'll be her knights, and wait on her: and when we're old, we'll say how we loved her. Dear, dear old George!"

Descending to his own chambers, Pen's eye caught the letter-box of his outer door, which he had previously overlooked. There, nestled inside, was a little note to *A. P., Esq.*, in George's unmistakable handwriting.

*I have my own opinion of the issue of matters about which we talked in J——— St. yesterday; and think my presence de trop.*

And so George was gone, leaving Mrs. Flanagan to rule over his empty chambers, while Pen felt the sudden, sharp pang of loss.

Of course, Pen had to visit his uncle the day after their colloquy. Not being admitted, he naturally turned to Lady Rockminster's apartments. The old lady instantly asked for Bluebeard, insisting he come to dinner.

"Bluebeard is gone," Pen said, taking out poor George's scrap of paper and handing it to Laura.

Laura looked at the note, her expression unreadable. She did not look at Pen in return, merely passed the paper back to him and walked away, leaving him standing there. Pen rushed into an eloquent eulogy upon his dear old George for Lady Rockminster, who was astonished at his enthusiasm. She had never heard him so warm in praise of anybody; she told him with her usual frankness that she didn't think it had been in his nature to care so much about any other person.

Later, as Mr. Pendennis passed through Waterloo Place on one of his many walks to the hotel where Laura lived, he saw an old friend issuing from Messrs. Gimcrack's celebrated shop. The gentleman was followed to his brougham by an obsequious shopman bearing parcels. The gentleman was in the deepest mourning; the brougham, the driver, and the horse were all draped in black. Grief in easy circumstances, supported by the comfortablest springs and cushions, was typified in the equipage and its little proprietor.

"What, Foker! Hail, Foker!" cried out Pen, recognizing Arthur's old schoolfellow, the heir of the late lamented John Henry Foker, Esq., master of Logwood and other houses, and principal partner in the great brewery of Foker and Co.

A little hand, covered with a glove of the deepest ebony and set off by three inches of a snowy wristband, was put forth to meet Arthur's salutation. The other little hand held a little morocco case, containing, no doubt, something precious of which Mr. Foker had just become proprietor in Messrs. Gimcrack's shop. Pen's keen eyes and satiric turn showed him at once upon what errand Mr. Foker had been employed; he thought of the heir in Horace pouring forth the gathered wine of his father's vats, and that human nature is pretty much the same in Regent Street as in the Via Sacra.

"Le Roi est mort. Vive le Roi!" said Arthur.

"Ah!" said the other. "Yes. Thank you—very much obliged. How do you do, Pen?—very busy—good-bye!" and he jumped into the black brougham, sitting like a little black Care behind the black coachman. He had blushed on seeing Pen, showing other signs of guilt and perturbation, which Pen attributed to the novelty of his situation.

"Yes: so wags the world," Pen thought, a sardonic smile touching his lips. "The stone closes over Harry the Fourth, and Harry the Fifth reigns in his stead. The old ministers at the brewery come and kneel before him with their books; the draymen, his subjects, fling up their red caps, and shout for him. What a grave deference and sympathy the bankers and the lawyers show! There was too great a stake at issue between those two that they should ever love each other very cordially. As long as one man keeps another out of twenty thousand a year, the younger must be always hankering after the crown, and the wish must be the father to the thought of possession. Thank Heaven, there was no thought of money between me and our dear mother, Laura."

"There never could have been. You would have spurned it!" cried Laura, appearing beside him. "Why make yourself more selfish than you are, Pen; and allow your mind to own for an instant that it would have entertained such—such dreadful meanness? You make me blush for you, Arthur: you make me——" her eyes finished this sentence, and she passed her handkerchief across them.

"There are some truths which women will never acknowledge," Pen said, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "and from which your modesty always turns away. I do not say that I ever knew the feeling, only that I am glad I had not the temptation. Is there any harm in that confession of weakness?"

"We are all taught to ask to be delivered from evil, Arthur," said Laura, in a low voice. "I am glad if you were spared from that great crime; and only sorry to think that you could by any possibility have been led into it. But you never could; and you don't think you could. Your acts are generous and kind: you disdain mean actions. You take Blanche without money, and without a bribe. Yes, thanks be to Heaven, dear brother. You could not have sold yourself away; I knew you could not when it came to the day, and you did not. Praise be—be where praise is due. Why does this horrid scepticism pursue you, my Arthur? Why doubt and sneer at your own heart—at every one's? Oh, if you knew the pain you give me—how I lie awake and think of those hard sentences, dear brother, and wish them unspoken, unthought!"

"Do I cause you many thoughts and many tears, Laura?" asked Arthur. The fulness of innocent love beamed from her in reply. A smile heavenly pure, a glance of unutterable tenderness, sympathy, pity, shone in her face—all which indications of love and purity Arthur beheld and worshipped in her, as one watches them in a child, as one fancies one might regard them in an angel.

"I—I don't know what I have done," he said, simply, "to have merited such regard from two such women. It is like undeserved praise, Laura—or too much good fortune, which frightens one—or a great post, when a man feels that he is not fit for it. Ah, sister, how weak and wicked we are; how spotless, and full of love and truth, Heaven made you! I think for some of you there has been no fall," he said, looking at the charming girl with an almost paternal glance of admiration. "You can't help having sweet thoughts, and doing good actions. Dear creature! they are the flowers which you bear."

"And what else, sir?" asked Laura. "I see a sneer coming over your face. What is it? Why does it come to drive all the good thoughts away?"

"A sneer, is there? I was thinking, my dear, that nature in making you so good and loving did very well: but——"

"But what? What is that wicked but? and why are you always calling it up?"

"But will come in spite of us. But is reflection. But is the sceptic's familiar, with whom he has made a compact; and if he forgets it, and indulges in happy day-dreams, or building of air-castles, or listens to sweet music let us say, or to the bells ringing to church, But taps at the door, and says, Master, I am here. You are my master; but I am yours. Go where you will you can't travel without me. I will whisper to you when you are on your knees at church. I will be at your marriage pillow. I will sit down at your table with your children. I will be behind your deathbed curtain. That is what But is," Pen said.

"Pen, you frighten me," cried Laura.

"Do you know what But came and said to me just now, when I was looking at you? But said, If that girl had reason as well as love, she would love you no more. If she knew you as you are—the sullied, selfish being which you know—she must part from you, and could give you no love and no sympathy. Didn't I say," he added fondly, "that some of you seem exempt from the fall? Love you know; but the knowledge of evil is kept from you."

"What is this you young folks are talking about?" asked Lady Rockminster, who at this moment made her appearance in the room, having performed, in the mystic retirement of her own apartments, and under the hands of her attendant, those elaborate toilet-rites without which the worthy old lady never presented herself to public view. "Mr. Pendennis, you are always coming here."

"It is very pleasant to be here," Arthur said. "and we were talking, when you came in, about my friend Foker, whom I met just now; and who, as your ladyship knows, has succeeded to his father's kingdom."

"He has a very fine property, he has fifteen thousand a year. He is my cousin. He is a very worthy young man. He must come and see me," said Lady Rockminster, with a look at Laura.

"He has been engaged for many years past to his cousin, Lady——"

"Lady Ann is a foolish little chit," Lady Rockminster said, with much dignity; "and I have no patience with her. She has outraged every feeling of society. She has broken her father's heart, and thrown away fifteen thousand a year."

"Thrown away? What has happened?" asked Pen.

"It will be the talk of the town in a day or two; and there is no need why I should keep the secret any longer," said Lady Rockminster, who had written and received a dozen letters on the subject. "I had a letter yesterday from my daughter, who was staying at Drummington until all the world was obliged to go away on account of the frightful catastrophe which happened there. When Mr. Foker came home from Nice, and after the funeral, Lady Ann went down on her knees to her father, said that she never could marry her cousin, that she had contracted another attachment, and that she must die rather than fulfil her contract. Poor Lord Rosherville, who is dreadfully embarrassed, showed his daughter what the state of his affairs was, and that it was necessary that the arrangements should take place; and in fine, we all supposed that she had listened to reason, and intended to comply with the desires of her family. But what has happened?—last Thursday she went out after breakfast with her maid, and was married in the very church in Drummington Park to Mr. Hobson, her father's own chaplain and her brother's tutor; a

Mr. and Mrs. Sam Huxter

"Dearest Blanche," Arthur wrote, "you are forever reading and dreaming up pretty dramas and exciting romances in real life. Are you now prepared to star in one? Not the delightful role where the heroine inherits her father's palace and wealth, welcoming her husband to loyal vassals and declaring, 'All of this is mine and thine.' No, I propose the part of the unfortunate lady who suddenly discovers she is not the Prince's wife, but Claude Melnotte's—the beggar. Or perhaps the wife of Alnaschar, who enters just as her husband has kicked over the tray of porcelain that was to be the making of his fortune. But wait; Alnaschar, who shattered the china, wasn't married. He had his eye on the Vizier's daughter, and his hopes crashed with the broken tea-cups.

"Will you be the Vizier's daughter, refusing and laughing at Alnaschar, or the Lady of Lyons, loving the penniless Claude Melnotte? I will play that part if you wish. I will love you my best in return. I will do my utmost to make your humble life happy—for humble it will be. The odds are heavily stacked against anything else; we shall live and die in a poor, prosy, humdrum way. There will be no stars or epaulettes for the hero of our story. I shall write one or two more tales that will soon be forgotten. I shall be called to the Bar and try to advance in my profession; perhaps, if I am very lucky and work very hard (which is absurd), I might secure a colonial appointment, and you could be an Indian Judge's lady. Meanwhile, I shall buy back the *Pall Mall Gazette*; the publishers are tired of it since the death of poor Shandon and will sell it for a small sum. Warrington will be my right hand, writing it up to a respectable sale. I will introduce you to Mr. Finucane, the sub-editor, and I know who in the end will be Mrs. Finucane—a very nice, gentle creature who has lived sweetly through a sad life. We shall jog along, look out for better times, and earn our living decently. You shall have the opera boxes and superintend the fashionable intelligence, breaking your little heart in the poet's corner. Shall we live over the offices? There are four very good rooms, a kitchen, and a garret for Laura in Catherine Street in the Strand; or would you prefer a house in Waterloo Road? It would be pleasant, though there is that halfpenny toll at the Bridge. The boys may go to King's College, mayn't they? Does all this sound like a joke to you?

"Ah, dear Blanche, it is no joke, and I am sober and telling the truth. Our fine day-dreams have vanished. Our carriage has whirled out of sight like Cinderella's; our Belgravia house has been whisked away into the air by a malevolent Genius, and I am no longer a Member of Parliament than I am a Bishop on his bench in the House of Lords, or a Duke with a garter at his knee. You know pretty well what my property is, and your own little fortune. We may have enough with those two to live in decent comfort—to take a cab sometimes when we go out to see our friends, and not to deny ourselves an omnibus when we are tired. But that is all: is that enough for you, my little dainty lady? I doubt sometimes whether you can bear the life I offer you—at least, it is fair that you should know what it will be. If you say, 'Yes, Arthur, I will follow your fate whatever it may be, and be a loyal and loving wife to aid and cheer you'—come to me, dear Blanche, and may God help me so that I may do my duty to you. If not, and you look to a higher station, I must not bar Blanche's fortune—I will stand in the crowd and see your ladyship go to Court when you are presented, and you shall give me a smile from your chariot window. I saw Lady Mirabel going to the drawing-room last season: the happy husband at her side glittered with stars and cordons. All the flowers in the garden bloomed in the coachman's bosom. Will you have these and the chariot, or walk on foot and mend your husband's stockings?

"I cannot tell you now—afterwards I might, should the day come when we may have no secrets from one another—what has happened within the last few hours which has changed all my prospects in life: but so it is, that I have learned something which forces me to give up the plans which I had formed, and many vain and ambitious hopes in which I had been indulging. I have written and despatched a letter to Sir Francis Clavering, saying that I cannot accept his seat in Parliament until after my marriage; in like manner I cannot and will not accept any larger fortune with you than that which has always belonged to you since your grandfather's death and the birth of your half-brother. Your good mother is not in the least aware—I hope she never may be—of the reasons which force me to this very strange decision. They arise from a painful circumstance, attributable to none of our faults; but, having once befallen, they are as fatal and irreparable as that shock which overset honest Alnaschar's porcelain and shattered all his hopes beyond the power of mending. I write gaily enough, for there is no use in bewailing such a hopeless mischance. We have not drawn the great prize in the lottery, dear Blanche: but I shall be contented enough without it, if you can be so; and I repeat, with all my heart, that I will do my best to make you happy.

"And now, what news shall I give you? My uncle is very unwell and takes my refusal of the seat in Parliament in sad dudgeon: the scheme was his, poor old gentleman, and he naturally bemoans its failure. But Warrington, Laura, and I had a council of war: they know this awful secret and back me in my decision. You must love George as you love what is generous and upright and noble; and as for Laura—she must be our Sister, Blanche, our Saint, our good Angel. With two such friends at home, what need we care for the world without; or who is member for Clavering, or who is asked or not asked to the great balls of the season?"

To this frank communication came back the letter from Blanche to Laura, and one to Pen himself, which perhaps his own letter justified. "You are spoiled by the world," Blanche wrote; "you do not love your poor Blanche as she would be loved, or you would not offer thus lightly to take her or to leave her, no, Arthur, you love me not—a man of the world, you have given me your plighted troth, and are ready to redeem it; but that entire affection, that love whole and abiding, where—where is that vision of my youth? I am but a pastime of your life, and I would be its all;—but a fleeting thought, and I would be your whole soul. I would have our two hearts one; but ah, my Arthur, how lonely yours is! how little you give me of it! You speak of our parting with a smile on your lip; of our meeting, and you care not to hasten it! Is life but a disillusion, then, and are the flowers of our garden faded away? I have wept—I have prayed—I have passed sleepless hours—I have shed bitter, bitter tears over your letter! To you I bring the gushing poesy of my being—the yearnings of the soul that longs to be loved—that pines for love, love, love, beyond all!—that flings itself at your feet, and cries, Love me, Arthur! Your heart beats no quicker at the kneeling appeal of my love!—your proud eye is dimmed by no tear of sympathy!—you accept my soul's treasure as though 'twere dross! not the pearls from the unfathomable deeps of affection! not the diamonds from the caverns of the heart. You treat me like a slave, and bid me bow to my master! Is this the guerdon of a free maiden—is this the price of a life's passion? Ah me! when was it otherwise? when did love meet with aught but disappointment? Could I hope (fond fool!) to be the exception to the lot of my race; and lay my fevered brow on a heart that comprehended my own? Foolish girl that I was! One by one, all the flowers of my young life have faded away; and this, the last, the sweetest, the dearest, the fondly, the madly loved, the wildly cherished—where is it? But no more of this. Heed not my bleeding heart.—Bless you, bless you always, Arthur!

"I will write more when I am more collected. My racking brain renders thought almost impossible. I long to see Laura! She will come to us directly we return from the country, will she not? And you, cold one!"

"The words of this letter were perfectly clear, and written in Blanche's neatest hand upon her scented paper; and yet the meaning of the composition not a little puzzled Pen. Did Blanche mean to accept or to refuse his polite offer? Her phrases either meant that Pen did not love her, and she declined him, or that she took him, and sacrificed herself to him, cold as he was. He laughed sardonically over the letter, and over the transaction which occasioned it. He laughed to think how Fortune had jilted him, and how he deserved his slippery fortune. He turned over and over the musky gilt-edged riddle. It amused his humour: he enjoyed it as if it had been a funny story.

He was thus seated, twiddling the queer manuscript in his hand, joking grimly to himself, when his servant came in with a card from a gentleman who wished to speak to him very particularly. And if Pen had gone out into the passage, he would have seen, sucking his stick, rolling his eyes, and showing great marks of anxiety, his old acquaintance, Mr. Samuel Huxter.

"Mr. Huxter on particular business! Pray, beg Mr. Huxter to come in," said Pen, amused rather; and not the less so when poor Sam appeared before him.

"Pray take a chair, Mr. Huxter," said Pen, in his most superb manner. "In what way can I be of service to you?"

"I had rather not speak before the flunk—before the man, Mr. Pendennis:" on which Mr. Arthur's attendant quitted the room.

"I'm in a fix," said Mr. Huxter, gloomily.

"Indeed."

"She sent me to you," continued the young surgeon.

"What, Fanny? Is she well? I was coming to see her, but I have had a great deal of business since my return to London."

"I heard of you through my governor and Jack Hobnell," broke in Huxter. "I wish you joy, Mr. Pendennis, both of the borough and the lady, sir. Fanny wishes you joy, too," he added, with something of a blush.

"There's many a slip between the cup and the lip! Who knows what may happen, Mr. Huxter, or who will sit in Parliament for Clavering next session?"

"You can do anything with my governor," continued Mr. Huxter. "You got him Clavering Park. The old boy was very much pleased, sir, at your calling him in. Hobnell wrote me so. Do you think you could speak to the governor for me, Mr. Pendennis?"

"And tell him what?"

"I've gone and done it, sir," said Huxter, with a particular look.

"You—you don't mean to say you have—you have done any wrong to that dear little creature, sir?" said Pen, starting up in a great fury.

"I hope not," said Huxter, with a hangdog look: "but I've married her. And I know there will be an awful shindy at home. It was agreed that I should be taken into partnership when I had passed the College, and it was to have been Huxter and Son. But I would have it, confound it. It's all over now, and the old boy's wrote me that he's coming up to town for drugs: he will be here to-morrow, and then it must all come out."

"And when did this event happen?" asked Pen, not over well pleased, most likely, that a person who had once attracted some portion of his royal good graces should have transferred her allegiance, and consoled herself for his loss.

"Last Thursday was five weeks—it was two days after Miss Amory came to Shepherd's Inn," Huxter answered.

Pen remembered that Blanche had written and mentioned her visit. "I was called in," Huxter said. "I was in the Inn looking after old Cos's leg; and about something else too, very likely: and I met Strong, who told me there was a woman taken ill in Chambers, and went up to give her my professional services. It was the old lady who attends Miss Amory—her housekeeper, or some such thing. She was taken with strong hysterics: I found her kicking and screaming like a good one—in Strong's chamber, along with him and Colonel Altamont, and Miss Amory crying and as pale as a sheet; and Altamont fuming about—a regular kick-up. They were two hours in the Chambers; and the old woman went whooping off in a cab. She was much worse than the young one. I called in Grosvenor Place next day to see if I could be of any service, but they were gone without so much as thanking me: and the day after I had business of my own to attend to—a bad business too," said Mr. Huxter, gloomily. "But it's done, and can't be undone; and we must make the best of it."

She has known the story for a month, thought Pen, with a sharp pang of

Arthur felt the train hurtling toward Tunbridge with a speed that seemed designed to rush him past the moment of reckoning, yet his mind was a stationary courtroom where every verdict of his past was being read aloud. He reviewed the ledger of his life with a grim satisfaction, acknowledging that his selfishness and waywardness had led him to a dead end. "Here is the end of hopes and aspirations," he muttered, watching the landscape blur. "Romance and ambition have both failed me. Whether I yield or I obstinately refuse, the result is the same: misfortune. My mother begs, and I refuse an angel! Had I taken her, Laura would never have been an angel to me. I could not have given my heart at another's instigation; I never would have known her true nature had I been forced to ask a third party to interpret her virtues."

He thought of Uncle Strong, the man who had guaranteed Blanche's fortune, a seat in Parliament, and a career. Now, fortune had arrived, leaving him with a wife but no dowry, a compensation for a heart he had foolishly withheld. "Why was I not more honest?" he asked the empty carriage. "Or am I less so? It would have cost my poor old uncle no pangs to accept Blanche's money whencesoever it came. He is bitter, heart-stricken, almost indignant at my scruples. I dissatisfy everyone. A maimed, weak, imperfect wretch, it seems I am unequal to any fortune. I make no one happy. What prospect is there for this poor little frivolous girl who must take my obscure name and share my poverty? I have no ambition to excite me, no self-esteem to console myself, much less her. If I wrote a book that sold twenty editions, I would be the first to sneer at my reputation. If I succeeded at the Bar, achieving wealth by bullying witnesses and twisting evidence, is that a fame that satisfies my longings? A calling where my life is well spent?"

His gaze drifted to the priest opposite, who never lifted his eyes from his breviary, except when they passed through Reigate Tunnel. "How I wish I could be that priest," Arthur thought, "or that old gentleman next him, who scowls with hatred over his newspaper. The priest shuts his eyes to the world but finds his directory in the book of the world to come. His neighbor hates him as a monster, a tyrant, a persecutor, yet fancies burning martyrs. These men have no doubts; they march on trustfully, bearing their load of logic."

"Would you like to look at the paper, sir?" interjected the stout gentleman, offering a journal with a flaming article against the black-coated clergyman. Pen thanked him, took the paper, and continued his reverie without reading a single sentence.

"And yet, would you take either of those men's creeds, with its consequences?" he mused. "Ah me! You must bear your own burden, fashion your own faith, think your own thoughts, and pray your own prayer. To what mortal ear could I tell all, if I had a mind? Or who could understand all? Who can tell another's shortcomings, lost opportunities, weigh the passions that overpower, the defects that incapacitate reason? What extent of truth and right is his neighbor's mind organized to perceive? What invisible accident, terror of youth, or chance of fortune may have altered the whole current of life? A grain of sand may alter it; the flinging of a pebble may end it. Who can weigh circumstances, passions, temptations that go to our good and evil account save One, before whose awful wisdom we kneel, and at whose mercy we ask absolution?"

"This day or tomorrow will wind up the account of my youth," Pen decided, his head falling onto his breast. "A weary retrospect, alas! a sad history, with many a page I would fain not look back on! But who has not been tired or fallen, and who has escaped without scars from that struggle?" His heart prostrated itself humbly and sadly before that Throne where wisdom, love, and pity for all reside, making its confession. "What matters about fame or poverty!" he thought. "If I marry this woman I have chosen, may I have strength and will to be true to her, and to make her happy. If I have children, pray God teach me to speak and do the truth among them, and to leave them an honest name. There are no splendours for my marriage. Does my life deserve any? I begin a new phase of it; a better than the last may it be, I pray Heaven!"

The train stopped at Tunbridge just as Pen finished his reflections. He handed the newspaper back to his neighbor, took his leave, and jumped out of the carriage with his carpet-bag in hand, briskly determined to face his fortune.

A fly carried him rapidly to Lady Clavering's house. As he was transported thither, Arthur composed a little speech in his head, an oration intended for Blanche. It was virtuous, honest, and well-minded, the best a man of his turn of mind could utter under the circumstances. The purport was clear: "Blanche, I cannot understand from your last letter what your meaning is, or whether my fair and frank proposal is acceptable. I think you know the reason which induces me to forgo the worldly advantages a union with you offered, advantages I could not accept without being dishonored. If you doubt my affection, here I am ready to prove it. Let Smirke be called in, and let us be married out of hand. With all my heart I purpose to keep my vow, to cherish you through life, and to be a true and loving husband."

From the fly, Arthur sprang to the hall-door, where he was met by a domestic he did not know. The man seemed surprised at the approach of the gentleman with the carpet-bag, making no attempt to take it. "Her Ladyship's not at home, sir," the man remarked.

"I am Mr. Pendennis," Arthur said. "Where is Lightfoot?"

"Lightfoot is gone," answered the man. "My Lady is out, and my orders was—"

"I hear Miss Amory's voice in the drawing-room," Arthur interrupted. "Take the bag to a dressing-room, if you please." Passing the porter, he walked straight toward that apartment, from which, as the door opened, a warble of melodious notes issued.

Their little Siren was at her piano, singing with all her might and fascinations. Master Clavering was asleep on the sofa, indifferent to the music; but near Blanche sat a gentleman who was perfectly enraptured with her strain, which was of a passionate and melancholy nature.

As the door opened, the gentleman started up with a "Hullo!" The music stopped with a little shriek from the singer. Frank Clavering woke from the sofa, and Arthur stepped forward, saying, "What, Foker! how do you do, Foker?" He looked at the piano, and there, by Miss Amory's side, was just such another purple-leather box as he had seen in Harry's hand three days before, when the heir of Logwood was coming out of a jeweller's shop in Waterloo Place. It was opened, and curled round the white satin cushion within was a magnificent serpentine bracelet, with a blazing ruby head and diamond tail!

"How de-do, Pendennis?" said Foker. Blanche made many motions of the shoulders, giving signs of unrest and agitation. She put her handkerchief over the bracelet, then advanced, with a hand trembling very much, to greet Pen.

"How is dearest Laura?" she said. The face of Foker, looking up from his profound mourning—that face so piteous and puzzled—was one the reader's imagination must depict for himself. Also that of Master Frank Clavering, who, looking at the three interesting individuals with an expression of the utmost knowingness, had only time to ejaculate, "Here's a jolly go!" and to disappear sniggering.

Pen, too, had restrained himself up to that minute; but looking still at Foker, whose ears and cheeks tingled with blushes, Arthur burst out into a fit of laughter, so wild and loud that it frightened Blanche more than any serious exhibition.

"And this was the secret, was it? Don't blush and turn away, Foker, my boy. Why, man, you are a pattern of fidelity. Could I stand between Blanche and such constancy—could I stand between Miss Amory and fifteen thousand a year?"

"It is not that, Mr. Pendennis," Blanche said, with great dignity. "It is not money, it is not rank, it is not gold that moves me; but it is constancy, it is fidelity, it is a whole trustful loving heart offered to me, that I treasure—yes, that I treasure!" She made for her handkerchief, but, reflecting what was underneath it, she paused. "I do not disown, I do not disguise—my life is above disguise—to him on whom it is bestowed, my heart must be forever bare—that I once thought I loved you, yes, thought I was beloved by you, I own! How I clung to that faith! How I strove, I prayed, I longed to believe it! But your conduct always—your own words so cold, so heartless, so unkind, have undeceived me. You trifled with the heart of the poor maiden! You flung me back with scorn the troth which I had plighted! I have explained all—all to Mr. Foker."

"That you have," said Foker, with devotion and conviction in his looks.

"What, all?" said Pen, with a meaning look at Blanche. "It is I am in fault, is it? Well, well, Blanche, be it so. I won't appeal against your sentence, and bear it in silence. I came down here looking to very different things, Heaven knows, and with a heart most truly and kindly disposed towards you. I hope you may be happy with another, as, on my word, it was my wish to make you so; and I hope my honest old friend here will have a wife worthy of his loyalty, his constancy, and affection. Indeed they deserve the regard of any woman—even Miss Blanche Amory. Shake hands, Harry; don't look askance at me. Has anybody told you that I was a false and heartless character?"

"I think you're a——" Foker was beginning, in his wrath, when Blanche interposed.

"Henry, not a word!—I pray you let there be forgiveness!"

"You're an angel, by Jove, you're an angel!" said Foker, at which Blanche looked seraphically up to the chandelier.

"In spite of what has passed, for the sake of what has passed, I must always regard Arthur as a brother," the seraph continued. "We have known each other years, we have trodden the same fields, and plucked the same flowers together. Arthur! Henry! I beseech you to take hands and to be friends! Forgive you!—I forgive you, Arthur, with my heart I do. Should I not do so for making me so happy?"

"There is only one person of us three whom I pity, Blanche," Arthur said, gravely, "and I say to you again, that I hope you will make this good fellow, this honest and loyal creature, happy."

"Happy! O Heavens!" said Harry. He could not speak. His happiness gushed out at his eyes. "She don't know—she can't know how fond I am of her, and—and who am I? a poor little beggar, and she takes me up and says she'll try and I—I—love me. I ain't worthy of so much happiness. Give us your hand, old boy, since she forgives you after your heartless conduct, and says she loves you. I'll make you welcome. I tell you I'll love everybody who loves her. By——, if she tells me to kiss the ground I'll kiss it. Tell me to kiss the ground! I say, tell me. I love you so. You see I love you so."

Blanche looked up seraphically again. Her gentle bosom heaved. She held out one hand as if to bless Harry, and then royally permitted him to kiss it. She took up the pocket-handkerchief and hid her own eyes as the other fair hand was abandoned to poor Harry's tearful embrace.

"I swear that is a villain who deceives such a loving creature as that," said Pen.

Blanche laid down the handkerchief and put hand No. 2 softly on Foker's head, which was bent down kissing and weeping over hand No. 1. "Foolish boy?" she said. "It shall be loved as it deserves: who could help loving such a silly creature!"

And at this moment Frank Clavering broke in upon the sentimental trio.

"I say, Pendennis!" he said.

"Well, Frank!"

"The man wants to be paid, and go back. He's had some beer."

"I'll go back with him," cried Pen. "Good-bye, Blanche. God bless you, Foker, old friend. You know, neither of you want me here." He longed to be off that instant.

"Stay—I must say one word to you. One word in private, if you please," Blanche said. "You can trust us together, can't you, Henry?" The tone in which the word Henry was spoken, and the appeal, ravished Foker with delight. "Trust you!" said he. "Oh, who wouldn't trust you! Come along, Franky, my boy."

"Let's have a cigar," said Frank, as they went into the hall.

"She don't like it," said Foker, gently.

"Law bless you—she don't mind. Pendennis used to smoke regular," said the candid youth.

"It was but a short word I had to say," said Blanche to Pen, with great calm, when they were alone. "You never loved me, Mr. Pendennis."

"I told you how much," said Arthur. "I never deceived you."

"I suppose you will go back and marry Laura

Upon the platform at Tunbridge, Pen fumed and fretted until the evening train finally lumbered into London, a journey that stretched in his agitated mind from a mere half-hour into six agonizing hours. Yet, even this immense interval dissolved as the train sped on, the London lights blurring into view. A gentleman who had forgotten his carpet-bag in the carriage rushed out, hailed a cab, and barked, "Drive as hard as you can to Jermyn Street!" The cabman, though a hansom driver, accepted the gratuity with a cheerful grin. Pen sprinted up the hotel stairs to Lady Rockminster's apartments.

Laura sat alone in the drawing-room, reading by the lamp with a pale, anxious face. She looked up as Pen burst through the door. *May we follow him?* The great moments of life are often decided in seconds. A single look, a pressure of the hand, or a quiver of the lip can seal one's fate.

Lady Rockminster, having just risen from her after-dinner nap, entered the sitting-room. "Upon my word, young people!" she exclaimed, her attendant casting wondering eyes over her shoulder. The young people were indeed in an attitude; Pen stood in a position every young lady who reads this has heard tell of, or seen, or hoped to see.

Directly he entered, Pen rushed to Laura, whose pale face hadn't yet registered the question, *What, back so soon?* Seizing her outstretched, trembling hand just as she rose from her chair, he fell to his knees before her and cried, "I have seen her. She has engaged herself to Harry Foker—and—and Now, Laura?"

The hand gave a pressure; the eyes beamed a reply; the quivering lips answered, though speechless. Pen's head sank into the girl's lap as he sobbed, "Come and bless us, dear mother," and arms as tender as Helen's once more enfolded him.

It was in this dramatic juncture that Lady Rockminster appeared, saying, "Upon my word, young people! Beck! leave the room. What do you want poking your nose in here?"

Pen sprang up with looks of triumph, still clutching Laura's hand. "She is consoling me for my misfortune, ma'am," he declared.

"What do you mean by kissing her hand? I don't know what you will be next doing."

Pen kissed her Ladyship's hand. "I have been to Tunbridge," he said, adopting a tragic air, "and seen Miss Amory; and find on my arrival that—that a villain has transplanted me in her affections."

"Is that all? Is that what you were whimpering on your knees about?" the old lady asked, growing angry. "You might have kept the news till tomorrow."

"Yes—another has superseded me," Pen continued. "But why call him a villain? He is brave, he is constant, he is young, he is wealthy, he is beautiful."

"What stuff are you talking, sir?" cried the old lady. "What has happened?"

"Miss Amory has jilted me, and accepted Henry Foker, Esq. I found her warbling ditties to him as he lay at her feet; presents had been accepted, vows exchanged, these ten days. Harry was old Mrs. Planter's rheumatism, which kept dearest Laura out of the house. He is the most constant and generous of men. He has promised the living of Logwood to Lady Ann's husband, and given her a splendid present on her marriage; and he rushed to fling himself at Blanche's feet the instant he found he was free."

"And so, as you can't get Blanche, you put up with Laura; is that it, sir?" asked the old lady.

"He acted nobly," Laura said softly.

"I acted as she bade me," Pen replied. "Never mind how, Lady Rockminster; but to the best of my knowledge and power. And if you mean that I am not worthy of Laura, I know it, and pray Heaven to better me; and if the love and company of the best and purest creature in the world can do so, at least I shall have these to help me."

"Hm, hm," replied the old lady, looking with a rather appeased air at the young people. "It is all very well; but I should have preferred Bluebeard."

To divert the conversation from a theme growing painful to some, Pen bethought himself of his interview with Huxter in the morning and Fanny Bolton's affairs, which he had forgotten under the immediate pressure of his own excitement. He told the ladies how Huxter had elevated Fanny to the rank of wife and the terrors he faced regarding his father's arrival. He described the scene with considerable humor, dwelling especially on Fanny's coquetry and irrepressible desire to captivate mankind. His meaning was clear: *You see, Laura, I was not so guilty in that little affair; it was the girl who made love to me, and I who resisted. As I am no longer present, the little siren practices her arts upon others. Let that transaction be forgotten in your mind, if you please; or visit me with a very gentle punishment for my error.*

Laura understood his meaning under the eagerness of his explanations. "If you did any wrong, you repented, dear Pen," she said. "And you know," she added, with meaning eyes and blushes, "that I have no right to reproach you."

"Hm!" grumbled the old lady. "I should have preferred Bluebeard."

"The past is broken away. The morrow is before us. I will do my best to make your morrow happy, dear Laura," Pen said. His heart was humbled by the prospect of his happiness; it stood awestruck in the contemplation of her sweet goodness and purity. He liked his wife better that she had owned to that passing feeling for Warrington and laid bare her generous heart to him. And she—very likely thinking, *How strange it is that I ever should have cared for another! I am vexed almost to think I care for him so little, am so little sorry that he is gone away. Oh, in these past two months how I have learned to love Arthur! I care about nothing but Arthur: my waking and sleeping thoughts are about him; he is never absent from me. And to think that he is to be mine, mine! and that I am to marry him, and not to be his servant as I expected to be only this morning; for I would have gone down on my knees to Blanche to beg her to let me live with him. And now—Oh, it is too much. Oh, mother! mother, that you were here!* Indeed, she felt as if Helen were there—by her actually, though invisibly. A halo of happiness beamed from her.

She moved with a different step and bloomed with a new beauty. Arthur saw the change; and the old Lady Rockminster remarked it with her shrewd eyes.

"What a sly demure little wretch you have been," she whispered to Laura—while Pen, in great spirits, was laughing and telling his story about Huxter—"and how you have kept your secret!"

"How are we to help the young couple?" said Laura. Of course Miss Laura felt an interest in all young couples, as generous lovers always love other lovers.

"We must go and see them," said Pen.

"Of course we must go and see them," said Laura. "I intend to be very fond of Fanny. Let us go this instant. Lady Rockminster, may I have the carriage?"

"Go now!—why, you stupid creature, it is eleven o'clock at night. Mr. and Mrs. Huxter have got their nightcaps on, I dare say. And it is time for you to go now. Good night, Mr. Pendennis."

Arthur and Laura begged for ten minutes more.

"We will go to-morrow morning, then. I will come and fetch you with Martha."

"An earl's coronet," said Pen, who, no doubt, was pleased himself, "will have a great effect in Lamb Court and Smithfield. Stay—Lady Rockminster, will you join us in a little conspiracy?"

"How do you mean conspiracy, young man?"

"Will you please to be a little ill to-morrow; and when old Mr. Huxter arrives, will you let me call him in? If he is put into a good humour at the notion of attending a baronet in the country, what influence won't a countess have on him? When he is softened—when he is quite ripe, we will break the secret upon him; bring in the young people, extort the paternal benediction, and finish the comedy."

"A parcel of stuff," said the old lady. "Take your hat, sir. Come away, miss. There—my head is turned another way. Good night, young people." And who knows but the old lady thought of her own early days as she went away on Laura's arm, nodding her head and humming to herself?

With the early morning came Laura and Martha according to appointment; and the desired sensation was, let us hope, effected in Lamb Court, whence the three proceeded to wait upon Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Huxter, at their residence in Charterhouse Lane.

The two ladies looked at each other with great interest, and not a little emotion on Fanny's part. She had not seen her "guardian," as she was pleased to call Pen in consequence of his bequest, since the event had occurred which had united her to Mr. Huxter.

"Samuel told me how kind you had been," she said. "You were always very kind, Mr. Pendennis. And—and I hope your friend is better, who was took ill in Shepherd's Inn, ma'am."

"My name is Laura," said the other, with a blush. "I am—that is, I was—that is, I am Arthur's sister; and we shall always love you for being so good to him when he was ill. And when we live in the country, I hope we shall see each other. And I shall be always happy to hear of your happiness, Fanny."

"We are going to do what you and Huxter have done, Fanny.—Where is Huxter? What nice, snug lodgings you've got! What a pretty cat!"

While Fanny answered these questions in reply to Pen, Laura said to herself—*Well, now really! is this the creature about whom we were all so frightened? What could he see in her? She's a homely little thing, but such manners! Well, she was very kind to him,—bless her for that.*

Mr. Samuel had gone out to meet his Pa. Mrs. Huxter said that the old gentleman was to arrive that day at the Somerset Coffee-house, in the Strand; and Fanny confessed that she was in a sad tremor about the meeting. "If his parent casts him off, what are we to do?" she said. "I shall never pardon myself for bringing ruin on my husband's head. You must intercede for us, Mr. Arthur. If mortal man can, you can bend and influence Mr. Huxter senior." Fanny still regarded Pen in the light of a superior being, that was evident. No doubt Arthur thought of the past, as he marked the solemn little tragedy-airs and looks, the little ways, the little trepidations, vanities, of the little bride.

As soon as the interview was over, entered Messrs. Linton and Blades, who came, of course, to visit Huxter, and brought with them a fine fragrance of tobacco. They had watched the carriage at the baker's door and remarked the coronet with awe. They asked of Fanny who was that uncommonly heavy swell who had just driven off? and pronounced the countess was of the right sort. And when they heard that it was Mr. Pendennis and his sister, they remarked that Pen's father was only a sawbones; and that he gave himself confounded airs; they had been in Huxter's company on the night of his little altercation with Pen in the Back Kitchen.

Returning homewards through Fleet Street, and as Laura was just stating to Pen's infinite amusement that Fanny was very well, but that really there was no beauty in her,—there might be, but she could not see it,—as they were locked near Temple Bar, they saw young Huxter returning to his bride. "The governor had arrived; was at the Somerset Coffee-house—was in tolerable good-humour—something about the railway: but he had been afraid to speak about—about that business. Would Mr. Pendennis try it on?"

Pen said he would go and call at that moment upon Mr. Huxter, and see what might be done. Huxter junior would lurk outside whilst that awful interview took place. The coronet on the carriage inspired his soul also with wonder; and old Mr. Huxter himself beheld it with delight, as he looked from the coffee-house window on that Strand which it was always a treat to him to survey.

"And I can afford to give myself a lark, sir," said Mr. Huxter, shaking hands with Pen. "Of course you know the news? we have got our bill, sir. We shall have our branch line—our shares are up, sir—and we buy your three fields along the Brawl, and put a pretty penny into your pocket, Mr. Pendennis."

"Indeed!—that was good news." Pen remembered that there was a letter from Mr. Tatham, at Chambers, these three days; but he had not opened the communication, being interested with other affairs.

"I hope you don't intend to grow rich, and give up practice," said Pen. "We can't lose you at Clavering, Mr. Huxter; though I hear very good accounts of your son. My friend, Dr. Goodenough speaks most highly of his talents. It is hard that a man of your eminence, though, should be kept in a country town."

"The metropolis would have been my sphere of action, sir," said Mr. Huxter, surveying the Strand. "But a man takes

The calendar had turned a month, and the stage of our little country town had been thoroughly scrubbed and repainted. Frederic Lightfoot, formerly a humble *maitre d'hôtel* to the illustrious Sir Francis Clavering, had boldly declared his independence. He now stood as the proprietor of the Clavering Arms, a venture marketed with the grandeur of a royal proclamation. His manifesto promised "ancient comfort" refurbished with modern luxuries: a billiard room for the discerning, cellars stocked with wines chosen "without regard to expense" by a man named C.L., and stabling fit for the Dumplingbeare hounds. The scale of charges, he noted with a wink to the times, had been "regulated for all" to suit the "economical spirit of the present era."

Indeed, the inn buzzed with a lively, almost feverish energy. The gateway gleamed with fresh paint, the coffee-room windows sparkled with Christmas holly, and the magistrates had convened their petty sessions in the very card-room of the old Assembly. The farmers' ordinary was packed, the patrons delighted by Mrs. Lightfoot's exotic Indian curries and Mulligatawny soup, a culinary coup endorsed by Major Stokes, Captain Glanders, and the local gentry. Even the incorporation of the Clavering Institute's reading-room featured a dinner where the hostess's cheer was celebrated by Sir Francis Clavering himself, seated beside the esteemed Rector, Dr. Portman.

Amidst this festive chaos, the local press—the *Chatteris Champion, Clavering Agriculturist, and Baymouth Fisherman*—printed a rumor that smelled faintly of scandal and romance. They whispered that Arthur Pendennis had abandoned his candidacy for the borough, allegedly because Sir Francis Clavering's failing health necessitated a vacancy. The paper speculated that a "young gentleman of colossal fortune," related to the highest aristocracy and soon to wed a lovely lady connected to Clavering Park, would fill the seat. The paper hinted that Lady Clavering and Miss Amory had arrived for the holidays, promising festivities of a "peculiarly interesting nature" at the Park's commencement of the new year.

The reader, armed with this gossip, can piece together the missing links. Lady Rockminster, though initially grumbling at Laura's preference for Pendennis over the mysterious Bluebeard, ultimately approved. Knowing Bluebeard's secret, she realized Laura had no other choice. The kind old lady, who had effectively adopted Miss Bell, was delighted that her ward was fulfilling the ultimate destiny of young ladies: marriage. That very night, she informed her maid, Mrs. Beck, who, being kept in the loop by Martha of Fairoaks, was equally thrilled. Mrs. Beck calculated the finances—Pendennis's income plus the railroad dividends versus Miss Bell's dowry—and decided to intervene. She planned to speak to her nephew, George Pynsent, and ensure Pendennis received a promotion, all while arranging a visit to Major Pendennis to smooth the path.

At one o'clock, the Dowager Lady Rockminster arrived at the Major's residence. The Major, expecting news of a broken engagement between Pen and Miss Amory, was instead greeted by a noblewoman. Pen, having just met his new servant, Mr. Frosch, had hurried to the hotel coffee-room to write a note to his guardian. *"Dear uncle,"* he had penned, *"if there has been any question between us, it is over. I found Miss A. has bestowed herself upon Harry Foker, with his fifteen thousand a year. I came upon their loves and left him in possession. Also, Tatham writes he sold three of my fields to the Railroad at a great figure. Yours affectionately, A. P."*

The Major, receiving the news via Lady Rockminster, smiled with courtly grace. He admitted he didn't much regret the affair's conclusion, despite his initial wish for it. Lady Rockminster, playing her part as ambassadress, gently steered the conversation toward consolation. *"We must get him a wife,"* she urged. The Major's mind cleared; he saw the purpose of her visit. The negotiation was surprisingly easy. There was no reason Pen shouldn't marry according to his mother's wishes. Lady Rockminster bolstered the Major's resolve with intimations of weighty consequence—though we shall not detail them, lest we offend the living Countess, who remains a formidable force. Overwhelmed by her determined graciousness and her evident fondness for Laura, the Major capitulated.

When the Major finally met Laura and Pen, shaking hands with "unaffected emotion," Laura blushed with happiness, her eyes beaming with the light of love. The storyteller pauses here to hope that one day, the eyes of his young audience may shine so brightly too.

Meanwhile, the subplot of Harry Foker and Blanche Amory reached its climax. Blanche, having pressed her affections upon the blushing bridegroom with fifteen thousand a year, found herself in a whirlwind of joy. Lady Clavering, buoyed by the news, descended from Tunbridge, and the family erupted in happiness unseen for years. Milliners and jewellers were hired, and Sir Francis, benefiting from his wife's good humor, reconciled with her, returning to London to preside over his own table and gamble with a flush of money.

One evening, dining in Grosvenor Place, Major Pendennis and Arthur encountered Mr. James Morgan, now established as the major-domo. The Chevalier Strong was also present, in high spirits, recounting his amusements abroad. Under his breath, Strong confided to Arthur that Morgan looked "black as thunder" and was "about no good." Strong planned to slip away to wait for them at Hyde Park Gate. As the Major departed, Morgan muttered about accepting a temporary engagement with the Clavering family. Arthur, overhearing a look of hatred, feigned a forgotten handkerchief to retreat to the drawing-room.

There, Blanche was still lingering near her "siren." Pen exchanged a meaningful look with her, and when Blanche emerged to the door, she whispered urgently, *"Oh, Arthur—Mr. Pendennis—I want you to tell dear Laura something!"*

*"What is it?"* she asked, shutting the door.

*"I know it,"* she replied.

*"Morgan will,"* Pen warned.

*"No, he won't,"* Blanche insisted. *"I have promised him—n'importe. Wait until after our marriage."* She suddenly collapsed into despair. *"Oh, how wretched I am,"* she cried, her previous gaiety evaporating.

Arthur implored her to tell Harry immediately. *"Tell him to-night. It is no fault of yours. He will pardon you anything."* Blanche, however, handed him a note from Strong and fled inside, singing and kissing her hand as Morgan ascended the carpeted stairs.

Pen joined his uncle, recounting the drama. The Major, with a shudder, advised them to leave it alone. *"Begad, let us be thankful that we are out of the business,"* he said. *"Miss Amory is a dev'lish wide-awake girl, sir, and must play her own cards."* Strong, attempting to interject, was firmly shut down by the Major, who clung to his nephew's arm, steering clear of the subject entirely.

Outside, Strong laughed as he met them. *"I'm an old soldier, too,"* he said. *"I congratulate you on your marriage and your escape. That rascal Morgan means mischief. He has been lurking about, found out Amory's secret, and bribed the inn porter. He's got into Clavering's service on the strength of his information. He will get very good pay for it."*

Strong explained that Amory was currently in Boulogne, having lost his temper and been thrashed by Strong at Baden for his insolence. *"He broke the bank several nights,"* Strong recounted, *"and was the fable of the place. He lied himself with a fellow named Bloundell, gathering a society of sharpers. I had to thrash him one day within an inch of his life. He begged my pardon afterward, but I couldn't live with him. I pawned everything to pay him what I owed, then went to roulette with my last ten florins. If I had lost, I should have let him shoot me. By Jove, sir, isn't it a shame that a man like me... shouldn't be able to turn his hand to anything?"*

Strong produced a handsome card for *Strong and Company*, sole agents of the celebrated Diamond Manzanilla and Toboso hams. *"Come and taste 'em,"* he urged. Pen laughed, noting he wouldn't be allowed to bachelors' parties soon.

The narrative then shifted to the Clavering Arms, where Harry Foker was in a state of ecstatic intoxication. He treated everyone to drink, dancing around Madame Fribsby, who sat pensively in the bar. Young Lightfoot, now in command of the cellar, was a tippling mess from morning till night, a piteous sight for his fond wife. Morgan, the new butler, watched Lightfoot's vagaries with savage sneers, making Mrs. Lightfoot doubly uncomfortable.

Foker, however, felt invincible. Yet, a shadow fell over him. A letter bearing the Boulogne postmark arrived for Mrs. Lightfoot. When her drunken husband seized it, screaming *"Who's J. A.?",* Foker burst in, shouting, *"Hands off, man alive!"* He snatched the letter, realizing it was for Miss Amory.

*"He's comin'—dammy, who's a-comin'?"* Lightfoot shrieked.

Mrs. Lightfoot, terrified, grabbed her bonnet and ran to the lodge-gate. Foker, reeling, saw a running figure vanish. He stopped, asking if it was Mrs. Bonner, his vision swimming.

Inside the Park, Lady Clavering was in the breakfast room with her son, Frank. *"What's the matter, my dear? What makes you so pale, Harry?"* she asked.

*"Where is Blanche!"* Harry cried in a sickening voice.

Blanche descended, looking pale but eager. Foker handed her the letter. *"I don't know—I should like to know,"* he stammered. *"Has Mrs. Bonner not told you?"*

Lady Clavering took the letter, her limbs shaking. As she read the superscription, she dropped it, clutching her son and bursting into hysterics. *"It's impossible!"* she screamed.

Blanche, with a ghastly smile, claimed the letter was from a poor pensioner. But Foker demanded to see it. *"Read it—and look at her!"* Blanche cried, pointing to her mother.

Foker read the letter aloud:

*"I have not wrote, my darling Betsy, this three weeks; but this is to give her a father's blessing... I don't know whether I quite approve of your throwing over Mr. P. for Mr. F., and don't think Foker's such a pretty name... But he has got the rowdy, which is the thing. So no more, my dear little Betsy, till we meet, from your affectionate father, J. Amory Altamont."*

The revelation shattered the room. Lady Clavering screamed that they had made an outcast of her son. *"They've dishonoured your old mother,"* she sobbed. *"I'm innocent, Frank; before God, I'm innocent."*

Foker, generous as ever, kissed her hand. *"Generous, generous Harry!"* Blanche cried. But Harry withdrew, his lip quivering. *"That's different,"* he said. *"It was for her sake—for her sake, Harry."*

Harry exploded. *"Everything's talked about in London. I knew that your father had come to grief. You don't think it was—it was for your connexion I married you? D—— it all! I've loved you with all my heart and soul for two years, and you've been playing with me, and cheating me!"* He covered his face and sobbed.

Lady Clavering, distraught, offered her fortune and disinherited Frank to save Blanche. *"Take her, take everything I have,"* she begged. *"This boy's disinherited."*

Frank, terrified, cried out. *"You ain't serious, Jemima? You don't mean that?"*

Clavering, the magistrate, roared, *"I'll commit him!"* He revealed the truth: Amory was a "d——d escaped convict scoundrel" who had forged his father-in-law's name, killed a man in Botany Bay, and robbed him years ago. *"And Pendennis knows it, and Strong knows it, and that d——d Morgan knows it, and she knows it, ever so long; and I never would tell it, never: and I kept it from my wife."*

Blanche, horrified, tried to approach her mother, but Lady Clavering shrank away. *"Don't touch me,"* she said. *"You've no heart; you never had. I see why that coward was going to give up his place in Parliament to Arthur... and why you threatened that you would make me give you half Frank's fortune."* She accused Blanche of leaving Arthur for Harry. *"Have nothing to do with her, Harry. You're good, you are. Don't marry that—that convict's daughter."*

Blanche, suddenly feeling exultant, declared she had no heart for her mother's shame. *"I appeal to my country's laws—yes, my country's laws! The persecuted one returns this day. I desire to go to my father."*

As a chaise approached, the women shrieked, expecting Amory. Instead, the door opened to reveal Mr. Morgan announcing Major Pendennis and Arthur Pendennis.

The following afternoon, Harry Foker rode away from the Park, followed by his groom in mourning. The transaction was over. Pen walked through the dark avenue
